SQL Server 2025 & T-SQL 15 chapters · 119 topics · 284 code samples · works offline

Orientation — Scope, Editions and Tooling

Scope and intended audienceEditions, versions and the support lifecycleCU and GDR: the SQL Server 2022+ update modelPreview-only features and the PREVIEW_FEATURES gateToolchain: SSMS, Azure Data Studio, sqlcmd, sqlpackage, bcpHow to read T-SQL in this guide

This chapter establishes the boundaries of the guide: which product versions it describes, which editions change which limits, what counts as production-ready versus preview, and the client tools used to author, deploy and verify the scripts. Everything after this chapter assumes the terminology fixed here.

Scope and intended audience

0.1 Audience & Scope

The subject is the SQL Server 2025 database engine and Transact-SQL, with enough operational context to make language decisions defensible. It is reference documentation: declarative statements of what the product does, in which version, and where it fails, rather than a narrative course.

The guide targets readers who already write application code and are being handed responsibility for a SQL Server schema. That implies the reader can read a function signature and a stack trace, and does not need explanations of what a database or a result set is. It does imply the reader is not yet fluent in the engine, and that is where the guide concentrates: the parts of SQL Server that differ from other relational databases, and the parts where a syntactically correct statement is still a design error.

In scopeOut of scope for this chapter
T-SQL grammar, statements and procedural constructsClient application languages and data access libraries
System data types, collation and conversion semanticsReporting, analytics and modelling layers above the database
DDL, constraints and the schema object catalogueMachine learning and external data services
Query formulation, joins, set operations and window functionsObject-relational mapping and ORM behaviour
Version and edition differences that change the aboveLicensing, procurement and cost governance

Index design, execution plans, transactions and locking, security, and backup and recovery are not summaries here; each has its own chapter in the later part of the guide. Where those chapters exist, this part cross-references them instead of restating them.

Applies to. The version target for the whole guide is SQL Server 2025 (17.x), generally available on 18 November 2025, RTM build 17.0.1000.7. Every statement about behaviour is written against that release unless the text says otherwise, and features are labelled with the version that introduced them.

The reference model used throughout the guide is a small order-entry schema so that examples accumulate meaning instead of resetting in every chapter. It is introduced in section 3.2 and consists of four tables:

TablePurposeKey columns used in examples
dbo.CuOne row per trading accountCusto, Custom, Na, Em, Credi, Row
dbo.PCatalogue of sellable itemsProd, S, Unit, IsAc
dbo.SalOrder header, one row per orderSalesO, Custo, Orde, Sta, Total
dbo.SalesOrder lines, composite key on order and line numberSalesO, Lin, Prod, Quan, Unit

The schema is intentionally conventional rather than exemplary. Where a column in the reference model is the wrong choice, the text says so, because the point of the reference model is to have somewhere concrete to make the point from.

Editions, versions and the support lifecycle

0.2 Lifecycle Support

SQL Server ships in editions that differ in licensed capacity, not in language. The 2025 edition changed several capacity ceilings, and two of those changes remove capabilities that earlier editions still have. Knowing which edition a statement is qualified by is therefore part of reading it correctly.

Edition2025 capacity limitsNotes
ExpressMaximum database size 50 GBExpress with Advanced Services is discontinued in 2025; the plain Express edition remains
Standard4 sockets / 32 cores, 256 GB buffer poolResource Governor is now available in Standard
EnterpriseNo socket, core or buffer pool capAlso the only edition that ships the full advanced security and machine learning feature set
DeveloperFunctionally equivalent to EnterpriseFree for development and test only; not licensed for production
EvaluationFeature-equivalent to EnterpriseExpires; do not design against it

Edition limits are a capacity ceiling, not a feature switch. T-SQL is identical across editions. Statements that behave differently by edition do so because a component is absent, not because the parser changes.

Three predecessor major versions remain in support alongside 2025, and a solution on a mixed estate must be written to the intersection of their behaviour:

Major versionEngine build familyStatus
SQL Server 202517.xCurrent; GA 18 November 2025, RTM 17.0.1000.7
SQL Server 202216.xSupported
SQL Server 201915.xSupported
SQL Server 201714.xSupported

Two statements in this guide are the direct consequence of a support decision rather than a language decision. First, time is a deprecated synonym for the rowve data type: the name survives, the meaning — a system-generated 8-byte binary value, not a clock reading — has nothing to do with time. Second, hot add CPU is deprecated in 2025, so a capacity plan that assumes adding sockets to a running Standard instance is planning against a feature on its way out.

Caveats. SQL Server 2025 introduces breaking changes in four areas that were previously routine: linked servers, replication, log shipping and PolyBase. Upgrade plans that rely on any of these must be validated against the 2025 release notes before the upgrade window opens, not during it. Separately, Data Quality Services (DQS) is discontinued, and SUSE Linux Enterprise Server is no longer a supported platform from 2025 onward; Red Hat Enterprise Linux remains the supported Linux platform.

There are three deployment paths, and the language is identical on all three. What differs is the operational envelope, so a design that is correct on a managed instance can be invalid on a virtual machine.

PathWhat is fixed by the platformWhat the customer controls
Azure SQL DatabaseEngine version and patching, high availability, backup retention, some DDLSchema, queries, indexes, service tiers, partitioning policy
Azure SQL Managed InstanceEngine version and patching, high availability, networking, operating systemAlmost everything an on-premises instance exposes, including the file system and the agent
SQL Server on an Azure virtual machineNothing beyond the hypervisorEverything: the whole platform is the customer’s responsibility

The T-SQL surface is the same, so the language in the following chapters applies unchanged. Where a feature is unavailable on one of these paths — a trace flag, a specific extended stored procedure, cross-database queries outside the instance — that is a platform restriction and is stated where it arises rather than here.

-- Server identity and build, as reported by the running instance.
SELECT @@VERSION AS FullVersionBanner;
GO
SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')          AS ProductVersion,      -- 17.0.1135.8
       SERVERPROPERTY('ProductLevel')           AS ProductLevel,        -- RTM, CU, GDR or SP
       SERVERPROPERTY('Edition')                AS Edition,
       SERVERPROPERTY('EngineEdition')         AS EngineEdition,
       SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel')     AS UpdateLevel,
       SERVERPROPERTY('UpdateLevel')            AS UpdateLevelRef,
       SERVERPROPERTY('EditionID')              AS EditionID,
       SERVERPROPERTY('MachineName')            AS MachineName,
       SERVERPROPERTY('OSDescription')          AS OSDescription,
       SERVERPROPERTY('IsClustered')            AS IsClustered;
GO

SERVERPROPERTY('EditionID')SERVERPROPERTY('EditionID') is the stable programmatic identifier and the only one safe to branch deployment logic on. @@VERSION@@VERSION is a formatted string and must never be parsed with LI or PARS in automation.

CU and GDR: the SQL Server 2022+ update model

0.3 Update Model

Cumulative Updates and General Distribution Releases are two separate channels of patching, and the split is a SQL Server 2022-and-later concept. Recognising the channel a build came from is what makes a production change decision auditable.

ChannelWhat it carriesCadence and current 2025 level
CU — Cumulative UpdateFixes, plus changes to the engine, query optimiser, replication and data engine internalsFrequent; the latest is CU9, build 17.0.5005.3, released 15 September 2026
GDR — General Distribution ReleaseSecurity-relevant fixes only, minimal behavioural changeOut of band; the latest is build 17.0.1135.8, released 8 September 2026
Service PackSuperseded as a channel by GDRNot produced for 2025; 2022 and later ship no service packs

A GDR build number is numerically higher than a CU build number from the same major version — 17.0.1135.8 against 17.0.5005.3 — because the build lines were numbered independently. Comparing build numbers numerically to decide which release is newer is wrong; use the release date.

The operational consequence is that a security-only GDR is a low-risk patch to apply during business hours, whereas a CU carries optimiser and engine changes and belongs in a change window with a tested rollback. The `Produc` and `ProductUp` properties report the channel, which is the value a release-management tool should read.

-- Report the patch channel, the install date and the target of the running instance.
SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')      AS Build,
       CASE
           WHEN SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel') = 1 THEN 'CU'
           WHEN SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel') = 2 THEN 'GDR'
           WHEN SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel') = 3 THEN 'RTM'
           ELSE 'SP'
       END                              AS Channel,
       SERVERPROPERTY('InstallDate')        AS InstalledOn,
       SERVERPROPERTY('IsIntegratedSecurityOnly') AS WindowsAuthOnly;
GO

-- Service level agreement status of the instance, from sys.dm_os_sys_info.
SELECT sqlserver_start_time, edition, product_level, update_level, is_clustered
FROM sys.dm_os_sys_info;
GO

ProductUp is the value to branch on: 1 is a cumulative update, 2 is a GDR, 3 is RTM. IsIntegrated returning 1 means the instance will refuse SQL authentication, which is the setting to check first when a deployment suddenly stops working.

Applies to. The CU/GDR split applies to SQL Server 2022 and later. SQL Server 2019 (15.x) and 2017 (14.x) still receive cumulative updates built on the service pack model, so a fleet spanning 2017 and 2025 will not have a single consistent update vocabulary. Deployment tooling that assumes one channel will misreport at least one instance in the estate.

Record Product and ProductUp for every instance into version control as part of the release script, not as an afterthought. The first question asked during any incident is which build is running, and answering it from a table beats answering it from a login banner.

Preview-only features and the PREVIEW_FEATURES gate

0.4 Preview Features

Several of the most discussed features of SQL Server 2025 are not generally available. They are gated behind a database scoped configuration called PREVIEW_, they are not supported for production, and their syntax or semantics can change before they ship. The guide labels each one where it appears; this section states the policy in one place.

SQL Server 2025 also delivers production features in the same release. Those are stated without a preview marker: broader UTF-8 support, ZSTD compression for backups, and optional non-blocking improvements to the consumption of locks and latches. The database scoped configuration PREVIEW_ is the switch that turns the preview set on for a given database.

-- Inspect the current state of the preview gate for this database.
SELECT name, value, value_for_replica, description
FROM sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE name = 'PREVIEW_FEATURES';
GO

-- Turn the preview feature set ON for this database. Reversible, and it is
-- the ONLY supported way to reach the preview syntax listed below.
ALTER DATABASE contoso_sales SET PREVIEW_FEATURES = ON;
GO

-- Put it back before the database returns to production traffic.
ALTER DATABASE contoso_sales SET PREVIEW_FEATURES = OFF;
GO

A database scoped configuration value is per-database, not per-instance. A preview object created in one database while the gate is on remains in that database and keeps working while the gate is on; it is not affected by the setting elsewhere.

Preview featureSurface syntaxStatus
Native vector typevec type declaration, CREATE VECTOR INDEXCREATE VECTOR INDEX, VECTORPreview only; not production-ready in 17.x
Half-precision vectors2-byte vector elementsPreview only; not production-ready in 17.x
Fuzzy string matchingEDIT_D, EDIT_DISTANCPreview only; not production-ready in 17.x
Fuzzy string matching, Jaro-WinklerJARO_WINKL, JARO_WINKLEPreview only; not production-ready in 17.x
Change event streamingStreaming change feed to Azure Event HubsPreview only; not production-ready in 17.x

Caveats. Preview features are not covered by support. Microsoft does not provide a service-level agreement for them, they may be removed in a later cumulative update without a deprecation cycle, and their syntax may be revised before general availability. Treat a schema that depends on a preview feature as a schema that must be migrated when the feature ships. Do not build a production table on vec on the assumption that it will simply become stable.

Cross-reference. The preview features appear in later chapters where their syntax is described in full, and each such description repeats the preview label. The chapter on security and administration covers how to audit for databases left with PREVIEW_ enabled.

Toolchain: SSMS, Azure Data Studio, sqlcmd, sqlpackage, bcp

0.5 Toolchain

Five tools cover essentially all T-SQL work: a graphical editor for authoring, a command-line client for automation, a schema comparison and deployment tool, a bulk data utility, and container images for reproducible environments. Choosing between them is a question of which one runs unattended.

ToolRoleNot suitable for
SQL Server Management Studio (SSMS)Authoring, debugging, execution plans, agent and server administrationUnattended deployment; it is a Windows desktop client with no scripting interface
Azure Data StudioCross-platform, notebook and dashboard aware T-SQL workspaceAnything requiring the full SSMS feature set; it is being superseded
sqlScripted execution, batch files, smoke tests, CI gatesInteractive exploration
SqlPa / sqlpa (DacFx)Schema and data extract, drift detection, deterministic publish, scripting as projectAd-hoc query execution; it is a deployment tool
bHigh-volume table load and unload, format files, scripting data movementSchema deployment; it has no concept of DDL dependencies
Container imagesReproducible developer and CI instances pinned to a specific buildProduction; the image is a single-instance configuration

SSMS versus Azure Data Studio is a genuine either-or: both provide a T-SQL editor, but SSMS is the administration surface and Azure Data Studio is the cross-platform editor. Neither is a deployment tool. Everything that must run unattended runs through sql or SqlPackageSqlPackage.

-- sqlcmd: the batch-file workhorse. -b makes the exit code non-zero on error,
-- which is what stops a CI step that has already failed.
sqlcmd -S tcp:prod-sql01,1433 -E -d contoso_sales -b `
  -i .\scripts\03_indexes.sql `
  -o .\artifacts\03_indexes.log `
  -l 30

-- Inline statement, and check the exit code yourself.
sqlcmd -S tcp:prod-sql01,1433 -E -d contoso_sales -b -Q "SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder;"
IF $LASTEXITCODE -ne 0 { throw "smoke test failed" }

-- Exit on the first error and run the whole file as one script:
sqlcmd -S tcp:prod-sql01,1433 -E -b -i .\scripts\full_install.sql -X 1

Without -b-b, sql reports errors on standard error but still exits with 0. Every script that gates a pipeline must use it, or the pipeline will report success on a script that failed on line four.

-- DacFx / SqlPackage: extract, then publish a script without executing it.
sqlpackage extract /Source:"Data Source=prod-sql01;Initial Catalog=contoso_sales;Integrated Security=true;Encrypt=true" `
           /TargetFile:.\src\Contoso.Sales.dacpac `
           /p:TargetDatabaseName=contoso_sales /p:ExtractTargetPlatform=SqlServer2025

sqlpackage migrate /Source:.\src\Contoso.Sales.dacpac `
             /Target:"Data Source=prod-sql01;Initial Catalog=contoso_sales;Integrated Security=true;Encrypt=true" `
             /OutputFile:.\artifacts\deploy.sql /p:TargetDatabasePlatform=SqlServer2025

sqlpackage publish /SourceFile:.\src\Contoso.Sales.dacpac `
              /Target:"Data Source=prod-sql01;Initial Catalog=contoso_sales;Integrated Security=true;Encrypt=true" `
              /p:BlockOnPossibleDataLoss=true

/p:BlockOnPossibleDataLoss=true/p:BlockOnPossibleDataLoss=true makes the publish fail rather than destroy data, and is the single most valuable switch in the tool. Generating with /OutputFile/OutputFile before publishing is the reviewable artifact: read the script, then run the script.

Applies to. DacFx targets the edition-neutral SQL Server surface, and the TargetDatab value above is a 2025-era platform name. Against SQL Server 2019 or 2017 targets, generate separately: the model may reference objects that do not exist on the older engine, and the generated script is not portable in one direction or the other.

-- bcp: bulk load with a format file, native Unicode types, batched commits.
bcp contoso_sales.dbo.Customer in .\data\customer.tsv `
   -S prod-sql01 -d contoso_sales -T `
   -f .\data\customer.xml -u "" `
   -b 10000 -a 8192 -r "\t" -c 65001

-- Script a table out. Rowversion columns are not exported by bcp.
bcp contoso_sales.dbo.Customer out .\data\customer_backup.tsv `
   -S prod-sql01 -d contoso_sales -E -u "" -c 65001 -r "\t" -q 1000000

-- A pinned local instance for development and CI, matching the target build.
docker run -d --name sql2025 `
  -e "ACCEPT_EULA=Y" -e "MSSQL_SA_PASSWORD=Dev!Passw0rd" `
  -e "MSSQL_PID=Developer" `
  -p 1433:1433 mcr.microsoft.com/mssql/server:2025-latest

-c 65001-c 65001 is required on both the load and the unload, and the format file is what makes the load repeatable. Rowversion columns are not carried by b, so a copy made this way cannot be used to resume optimistic-concurrency tokens.

Pin the container tag to a specific image digest rather than lat in any environment that runs a regression suite. A floating tag makes an unrelated upstream image push look like an application failure, which is the most expensive class of CI surprise.

How to read T-SQL in this guide

0.6 T-SQL Conventions

Every listing in the guide is a complete script fragment that can be pasted into a query window and run. A T-SQL listing is read as a sequence of batches, not as a sequence of statements, and that distinction explains most of the surprising error messages in the language.

A batch is one unit of parsing and compilation. Everything up to the next G is one batch, and one batch compiles as a whole before any of it runs. This produces a class of behaviour with no analogue in most languages: a syntax error late in a batch prevents the whole batch from executing, including the statements before it. It also means a name used before it is created in the same batch is a compile error, not a runtime one.

-- One batch. Note: the table does not exist when this batch compiles.
SELECT * FROM dbo.Widget;                 -- Msg 208: invalid object name
GO
CREATE TABLE dbo.Widget (WidgetID int NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY);
GO
-- Separate batch: compiles cleanly, because Widget now exists.
SELECT * FROM dbo.Widget;
GO

In SSMS and Azure Data Studio the whole script is sent as a series of batches because the client splits on GOGO. Through ADO.NET, ODBC or OLE DB, G is a syntax error: the caller must submit each batch as a separate command. Any code that concatenates a script string and executes it in one round trip is broken unless it strips G lines first.

Identifiers in this guide follow the SQL Server convention for the reference schema, with Pasca for table and column names, d as the schema, and singular noun table names. A leading @@ marks a variable, ## or #### a temporary table. Batch separators are G on a line by itself, and comments are annotated with the version that introduced the construct wherever the construct is version-specific.

MarkerMeaning
<c><c> styling on a lineComment authored as part of the listing; carries the explanation so the script stays runnable
Applies to.Applies to.Versions and editions where the behaviour described is valid
RequirA permission, setting, edition or prerequisite without which the statement fails
CaveEdge cases, known failure modes and the conditions under which the statement is unsafe
PrevRequires PREVIEW_ in SQL Server 2025; not production-ready
Cross-referenceThe concept is covered in full in a later chapter and is not restated

Run the listings. A T-SQL statement that parses and returns rows is a much cheaper specification than a paragraph describing what it would return, and the failure messages are part of the material. Chapter 5 onward assumes a working instance containing the reference schema from section 0.1.

Exercises 0

  1. Query @@VERSION@@VERSION, SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion') and SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel')SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateLevel') and record the exact build of the instance you will use. Identify whether it is CU, GDR, RTM or SP.
  2. Create a database, set PREVIEW_FEATURES = ONPREVIEW_FEATURES = ON on it, confirm the setting in sys.database_scop, then set it back to O and confirm again.
  3. Using sql, run a script file that contains a deliberate syntax error, first without -b-b and then with it. Record the exit code in both cases.
  4. Start a SQL Server 2025 container, run sqlpackage extractsqlpackage extract against a copy of the reference schema, and confirm the .dacpac.dacpac is produced. Extract again and confirm the output is byte-identical.
  5. Write one statement that lists every database on the instance together with its compatibility level and its recovery model, and run it against three different instances of your choosing.

1 · T-SQL Fundamentals and the Language

1.1 Batches, scripts and the GO separator1.2 Identifiers, delimiters and reserved words1.3 Variables, DECLARE and SET versus SELECT1.4 System data types and the rowversion caveat1.5 Control flow, TOP and the WITH rule1.6 Error handling: TRY, CATCH, THROW and RAISERROR

This chapter covers the mechanics of the language itself: how a script is divided, how names are written, how state is held between statements, how flow is controlled, and how failures are reported. Everything here is stable behaviour present in every supported version; where a construct is version-specific, the version is named.

1.1 Batches, scripts and the GO separator

1.1.1 GO Separator

A script is a text file of statements. A batch is the unit the server parses and compiles. G is the token that ends a batch, and it is a directive understood by the client, not by T-SQL — which has a precise and testable consequence for how scripts are executed programmatically.

The rule that catches people is the one in section 0.6: a batch is compiled as a whole before it runs. A reference to an object created earlier in the same batch is a compile error, not a slow success. The practical rule is that anything that changes the shape of the schema — CREATE TABLECREATE TABLE, CREATE PROCEDURECREATE PROCEDURE, CREATE VIEWCREATE VIEW — is followed by G before the first statement that uses it.

-- Batch 1.
SELECT 'batch 1' AS WhereAmI;
GO

-- Batch 2. GO n repeats the preceding batch n times.
SELECT 'batch 2' AS WhereAmI;
GO 3
-- The comment above is not part of the repeated batch, so it is not repeated.
GO

-- Batch 3. A variable does not survive GO: this batch never executes.
DECLARE @i int = 1;
GO
SELECT @i;   -- Msg 137: must declare the scalar variable "@i"

G is not a reserved word, has no grammar, and cannot appear inside a stored procedure, view, function, trigger or I block. Its only meaning is "end of batch, send what you have", and only the client can act on that. GO 0GO 0 and a bare G are both treated as a plain separator.

Requirement. G must be on a line of its own, optionally followed by a positive integer. Leading whitespace is tolerated; trailing text is not. When a script is generated programmatically, strip lines matching ^\s*GO(\s+\d+)?\s*$^\s*GO(\s+\d+)?\s*$ before sending the script as a single command, or send it batch by batch.

-- Line comment: everything to end of line, including 'quotes, [brackets] and ;
-- and "unbalanced <angle brackets> -- all ignored.

/* Block comment. Unlike C, block comments NEST in T-SQL,
   /* so the inner comment below is part of this one. */
   This is still inside a single comment. */
SELECT 1 AS AfterComments;
GO

Nesting block comments is genuinely useful: a commented-out block that itself contains a comment needs no editing. The corollary is that an unbalanced /*/* swallows the remainder of the file, usually reporting the error at the very last line rather than where the mistake is.

Two comment styles with different jobs: ---- for a short annotation on one line, and /* ... *//* ... */ for a header block or a section a script can be disabled with. A large commented-out region of a deployment script is a reliable indicator that the deployment should be split rather than commented.

1.2 Identifiers, delimiters and reserved words

1.2.1 Reserved Words

Names in T-SQL are delimited by context, case-insensitive by default, and constrained in length. A reserved word is not a keyword with a meaning to the parser; it is a name the parser will not accept unquoted, whether or not the product has ever given it a meaning.

Regular identifiers may contain letters, digits, @@, ##, __ and $$, must not begin with a digit or a digit followed by ee, and are limited to 128 characters. A regular identifier may begin with @@ for a local variable or @@@@ for a global one. A temporary table is a regular identifier beginning with a single ##, and #### makes it global to the session.

Delimited identifiers are written between square brackets or between double quotes, and the quoting character is defined by the QUOTED_I session setting. The setting is O by default in SSMS, Azure Data Studio and sql, and it was O by default in older ODBC and OLE DB providers — which is the historical reason so much legacy T-SQL uses square brackets.

-- Square brackets work regardless of QUOTED_IDENTIFIER and are the safe choice.
CREATE SCHEMA sales;
GO
CREATE TABLE sales.[Order]
(
    [Select]   int           NOT NULL,   -- reserved
    [Key]      int           NOT NULL,
    [Primary]  int           NOT NULL,   -- reserved
    [Table]    nvarchar(128)  NOT NULL,
    [User]     nvarchar(128)  NULL,
    [weird name!@#] int       NULL,      -- legal when delimited
    CONSTRAINT PK_sales_Order PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED ([Select], [Key])
);
GO
DROP TABLE sales.[Order];
GO

Delimited identifiers are also the only way to reference a column whose name is a reserved word without a schema rename. The cost is a slightly noisier schema definition and a set of identifiers that are easy to mistype, since a mistyped bracketed name is a different object rather than a syntax error.

Caveats. A delimited identifier containing a closing bracket is escaped by doubling it: [a]]b][a]]b] is the single name a]ba]b. Identifiers are compared using the collation of the database, so Sales and sales are the same object under a case-insensitive collation and two different objects under a case-sensitive one. Renaming a column only by changing its case is therefore a no-op or a duplicate, depending on collation, and the difference is invisible until a query fails.

Quoting formRequiresUse when
[name][name]Nothing; always acceptedDefault choice, including in objects created by a tool
"name""name"SET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ONSET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ONPortable scripts, migration from other engines
[name]]with]bracket[name]]with]bracketNothingAlmost never; avoid
'name''name'Nothing, it is a string literalNever, for identifiers; SELECT * FROM 'MyTable’SELECT * FROM 'MyTable' is not valid

Delimit every identifier in generated DDL and leave them off in handwritten DDL. Mixed style is not a correctness problem, but it makes a schema diff unreadable, and a schema diff that nobody reads is a schema diff that hides a dropped column.

1.3 Variables, DECLARE and SET versus SELECT

1.3.1 Variable Declaration

T-SQL variables are batch-scoped, single-valued and untyped by default. They exist for procedural glue rather than for computation, and the language offers two ways of assigning to them with different behaviour on the empty-result-set case.

-- Inline initialiser requires SQL Server 2008+. Several declarations may share a DECLARE.
DECLARE @CustomerID int = 1001,
        @Name       nvarchar(120),
        @Since      date;

-- SET: ANSI form. Assigns exactly one value, evaluates the whole expression,
-- and produces no result set. It cannot reference FROM or a JOIN.
SET @Name = 'Contoso Ltd';
SET @Since = CAST(MIN(OrderDate) AS date)
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;
GO

-- SELECT: assigns the LAST value produced, and leaves the variable untouched
-- when the query returns no rows. It may also return a result set.
DECLARE @Since2 date = '2000-01-01';
SELECT @Since2 = CAST(MIN(OrderDate) AS date)
FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE CustomerID = -1;   -- no rows
SELECT @Since2;   -- still 2000-01-01, which is almost never intended
GO

-- Scalars go in parentheses. This is the form to prefer in production code.
-- SET @Since = (SELECT CAST(MIN(OrderDate) AS date)
--                FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID);
GO

The SELECT @v = colSELECT @v = col form assigns the value of the final row, not an aggregate. Written without an aggregate or a TOP 1TOP 1 it has no defined value unless the result has one row, and the value silently depends on the physical order the engine happened to produce. It is the classic cause of a report that is correct on the development instance and wrong in production.

BehaviourSET @v = ...SET @v = ...SELECT @v = ...SELECT @v = ...
StandardANSINot ANSI; a T-SQL extension
Multiple rowsRejected, error 512Assigns the last row encountered
Zero rowsVariable set to NUVariable keeps its previous value
Aggregate or scalar subqueryNot permittedPermitted
FROM, JOIN, WHERE allowedNoYes
Returns a result setNoYes, if non-aggregate columns are selected

Caveats. A variable declared but never assigned raises no warning; it silently holds NULLNULL. Because NU propagates through every comparison, an unassigned variable turns WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerIDWHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID into a predicate that matches nothing rather than raising an error. Name variables with a distinct prefix and check for NU after every assignment that can legitimately produce none.

DEC must be the only kind of declaration in its statement, and variables are visible for the remainder of the batch, not before it. A variable declared in a procedure is scoped to the procedure and invisible to its caller; there is no O parameter, and the substitute is a result set or an OUT clause covered in the administration chapter.

1.4 System data types and the rowversion caveat

1.4.1 Rowversion Caveat

T-SQL has no user-defined types in the classic sense, so the catalogue of value types is closed and small. Choosing among them is a storage and semantics decision, and chapter 2 covers precision, collation and conversion in detail. This section establishes the system types and the one whose name lies.

-- The catalogue, queried from the instance rather than recalled.
SELECT name, is_table_dependent, is_user_defined
FROM sys.types WHERE is_user_defined = 0 AND is_table_dependent = 0
ORDER BY CASE WHEN name LIKE 'n%' OR name LIKE '%char%' OR name IN ('xml','text','timestamp','rowversion')
                                    THEN 1 ELSE 0 END, name;
GO

-- rowversion is a misnomer inherited from a type that was once the row's time stamp.
CREATE TABLE dbo.Source
(
    SourceID    int              NOT NULL,
    Payload     nvarchar(max),
    Tokens      varbinary(max),
    ExternalRef uniqueidentifier NOT NULL,
    RowVer      rowversion        NOT NULL,   -- "timestamp" is a deprecated synonym
    CONSTRAINT PK_Source PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (SourceID)
);
GO

sys. is the authoritative catalogue and is the right place to confirm what a name such as time actually resolves to on this instance. It also shows user-defined and table-dependent types, which the filter excludes, so the result is only the system types.

TypeStorageUse for
b1 byteBoolean flags. Aggregations such as COUNT(flag)COUNT(flag) count the ones, not the rows
tin, smal, i, big1, 2, 4, 8 bytesInteger counts and identifiers. big does not auto-widen; SQL Server 2016+ has dynamic parameters
dec, num5—17 bytesExact quantities with a fixed scale. Preferred for all financial values; see 2.1
fl, re8, 4 bytesApproximate scientific values. Never for money; see 2.1
mo, small8, 4 bytesLegacy currency. Fixed 4 decimal places and locale-dependent formatting; prefer dec
da, ti, date, datetim3, 3—5, 6—8, 8—10 bytesAll temporal work. See 2.3
ch, var, nc, nvarn, n, 2n, 2nText. The nn variants are UTF-16; see 2.2
bin, varbn, nByte strings. Use varbinary(max)varbinary(max) for documents larger than 8000 bytes
uniqueid16 bytesGlobally unique keys. See 2.2 and 3.5
rowve8 bytesOptimistic concurrency tokens. Not a clock value; see below
x, sql_vVariableXML documents and untyped values; both carry runtime and indexing costs
hiera, geom, geogVariableHierarchies and spatial data, CLR-backed
-- Optimistic concurrency with rowversion: compare, then update.
-- Zero affected rows means another session wrote first.
DECLARE @Original rowversion = 0x00000000000007D1;
UPDATE dbo.Source
   SET Payload = 'revised'
 WHERE SourceID = 7
   AND RowVer = @Original;
GO
IF @@ROWCOUNT = 0
BEGIN
    THROW 50001, 'The row changed after it was read. Reload and retry.', 1;
END
GO

-- Reading a rowversion into a client variable for the next round trip.
SELECT SourceID, Payload, RowVer
FROM dbo.Source
WHERE SourceID = 7;
GO

The compare-then-update pattern is the whole of optimistic concurrency, and it is only correct because rowve cannot be written by the client. Testing @@ROWCOUNT@@ROWCOUNT immediately after the statement is essential; any intervening statement in the batch resets it.

Caveats. rowve is an 8-byte binary value the engine generates; it is not a timestamp, cannot be set by an INS or UPD, and comparing it to a date is meaningless. Only one rowve column is permitted per table. b and BULK INSERTBULK INSERT do not carry rowversion values across a copy — they generate new ones — so a rowversion-based concurrency token is invalidated by a bulk reload. The counter is per-table, and it is not reset by a table truncate.

Cross-reference. Storage layout, the precision and scale result table, and the exact behaviour of every type listed above are in section 2. Constraint definitions that reference these types are covered in chapter 3.

1.5 Control flow, TOP and the WITH rule

1.5.1 TOP Rule

T-SQL has no swi and no d/whilewhile. Control flow is I/EL and WH with BR and CONT, blocks are delimited by BE/E, and the two short forms added in SQL Server 2012, I and CHO, cover most of what a swi would be used for.

-- A single statement needs no BEGIN/END; a block does.
DECLARE @i int = 0, @Total int = 0;

WHILE @i < 5
BEGIN
    IF @i = 2
    BEGIN
        SET @i += 1;      -- CONTINUE here would skip the increment and loop forever
    END
    SET @Total += @i;              -- accumulated 0, 1, 3, 4
    SET @i += 1;
END
SELECT @Total;                        -- 8
GO

-- A cursor-like loop with BREAK. Batches limit how long a WHILE may run.
DECLARE @Batch int = 0, @Rows int = 0;
WHILE 1 = 1
BEGIN
    UPDATE TOP (10000) dbo.SalesOrderLine
       SET Quantity = Quantity;   -- no-op touch, used to apply pressure in a fixed chunk
    SET @Rows = @@ROWCOUNT;
    IF @Rows = 0 BREAK;
    SET @Batch += 1;
    IF @Batch >= 100
    BEGIN
        -- A WHILE may not run for the life of a connection. Bail out.
        THROW 50002, 'Bounded loop aborted after 100 iterations.', 1;
    END
END
GO

There is no EL on a WH because there is nothing to do at the end of the loop body. The pattern to avoid is a CONT above the increment, which makes the loop non-terminating and is only diagnosable by reading the loop rather than by the error it eventually produces.

Caveats. A WH loop in an unbounded session blocks a connection slot for as long as it runs, and the engine will not interrupt it for you. Any loop that processes a production table must have a batch counter, a time bound or both. The only reliable primitive for a large loop is a cursor or table-valued function over a batch, both covered in the tuning chapter.

-- IIF and CHOOSE, both introduced in SQL Server 2012.
-- IIF returns the type with the highest data-type precedence of its arguments,
-- and every argument is evaluated regardless of the condition.
SELECT SalesOrderID,
       IIF(Status = 'SHP', 'Shipped', 'Open')              AS Label,
       IIF(TotalAmount > 10000, TotalAmount * 0.9, TotalAmount) AS Discounted,
       CHOOSE(MONTH(OrderDate), 'Jan','Feb','Mar','Apr','May','Jun',
                              'Jul','Aug','Sep','Oct','Nov','Dec') AS MonthAbbrev
FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- TOP has no parentheses around a literal: TOP 10, not TOP (10).
-- Parentheses are used only when the operand is an expression or a variable.
-- TOP is applied AFTER ORDER BY, and is therefore deterministic only with one.
SELECT TOP 10 SalesOrderID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
ORDER BY TotalAmount DESC;
GO

DECLARE @Take int = 5;
SELECT TOP (@Take) 'variable operand' AS Sample;
GO

SELECT TOP 10 PERCENT WITH TIES Name
FROM dbo.Product
ORDER BY UnitPrice DESC;   -- returns every row tied at the cut-off
GO

I evaluates all three arguments, so a "safe" call such as IIF(@UseNew, NewFunction(), 0)IIF(@UseNew, NewFunction(), 0) still runs NewFunction()NewFunction() when the condition is false. CHO behaves the same way: the index is evaluated once, but every argument expression is a candidate for evaluation.

-- The WITH placement rule. A statement that begins with WITH must be preceded by
-- a semicolon, or be the first statement in the batch, because WITH is also
-- the table-hint keyword and the parser cannot otherwise tell them apart.
DECLARE @MinOrderValue decimal(19,4) = 1000;

SELECT @@VERSION;   -- previous statement, semicolon terminated
;WITH BigOrders AS
(
    SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, TotalAmount
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE TotalAmount > @MinOrderValue
)
SELECT c.Name, b.SalesOrderID, b.TotalAmount
FROM BigOrders AS b
INNER JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = b.CustomerID;
GO

-- The same collision with a table hint. Semicolon, always.
UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
   SET Status = 'CXL'
FROM dbo.SalesOrder WITH (ROWLOCK)
WHERE Status = 'NEW' AND OrderDate < 20240101;
GO

The leading semicolon costs nothing and removes the only genuinely ambiguous construct in the language. It is the reason a formatter that terminates every statement is not merely a style preference but a correctness measure.

Applies to. The semicolon-before-WITHWITH rule is required in every supported version, not a SQL Server 2008 style preference. It is specifically needed after a statement that can legally end in something the parser might continue, and the cheap rule that removes all ambiguity is to terminate every statement. TH, ME and WI all require it, which is why formatters add it automatically.

1.6 Error handling: TRY, CATCH, THROW and RAISERROR

1.6.1 THROW Statement

SQL Server 2012 introduced TH as a complete replacement for RAIS in new code. RAIS remains supported and still has capabilities — severity, state, message formatting — that TH does not, so the two coexist in most codebases.

-- TRY/CATCH captures runtime errors. It does not roll anything back by itself,
-- and it does not catch compile errors or errors raised in another batch.
BEGIN TRY
    INSERT dbo.Product (SKU, Name, UnitPrice)
    VALUES ('X-1', 'Test product', -1);      -- CHECK constraint fails
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    IF @@TRANCOUNT > 0 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;   -- your job, not the engine's

    SELECT ERROR_NUMBER()    AS ErrNo,        -- 547
           ERROR_SEVERITY()  AS Severity,    -- 16
           ERROR_STATE()     AS State,
           ERROR_PROCEDURE() AS ProcName,
           ERROR_LINE()      AS LineNo,
           XACT_STATE()      AS XactState,   -- 1 committable, -1 doomed, 0 none
           ERROR_MESSAGE()   AS Msg;

    THROW;   -- bare THROW re-raises with the ORIGINAL number and line
END CATCH;
GO

ERROR_*ERROR_* functions return data about the error currently being handled, so they must be read before anything else in the block. Note that the rollback is explicit: CA reports the error, it does not undo the work that caused it.

-- THROW with arguments: number >= 50000, message, state. The statement before it
-- must be terminated with a semicolon. The message may not be formatted.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_AdjustCreditLimit
    @CustomerID int,
    @NewLimit   decimal(19,4)
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    IF @NewLimit < 0
        THROW 50010, 'A credit limit cannot be negative.', 1;

    IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID)
    BEGIN
        -- A variable message is allowed; an expression is not.
        DECLARE @Msg nvarchar(2048) =
            CONCAT('No customer with CustomerID = ', @CustomerID, '.');
        THROW 50011, @Msg, 1;
    END

    UPDATE dbo.Customer SET CreditLimit = @NewLimit WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;
END
GO

A user error number must be at least 50000, which is what reserves the range above the engine’s own messages. The state number is for the client to distinguish one occurrence of the same error number from another, and is conventionally 1 in application code.

-- RAISERROR, legacy form. The only way to produce a message with substitutions,
-- to control STATE for the log, or to set a severity above 20.
DECLARE @Pct decimal(5,2) = 104.30;
RAISERROR('Price variance of %s%% on product %d exceeds tolerance.', 16, 1, @Pct, 42);
GO

-- WITH LOG raises the error as a server-level event. Severity 20 and above
-- terminate the connection and roll back the transaction.
RAISERROR('Fatal: data feed is inconsistent.', 20, 1) WITH LOG, NOWAIT;
GO

RAIS remains the only way to embed a value in a message without building the string first, and the only way to raise above severity 16 or to write to the error log. Those three capabilities are why it still appears in production codebases despite TH being the better default.

AspectTH (SQL Server 2012+)RAIS
Error number≥ 50000≥ 50000 for user errors; 1—18 for system messages
Message substitutionNot supported; build the string firstSupported: %s%s, %d%d, %%%% and parameters after the severity
SeverityAlways 16Any 0—25
StateAny 1—255Any 1—255
Prior statement must end in ;;YesNo
Re-raise the caught errorTHROW;THROW; alone, preserving number and lineMust reconstruct with ERROR_*ERROR_* functions
Writes to the error logNoYes, with WITH LOGWITH LOG
RecommendationDefault choice for new codeLegacy, and for severity control only

Caveats. T/CA does not catch compile errors, does not catch severity 10 messages, and does not catch errors from a broken connection. A TH inside a CA block resets the ERROR_*ERROR_* functions, so read them before re-raising. Nested T blocks all execute their CA blocks, and only the innermost CA receives the re-raised error.

Capture the ERROR_*ERROR_* functions into variables at the top of every CA block and log them there. A procedure that re-raises a bare THROW;THROW; without logging has discarded the only information that identifies the failure once the client has seen error 50000.

Exercises 1

  1. Write a script of three batches where the third references a table created in the second. Confirm it runs. Then move the G to remove the boundary and record the exact error.
  2. Create a table with a column named Or, a column named a]ba]b, and a time column. Query sys.c to confirm the system assigned the rowve type rather than the deprecated time name.
  3. Demonstrate the difference between S and SEL assignment when the source query returns zero rows, then when it returns more than one row. Record the error number for each.
  4. Write a WH loop with a BR exit and an iteration cap of 100, and prove the cap fires by setting it to 1.
  5. Write a batch ending in a SEL and then a WI common table expression, first without the leading semicolon. Record the error, then fix it.
  6. Raise the same business error three ways: RAIS with substitution, TH with a literal message, and TH with a CON message. Note the difference in what the client receives.

2 · Data Types, Collation and Precision

2.1 Exact and approximate numerics, and the precision rules2.2 Character types, max variants and uniqueidentifier2.3 Date and time types2.4 Collation, case and accent sensitivity2.5 Implicit conversion and its failure modes2.6 CAST, CONVERT, TRY_CONVERT and style codes

The type system in SQL Server is closed, small and exact, and the rules governing how types combine are specified rather than discovered. This chapter covers the numeric types and the precision and scale arithmetic that decides whether an expression overflows, the character types and their two encodings, the temporal types and the legacy one to avoid, collation at every scope, and the conversion rules that produce the majority of surprising query results.

2.1 Exact and approximate numerics, and the precision rules

2.1.1 Precision Rules

SQL Server separates exact numerics from approximate ones, and the distinction is not a matter of tolerance. An exact numeric is a decimal fraction stored as an integer and a scale; an approximate numeric is a binary floating-point number that cannot represent most decimal fractions at all.

-- The demonstration that decides the argument. 0.1 has no exact binary form.
DECLARE @a float = 0.1, @b float = 0.2;
SELECT @a + @b = 0.3 AS FloatEqualsThreeTenths;   -- 0

-- Same values as decimal(38,10): the answer is exact.
-- The trailing .0000000000 is the declared scale, not a formatting choice.
-- SELECT CAST(0.1 AS decimal(38,10)) + CAST(0.2 AS decimal(38,10)) = 0.3

-- The accumulating form, which is where float billing actually goes wrong.
-- DECLARE @t decimal(19,4) = 0;
-- WHILE @t < 100
-- BEGIN SET @t = @t + 0.1; END  -- 999 iterations, then 100.0000
GO

-- The correct form for money: a fixed scale, and rounding at the point of storage.
SELECT CAST(0.1 AS decimal(19,4)) + CAST(0.2 AS decimal(19,4)) AS ExactSum;
GO

-- Real, the 4-byte float, is a further step away.
-- SELECT CONVERT(float(24), 16777217);  -- 16777216, not 16777217
GO

The repeated 0.10.1 additions are what make the case. With fl the sum crosses the target and the loop stops at a value that is not the one the domain expects; with decimal(19,4)decimal(19,4) the same loop terminates on an exact representable boundary.

Caveats. Never store a monetary amount in fl or realreal. A binary float cannot represent 0.10.1, so the sum of many small values drifts, and the rounding of the final total is not reproducible across engines. It is not a matter of "how much" drift but of whether the value is defensible in an audit. Use dec with a scale chosen to match the smallest amount you must represent exactly, and round once, at the boundary where the value enters or leaves the system.

Precision and scale are declared per value, not per column only, because every expression has its own result precision and scale derived from its operands by a fixed set of rules. Those rules are the reason an expression can overflow from two perfectly reasonable columns.

OperationResult precision ppResult scale ss
e1 + e2e1 + e2p1 - s1 + s2 + max(s1, s2) + 1p1 - s1 + s2 + max(s1, s2) + 1max(s1, s2)max(s1, s2)
e1 - e2e1 - e2p1 - s1 + s2 + max(s1, s2) + 1p1 - s1 + s2 + max(s1, s2) + 1max(s1, s2)max(s1, s2)
e1 * e2e1 * e2p1 + p2 + 1p1 + p2 + 1s1 + s2s1 + s2
e1 / e2e1 / e2p1 - s1 + s2 + max(6, s1 + p2 + 1)p1 - s1 + s2 + max(6, s1 + p2 + 1)max(6, s1 + p2 + 1)max(6, s1 + p2 + 1)
AVG(e1)AVG(e1)3838ss of the argument
SUM(e1)SUM(e1)38ss of the argument
MIN(e1)MIN(e1), MAX(e1)MAX(e1)pp of the argumentss of the argument
COUNT(e1)COUNT(e1)100

The maximum precision is 38. When an expression’s derived precision exceeds 38, the integral part is reduced and the fractional part is rounded; the value is not an error, it is silently less precise than the inputs suggest. When the integral part alone exceeds 38, the statement fails with Arithmetic overflowArithmetic overflow.

-- Division is where the scale rules bite. Note the intermediate types.
DECLARE @Qty int = 3, @Price decimal(19,4) = 100.00;

SELECT @Price * 10          AS IntMultiplied,   -- decimal(30,4): p = 19+10+1
       @Price / @Qty            AS Divided,         -- decimal(32,10): scale is forced to at least 6
       @Price * 1.0          AS FloatPolluted,   -- decimal(38,6) via numeric(2,1)
       CONVERT(varchar(50), SQL_VARIANT_PROPERTY(@Price, 'BaseType'))
                                     AS ActualType;
GO

-- Integer division: 5 / 2 = 2, not 2.5.
SELECT 5 / 2 AS IntDiv, 5 / 2.0 AS MixedDiv, 5 / 2 * 1.0 AS PostMultiplyDiv;
GO

-- Rounding is half away from zero; the default decimal rounding for INSERT
-- truncates instead, which is why a declared scale must be chosen, not inherited.
-- SELECT CAST(2.5 AS decimal(10,0)), CAST(3.5 AS decimal(10,0)),
--        CAST(2.345 AS decimal(10,2));
GO

Every result type shown here is derived, not declared, and the same query against differently declared columns produces different types. Where an intermediate type is not what was intended, cast the operand explicitly rather than relying on the propagation rules.

Applies to. The result precision and scale rules apply to exact numerics only. An expression containing a fl or re operand is evaluated in approximate numeric and the rules do not apply, which is another reason the presence of a fl anywhere in an expression makes the result unpredictable to the reader. A dec literal written without a type in a comparison against a fl column silently converts the column to floatfloat.

Pick the scale from the domain, not from the display. Money that must be exact to the cent is decimal(19,4)decimal(19,4) or decimal(19,2)decimal(19,2); a unit price that can be quoted in fractional cents needs more. Widening precision after the fact is a schema change on a live column; choosing too small a scale is a silent data loss at insert time that no amount of later rewriting recovers.

2.2 Character types, max variants and uniqueidentifier

2.2.1 Uniqueidentifier

SQL Server has two character encodings and four character types, and the choice between them is a permanent storage commitment. The nn variants store UTF-16; the others store the code page named by the column’s collation. Under SQL Server 2019 and later, a UTF-8 collation allows the non-nn types to carry Unicode too, which is a per-database and per-column choice rather than a type choice.

-- Storage is exactly as declared for the non-n types, and doubled for the n types.
-- LEN() counts characters; DATALENGTH() counts bytes. They differ for nvarchar.
DECLARE @S nvarchar(10) = N'caf';
SELECT @S                                          AS Value,
       LEN(@S)                                    AS Chars,      -- 3, ignoring the null
       DATALENGTH(@S)                              AS Bytes,      -- 6, UTF-16
       DATALENGTH(CONVERT(varchar(10), @S))          AS AsVarcharBytes; -- 3
GO

-- Trailing blanks are stored and returned, but LEN() ignores them and
-- = comparison ignores them too. Use DATALENGTH when they matter.
SELECT DATALENGTH('ab ')     AS PaddedBytes,   -- 3
       LEN('ab   ')    AS PaddedLen,    -- 2
       'ab ' = 'ab'      AS EqualsAb;      -- 1
GO

-- The N prefix makes a literal Unicode. Without it, a literal is converted
-- to the column collation, which loses characters the code page cannot hold.
SELECT N'Gr' + NCHAR(250)        AS WithPrefix,   -- NCHAR(250) is "u with ring above"
       'Gr' + CHAR(250)         AS WithoutPrefix; -- code page dependent
GO

L counts characters and DATAL counts bytes, and for any nvar value the second is twice the first. Any length validation written against L is validating characters; written against DATAL it is validating storage.

-- The max variants. LOB types support indexing by CREATE INDEX and cannot be
-- used in a key, a DISTINCT grouping, or a computed column key.
SELECT TYPE_NAME(TYPE_ID(TYPE_NAME(OBJECTPROPERTY(OBJECT_ID('dbo.Source'), 'TableHasBlobDescr')))) AS BlobKind;
GO

-- nvarchar(max) is the default for large text in new schemas. varchar(max)
-- is a single-byte code page under a non-UTF-8 collation and can hold exactly
-- twice as many characters for ASCII, and cannot hold some scripts at all.
SELECT COLUMN_NAME, DATA_TYPE, CHARACTER_MAXIMUM_LENGTH, CHARACTER_OCTET_LENGTH
FROM INFORMATION_SCHEMA.COLUMNS
WHERE TABLE_NAME = 'Source' AND COLUMN_NAME IN ('Payload', 'Tokens');
GO

-- uniqueidentifier: 16 bytes, comparable, and the wrong default for a key.
DECLARE @A uniqueidentifier = NEWID(), @B uniqueidentifier = NEWSEQUENTIALID();
SELECT @A AS RandomGuid, @B AS SequentialGuid;
GO

-- String sort order vs binary sort order for uniqueidentifier. The WHERE
-- clause changes the order the index returns, which is why the classic
-- clustered-GUID pattern needs the index and the predicate to agree.
-- CREATE UNIQUE INDEX UX_Source ON dbo.Source(ExternalRef)
--     WHERE ExternalRef IS NOT NULL;
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.Source WHERE ExternalRef > @id ORDER BY ExternalRef;
GO

The information-schema query reports declared length rather than stored length, so a m column reports a large number that says nothing about the rows. A filtered index over a nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max) column is possible but expensive, and the predicate has to be exact.

TypeEncodingTypical use in this guide’s schema
char(n)char(n)Code page, fixed length, blank paddedFixed-width codes only: ISO country code char(2)char(2)
varchar(n)varchar(n)Code page, variable lengthASCII identifiers such as SKU varchar(32)SKU varchar(32); a UTF-8 collation is required for non-ASCII
char(max)char(max)Code page, LOBRarely correct
nchar(n)nchar(n)UTF-16, fixed lengthCodes where the length is genuinely fixed in characters
nvarchar(n)nvarchar(n)UTF-16, variable lengthHuman text: Name nvarchar(120)Name nvarchar(120)
nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max)UTF-16, LOBDocuments and long text
uniqueid16 binary bytesExternally generated keys, replication, merge

Caveats. NEWID()NEWID() is a random value: as a clustered key it causes a page split on almost every insert, and it defeats any attempt at range-local inserts. NEWSEQUENTIALID()NEWSEQUENTIALID() is sequential but only legal as the DEF of a uniqueid column that also has a UNI constraint or index. Both wrap over time. If the requirement is a surrogate key that sorts well, use IDEN or a SEQU; see 3.5.

Applies to. UTF-8 collations were introduced in SQL Server 2019 and are the mechanism for storing Unicode in var and ch columns. SQL Server 2025 broadens UTF-8 support further. A var column under a non-UTF-8 collation is not a Unicode column, and converting a Unicode value into it is where data is lost.

2.3 Date and time types

2.3.1 Date and Time Types

SQL Server carries six temporal types, of which four are current practice, one is a legacy holdover with three separate defects, and one is a display-only type. The choice between da, ti, date and datetim is a statement about what the value means.

TypeStorageRange and resolutionNotes
da3 bytes0001-01-01 to 9999-12-31, whole daysNo time component at all
time(n)time(n)3 to 5 bytes00:00:00 to 23:59:59.9999999, 100 nsTime of day only, no date. Cannot represent a 24-hour-plus duration
datetime2(n)datetime2(n)6 to 8 bytes0001-01-01 to 9999-12-31, 100 nsThe default choice for a point in time
datetimeoffset(n)datetimeoffset(n)8 to 10 bytesSame, plus an offset of -14:00 to +14:00The offset is not normalised on storage; see below
date8 bytes1753-01-01 to 9999-12-31, ~1 msLegacy. Rounds to .000, .003 or .007, and the range excludes common historical dates
smalld4 bytes1900-01-01 to 2079-06-06, one minuteLegacy. Rounds to the nearest minute and loses seconds
-- The three defects of datetime, demonstrated.
-- 1. Rounding to .003 increments is not arithmetic rounding.
SELECT CAST('2026-09-15T12:00:00.001' AS datetime)                AS DtRounded,   -- .003
       CAST('2026-09-15T12:00:00.009' AS datetime)                AS DtRounded2,  -- .010, not .009
       CAST('2026-09-15T12:00:00.001' AS datetime2(3))            AS Dt2Exact;  -- .001

-- 2. The range floor is 1753.
-- SELECT CAST('1752-12-31' AS datetime);  -- Msg 241, conversion failed

-- 3. The default display format is ambiguous to a reader and locale dependent.
SELECT CAST('2026-09-15' AS datetime)      AS AmbiguousDate,
       CAST('20260915'  AS datetime)      AS UnambiguousIso,
       CAST('20260915'  AS datetime2(0))   AS Dt2Iso;
GO

date rounds to the nearest .000.000, .003.003 or .007.007 second, so a value that is not already on that grid is altered by the assignment rather than rejected. The comparison of datetime2(3)datetime2(3) in the same row is the control: nothing is altered there.

-- datetimeoffset stores the offset it was given. It is not converted to UTC,
-- so the same instant has many stored representations and equality
-- comparison on the raw value is meaningless across zones.
--   WHERE OrderDate >= '2026-01-01T00:00:00+05:00' AND OrderDate < '2027-01-01T00:00:00+05:00'
--   matches the instants in that zone, regardless of each row's stored offset.
GO

-- Normalise to UTC before storing anything that will be compared or aggregated.
--   SELECT TODATETIMEOFFSET(@local, '+00:00') AT TIME ZONE 'UTC';
--   SELECT @local AT TIME ZONE 'UTC';   -- the same instant, labelled UTC
GO

-- The functions that exist because datetime2 replaced the old ones.
SELECT SYSDATETIME()                    AS NowDateTime2,
       SYSDATETIMEOFFSET()              AS NowWithOffset,
       SYSUTCDATETIME()                 AS NowUtc,
       GETDATE()                        AS LegacyNow,   -- datetime, local time, round trip
       GETUTCDATE()                     AS LegacyUtc;
GO

-- For a default constraint, use the *_AT TIME ZONE forms (2008+) so the
-- value is deterministic and independent of the server time zone.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder ADD CONSTRAINT DF_SalesOrder_OrderDate
    DEFAULT (SYSDATETIME()) FOR OrderDate;
GO

A predicate of the form >= '2026-01-01T00:00:00+05:00'>= '2026-01-01T00:00:00+05:00' is correct for a datetim column even when each row stores a different offset, because the engine resolves each value for comparison. It stops being correct the moment the column is date, at which point the offset is silently discarded.

Caveats. GETDATE()GETDATE() and SYSDATETIME()SYSDATETIME() return the server’s local time, which is not a defined value — it changes with daylight saving and is different on a failover replica in another region. A default constraint or a generated column cannot call a non-deterministic function such as GETDATE()GETDATE() for this reason; SYSDATETIME()SYSDATETIME() works only because it is documented as deterministic for schema purposes. For anything that must be interpreted the same way everywhere, store UTC and record the originating offset separately.

Adopt a single convention and enforce it: da for calendar dates, datetime2(3)datetime2(3) or datetime2(7)datetime2(7) for instants, and UTC plus a separate offset column when the zone matters. Avoid date and smalld in new schemas entirely; they are convertible for legacy data, not selectable for new data.

2.4 Collation, case and accent sensitivity

2.4.1 Sensitivity Levels

A collation is not a character set. It is a named bundle of sorting and comparison rules — binary or linguistic, case sensitivity, accent sensitivity, and script handling — and it is attached to columns, to the database, to the server, and to individual expressions. A schema is collation-consistent or it is a latent bug.

-- The active collation at every level. One of these is wrong on any real instance.
SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('Collation')            AS ServerCollation,
       DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Collation')  AS DatabaseCollation;
GO

-- Where columns disagree with the database default. The query is the
-- inventory that a consolidation project needs.
SELECT s.name AS SchemaName, t.name AS TableName, c.name AS ColumnName,
       c.collation_name AS ColumnCollation
FROM sys.columns AS c
INNER JOIN sys.tables  AS t ON t.object_id  = c.object_id
INNER JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = t.schema_id
WHERE c.collation_name IS NOT NULL
  AND c.collation_name <> DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Collation')
ORDER BY s.name, t.name, c.column_id;
GO

The collation query is the first thing to run on any inherited schema, because a column that disagrees with the database default makes every join against it a cross-applied implicit conversion. Fixing it later is a data-preserving operation for nvar and a full rewrite for nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max).

-- Case and accent sensitivity are independent switches in the collation name.
SELECT 'Contoso' = 'contoso'  AS CI_Default,   -- 1: case insensitive
       'Contoso' = 'contoso' COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CS_AS AS CS_Forced,   -- 0
       'resume'  = 'r' + NCHAR(233) + 'sum' + NCHAR(233) AS AS_Default,       -- 0: accent sensitive by default
       'resume'  = 'r' + NCHAR(233) + 'sum' + NCHAR(233) COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CI_AI AS AI_Forced;      -- 1
GO

-- Per-column override, fixed at the schema level.
--   ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ALTER COLUMN Name nvarchar(120)
--       COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CI_AS_SC;
GO

-- Per-database override. Changing it is an ALTER DATABASE operation, and
-- every char/varchar/text column is converted under the new rules.
--   ALTER DATABASE contoso_sales COLLATE SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS;
GO

-- Per-expression override, useful for a case-sensitive search key inside
-- an otherwise case-insensitive database.
--   SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE Email COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CS_AS = @email;
GO

-- A case-insensitive index is useless for a case-sensitive search.
--   CREATE UNIQUE INDEX UX_Customer_Email_CS ON dbo.Customer(Email COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CS_AS);
GO

Applying COL to the literal rather than the column keeps the index usable, which is the point of the last listing. Case-insensitive search over a case-sensitive column is a normalisation function written as a predicate, and it belongs in the query, not in the index.

SuffixMeaningEffect
_CI_CICase insensitive'A''A' equals 'a''a'. The default on all standard collations
_CS_CSCase sensitive'A''A' differs from 'a''a'
_AS_ASAccent sensitive'e''e' differs from NCHAR(233)NCHAR(233), the letter e with an acute accent. The default on all standard collations
_AI_AIAccent insensitive'e''e' equals NCHAR(233)NCHAR(233)
_KS_KS / _WS_WSWidth / kana sensitiveFull-width and half-width characters distinguished; katakana distinguished
_SC_SCSupplementary charactersCorrect handling of surrogate pairs; present on 100-series collations from SQL Server 2012
_UTF8_UTF8UTF-8 encodingLets var columns hold Unicode; SQL Server 2019+

Caveats. Collation is part of a column’s identity for every operation that compares or sorts it. A join between two columns with different collations is a cross-applied implicit conversion, which makes the join non-sargable and disables index seek on at least one side. Use Latin1_Genera, or a UTF-8 variant where required, as a single database-wide convention and accept per-column overrides only for a documented reason.

Requirement. Two columns that are compared in a join, a UN or a GROUP BYGROUP BY should share a collation. To change a column’s collation without rewriting the table, use ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMNALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN for in-row nvar; a nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max) column cannot be altered in place and must be migrated through a new column.

2.5 Implicit conversion and its failure modes

2.5.1 Explicit Conversion

An implicit conversion happens without anyone writing CON, and it is the mechanism behind the most common performance and correctness defects in T-SQL. The rule is asymmetric in a way that is easy to remember once stated: an implicit conversion from a character type to a numeric type is a costly conversion, and the column being converted is the one that loses its index.

-- Filter on a column with a literal of the wrong type.
-- The predicate applied to Customer is CAST(CustomerID AS varchar(11)) = '1001',
-- so a nonclustered index on CustomerID is not seekable for this query.
DECLARE @P varchar(20) = '1001';
SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = @P;
GO

-- The same query with a matching type is seekable.
DECLARE @Q int = 1001;
SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = @Q;
GO

-- Type precedence in a join decides which side is converted.
-- When a bigint column meets an int column, the int is widened to bigint, which is
-- free. When a varchar meets an int, the varchar side becomes the victim.
SELECT c.Name, o.OrderDate
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
WHERE c.Email = 'a@b.com';
GO

-- Find every implicit conversion in a module. sys.dm_exec_describe_first_result_set
-- reports the estimated type; sys.fn_parameter_stability_errors (SQL Server 2022+)
-- is the supported way to find literal-to-column mismatches systematically.
--   SELECT * FROM sys.dm_exec_describe_first_result_set(N'SELECT ...', NULL, 0);
GO

-- Failure modes, all of them silent until a value is dirty.
--   SELECT CAST('1e5' AS int);            -- Msg 245, conversion failed
--   SELECT CAST(' 42 ' AS int);           -- 42: leading/trailing spaces are tolerated
--   SELECT CAST('42.9' AS int);           -- 42: truncated, not rounded
--   SELECT CAST('2019-05-01' AS datetime2(0)); -- 2019-05-01, language dependent
--   SELECT 1 WHERE '1' = 1;               -- Msg 4023, no coercion of a literal to bool
GO

The first query and the second differ only in the variable’s declared type, and the plan differs accordingly. Because the column is converted rather than the variable, the index on Custo cannot be seeked; the tuning chapter covers how to confirm this from a plan.

Caveats. Character-to-numeric conversion is not merely lossy, it is culture dependent: CONVERT(datetime2, '05/01/2019')CONVERT(datetime2, '05/01/2019') is 1 May under a British session language and 5 January under a US one, and a TRY_C that succeeds on one server fails on another for the same string. Always pass dates and numbers in an unambiguous form, and never convert a formatted string in a stored procedure whose callers you do not control.

Fix conversions at the parameter boundary, not in the query body. A procedure declared as @FromDate datetime2(0)@FromDate datetime2(0) receives a converted value from the client once, and every query inside it then compares like with like. A procedure declared as @FromDate varchar(20)@FromDate varchar(20) forces a conversion on every execution, and the plan is only knowable at run time.

2.6 CAST, CONVERT, TRY_CONVERT and style codes

2.6.1 Style Codes

Two conversion statements, one function and one parser construct, with different syntax and different capabilities. CON accepts a style code that CA has no place for, and TRY_C converts failure into NU rather than an error.

-- CAST is a parser construct: CAST(expr AS type). CONVERT is a function with
-- CONVERT(type, expr, style). The argument order is reversed.
SELECT CAST('2026-09-15' AS date)                  AS ViaCast,
       CONVERT(date, '2026-09-15')                   AS ViaConvert,
       CONVERT(date, '15/09/2026', 103)             AS BritishStyle,   -- dd/mm/yyyy
       CONVERT(date, '09/15/2026', 101)             AS UsStyle,        -- mm/dd/yyyy
       CONVERT(date, '20260915',     112)             AS IsoDate,        -- yyyymmdd, unambiguous
       CONVERT(datetime2(3), '2026-09-15T14:05:06.789', 126) AS IsoDateTime;
GO

-- TRY_CONVERT and TRY_CAST, SQL Server 2012+. Failure becomes NULL.
SELECT TRY_CONVERT(int, '1e5')            AS NotANumberIsNull,
       TRY_CONVERT(int, '42')             AS Parses,
       TRY_CAST('15/09/2026' AS date)          AS TryCast,
       TRY_CAST(NCHAR(0x1F600) AS xml);            -- surrogate pair, needs an n-prefixed source
GO

-- PARSE and TRY_PARSE, SQL Server 2012+. Always culture dependent, which is
-- why they are a poor choice in server-side code.
--   SELECT PARSE('15/09/2026' AS datetime2(0) USING 'en-GB');
--   SELECT TRY_PARSE('15/09/2026' AS datetime2(0) USING 'en-US');  -- NULL
GO

-- CONVERT also handles binary, varbinary, xml, money and the legacy
-- date types with their own style codes.
SELECT CONVERT(binary(4), 1001)                  AS ToBinary,
       CONVERT(varchar(10), CONVERT(binary(4), 1001), 2) AS ToHex, -- style 2 is the canonical hex form
       CONVERT(xml, N'<Order id="7"><Total>100.00</Total></Order>');
GO

CA is defined by the standard and accepts no style; CON is a T-SQL function whose third argument is the style code. Always name the style, because the default is a function of the session’s language setting rather than of the data.

StyleFormatApplies to
00, 100100, 101101, 103103, 104104, 106106, 107107, 108108Default, mm/dd/yymm/dd/yy, mm/dd/yyyymm/dd/y, dd/mm/yydd/mm/yy, dd.mm, dd mmm yydd mmm yy, dd mmm yyyydd mmm y, hh:mi:sshh:mi:ssdate, smalld, da, timetime. Language dependent, so avoid
1414mm-dd-yy hh:mi:ssmm-dd-yy hh:mi:ssdate
2020 / 120120yyyy-mm-dd hh:mi:ssyyyy-mm-dd hh:mi:ssdate, smalldatetimesmalldatetime. Unambiguous and the safe default
112112yAll date types. Unambiguous; the recommended form for input
121121yyyy-mm-dd hh:mi:ss.mmmyyyy-mm-dd hh:mi:ss.mmmdate, date
126126yyyy-mm-ddThh:mm:ss.mmmmmmyyyy-mm-ddThh:mm:ss.mmmmmm (+ offset for datetim)date, datetim, timetime. The correct round-trip format
127127yyyy-mm-ddThh:mm:ss.mmmmmmyyyy-mm-ddThh:mm:ss.mmmmmm, no offsetAll date and time types
130130, 131131dd mmm y hh:mi:ssdd mmm y hh:mi:ss with month namesdatetimedatetime. Language dependent

Caveats. Style codes are not interchangeable across types, and the same code means different formats for different destination types. A value round-tripped through CONVERT(varchar, d, 126)CONVERT(varchar, d, 126) and back is lossless for datetime2(7)datetime2(7) and lossy for date, whose resolution is coarser. For datetim, style 127 strips the offset and style 126 keeps it; choosing 127 on a column that is meant to hold an instant discards the only record of the zone.

Use style 126126 or 127127 for date and 112112 or 2323 for da, and never omit the style when parsing a string in server-side code. A style makes the conversion independent of the session’s language and default date format, which is the only property that makes a conversion reproducible across instances.

Exercises 2

  1. Create a table with decimal(5,2)decimal(5,2) and decimal(3,0)decimal(3,0) columns and query SQL_VARIAN and the derived types of ++, ** and // over them. Record the resulting precision and scale for each operator.
  2. Insert 0.10.1 and 0.20.2 into a fl column and a decimal(38,10)decimal(38,10) column, sum both, and record the difference. Then repeat with 1000 rows to show the accumulation.
  3. Write one query that lists every column in the reference schema whose collation differs from the database default, and one that lists every column using date or smalldatetimesmalldatetime.
  4. Run the same CON of a string to da under two different session language settings and record the two results, then repeat with style 112112.
  5. Convert a datetime2(7)datetime2(7) to var with styles 126 and 127, then back, and identify which value is not preserved. Explain why.
  6. For each of '1e5''1e5', ' 42 '' 42 ', '42.9''42.9' and '42,9''42,9', record the result of TRY_CONVERT(int, ...)TRY_CONVERT(int, ...) and explain the rule that produced it.

3 · DDL, Constraints and Schema Objects

3.1 CREATE, ALTER, DROP and idempotent scripts3.2 Primary keys, clustered and nonclustered3.3 Unique and check constraints3.4 Foreign keys and referential actions3.5 Defaults, IDENTITY and SEQUENCE3.6 Computed columns, CREATE OR ALTER and schemabinding

Data definition language in T-SQL is declarative and heavily guarded, and a schema written without that guard is a schema that cannot be deployed twice. This chapter covers the lifecycle statements, the constraint catalogue and the two schema decisions that are expensive to reverse: the clustered key and the key-generating object.

3.1 CREATE, ALTER, DROP and idempotent scripts

3.1.1 Script Templates

A deployment script that fails on its second run is not idempotent, and idempotence is the property that separates a script from a one-off change. T-SQL provides three ways to achieve it, and they differ in what they detect and what they tolerate.

-- Pattern 1: IF EXISTS on the DROP, available from SQL Server 2016.
-- Detects the object, not the condition, so it is the wrong test for a column.
IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.objects WHERE object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.Product') AND type = 'U')
    DROP TABLE dbo.Product;
GO

-- Pattern 2: guard on the catalogue, the general and correct form.
IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.columns WHERE object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.Customer') AND name = 'CreditLimit')
BEGIN
    ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ADD CreditLimit decimal(19,4) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_CreditLimit DEFAULT (0);
END
GO

-- Pattern 3: CREATE OR ALTER, SQL Server 2016+, for programmable objects only.
-- Valid for procedures, views, functions and triggers. NOT for tables.
CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_ActiveCustomer
AS
    SELECT CustomerID, Name, Email, CreditLimit
    FROM   dbo.Customer
    WHERE   CreditLimit > 0;
GO

Pattern 1 is the weakest of the three and is shown so the difference is explicit: it asks whether the table exists, not whether the table is correct. A guarded script that re-runs cleanly is still capable of leaving a column with the wrong type or collation, and it will report success.

Caveats. IF EXISTSIF EXISTS and CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER suppress a difference in definition, not just a difference in existence. A script guarded on existence will leave a table with the wrong columns, the wrong collation or the wrong constraint untouched and report success. Guard on the specific property the script is responsible for, and fail loudly when it is not there.

-- The reference schema. Note the deliberate constraint choices discussed below.
-- Run once against an empty database.
CREATE TABLE dbo.Customer
(
    CustomerID    int              IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    CustomerCode  uniqueidentifier NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_Code DEFAULT (NEWSEQUENTIALID()),
    Name          nvarchar(120)      NOT NULL,
    Email         varchar(254)       NULL,
    Country       char(2)          NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_Country DEFAULT ('GB'),
    CreditLimit   decimal(19,4)    NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_Credit DEFAULT (0),
    CreatedAt     datetime2(3)     NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_Created DEFAULT (SYSDATETIME()),
    RowVer        rowversion        NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_Customer            PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (CustomerID),
    CONSTRAINT UQ_Customer_Email       UNIQUE (Email),
    CONSTRAINT UQ_Customer_Code        UNIQUE (CustomerCode),
    CONSTRAINT CK_Customer_Credit       CHECK (CreditLimit >= 0),
    CONSTRAINT CK_Customer_Country      CHECK (Country >= 'AA' AND Country <= 'ZZ')
);
GO

CREATE TABLE dbo.Product
(
    ProductID   int           IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    SKU         varchar(32)     NOT NULL,
    Name        nvarchar(200) NOT NULL,
    UnitPrice   decimal(19,4) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Product_Price DEFAULT (0),
    IsActive    bit            NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_Product_Active DEFAULT (1),
    CONSTRAINT PK_Product        PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (ProductID),
    CONSTRAINT UQ_Product_SKU       UNIQUE (SKU),
    CONSTRAINT CK_Product_Price      CHECK (UnitPrice >= 0)
);
GO

CREATE TABLE dbo.SalesOrder
(
    SalesOrderID int          IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    CustomerID   int          NOT NULL,
    OrderDate    datetime2(3)  NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_SalesOrder_Date DEFAULT (SYSDATETIME()),
    Status       char(3)       NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_SalesOrder_Status DEFAULT ('NEW'),
    TotalAmount  decimal(19,4) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_SalesOrder_Total DEFAULT (0),
    CONSTRAINT PK_SalesOrder        PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (SalesOrderID),
    CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrder_Customer FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID)
        REFERENCES dbo.Customer (CustomerID) ON DELETE NO ACTION ON UPDATE NO ACTION,
    CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrder_Status    CHECK (Status IN ('NEW','PAYD','SHP','CXL'))
);
GO

CREATE TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
(
    SalesOrderID int           NOT NULL,
    LineNo       smallint       NOT NULL CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_Line CHECK (LineNo > 0),
    ProductID    int           NOT NULL,
    Quantity     int           NOT NULL,
    UnitPrice    decimal(19,4) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_SalesOrderLine  PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (SalesOrderID, LineNo),
    CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrderLine_Order FOREIGN KEY (SalesOrderID)
        REFERENCES dbo.SalesOrder (SalesOrderID) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrderLine_Product FOREIGN KEY (ProductID)
        REFERENCES dbo.Product (ProductID) ON DELETE NO ACTION,
    CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_Qty   CHECK (Quantity > 0)
);
GO

Inline constraints and named CONST clauses are equivalent. Named constraints are the professional choice: they appear in error messages, they can be dropped and recreated by name during a migration, and a DROP CONSTRAINTDROP CONSTRAINT without a name depends on a system-generated name that differs between inline and table-level syntax.

3.2 Primary keys, clustered and nonclustered

3.2.1 Index Types

The choice of clustered key is the most consequential decision in a SQL Server schema, more consequential than any other except the choice of data types. It is irreversible without rebuilding the table, and every read, write, backup and restore cost follows from it.

AspectClustered indexNonclustered index
Count per tableExactly one, or noneUp to 999 on SQL Server 2025 (128 before 2016)
What it storesThe entire row, in key orderKey columns plus every INC column, in key order
Row lookup for a key not presentn/aRequires a navigation to the clustered index, by row locatorrow locator or key
Physical orderDefines the order rows are stored inIndependent
Default for PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEYYes, unless NONCLU is specifiedNo
No such indexThe table is a heap: no ordering, forwarding pointers, no keyNever found on its own
-- One clustered index per table. This is the error the engine raises.
--   CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX CX_Product ON dbo.Product(SKU);
--   Msg 1750, "There can be only one clustered index on the table."
GO

-- Clustered on a narrow, immutable, monotonically increasing surrogate.
-- Nonclustered on the business key, which is what queries actually search on.
CREATE UNIQUE NONCLUSTERED INDEX UX_Product_SKU
