    ON dbo.Product (SKU)
    INCLUDE (Name, UnitPrice, IsActive);
GO

-- A covering index: every column the query needs is in the leaf, so the
-- clustered index is never touched. Size the INCLUDE list deliberately.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_Customer_Date
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate)
    -- INCLUDE (Status, TotalAmount);   -- narrow; status is a filtered predicate candidate
    WITH (FILLFACTOR = 90, DATA_COMPRESSION = PAGE);
GO

-- Filtered index: only NEW orders, and the predicate must be implied by the
-- WHERE clause of any query that uses it. SQL Server 2008+.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_Open
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (OrderDate)
    -- INCLUDE (CustomerID, TotalAmount)
    WHERE Status = 'NEW';
GO

-- Inventory of what the table actually has.
SELECT t.name        AS TableName,
       i.name        AS IndexName,
       i.type_desc   AS IndexType,
       i.is_primary_key AS IsPK,
       STUFF(FOR SELECT ', ' + c.name
                      FROM sys.index_columns AS ic
                      INNER JOIN sys.columns AS c ON c.object_id = ic.object_id AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
                      WHERE ic.object_id = i.object_id AND ic.index_id = i.index_id AND ic.key_ordinal > 0
                      ORDER BY ic.key_ordinal
                      FOR XML PATH(''), TYPE).value('.', NVARCHAR(4000)), '', '') AS KeyColumns,
       i.size        AS SizeKB
FROM sys.indexes AS i
INNER JOIN sys.tables AS t ON t.object_id = i.object_id
WHERE t.name IN ('Customer', 'Product', 'SalesOrder', 'SalesOrderLine')
  AND i.index_id > 0
ORDER BY t.name, i.type_desc DESC;
GO

CREATE INDEX ... WITH (SCHEMABINDING)CREATE INDEX ... WITH (SCHEMABINDING) is implied for a filtered index, which is why a filtered index prevents the underlying column from being altered while it exists. Dropping and recreating an index to make a schema change is a normal part of a migration script, not a workaround.

Caveats. A wide or non-monotonic clustered key is the most common cause of a database that degrades over time. uniqueid as the clustered key randomises insert position and fragments every page. An IDEN column is the safe default, with two exceptions: a table that is more than 50 percent updates and deletes, where fragmentation is worse than the insert cost; and a key that must be generated across several tables, where a SEQU is preferable. A heap has no way to find a row without a nonclustered index, and every nonclustered index on it carries a 8-byte forwarding pointer that must be followed.

Applies to. The 999 nonclustered index limit and the 128 limit before it both apply to SQL Server 2025. INC columns do not participate in key order and are limited to 1023 columns, but a wide INC list makes every index entry large enough to need extra pages, which is why covering indexes are used for one or two narrow columns and not for the whole row.

3.3 Unique and check constraints

3.3.1 Constraint Details

Two constraints that look similar and enforce entirely different things. A unique constraint is a rule about the whole table, enforced with an index; a check constraint is a rule about one row at a time, enforced by evaluating an expression. Neither can be allowed to lie about data that already exists.

-- UNIQUE and NULL in SQL Server. This is stricter than most other engines.
--   CREATE TABLE t (a int NULL UNIQUE);
--   INSERT INTO t (a) VALUES (NULL);   -- ok
--   INSERT INTO t (a) VALUES (NULL);   -- Msg 2601, duplicate key: a NULL is a value
GO

-- The usual requirement is "at most one row with no email", which a plain
-- UNIQUE constraint cannot express. A filtered unique index can.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer DROP CONSTRAINT UQ_Customer_Email;
GO
CREATE UNIQUE NONCLUSTERED INDEX UX_Customer_Email
    ON dbo.Customer (Email)
    WHERE Email IS NOT NULL;
GO

-- A composite UNIQUE treats the tuple as the value, so NULL in one column
-- still permits distinct rows.
--   CREATE TABLE t (a int, b int, CONSTRAINT UQ_t UNIQUE (a, b));
--   INSERT INTO t VALUES (1, NULL);  -- ok
--   INSERT INTO t VALUES (1, NULL);  -- duplicate
--   INSERT INTO t VALUES (1, 5);    -- different tuple, ok
GO

-- Check constraints are evaluated per row, and a row that does not supply the
-- column is not checked at all: the predicate evaluates to UNKNOWN, which
-- passes. The condition must reject NULL explicitly if that is not wanted.
--   CHECK (Quantity > 0)          accepts NULL
--   CHECK (Quantity IS NOT NULL AND Quantity > 0)   rejects NULL
GO

SQL Server treats NU as a value for the purpose of uniqueness, which makes it stricter than engines where several NU rows are accepted. A filtered unique index expresses the permissive rule explicitly, and is also the only way to write a partial-uniqueness requirement at all.

Caveats. A CH constraint is a row-local predicate. It cannot reference another row, another table, or a non-deterministic function, so "the sum of the lines must equal the header total" is not expressible as a check constraint, and the belief that an INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT is exempt from checks is wrong: the constraint is enforced on every inserted row exactly as it is on an INSERTINSERT. What INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT cannot do is enforce a rule that spans rows, which is why that rule needs a trigger, a materialised total maintained by the application, or a validation query run as a job. BULK INSERTBULK INSERT and b do suppress check constraints, but only with IGNORE_CONSTRAINTSIGNORE_CONSTRAINTS.

-- Adding a constraint validates existing data by default, and fails if any row
-- violates it. WITH NOCHECK skips validation of existing rows permanently.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
    WITH CHECK ADD CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_Qty_Max CHECK (Quantity <= 9999);
GO

-- Trusting a constraint after loading data. WITH NOCHECK is recorded in the
-- catalogue, and the optimiser will not rely on the constraint until re-enabled.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine NOCHECK CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_Qty_Max;
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine CHECK CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_Qty_Max;
GO

-- The audit query: which constraints exist, and are they trusted.
SELECT s.name AS SchemaName, t.name AS TableName, cc.name AS ConstraintName,
       cc.definition AS Definition, cc.is_disabled AS Disabled,
       cc.is_not_trusted AS NotTrusted
FROM sys.check_constraints AS cc
INNER JOIN sys.tables  AS t ON t.object_id = cc.parent_object_id
INNER JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = t.schema_id
WHERE cc.is_disabled = 1 OR cc.is_not_trusted = 1;
GO

WITH NOCHECKWITH NOCHECK skips validation of existing rows and marks the constraint untrusted, which the optimiser then declines to rely on. A migration that loads data with the constraint disabled and then forgets to re-enable it leaves a rule the engine is not enforcing.

3.4 Foreign keys and referential actions

3.4.1 Referential Actions

A foreign key is a constraint that requires a value in one column to exist in another table’s key. It is enforced on every insert and update of the referencing side, and its referential action decides what happens to children when the parent row disappears. The default action is the safe one, and the alternatives each carry a cost that is invisible in the DDL.

ActionEffect on child rows when the parent is deleted or updatedCost
NO ACTIONNO ACTIONThe operation is rejected if any child references the parentNone; the default and the only action that is a pure constraint
CASChild rows are deleted, or their key is updatedBounded only by the size of the child table; locks are held for the whole cascade
SET NULLSET NULLChild key columns are set to NURequires every referencing column to be nullable, which usually means the key is no longer a key
SET DEFAULTSET DEFAULTChild key columns are set to their default valueRequires a default on every referencing column, and the default must itself exist
-- The composite key, and the cascade on the order lines. One order delete
-- therefore removes every line of that order in the same transaction.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
    ADD CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrderLine_Order
    FOREIGN KEY (SalesOrderID)
    REFERENCES dbo.SalesOrder (SalesOrderID)
    ON DELETE CASCADE
    ON UPDATE NO ACTION;   -- ON UPDATE CASCADE on a surrogate key is unnecessary
GO

-- The product reference deliberately has no cascade: a product that has ever
-- been ordered cannot be deleted, which is the correct rule for a catalogue.
--   DELETE FROM dbo.Product WHERE ProductID = 1;  -- Msg 547
GO

-- Changing the action on an existing key.
--   ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine DROP CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrderLine_Order;
--   ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine ADD CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrderLine_Order
--       FOREIGN KEY (SalesOrderID) REFERENCES dbo.SalesOrder (SalesOrderID) ON DELETE NO ACTION;
GO

-- The full inventory of referential actions, for a schema review.
SELECT fk.name          AS FKName,
       OBJECT_NAME(fk.parent_object_id) AS ChildTable,
       OBJECT_NAME(fk.referenced_object_id) AS ParentTable,
       fk.delete_referential_action_desc AS OnDelete,
       fk.update_referential_action_desc AS OnUpdate,
       fk.is_disabled          AS Disabled,
       fk.is_not_trusted      AS NotTrusted,
       fk.is_not_for_replication AS SkippedForReplication
FROM sys.foreign_keys AS fk
ORDER BY ChildTable, FKName;
GO

A cascade is bounded only by the size of the child table, so its cost is a property of the data rather than of the statement. The audit query is the first thing to run when a deletion has taken longer than expected, because it shows which constraints can multiply a single delete into a large one.

Caveats. ON DELETE CASCADEON DELETE CASCADE is a statement about worst-case work, not about average work. Deleting one customer with a cascade across five levels can remove millions of rows while holding locks on all of them, and the transaction log grows accordingly; the statement does not know or care how large the child set is. Cascades also do not compose predictably with triggers on the child table, and the order in which they fire is not something to build on. A foreign key to a uniqueid column costs 16 bytes per referencing row, which is a real cost on a wide composite key.

Requirement. The referenced columns must be the primary key or a unique constraint of the parent table; a foreign key cannot reference a bare nonclustered index. A foreign key to a nullable column is legal but then NU in the child satisfies the constraint without matching any parent, which is usually not what the constraint was intended to express. See 2.5 for the implicit-conversion cost of joining columns whose types differ.

3.5 Defaults, IDENTITY and SEQUENCE

Three mechanisms produce a value when a column is not supplied, and the choice between them is decided by whether the value is a property of the column or of the row. A default is a column property. An identity is a column property too, and carries consequences for data movement. A sequence is a schema object, shared and explicitly consumed.

-- Defaults. A default applies when the column is omitted or the DEFAULT keyword
-- is used, NOT when an explicit NULL is supplied to a nullable column.
INSERT INTO dbo.Product (SKU, Name) VALUES ('A-1', 'A');            -- UnitPrice = 0, IsActive = 1
INSERT INTO dbo.Product (SKU, Name, UnitPrice, IsActive)
       VALUES ('A-2', 'B', 9.99, NULL); -- IsActive = NULL, not 1
INSERT INTO dbo.Product (SKU, Name, UnitPrice, IsActive)
       VALUES ('A-3', 'C', 9.99, DEFAULT);  -- IsActive = 1
GO

-- IDENTITY. Values are generated by the storage engine and cannot be
-- supplied unless IDENTITY_INSERT is on, and only for one table per session.
--   SET IDENTITY_INSERT dbo.Product ON;
--   INSERT INTO dbo.Product (ProductID, SKU, Name) VALUES (500, 'A-4', 'D');
--   SET IDENTITY_INSERT dbo.Product OFF;
GO

-- Gaps are normal and unavoidable: a rolled-back transaction burns the value.
--   SET IDENTITY_INSERT dbo.Product ON;
--   DELETE FROM dbo.Product WHERE ProductID = 500;
--   SET IDENTITY_INSERT dbo.Product OFF;
--   SELECT IDENT_CURRENT('dbo.Product');   -- advanced past 500
GO

-- SEQUENCE, SQL Server 2012+. A schema object, not part of a column.
CREATE SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber
    AS bigint
    START WITH 100000
    INCREMENT BY 1
    MINVALUE 1
    MAXVALUE 9223372036854775807
    NO CYCLE
    CACHE 1000;
GO

-- Consuming it. NEXT VALUE FOR is not permitted in a DEFAULT definition.
INSERT INTO dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, Status, TotalAmount)
VALUES (1, 'NEW', 0);
GO
SELECT NEXT VALUE FOR dbo.OrderNumber AS NextOrderNumber;
GO

-- Reset, and observe the cache behaviour on a restart.
--   ALTER SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber RESTART WITH 100000;
--   SELECT name, current_value, start_value, increment, minimum_value, is_cycling
--   FROM sys.sequences;
GO

Inserting an explicit NU into a nullable column with a default stores NU; the default is only applied when the column is omitted or the keyword DEF is used. This is the single most common cause of a default that "does not work".

AspectIDENSEQU
ScopeOne column of one tableOne schema object, readable by any table or statement
Value generated byThe INS statement, automaticallyNEXT VALUE FORNEXT VALUE FOR or a scalar function, explicitly
Order of allocationOrder of physical insert, so concurrent sessions may receive out-of-order keysOrder of execution of NEXT VALUE FORNEXT VALUE FOR
RollbackValues are consumed; gaps appearValues are consumed; gaps appear. A RES can reuse them
Server restartPersisted with the tablePersisted in sys.se; a CA greater than 1 may skip unused values on restart
Usable in a DEFYes, that is what it isNo. Use DEFAULT (NEXT VALUE FOR ...)DEFAULT (NEXT VALUE FOR ...) only in a documented SQL Server 2012+ sequence-backed default, which the product still does not support on all paths
Multiple tablesNoYes, which is the reason to choose it
Dropped with the tableYesNo; the object remains until dropped explicitly
Explicit value insertIDENTITY_INSERT ONIDENTITY_INSERT ON, one table per sessionOrdinary insert; the sequence is simply not consulted

Caveats. Do not assume identity values are gapless or ordered, and do not derive business meaning from them. They are skipped on rollback, on failed inserts and on multi-row inserts that fail partway, and with concurrent sessions two transactions can commit in the reverse of the order their keys were allocated. If a value must appear in a document, allocate it from a sequence in the application and insert it explicitly.

3.6 Computed columns, CREATE OR ALTER and schemabinding

Three features that look unrelated and share one purpose: they let the engine know about a dependency instead of finding out at run time. A persisted computed column stores a value that could be derived. CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER makes a script re-runnable for programmable objects. Schemabinding makes a dependency a hard, checkable one.

-- Computed columns. A PERSISTED column is stored; a non-persisted one is
-- computed on read. Only a deterministic, precise expression may be persisted,
-- and only a persisted one may be indexed or used in an indexed view.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
    ADD LineTotal AS (CONVERT(decimal(19,4), Quantity * UnitPrice)) PERSISTED NOT NULL;
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
    ADD CONSTRAINT CK_SalesOrderLine_LineTotal CHECK (LineTotal >= 0);
GO

-- Determinism. GETDATE() is not deterministic, so it cannot be persisted,
-- and it cannot appear in an index or a filtered index predicate.
--   ALTER TABLE t ADD BadCol AS (GETDATE()) PERSISTED;   -- Msg 414
GO

-- Schemabinding on a view. Every reference must be two-part, and the view
-- may not use SELECT *. The dependency then becomes a hard constraint on
-- the base tables, which is what makes an indexed view possible.
--   CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_CustomerRevenue WITH SCHEMABINDING AS
--       SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, SUM(o.TotalAmount) AS Revenue
--       FROM dbo.Customer AS c
--       JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
--       GROUP BY c.CustomerID, c.Name;
GO

-- What the catalogue reports about a dependency. The runtime error is a
-- by-product; the real value is that the engine can see the lineage.
SELECT s.name AS SchemaName, v.name AS ViewName,
       REPLACE(CONVERT(nvarchar(4000), v.definition), CHAR(13), ' ') AS Definition,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(v.object_id, 'IsSchemabound')  AS IsSchemabound,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(v.object_id, 'IsIndexed')      AS IsIndexed,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(v.object_id, 'IsUpdatable')   AS IsUpdatable
FROM sys.views AS v
INNER JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = v.schema_id;
GO

-- Every object in a schema, for a deployment diff. This is the catalogue query
-- to build first; it answers "what is defined here" without a dacpac.
SELECT s.name AS SchemaName, o.name AS ObjectName, o.type_desc AS Type,
       o.create_date AS Created, o.modify_date AS LastModified
FROM sys.objects AS o
INNER JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = o.schema_id
WHERE o.is_ms_shipped = 0 AND o.type IN ('U','V','P','FN','IF','TF','TR','D','C','PK','UQ','F','C ','SN')
ORDER BY s.name, o.type_desc, o.name;
GO

Determinism is the gate on everything here. A computed column that cannot be persisted cannot be indexed, cannot participate in an indexed view and cannot appear in a filtered index predicate, which is what makes non-deterministic expressions unusable in a schema.

Applies to. CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER requires SQL Server 2016 or later and applies to procedures, views, functions and triggers only. It is not accepted for tables, indexes or constraints. WITH SCHEMABINDINGWITH SCHEMABINDING on a view or scalar function has existed since SQL Server 2005 and is mandatory for an indexed view, an indexed computed column and a filtered index over a computed column.

Always name a DEC, CH or F constraint explicitly. A system-generated name is stable only until the constraint is dropped and recreated, and an ALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINTALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINT script that references one is one schema change away from failing at 02:00.

Exercises 3

  1. Create the four-table reference schema from 3.1. Run the script twice and confirm the second run is a no-op or fails cleanly. Then add IF EXISTSIF EXISTS guards and confirm both runs succeed.
  2. Create a table with a nullable column and a UNI constraint, insert two NU values, and record the error. Replace it with a filtered unique index and confirm both rows are now accepted.
  3. Add a CH constraint that accepts NU, then one that rejects it, and demonstrate the difference with a single-row INS table for each.
  4. Attempt an INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT that would produce rows violating an existing CH constraint, and record the error. Then repeat the same load with b and IGNORE_CO and record what changed.
  5. Create a SEQU, allocate three values inside a transaction, roll it back, and show that the next allocation continues rather than restarting. Do the same with an IDEN column and compare.
  6. Add a persisted computed column to dbo.Sales, then attempt to add the same column as AS (GETDATE()) PERSISTEDAS (GETDATE()) PERSISTED and record the error number.

4 · Querying, Joins and Set Operations

4.1 Projection, aliases and the shape of a SELECT4.2 Joins, the LEFT and WHERE anti-pattern4.3 UNION, UNION ALL, EXCEPT and INTERSECT4.4 APPLY, GROUP BY and HAVING4.5 Window functions and frame clauses4.6 PIVOT, UNPIVOT and deterministic pagination

T-SQL is declarative, and the query is a description of a result rather than a procedure for producing one. That gives a small set of composable operators with precise semantics, several of which differ from other engines, and a handful of ways to write the same request with materially different cost. This chapter covers the operators and the semantics; the cost model is in the tuning chapter.

4.1 Projection, aliases and the shape of a SELECT

The logical order of a T-SQL query is fixed: FR, then WH, then GROUP BYGROUP BY, then HAV, then SEL, then ORDER BYORDER BY. The order in which clauses are written is not the order in which they are evaluated, and the single most consequential consequence is that a column alias does not exist yet when WH is evaluated.

-- The five clauses. The written order is not the evaluation order.
-- WHERE is evaluated before SELECT, so an alias cannot be used in WHERE.
--   SELECT  [ SELECT ]  FROM  [ FROM ]  WHERE  [ WHERE ]  GROUP BY  [ GROUP BY ]  HAVING  [ HAVING ]  ORDER BY  [ ORDER BY ]
GO
SELECT c.Name AS CustomerName,
       SUM(o.TotalAmount) AS Revenue
FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
INNER JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = o.CustomerID
WHERE o.OrderDate >= '20260101'          -- Revenue is not yet defined here
GROUP BY c.Name
HAVING SUM(o.TotalAmount) > 0       -- an alias IS allowed here
ORDER BY Revenue DESC;
GO

-- Ordering a column by its alias is the documented exception.
SELECT c.Name AS CustomerName
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
ORDER BY CustomerName;   -- permitted: ORDER BY runs after SELECT
GO

-- Derived tables and CTEs are named and therefore aliasable in the outer query.
-- The WITH must be preceded by a semicolon; see 1.5.
-- ;WITH recent AS (
--     SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, TotalAmount
--     FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
--     WHERE  OrderDate >= '20260101')
-- SELECT r.CustomerID, c.Name, r.TotalAmount
-- FROM   recent AS r
-- JOIN   dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = r.CustomerID;
GO

-- Never SELECT * in application code. The result shape is a contract,
-- and a new column changes it without any code change.
GO

There is no form of the query in which an alias defined in the SEL list is visible to the WH clause, because WH is evaluated first. The exception is ORDER BYORDER BY, which is evaluated after SELECTSELECT.

ClauseEvaluatedSees column aliasesFilters
FR1NoSource of rows
WH2NoRows, before grouping
GROUP BYGROUP BY3NoGroups
HAV4YesGroups
SEL5YesProjects columns
ORDER BYORDER BY6YesOrders rows

Requirement. A table alias is mandatory for clarity whenever more than one table is in scope, and required to distinguish a self join. Qualify every column reference with its alias: an unqualified name that exists in two tables is a compile error, and one that exists in only one may still be the wrong one after a future change.

4.2 Joins, the LEFT and WHERE anti-pattern

A join is a filter on a Cartesian product, and the join type decides which rows survive. The single most common defect in hand-written T-SQL is a LE join whose WH clause then discards the very rows the outer join preserved, silently converting it into an inner join.

-- INNER: only customers with at least one order.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, o.OrderDate
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID;
GO

-- LEFT: every customer, with orders where they exist. Note the IS NULL in the
-- join predicate, which is what makes it a genuine outer join.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, o.OrderDate
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
LEFT JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o
       ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
      AND o.OrderDate >= '20260101';
GO

-- The anti-pattern. This predicate is evaluated after the join and discards
-- exactly the unmatched rows a LEFT JOIN exists to keep, turning it into
-- an INNER JOIN. If an inner join is what is wanted, write one.
--   WHERE o.OrderDate >= '20260101'
GO

-- The correct form: to keep every customer and report whether they ordered.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, o.SalesOrderID
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
LEFT JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
WHERE o.SalesOrderID IS NULL;   -- customers with no orders at all
GO

-- CROSS JOIN: every pair. Here it generates a complete grid of months and
-- customers, so that a LEFT JOIN can then find the gaps.
SELECT m.MonthStart, c.CustomerID, o.TotalAmount
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
CROSS JOIN dbo.Month AS m
LEFT JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
   AND o.OrderDate >= m.MonthStart
   AND o.OrderDate < DATEADD(MONTH, 1, m.MonthStart)
WHERE o.SalesOrderID IS NULL AND o.TotalAmount IS NULL
   AND m.MonthStart >= '2026-01-01' AND m.MonthStart < '2026-07-01';
GO

The three joins differ only in which rows survive, and the fourth listing is the version that does not. Note that the filter on the right table belongs in the O clause; moved to WH it discards the unmatched rows the outer join was written to keep.

-- Self join. Aliases are mandatory, and the two instances are unrelated objects
-- to the engine: this is a manager hierarchy from one table.
SELECT e.CustomerID AS EmployeeID, e.Name AS EmployeeName,
       m.CustomerID AS ManagerID,   m.Name AS ManagerName
FROM dbo.Customer AS e
LEFT JOIN dbo.Customer AS m ON m.CustomerID = e.ManagerID
-- where the reference schema has no ManagerID, the pattern is:
--   ON m.CustomerID = e.ManagerID AND m.ManagerID IS NOT NULL
GO

-- A self join is also how a duplicate value is found without a window function.
SELECT a.Name, a.CustomerID
FROM dbo.Customer AS a
INNER JOIN dbo.Customer AS b
        ON a.Name = b.Name   AND a.CustomerID < b.CustomerID;
GO

-- FULL OUTER JOIN exists and works. It is rarely what is wanted, because a
-- row that matches nothing on both sides produces a row of NULLs that is
-- difficult to reason about. Two LEFT JOINs, or EXCEPT, express the intent
-- more clearly and are usually cheaper.
--   SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer FULL OUTER JOIN dbo.Product ON 1 = 0;
GO

-- JOIN ... USING-style column coalescing: make the outer side's NULLs explicit.
SELECT c.CustomerID,
       COALESCE(o.OrderDate, CAST('1900-01-01' AS datetime2(3))) AS FirstOrder
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
LEFT JOIN (SELECT CustomerID, MIN(OrderDate) AS OrderDate
             FROM dbo.SalesOrder GROUP BY CustomerID) AS o
       ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID;
GO

A self join is a join of a table to itself, and the two instances are unrelated to the engine. The aliases are therefore not cosmetic: without them there is no way to say which role a column plays, and the query cannot be written at all.

Caveats. IS NULLIS NULL in a LEFT JOINLEFT JOIN predicate means something different from == NU: the former is a deliberate inclusion of unmatched rows, and the latter is the overwhelmingly common bug where it is written in the O clause of a LEFT JOINLEFT JOIN and the query then behaves as an inner join. If a nullable column of the right table is used for the anti-join test, a matched row whose column is itself NU is indistinguishable from an unmatched row; test the key column, not a data column.

4.3 UNION, UNION ALL, EXCEPT and INTERSECT

The set operators combine the results of two queries. Three of them are defined to remove duplicates and one is not, and the difference is the largest single cost difference available in query formulation. EXC and INTE are always distinct.

-- UNION removes duplicates, which requires a sort or hash aggregate over the whole
-- result. UNION ALL does not, and is the correct choice unless a duplicate is
-- genuinely meaningful.
SELECT CustomerID FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE Status = 'NEW'
UNION ALL
SELECT CustomerID FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE Status = 'PAYD'
ORDER BY CustomerID;
GO

-- EXCEPT and INTERSECT: always distinct, and never preserve row order.
-- Customers who have never paid.
SELECT CustomerID FROM dbo.SalesOrder
EXCEPT
SELECT CustomerID FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE Status = 'PAYD';
GO

-- Products ordered in 2026 and also in 2025.
-- ;WITH y26 AS (SELECT DISTINCT ProductID FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine
--                   JOIN dbo.SalesOrder ON SalesOrder.SalesOrderID = SalesOrderLine.SalesOrderID
--                  WHERE OrderDate >= '20260101'),
--      y25 AS (SELECT DISTINCT ProductID FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine
--                   JOIN dbo.SalesOrder ON SalesOrder.SalesOrderID = SalesOrderLine.SalesOrderID
--                  WHERE OrderDate <  '20260101')
-- SELECT ProductID FROM y26 INTERSECT SELECT ProductID FROM y25;
GO

-- Type resolution follows the same precedence rules as a UNION in any engine:
-- the result column takes the higher-precedence type of the two branches.
SELECT CAST(1 AS int) AS Ints
UNION ALL
SELECT CAST('2' AS varchar(5));
GO

-- Duplicates are values, including NULL, for set comparison purposes.
--   SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1;  -- three rows
--   SELECT 1 UNION    SELECT 1 UNION    SELECT 1;  -- one row
--   SELECT NULL UNION SELECT NULL;               -- no rows
GO

UN pays for a sort or a hash aggregate across the combined result to eliminate duplicates, and that cost is proportional to the whole result rather than to the number of duplicates. Where disjointness is known, UNION ALLUNION ALL is not a style preference but a different query with a different cost.

OperatorRemoves duplicatesRequires matching column countsPreserves duplicatesTypical use
UNYesYesNoCombining result sets that are known to be disjoint, or where distinctness is required
UNION ALLUNION ALLNoYesYesThe default. Concatenating, staging and load tables
EXCAlwaysYesNoSet difference; an anti-join without a join
INTEAlwaysYesNoSet intersection; usually an inner join is clearer

Applies to. All four operators require the same number of columns in both branches, and the result column names come from the first branch. The precedence is INTE before UN and EXC, which is evaluated left to right; where that precedence is not what was intended, parenthesise each branch with a derived table.

4.4 APPLY, GROUP BY and HAVING

SQL Server 2005 introduced AP, which extends a join to a correlated right-hand expression, and that is the only way to express a per-row subquery in the result of another query. It is also the correct tool for the "top N per group" pattern, which a plain join cannot express.

-- CROSS APPLY behaves as an inner join; OUTER APPLY as a left join. The right-hand
-- side is a table expression, and may reference the left row.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, t.LastOrderDate, t.OrderCount
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
OUTER APPLY (
    SELECT TOP (1) OrderDate AS LastOrderDate, COUNT_BIG(*) AS OrderCount
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
    WHERE o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
    ORDER BY o.OrderDate DESC
) AS t;
GO

-- The top N per group pattern. A join cannot express "the three most",
-- because the number of rows per group is not a join condition.
SELECT r.Rank, r.ProductID, r.Name, r.TotalQty
FROM (
    SELECT p.ProductID, p.Name, SUM(l.Quantity) AS TotalQty,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY l.ProductID ORDER BY SUM(l.Quantity) DESC, l.LineNo) AS Rank
    FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l
    INNER JOIN dbo.Product AS p ON p.ProductID = l.ProductID
    GROUP BY p.ProductID, p.Name, l.ProductID, l.LineNo
) AS r
WHERE r.Rank <= 3
ORDER BY r.Rank, r.ProductID;
GO

-- APPLY over a table-valued function, and over OPENJSON (SQL Server 2016+),
-- which is the canonical use: a per-row parser.
--   SELECT o.SalesOrderID, j.[key], j.value
--   FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
--   CROSS APPLY OPENJSON(o.Payload) AS j;
GO

OUTER APPLYOUTER APPLY is the form that returns a null-extended row when the correlated subquery produces nothing, and that is what makes the per-customer figures appear for customers with no orders. Note the ORDER BYORDER BY inside the subquery: without it, TOP (1)TOP (1) returns an arbitrary row.

-- GROUP BY: every non-aggregated column must be in the list. SQL Server does not
-- detect functional dependency except for the key of a table it can see,
-- which is why grouping by a key lets you select the rest of that table only.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, COUNT_BIG(*) AS OrderCount,
       SUM(o.TotalAmount) AS Revenue
FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
INNER JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = o.CustomerID
GROUP BY c.CustomerID, c.Name
HAVING COUNT_BIG(*) > 3 AND SUM(o.TotalAmount) >= 1000
ORDER BY Revenue DESC;
GO

-- WHERE filters rows; HAVING filters groups. Moving a filter from WHERE to
-- HAVING does not change the answer for a deterministic aggregate, and does
-- change the work done. WHERE is always cheaper.
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE Status = 'SHP' HAVING COUNT(*) > 0;  -- valid
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE COUNT(*) > 0;                      -- Msg 414
GO

-- COUNT semantics that catch everyone out.
SELECT COUNT(*)                          AS AllRows,
       COUNT(UnitPrice)                    AS NonNullPrices,
       COUNT(DISTINCT UnitPrice)          AS DistinctPrices,
       SUM(UnitPrice) / NULLIF(COUNT(UnitPrice), 0) AS AvgPrice,
       COUNT_BIG(*)                        AS BigCount,   -- returns bigint, does not overflow
       MIN(UnitPrice)                    AS Cheapest
FROM dbo.Product;
GO

-- Grouping sets, rollup and cube: subtotals in one pass. 2005+.
SELECT YEAR(OrderDate) AS OrderYear, DATEDIFF(MONTH, 0, OrderDate) AS OrderMonth,
       SUM(TotalAmount) AS Revenue
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
GROUP BY ROLLUP(YEAR(OrderDate), DATEDIFF(MONTH, 0, OrderDate));
GO

GROUP BYGROUP BY is evaluated before SEL, which is why a non-aggregated column must be in the list. A HAV that references an alias works because HAV is evaluated after SEL, which is the same rule as for ORDER BYORDER BY.

Applies to. AP requires SQL Server 2005 or later. COUN is the correct aggregate where the row count can exceed i range, which is 2.1 billion rows — reached by a single large table, and by any table that is not filtered. GROUPING SETSGROUPING SETS, ROL and CU require SQL Server 2005; GROU and GROUP are needed to tell a subtotal total from a real value of NULLNULL.

Caveats. A GROUP BYGROUP BY in a query that selects a large non-key text column aggregates on that column, and a hash aggregate that spills to disk is one of the most expensive operations available. A HAV on a column that is not aggregated and not grouped is a compile error, not a silent extension of the grouping. And a CO over a nullable column never returns 0 for an empty group; it returns 0 for a group with rows, and no group at all for no rows.

4.5 Window functions and frame clauses

A window function aggregates or ranks without collapsing rows, which is the operation a GROUP BYGROUP BY structurally cannot express. Windows were introduced in SQL Server 2005 and gained the frame clause in 2012. The ranking functions differ only in how they treat ties, and the frame clause determines exactly which rows a function sees.

-- The four ranking functions on the same data, differing only in tie handling.
SELECT p.SKU, p.UnitPrice,
       ROW_NUMBER()  OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice DESC) AS RowNum,   -- 1,2,3,4: no gaps, arbitrary order within a tie
       RANK()        OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice DESC) AS Rank,     -- 1,1,3,4: gaps after a tie
       DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice DESC) AS DenseRank, -- 1,1,2,3: no gaps
       NTILE(4)      OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice DESC) AS Quartile, -- 1..4, buckets not quartiles by value
       PERCENT_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice)  AS PctRank,  -- 0..1
       CUME_DIST()   OVER (ORDER BY p.UnitPrice)  AS CumeDist   -- 0..1, inclusive
FROM dbo.Product AS p
ORDER BY p.UnitPrice DESC, p.SKU;   -- the outer ORDER BY makes it deterministic
GO

-- LAG and LEAD. The default offset is 1 and the default return is NULL.
SELECT OrderDate, TotalAmount,
       LAG(TotalAmount, 1, 0)  OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate) AS PrevAmount,
       LAG(TotalAmount, 1, 0)  OVER (PARTITION BY CustomerID ORDER BY OrderDate) AS PrevByCustomer,
       LEAD(TotalAmount, 2, NULL) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate) AS Next2Amount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- Moving aggregates. SUM() OVER () replaces the self-join that adds a
-- grand total to every row.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount,
       SUM(TotalAmount) OVER ()                                          AS GrandTotal,
       SUM(TotalAmount) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate
                        ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS RunningTotal,
       AVG(TotalAmount) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate
                        ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)             AS Trailing3Avg,
       TotalAmount - LAG(TotalAmount, 1, 0) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate) AS Delta
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
ORDER BY OrderDate;
GO

The four functions differ only in tie handling, and the outer ORDER BYORDER BY includes a tiebreaker so the output is stable. Without it, two rows with the same price may be returned in either order on different executions, and any client that pages over this result will show them inconsistently.

FunctionClassificationRequires ORDER BYORDER BY in the windowNotes
ROW_NRankingYes1..n, no gaps. Arbitrary within ties unless a tiebreaker is added
RARankingYesTies share a rank; the next rank skips
DENSERankingYesTies share a rank; the next rank follows
NTRankingYesDistributes rows into nn groups, larger groups first. Not a value-based percentile
PERCENRankingYes0..1, always 0 for the first row
CUMERankingYes0..1 inclusive
L, LEValueYesOffset defaults to 1, default value to NU
FIRST, LAST_ValueNoFrame dependent. LAST_ defaults to the current row and usually needs an explicit frame
S, A, M, M, COAggregateNoAn empty frame yields NU, not 0
-- The default frame. With ORDER BY and no ROWS/RANGE clause, SQL Server uses
-- RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW, which for ties includes
-- every peer row. With no ORDER BY, the whole partition is the frame.
-- That difference makes these two rows differ despite looking similar:
SELECT OrderDate,
       LAST_VALUE(TotalAmount) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate)                          AS DefaultIsCurrentRow,
       LAST_VALUE(TotalAmount) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate
                             ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING) AS ActualLastValue
FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- Frame units. ROWS is positional, RANGE is value based and includes peers,
-- GROUPS is peer-group based and requires 2012+.
SELECT OrderDate, TotalAmount,
       SUM(TotalAmount) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate
                             RANGE BETWEEN INTERVAL 1 DAY PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS ByCalendarDay
FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- Two windows in one query: partition, then rank within the partition.
SELECT CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount,
       SUM(TotalAmount) OVER (PARTITION BY CustomerID)                     AS CustomerTotal,
       TotalAmount * 1.0 / SUM(TotalAmount) OVER (PARTITION BY CustomerID) AS ShareOfCustomer
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
ORDER BY CustomerID, OrderDate;
GO

LAST_ with an ORDER BYORDER BY and no frame clause returns the current row, not the last row in the partition. This is the default frame, and it is almost never what the name suggests. FIRST does not have the same surprise, which is why the asymmetry is worth memorising.

Caveats. A window function cannot be nested inside another window function; a separate SEL layer is required. ROW_N, RA and LE are all non-deterministic without a unique tiebreaker in the window’s ORDER BYORDER BY, and a non-deterministic ordering is the reason a paginated report can show row 17 twice and skip row 42. A window function with no PARTITION BYPARTITION BY treats the whole result as one partition, and an aggregate window over a large table sorts or spills the entire partition.

4.6 PIVOT, UNPIVOT and deterministic pagination

PI rotates rows into columns and UNP rotates columns into rows, in both cases at query time and without changing the stored data. Pagination through OFF/FE, added in SQL Server 2012, replaced the TOPTOP-without-ORDER BYORDER BY idiom and carries one requirement that the idiom did not.

-- PIVOT. The IN list must contain literal values, and the source query is
-- ordered to make the column order deterministic, because a PIVOT preserves
-- the order of its source. The reference schema has no MonthlyRevenue table,
-- so this is written against SalesOrder with Status as the pivot column.
SELECT CustomerID,
       [NEW] AS New, [PAYD] AS Paid, [SHP] AS Shipped
FROM (
    SELECT CustomerID, Status, TotalAmount,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY CustomerID, Status
                                   ORDER BY OrderDate, SalesOrderID) AS Tie
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder
) AS src
PIVOT (
    SUM(TotalAmount) FOR Status IN ([NEW], [PAYD], [SHP], [CXL])
) AS pvt
ORDER BY CustomerID;
GO

-- A dynamic PIVOT. The column list has to be built as text because the IN
-- list cannot be a variable, which is the practical cost of PIVOT.
--   DECLARE @cols nvarchar(max), @sql nvarchar(max);
--   SELECT @cols = STRING_AGG(QUOTENAME(Status), ',') FROM (
--       SELECT DISTINCT Status FROM dbo.SalesOrder) AS s;
--   SET @sql = N'SELECT CustomerID, ' + @cols + N'
--            FROM dbo.SalesOrder PIVOT (SUM(TotalAmount)
--                 FOR Status IN (' + @cols + N')) AS p;';
--   EXEC sp_executesql @sql;
GO

-- UNPIVOT is the inverse and is used to normalise a wide report table.
SELECT CustomerID, MetricName, MetricValue
FROM dbo.CustomerMetricPivot
UNPIVOT (
    MetricValue FOR MetricName IN ([Revenue], [New], [Shipped])
) AS unp;
GO

-- Deterministic pagination. OFFSET and FETCH both require an ORDER BY, and
-- both take a parenthesised expression. The ORDER BY must be unique and
-- total, or rows repeat and rows are skipped between pages.
--   SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
--   FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
--   ORDER BY OrderDate ASC, SalesOrderID ASC      -- tiebreaker makes it total
--   OFFSET @Skip ROWS FETCH NEXT @Take ROWS ONLY;
GO
DECLARE @Skip int = 40, @Take int = 20;
SELECT l.SalesOrderID, l.OrderDate, l.TotalAmount
FROM (
    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY o.OrderDate, o.SalesOrderID) AS Seq
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
) AS l
WHERE l.Seq > @Skip AND l.Seq <= @Skip + @Take
ORDER BY l.Seq;   -- seek-based, survives a seek predicate if indexes support it
GO

Neither PI nor UNP changes what is stored; the transformation happens when the query runs. The I list must be literal, which is what makes a dynamic pivot require generated SQL, and generated SQL is where the injection risk enters.

FormRequires ORDER BYORDER BYDeterministic when ties existNotes
OFFSET n ROWSOFFSET n ROWSYesNoWithout ORDER BYORDER BY the result is in whatever order the plan produces, and the optimiser is free to change it
OFFSET n ROWS FETCH NEXT m ROWS ONLYOFFSET n ROWS FETCH NEXT m ROWS ONLYYesNo, unless the order is totalThe documented pagination form. Add a unique tiebreaker column
OFFSET 0 ROWSOFFSET 0 ROWSYesSame as aboveThe valid way to say "all rows, ordered", which T without a percentage cannot
TOP nTOP nNoNoAccepted without ORDER BYORDER BY and returns an arbitrary subset; the optimiser may choose any n rows it likes
TOP n PERCENTTOP n PERCENTNoNoRounds up; can return more rows than nn implies
TOP n WITH TIESTOP n WITH TIESYesYes for the cut-offReturns every row tied with the n-th; the number of rows is not predictable

Caveats. A PI excludes rows whose pivot value is not in the I list, and a NU in a pivoted column is excluded whether or not NU appears in the list. A dynamic pivot built with STRIN and EXEC sp_executesqlEXEC sp_executesql is a string concatenation with SQL in it: every element must be QUOTENAMEQUOTENAMEd and every value passed to sp_exe must be a parameter, never concatenated. And OFF without FETCH NEXTFETCH NEXT is legal and returns every row from that offset, which is a common way to write a query that was intended to return a page.

Applies to. OFF/FE requires SQL Server 2012 or later. STRIN requires SQL Server 2017. WIN, the named window clause, requires SQL Server 2012 and is the form to use when several functions share one window definition, since it lets the engine compute the sort once.

For a paged result that must be stable while rows are being inserted, keyset pagination is the robust form: remember the last key of the previous page and use WHERE (OrderDate, SalesOrderID) > (@LastDate, @LastID)WHERE (OrderDate, SalesOrderID) > (@LastDate, @LastID). It seeks an index, does not degrade with page depth, and does not shift rows between pages the way an offset does.

Exercises 4

  1. Write three versions of "every customer with their order count": a LEFT JOINLEFT JOIN that discards unmatched customers, a correct LEFT JOINLEFT JOIN that keeps them, and a correlated subquery. Record the row count of each.
  2. Add a manager hierarchy to dbo.Cu as Mana, then write the self join that returns each employee with their manager, including rows where the manager is NULLNULL.
  3. Compare UN and UNION ALLUNION ALL over 100,000 rows of a generated staging table and record both elapsed times and both row counts.
  4. Use CROSS APPLYCROSS APPLY to return the three most expensive lines per order, then rewrite the same query with ROW_N in a derived table and compare the two results for equality.
  5. Rank products by Unit with all four ranking functions over a set containing deliberate ties, and tabulate the output to show exactly where RA and DENSE differ.
  6. Page through dbo.Sal with OFF/FE in pages of 20 using (a) a non-unique ORDER BYORDER BY and (b) one with a unique tiebreaker. Record any row that appears twice or is skipped in the non-unique case.

5 · Indexes and Storage

5.1 Pages, extents and the buffer pool5.2 The clustered index: the leaf level is the table5.3 The narrow clustering key and the GUID bloat pattern5.4 Nonclustered indexes, key lookups and covering5.5 Composite keys and the leftmost-prefix rule5.6 Seek or scan, and when a scan is correct5.7 Filtered indexes for a WHERE subset5.8 ONLINE = ON and its edition restriction5.9 Columnstore indexes and batch-mode execution5.10 Maintenance: REBUILD, REORGANIZE and the fragmentation myth5.11 Statistics and reading index usage

Every cost in T-SQL is a count of pages read, so index design is the only lever that changes the number before the optimiser runs. This chapter works from the physical unit upward: pages and extents, the clustered index whose leaves are the table, the rule that decides how wide that key may be, nonclustered indexes and the lookup that makes them expensive, the column order that makes them usable, and the two access paths the optimiser chooses between. It closes with columnstore for analytical shapes and with the maintenance advice, which on solid-state storage is mostly advice not to do things.

5.1 Pages, extents and the buffer pool

SQL Server reads and writes its tables in fixed 8 KB pages. A page is the smallest unit of I/O, the unit the buffer pool manages, and the unit that the server counts when it reports how much work a query did. Nothing in T-SQL talks about pages, but almost every performance question eventually reduces to how many of them a statement had to touch.

A page is 8192 bytes. The first 96 bytes are a header holding the page address, the link to the next and previous page, and the allocation maps. The remaining 8060 bytes hold row data. A row occupies one slot; a variable-length row has a two-byte slot pointer, and the slot array grows downwards from the end of the page. Rows longer than 8050 bytes cannot be stored in row, so SQL Server leaves a 24-byte root on the page and puts the tail in a chain of L pages — a row-overflow chain, which turns a one-page operation into a chain walk.

UnitSizeConsequence
Page8 KBMinimum read and write unit; a 1-row update rewrites an 8 KB page
Extent64 KB (8 pages)Allocation unit; mixed extents hold rows from anywhere, uniform extents one object
Buffer pool8 KB page frames in RAMThe page cache; a page read is a memory read once the page is resident
Row-overflow chain24-byte root plus LOB pagesWide rows turn a single read into several, and the cost is invisible in the query

The buffer pool holds the pages read from disk. A read of a page that is already resident costs almost nothing, so the number that matters is not how many pages a query touches but how many it had to fetch from outside memory. DBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERSDBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERS empties it and is a benchmarking tool, not an optimisation; it is the closest thing to a cold start and it makes every measurement worse until the working set reloads.

-- Resident pages for one database, by type. page_type 1 is the data page
-- of a clustered index or heap; 2 is an index page.
SELECT page_type, COUNT_BIG(*) AS ResidentPages, SUM(row_count) AS RowsResident
FROM sys.dm_os_buffer_descriptors
WHERE database_id = DB_ID()
GROUP BY page_type
ORDER BY ResidentPages DESC;
GO

-- Where a specific row lives: file, page and slot.
DBCC PAGE ('SalesDB', 1, 4127, 3) WITH TABLOCK;
GO

-- Space actually allocated, versus space the data needs.
EXEC sys.dm_db_space_usage;
GO

The unit of cost follows directly: a table scan of a 40,000-row table with 200 bytes per row reads roughly 1000 pages, not 40,000 rows. Row counts are a design-time intuition; page counts are the runtime truth, and the plan reports page counts.

Width matters because pages are the currency. Two tables holding the same number of rows with the same number of columns do not read the same number of pages if the data types differ. A char(200)char(200) column where a da would do multiplies page count by roughly ten and multiplies every scan with it. There is no substitute for fixing the declared type.

5.2 The clustered index: the leaf level is the table

A clustered index is not a copy of the table. It is the table: the leaf level of the B-tree holds the actual rows, sorted by the clustering key, and the pages above it hold only keys. That single fact produces almost every rule in this chapter, because it means a table can have at most one and because every other index on the table has to carry a way back to it.

The B-tree has a root page, zero or more interior pages, and a level of leaves that holds the data. Interior pages exist only to route a seek down to the right leaf, so they are negligible in size for any table a business actually runs. A table without a clustered index is a heap: rows are stored in no defined order and a nonclustered index on a heap points to rows by physical row identifier instead of by a key.

PropertyClustered tableHeap
Row orderClustering key order, permanentlyInsert order, which becomes meaningless after any update
Nonclustered row locatorThe clustering key columnsThe physical RID, which can change
Scan without an indexNeeds the clustered indexPossible, but unordered
Forwarded recordsNot possiblePossible after a variable-length update; rebuild removes them
Constraint supportFK and unique constraints are cheapEvery check must navigate the RID chain

A nonclustered index on a clustered table does not store a pointer to a page and slot, because the page is not stable. It stores the clustering key. The engine has to be able to reconstruct a lookup from any nonclustered entry, so the clustering key is appended to every nonclustered row, whether it was named as a key column or not.

-- Every nonclustered index on this table carries SalesOrderID whether or not
-- it was declared. The clustered index rows are wider for that reason alone.
CREATE TABLE dbo.SalesOrder (
    SalesOrderID  int          NOT NULL,
    CustomerID    int          NOT NULL,
    OrderDate     date         NOT NULL,
    Status        char(1)     NOT NULL,
    TotalAmount   decimal(12,2) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_SalesOrder PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (SalesOrderID)
);
GO

-- Where the extra width shows up.
SELECT i.name AS IndexName,
       i.index_id,
       i.type_desc AS IndexType,
       i.is_primary_key,
       c.name AS CarriedColumn,
       c.column_id,
       c.is_included_column AS IsIncluded
FROM sys.indexes AS i
JOIN sys.index_columns AS ic
       ON ic.object_id = i.object_id AND ic.index_id = i.index_id
JOIN sys.columns AS c
       ON c.object_id = ic.object_id AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
WHERE i.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder')
  AND i.index_id > 0
ORDER BY i.index_id, ic.key_ordinal, ic.index_column_id;
GO

Heaps are not free. On a heap, every nonclustered lookup is a physical RID read that can hit a forwarded record, foreign key checks traverse RIDs instead of keys, and there is no defined order to exploit. The default recommendation stands: give every table a clustered index, even if it is on a surrogate key that is never queried.

A clustering key must be immutable. If a column used as the clustering key is UPDATEUPDATEd, every nonclustered index containing it must be updated too, and the row must be relocated if the key change moves it in sort order. Choose a key that never changes: an IDEN column, a business code that the business has agreed never to reuse, or a GUID created once and stored as uniqueid or, better, as a 16-byte binary(16)binary(16).

5.3 The narrow clustering key and the GUID bloat pattern

Because the clustering key is copied into every nonclustered index on the table, the width of the clustering key is a multiplier on the width of the whole schema. This is the single highest-leverage decision in index design, and it is made before any code is written, in the PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEY clause.

Two costs follow from a wide clustering key, and they are independent. The first is width: 16 bytes of uniqueid in the leaf of every nonclustered index instead of 4 bytes of intint. The second is randomness: a GUID generated by NEWID()NEWID() or NEWSEQUENTIALID()NEWSEQUENTIALID() is not in insert order, so every insert lands in an arbitrary leaf page, splits it, and leaves the file extents permanently half full. Randomness is invisible in the schema and expensive in the file.

-- The pattern that costs the most: 16-byte random clustering key.
CREATE TABLE dbo.OrderRef (
    OrderRefID uniqueidentifier NOT NULL DEFAULT NEWID(),
    CustomerID  int            NOT NULL,
    PlacedAt     datetime2(3)    NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_OrderRef PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (OrderRefID)
);
GO

-- The fix: a narrow sequential clustering key, plus a nonclustered unique
-- index that enforces the business rule. Same guarantees, far less width.
CREATE TABLE dbo.OrderRef (
    OrderRefID  bigint           IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    PublicRef    uniqueidentifier NOT NULL DEFAULT NEWID(),
    CustomerID   int             NOT NULL,
    PlacedAt      datetime2(3)     NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_OrderRef  PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED  (OrderRefID),
    CONSTRAINT UQ_OrderRef_PublicRef UNIQUE NONCLUSTERED (PublicRef)
);
GO

-- 16 bytes stored in an arbitrary place, 4 or 8 bytes in every leaf page,
-- and the uniqueness requirement is still enforced by the engine.
SELECT i.name, ps.page_count, ps.avg_fragmentation_in_percent
FROM sys.indexes AS i
JOIN sys.dm_db_partition_stats AS ps
  ON ps.object_id = i.object_id AND ps.index_id = i.index_id
WHERE i.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.OrderRef') AND ps.index_id > 0;
GO

NEWSEQUENTIALID()NEWSEQUENTIALID() does produce ordered values, but they are ordered per process and are meaningless across hosts, so concurrent writers from two instances interleave into the same page. It solves the page-split problem and creates a collision and coordination problem. For a durable identifier, generate the GUID in the client layer, store it once, and let IDEN own the physical order.

Two further consequences of a random clustering key are worth stating explicitly, because both appear in plans. Sorts on uniqueid cannot use an index to deliver order, since the type has no useful ordering, so an ORDER BYORDER BY on a GUID column always sorts. And page splits driven by randomness leave low page density, so the table has more pages than its row count implies, and every subsequent scan pays for the empty space.

The test is arithmetic, not intuition. For a table with eight nonclustered indexes, a 4-byte clustering key adds 32 bytes to every nonclustered leaf; a 16-byte key adds 128. On millions of rows that is the difference between one page and four per lookup, multiplied by every seek, every scan, and every rebuild you will ever run.

5.4 Nonclustered indexes, key lookups and covering

A nonclustered index is a separate B-tree whose entries are (index key, clustering key) pairs. A seek on it is fast. What happens next is where the cost appears: if the query needs a column that is not in the index, the engine must go back to the clustered index once per row, and that operation is a key lookup.

A key lookup is a random single-row read into the clustered index, executed as many times as there are rows returned. One thousand lookups is a thousand unpredictable reads spread across a table that may be far larger than the buffer pool. The plan shows this as a Key LookupKey Lookup operator sitting above the nonclustered seek, and its Actual Executions is the number that tells you how bad it got.

-- Needs Status, OrderDate and TotalAmount for an export.
-- The index supplies two of the three, so the third forces a lookup per row.
SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = 'N'
  AND OrderDate >= '2026-01-01';
GO

-- The covering version. TotalAmount is an INCLUDE column: it lives in the
-- leaf, it is not part of the sort, and it may be a wide type.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_Status_Date
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status, OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (TotalAmount)
    WITH (FILLFACTOR = 90, ONLINE = ON);
GO

A Key LookupKey Lookup also appears when a SELECT *SELECT * is issued against a table with a narrow nonclustered index that the optimiser nonetheless found attractive for the predicate. The optimiser picks the seek because it is cheaper on its estimate, then discovers the lookup is expensive. If the estimate is right, the covering index is the answer; if the estimate is wrong, the answer is in section 6.2.

LimitValueNote
Key columns per index16Applies to both clustered and nonclustered
Combined key width900 bytes1700 bytes when every key column is fixed length
Non-key (INC) columns1024Any total width; the row is still bounded by 8060 bytes
Nonclustered indexes per table999Not a design target

Why INC exists. A nonclustered index is ordered by its key columns, so a column in the key must be narrow and is duplicated on every entry. A column in INC is stored in the leaf only, takes no part in the sort, and is not a candidate for a seek. The rule is: put a column in the key if you will filter or sort on it, and in INC if you only need to read it.

5.5 Composite keys and the leftmost-prefix rule

Column order inside a composite index decides which queries can use it, and the rule that decides is the leftmost-prefix rule. It is not a heuristic and it has no exceptions: a composite index on (A, B, C) can serve a seek on A, on (A, B), or on (A, B, C), and cannot serve a seek that omits A.

-- Target query: equality on Status, a range on OrderDate.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = 'N'
  AND OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
  AND OrderDate <  '2026-04-01';
GO

-- Correct order: equality columns first, then the range column.
-- Both predicates become part of the seek key, so the scan is bounded.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SO_Status_OrderDate
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status, OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (TotalAmount);
GO

-- The reverse order is still a usable index, but only Status participates in
-- the seek. Every matching row across all dates is visited and then filtered,
-- so the index is equivalent to an index on (Status) with a residual predicate.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SO_OrderDate_Status
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (OrderDate, Status)
    INCLUDE (TotalAmount);
GO

With the range on the second column, the engine seeks to ('N', '2026-01-01')('N', '2026-01-01') and walks forward, stopping at the first entry past the upper bound. With the range on the first column, it must seek to ('2026-01-01', ...)('2026-01-01', ...) and then test Sta on every row in the whole quarter, because rows with a different status are interleaved by date. Same index size, same number of pages, completely different behaviour.

Only one column can be the open end of a seek. Two range predicates on two columns in one index means the second is a filter, not a seek component. The ordering rule that follows from this is the standard one:

-- Precedence for key column order, highest first:
--   1. equality predicates        WHERE Status = 'N'
--   2. the single range predicate  WHERE OrderDate >= @From
--   3. the ORDER BY columns         ORDER BY OrderDate DESC
--   4. everything else, as INCLUDE

-- One index serving a filter and an ordered read, in that order.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SO_Status_OrderDate_Total
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status, OrderDate DESC)
    INCLUDE (CustomerID, TotalAmount);
GO

-- Which usable prefixes this index offers. (Status) and (Status, OrderDate)
-- are seekable; (OrderDate) and (OrderDate, Status) are not.
SELECT i.index_id, i.name, c.name AS KeyColumn,
       ic.key_ordinal, ic.is_descending_key
FROM sys.indexes AS i
JOIN sys.index_columns AS ic ON ic.object_id = i.object_id AND ic.index_id = i.index_id
JOIN sys.columns AS c
  ON c.object_id = ic.object_id AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
WHERE i.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder')
  AND i.name = IX_SO_Status_OrderDate_Total
ORDER BY ic.key_ordinal;
GO

Direction matters. A composite index whose key columns are all ascending can be read backwards, so it serves both A and DE ordering. An index declared with mixed directions, such as (Status ASC, OrderDate DESC)(Status ASC, OrderDate DESC), cannot be scanned in either single direction, so it will not satisfy a seek plus ORDER BYORDER BY unless the query asks for exactly that mixed order. If you need both directions, declare two indexes or use a computed ROW_N rather than relying on one mixed index.

5.6 Seek or scan, and when a scan is correct

A seek reads pages by navigating the B-tree; a scan reads a range of pages, or the whole index, in physical order. Seeks are cheap per row and expensive per page; scans are the reverse. The optimiser chooses between them using its row estimate, which means the choice is only as good as the estimate — and that a scan is frequently the right answer.

SeekScan
Access patternTree descent, then a walkContiguous pages
Best whenThe estimate is a small fraction of the tableThe estimate is most of the table
Per-page costHigh, one or more random readsLow, read-ahead and sequential
Worst whenIt is actually returning a large share of the rowsThe estimate was tiny and the rows are scattered
-- A scan is the correct plan here. The optimiser should choose it, and does.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE OrderDate >= '2020-01-01';
GO

-- A seek is the correct plan here: one row, found by the key.
SELECT *
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;
GO

-- Forcing a scan to see what it costs. The hint is a measurement device
-- here, not a remedy; see 6.8.
SELECT *
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE SalesOrderID >= 1000
OPTION (FORCESCAN);
GO

There is a second, subtler reason a scan wins: the cost of a lookup. When a nonclustered seek would be followed by a key lookup on nearly every row, the two operations together cost more than a sequential pass over the clustered index. The optimiser computes this and will switch to a scan on its own. The number of key lookups at which the crossover happens depends on the table width and the buffer, and it is not a constant you should try to memorise.

A clustered index scan with a filter, or a nonclustered index scan whose narrowness beats a lookup, are both normal outcomes and not symptoms. Diagnose by page count, not by operator name. A clustered index scan of 20 pages is cheaper than any seek; a clustered index scan of 200,000 pages is a bug in the index design, a missing filter, or a parameter that arrived with the wrong value.

Parallelism changes the picture and is the usual reason a scan of a large table is fast. When a query exceeds the cost threshold for parallel plan, the scan is split across threads and the per-page cost of randomness is largely hidden. This is why an unindexed report over a small table can beat an indexed point lookup, and why adding MAXDOP 1MAXDOP 1 sometimes makes a query look dramatically worse: it removes the parallel scan, not the inefficiency.

The practical consequence: do not design indexes from intuition about which queries exist. Capture SET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO output for the real queries, note the logical reads, and change the index that produces the number you do not like. Section 6.3 covers the measurement and section 5.11 covers the usage evidence.

5.7 Filtered indexes for a WHERE subset

A filtered index indexes only the rows that match a predicate. When a table is large and only a fraction is ever queried, the filtered index is smaller, denser, cheaper to maintain, and cheaper to scan — and the optimiser will use it for any query whose predicate logically implies the filter.

-- Most products in a catalogue are discontinued, and almost no query wants them.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_Product_Active_SKU
    ON dbo.Product (SKU)
    INCLUDE (Name, UnitPrice)
    WHERE IsActive = 1;
GO

-- Used: the predicate implies IsActive = 1.
SELECT p.SKU, p.Name, p.UnitPrice
FROM dbo.Product AS p
WHERE p.IsActive = 1
  AND p.SKU = 'SKU-9931';
GO

-- Also used: IN (1, 0) is equivalent to a row being true.
SELECT SKU FROM dbo.Product WHERE IsActive IN (1, 0) AND SKU < 'SKU-2000';
GO

-- Not used: a NULL or a 0 value does not imply the filter.
SELECT SKU FROM dbo.Product WHERE SKU = 'SKU-9931';
GO

The rule is implication, not equality. A query that constrains a superset of the filter’s rows can use the index, because every row it could return is guaranteed to be in the index. That is why IsActive IN (1, 0)IsActive IN (1, 0) works and a bare S equality does not. A common practical case is a date range: an index filtered to Status = 'N’Status = 'N' is usable by a query that says Status IN ('N', 'X')Status IN ('N', 'X').

Creation-time SET options are recorded and enforced. A filtered index is created under a specific set of S options (ANSI_, ANSI_P, ANSI_W, ARITH, CONCAT_NULL, QUOTED_I ON, NUMERIC_R OFF) and can only be used by a query executing under the same options. Most connection pools set these correctly, which is why this bites in ad hoc tools and legacy drivers. IGNORE_ must be OFF. Both facts are recorded in sys.i and are queryable.

-- Filtered indexes advertise themselves. A NULL filter_column means
-- "no filter", which is how a full nonclustered index looks here.
SELECT s.name AS SchemaName, o.name AS ObjectName,
       i.name AS IndexName,
       i.filter_definition,
       i.has_filter,
       i.filter_column_id
FROM sys.indexes AS i
JOIN sys.objects AS o ON o.object_id = i.object_id
JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = o.schema_id
WHERE i.has_filter = 1
ORDER BY s.name, o.name, i.name;
GO

Filtered statistics without a filtered index. If a query needs only the statistics, not a faster access path, CREATE STATISTICS ... ON dbo.Product (IsActive) WHERE IsActive = 1CREATE STATISTICS ... ON dbo.Product (IsActive) WHERE IsActive = 1 gives the optimiser a histogram for the subset without creating any index. That is often the cheaper fix for a cardinality problem in section 6.2. Both objects can be listed in sys. with a non-NULL filter_definitionfilter_definition.

5.8 ONLINE = ON and its edition restriction

An online index operation keeps the table available to readers and writers for the duration of the operation, taking a brief shared lock at the start and a brief update lock at the end. It is the correct way to add an index to a production table, and it is available only in Enterprise, Developer and Evaluation editions.

-- Enterprise, Developer and Evaluation only. On Standard, Web and Express this
-- fails with Msg 1909: "Online index operations can only be performed in
-- Enterprise edition of SQL Server. The supported edition does not
-- support online index operations."
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_Customer_Email
    ON dbo.Customer (Email)
    INCLUDE (Name, CreditLimit)
    WITH (ONLINE = ON, SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON, MAXDOP = 1);
GO

-- The same restriction applies to index rebuilds and to adding a
-- constraint WITH (ONLINE = ON).
ALTER INDEX IX_Customer_Email ON dbo.Customer REBUILD
    WITH (ONLINE = ON, SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON);
GO

-- Resumable operations, also Enterprise only, so a long rebuild that is
-- interrupted by a failover can be resumed rather than restarted.
ALTER INDEX IX_SalesOrder_Status_Date ON dbo.SalesOrder REBUILD
    WITH (ONLINE = ON, RESUMABLE = ON,
          MAX_DURATION = 240 MINUTES);
GO

-- WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY is the online equivalent of the lock-wait policy in
-- section 7.8: new sessions queue behind user sessions instead of
-- receiving an error when the final lock cannot be taken.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SO_Open_Quick
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (CustomerID)
    WHERE Status = 'N'
    WITH (ONLINE = ON,
          WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY (MAX_DURATION = 5 MINUTES,
                                       ABORT_AFTER_WAIT = NONE));
GO

Restrictions worth knowing before scheduling. An online operation cannot run inside an explicit user transaction. It cannot run while a snapshot isolation transaction is open on the table, and it is blocked by a Sch modification lock, so any open DDL statement on the object stops it. SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ONSORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON moves the sort work to tem and needs free space there; it does not reduce the amount of log the operation writes. For a nonclustered index, online means build a new copy and swap; for a clustered index it means rebuild and convert, which is the slowest of the operations.

An offline operation on a busy table takes a Schema-Mo lock and blocks every concurrent reader and writer until it finishes. There is no partial availability: the table is simply not there for other sessions. If a nightly REB without ONLINE = ONONLINE = ON is scheduled on a table that has online consumers, the block is invisible until someone reports a timeout, and then it looks like a platform problem. This is the strongest single reason to check the edition before planning maintenance.

5.9 Columnstore indexes and batch-mode execution

A rowstore index stores a whole row together, which is efficient for touching one row and wasteful for reading one column from every row. A columnstore index stores each column separately, compressed in segments of 60,000 to 900 values, which is why aggregating over many rows in columnstore is a different order of magnitude of work rather than an incremental improvement.

Two forms exist. A clustered columnstore index is the table: the whole table is columnar, there is no rowstore underneath, and the clustered index on the table is the columnstore. A nonclustered columnstore index (SQL Server 2016 and later) is a copy of the table in columnar form alongside the existing rowstore, so an operational table can gain analytical performance without being rewritten. A table may have at most one columnstore index of each kind, and at most one columnstore index in total on a given table with a rowstore backing it.

-- Analytics on an operational table without rewriting it: SQL Server 2016+.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED COLUMNSTORE INDEX NCCX_SalesOrder
    ON dbo.SalesOrder
    ORDER (SalesOrderID);
GO

-- The reporting query it is for. Filtered aggregates over millions of rows
-- with no seek and no key lookup.
SELECT DATEFROMPARTS(YEAR(o.OrderDate), MONTH(o.OrderDate), 1) AS MonthStart,
       SUM(l.Quantity) AS Units,
       SUM(CAST(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice AS decimal(18,2)) AS Revenue
FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
JOIN dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
WHERE o.OrderDate >= '2024-01-01'
GROUP BY DATEFROMPARTS(YEAR(o.OrderDate), MONTH(o.OrderDate), 1)
ORDER BY MonthStart;
GO

-- ORDER BY inside CREATE CLUSTERED COLUMNSTORE INDEX requires SQL Server
-- 2022 and compatibility level 160 or higher. It makes the load ordered,
-- which is what keeps row groups and segments efficient.
-- CREATE CLUSTERED COLUMNSTORE INDEX CCI_SalesOrderLine
--     ON dbo.SalesOrderLine ORDER (SalesOrderID, LineNo);
GO

The second half of the columnstore story is batch mode. Since SQL Server 2019, a columnstore query can execute in batch mode, where operators process roughly a thousand rows at a time using vectorised instructions rather than one row per call. Batch mode is a large constant-factor win on aggregation and it is only available at compatibility level 150 and above. The actual execution plan shows the mode on each operator as Estimated execution mode and Actual execution mode.

-- Batch mode needs a high enough compatibility level.
ALTER DATABASE SalesDB SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL = 170;
GO

-- Read a column for many rows, or take a tiny subset by row, not by column.
-- Point lookups and small ranges are the case where rowstore wins.
SELECT column_store_index_type, state_description, total_rows, row_group_id
FROM sys.column_store_row_groups
ORDER BY row_group_id;
GO

-- Columnstore quality, with the calculated size and compression ratio.
EXEC sys.sp_column_store_external_schema_snapshot @object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder');
GO

Columnstore is not free. Deletes and updates of individual rows are expensive: the row is marked deleted in the delta store rather than removed from the compressed segment, and small single-row transactions repeatedly open delta row groups. If a table is a high-churn operational table, prefer a nonclustered columnstore on a stable subset of columns, or a filtered index, over converting the table itself. Delta row groups are visible in sys.column_st with state_description = 'CLOSED’state_description = 'CLOSED' and a small total_rowstotal_rows.

5.10 Maintenance: REBUILD, REORGANIZE and the fragmentation myth

Index maintenance in SQL Server is a copy of advice written for spinning disks in the early 2000s. On solid-state storage most of it is harmless but pointless, and some of it costs a great deal for nothing. The operations themselves are worth knowing precisely; the thresholds that decide when to run them are not.

OperationEffectLoggingStatistics
REORGCompacts leaf pages to remove free space, up to a fill factor of 100; can move rows only within the pageMinimally loggedNot updated
REBRebuilds every level, removing all fragmentation; can change the fill factor and reorder rowsFully loggedUpdated
NeitherNothing——
-- Measure before deciding. LIMITED samples and is fast enough for a job;
-- DETAILED scans every page and is the correct answer for a table you are
-- about to rebuild anyway.
SELECT i.name AS IndexName,
       i.type_desc AS IndexType,
       ps.avg_fragmentation_in_percent AS FragPct,
       ps.page_count AS Pages,
       ps.page_space_used_percent AS SpaceUsedPct,
       ps.row_count
FROM sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats(DB_ID(), OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder'), NULL, NULL, 'LIMITED') AS ps
JOIN sys.indexes AS i
  ON ps.object_id = i.object_id AND ps.index_id = i.index_id
WHERE i.index_id > 0
ORDER BY ps.avg_fragmentation_in_percent DESC;
GO

-- Reorganise: cheap, minimally logged, no statistics update.
ALTER INDEX IX_SO_Status_OrderDate ON dbo.SalesOrder REORGANIZE;
GO

-- Rebuild: fully logged, updates statistics, needs space in the file and
-- room in the log. SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON sorts in tempdb so the pages do not
-- have to move twice in the data file; it does NOT reduce the log used.
ALTER INDEX IX_SO_Status_OrderDate ON dbo.SalesOrder
    REBUILD WITH (SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON, MAXDOP = 1);
GO

-- Only clustered indexes can be rebuilt this way. The heap equivalent
-- also removes forwarded records, which a heap can accumulate.
ALTER TABLE dbo.AuditLog REBUILD;
GO

Do not rebuild reflexively. The thresholds that circulate in maintenance scripts — under 5 percent reorganise, 5 to 10 percent do nothing, over 30 percent rebuild — come from a Microsoft whitepaper about SQL Server 2000 on magnetic disk, where a seek cost a mechanical head movement. On SSD and NVMe a random page read is three to four orders of magnitude cheaper, so a fragmented index on a table whose working set fits in the buffer pool performs identically before and after a rebuild. A nightly rebuild of a 400 GB table is a full log write, a large tempdb allocation and hours of CPU, bought for nothing, and it is a common cause of the outages it was meant to prevent. Rebuild when a measured query is slow and fragmentation is part of the explanation, not on a schedule nobody has justified.

Where fragmentation does still matter is a large index that is repeatedly scanned: sequential reads benefit from density, and pages that are 40 percent empty make every scan 60 percent longer. The distinguishing measurement is not fragmentation percentage but whether the index is read as a stream. If a report scans the index weekly and the index does not fit in memory, compaction is defensible. If the index serves point lookups, it is not.

FILLF applies only at creation, only to nonclustered indexes, and PAD_ has an effect only when the fill factor is below 100. Leaving room on nonclustered pages is a hedge against page splits during a bulk load, and it costs density permanently on every read. The correct default is 100 with no padding, plus ONL so the load is not blocked, unless measurements say otherwise.

5.11 Statistics and reading index usage

Indexes are chosen by cardinality estimates, and cardinality estimates come from statistics. Two DMVs close this chapter: one tells you whether the optimiser’s picture of the data is good enough, and one tells you which indexes the workload actually uses.

SQL Server maintains a histogram per indexed column automatically and samples the data when it creates or updates it. That sampling is a guess, and a guess that is wrong whenever the data is skewed: a column where 99 percent of rows share one value gets a histogram that cannot describe the shape of the other one percent, and the optimiser then multiplies or divides the estimate instead of counting.

-- What the optimiser believes about a column, step by step.
DBCC SHOW_STATISTICS ('dbo.SalesOrder', IX_SO_Status_OrderDate_Total) WITH HISTOGRAM;
GO

-- When it was last refreshed, how it was sampled, and its row count.
SELECT s.name AS StatName, s.auto_created, s.user_created,
       s.is_filtered, s.sample_percent,
       sp.rows, sp.rows_sampled, sp.last_updated
FROM sys.stats AS s
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_db_stats_properties(s.object_id, s.stats_id) AS sp
WHERE s.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder')
ORDER BY sp.last_updated;
GO

-- When the automatic sampler is not good enough: sample everything.
CREATE STATISTICS ST_SalesOrder_Status_OrderDate
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status, OrderDate)
    WITH FULLSCAN;
GO

-- Skewed data sometimes needs a directed statistic rather than a full scan.
-- 65 steps rather than the default 101, so the rare values get their own step.
CREATE STATISTICS ST_SO_Status
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status)
    WITH FULLSCAN, HISTOGRAM_STEPS = 128;
GO

-- Re-sample everything in the database by hand after a bulk load.
EXEC sys.sp_updatestats;
GO

Four situations justify a hand-written statistic: the automatic sampler ran on a small percentage of a large table; the distribution is skewed beyond what 101 steps can describe; two columns are correlated, so independent per-column histograms multiply two guesses into a very wrong one; or a parameter consistently arrives with a value the histogram handles badly. In each case, measure the plan before and after with SET STATISTICS XML ONSET STATISTICS XML ON.

The second DMV answers a different question: of the indexes you have, which ones does the workload actually use. Every index that is never used is pure cost on every insert, update and delete, and pure memory for its pages.

-- Usage since the last restart of the instance, or since the last time
-- sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats was cleared. Never-seen rows are NULL.
SELECT OBJECT_NAME(s.object_id) AS TableName,
       s.name AS IndexName,
       s.index_id,
       s.user_seeks,
