       s.user_scans,
       s.user_lookups,
       s.user_updates,
       s.last_user_seek,
       s.last_user_scan
FROM sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats AS s
JOIN sys.indexes AS i
  ON i.object_id = s.object_id AND i.index_id = s.index_id
WHERE s.database_id = DB_ID()
  AND i.index_id > 0
ORDER BY s.user_updates DESC, s.user_seeks DESC;
GO

-- Columns that exist in no index: a seek on any of them scans.
SELECT c.name AS UnindexedColumn, t.name AS DataType, c.max_length
FROM sys.columns AS c
JOIN sys.types AS t ON t.user_type_id = c.user_type_id
WHERE c.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder')
  AND c.is_computed = 0
  AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1
                    FROM sys.index_columns AS ic
                    JOIN sys.indexes AS i2
                      ON i2.object_id = ic.object_id AND i2.index_id = ic.index_id
                    WHERE ic.object_id = c.object_id
                      AND ic.column_id = c.column_id
                      AND i2.index_id > 0)
ORDER BY c.column_id;
GO

Read the three usage counters together rather than alone. A high user_updates with near-zero seeks is the signature of an index that costs writes and returns nothing, and it is the one case where dropping the index is unambiguously correct. High user_scans on an index that was only ever built for seeks means the seek queries stopped happening and the index is now a scan tax. High user_lookups is a hint that the covering index is missing rather than a sign of a bad plan.

Clearing the counters. sys.dm_db_ind is not persisted, so it starts empty after a restart. If you need a window, capture the delta rather than trusting a single reading, and remember that a Friday sample of a workload with a monthly report proves nothing about the report’s index.

Exercises 5

  1. Load 500,000 rows into dbo.Sal and record the page_ and avg_fragmentation_in_percentavg_fragmentation_in_percent. Repeat with every Sta value declared as char(200)char(200) instead of char(1)char(1), and tabulate the page-count difference to show the effect of declared width alone.
  2. Create dbo.Or three ways: with a uniqueid clustered primary key, with a bigint IDENTITYbigint IDENTITY clustered key plus a unique nonclustered index on the GUID, and as a heap. Load the same 200,000 rows into each and compare total page counts per index, user_u from sys.dm_db_ind, and logical reads for a single-row lookup by the GUID.
  3. Create a nonclustered index on dbo.SalesOrder(Status, OrderDate)dbo.SalesOrder(Status, OrderDate) and run the export query from 5.4 with SET STATISTICS IO ONSET STATISTICS IO ON. Note the logical reads and the Key LookupKey Lookup count, then add Total to INC and re-measure. Record both figures.
  4. Create both (Status, OrderDate)(Status, OrderDate) and (OrderDate, Status)(OrderDate, Status) indexes on a copy, run the range query from 5.5 against each, and record the seek predicate and the row count at each operator to show which column becomes a filter rather than a seek component.
  5. Run a point-lookup query with OPTION (FORCESCAN)OPTION (FORCESCAN) and with OPTION (MAXDOP 1)OPTION (MAXDOP 1), and record logical reads and elapsed time for all three variants. Explain each difference in terms of pages read.
  6. Create a filtered index on dbo.P for IsActive = 1IsActive = 1, then run four queries against it: WHERE IsActive = 1WHERE IsActive = 1, WHERE IsActive IN (1, 0)WHERE IsActive IN (1, 0), WHERE IsActive IS NOT NULLWHERE IsActive IS NOT NULL, and a bare WHERE SKU = ...WHERE SKU = .... Record which of the four use the index and explain each result by implication.
  7. Build a large table and index it offline, then time the blocking it causes for a concurrent reader. Repeat with WITH (ONLINE = ON)WITH (ONLINE = ON) and compare. Record the edition you tested on and the exact error the online form returns on Standard.
  8. Create a clustered columnstore index on a copy of dbo.Sales and run the monthly aggregate from 5.9, recording the estimated and actual execution mode from the plan. Set the compatibility level below 150 and re-run to show the batch-mode win disappear.
  9. Measure fragmentation on every index in the database with sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats(..., 'LIMITED')sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats(..., 'LIMITED'), then REORG one index and REB another, and record the fragmentation, page count, stats_ and elapsed time for each. Note which of the two updated statistics.
  10. Create a skewed column by hand (one value on 99 percent of rows), capture the plan for a predicate on it, and show the estimate being wrong. Fix it with CREATE STATISTICS ... WITH FULLSCANCREATE STATISTICS ... WITH FULLSCAN and confirm the estimate, then repeat with a filtered statistic on the rare value.

6 · Execution Plans and Query Optimisation

6.1 The optimiser pipeline: parse, bind, optimise, execute6.2 Estimated versus actual row counts6.3 Measuring cost: SET STATISTICS IO, TIME and logical reads6.4 Estimated and actual plans: SET STATISTICS XML and Ctrl+M6.5 The operator set and what each one costs6.6 Join algorithm selection6.7 Reading a plan: two patterns worth memorising6.8 OPTION hints: RECOMPILE, OPTIMIZE FOR and their limits6.9 Parameter sniffing and the plan cache6.10 MAXDOP, parallelism and FORCESEEK

The optimiser is a cost-based search over access paths, join orders and join algorithms, driven entirely by cardinality estimates. This chapter describes what it does, how to see what it decided, how to measure the decision, and — because this is most of the work — how to recognise the two failure modes that account for the majority of production performance problems: a wrong row estimate, and a plan compiled for a parameter value the query is not receiving today.

6.1 The optimiser pipeline: parse, bind, optimise, execute

A statement passes through four stages on its way to a result set, and each stage can fail in a way that produces a completely different symptom. Knowing which stage produced a problem is most of the diagnosis.

StageWhat happensWhat goes wrong here
ParseText becomes a syntax tree; G and comments are removed by the client before thisSyntax error, or a reference to a name created later in the same batch, reported as "Invalid object name"
BindNames are resolved to objects, columns checked, types and collations compared, and implicit conversions insertedThe conversion in section 2.5, and the loss of index seekability that follows from it
OptimiseRow estimates are computed, then join order, access paths, join algorithms and parallelism are chosen by costA wrong estimate, which produces a plan that is optimal for a data set that does not exist
ExecuteThe physical plan runs; requests for memory, locks and I/O are madeLock waits, spills, and the differences between the plan you drew and the plan you got

Optimisation is by far the most expensive stage, and it is skipped whenever a matching plan is already in the cache. That single fact is the source of both good news and most bad: identical text with identical parameters runs the plan chosen for the first execution, whatever the data looks like now.

-- The optimiser runs once per parameter set, not once per execution. Run
-- this 200 times and the compile count stays at 1 unless the parameters change.
SELECT qs.execution_count,
       qs.creation_time,
       qs.last_execution_time,
       qs.plan_handle
FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
WHERE st.text LIKE '%SalesOrder%'
ORDER BY qs.total_elapsed_time DESC;
GO

-- The optimiser's decision to compile, and its verdict on the result.
SET SHOWPLAN_XML ON;
GO
SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE Status = 'N';
GO
SET SHOWPLAN_XML OFF;
GO

Ad hoc and parameterised plans. Because the plan depends on parameter values, a cached plan is specific to a parameter set. The server records every distinct set it has seen; a parameter sniffparameter sniff on a particular value makes it available to all callers of the same text. Section 6.9 covers the consequences, and OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) is usually the answer.

6.2 Estimated versus actual row counts

Every operator in a plan carries an estimated row count, decided before execution, and an actual row count, observed during it. The two are computed by completely different means. When they disagree by orders of magnitude, the plan was built on a false premise, and every choice downstream of that operator is wrong in a way that no amount of index tweaking will fix.

Estimates come from histograms on statistics objects: a set of steps, each with a value range and a count. A predicate falls between two steps, and the optimiser interpolates. Two consequences follow. A predicate the optimiser believes is newer than the last value in the histogram gets a fixed multiplier, which is wrong when the value is the common case. And columns are estimated independently, so a predicate on two correlated columns multiplies two guesses, which compounds the error.

-- The plan view of the same statement. 'Estimated Rows' is what the
-- optimiser chose; 'Actual Rows' is what happened. Compare them per operator.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = 'N'
  AND OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO

-- Find the queries whose cached plans are being executed far more often
-- than they were compiled for: the classic sniffing signature.
SELECT SUBSTRING(st.text, (qs.statement_start_offset/2) + 1,
             ((CASE WHEN qs.statement_end_offset = -1
                     THEN DATALENGTH(st.text)
                     ELSE qs.statement_end_offset END
                - qs.statement_start_offset)/2 + 1) AS StatementText,
       qs.execution_count,
       qs.creation_time,
       qs.last_execution_time,
       qs.total_logical_reads,
       qs.total_elapsed_time / 1000 AS TotalElapsedMs
FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
WHERE st.text LIKE '%SalesOrderLine%'
ORDER BY qs.total_logical_reads DESC;
GO

Bad estimates are the root cause, and they are usually a data problem, not a query problem. If the estimate for Status = 'N’Status = 'N' is 40 rows and the actual is 4,000,000, then every cost downstream is computed from 40 and every decision is wrong. The query is not wrong, the index is not missing, and no hint is the answer. The answers, in the order worth trying, are: fix the parameter (section 6.8), fix the statistics (section 5.11), or add a filtered statistic that describes the skewed subset.

A second, subtler form of the problem is correct estimates with the wrong granularity. An operator that estimates 1,000 rows and receives 1,000,000 still produces a plan that is too optimistic about memory, about which join to use, and about whether to go parallel, and it does so without any visible gap. A parallel plan that degrades into a serial spill is usually this, not a bad estimate.

6.3 Measuring cost: SET STATISTICS IO, TIME and logical reads

Elapsed time is a symptom and is distorted by concurrency, cache state and client behaviour. Logical reads is the metric that maps directly onto the cost model the optimiser used, and it is the number to compare before and after a change.

A logical read is an 8 KB page accessed by the engine, whether or not it came off disk. It is the unit the optimiser prices. A physical read is a page that had to be fetched from storage, and it only matters once, when the working set exceeds memory; a read-ahead read is a speculative prefetch. In a steady state on a warm buffer pool, logical reads are what a query costs and physical reads are approximately zero.

SET STATISTICS IO, TIME ON;
GO

-- Watch the 'lognical reads' line, not the elapsed time.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = 'N'
  AND OrderDate >= '2026-01-01';
GO
SET STATISTICS IO, TIME OFF;
GO

-- CPU time is separate from elapsed time. A query that is 400 ms of CPU in
-- 40 ms of elapsed time was parallel and used four threads.
SET STATISTICS ON;
GO
SELECT -- query under test;
GO
SET STATISTICS OFF;
GO

Typical SET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO output names one table per access and shows logical, physical, read-ahead and LOB reads. Reading it: the seek that returns 40 rows and costs 3 logical reads is efficient. The lookup that returns 40 rows and costs 41 reads is not, and the difference between the two lines is exactly the number of extra page reads per row. When a statement returns a result set with 200,000 rows, an lob logical readslob logical reads figure much larger than the plain logical reads means large objects are being dragged along, usually because of SELECT *SELECT *.

MetricStatementReads
Logical reads, statementsmall result setWhat the optimiser priced
Logical reads, statementlarge result setAlso the cost of shipping rows to the client, so it grows with the row count
Physical readsafter DBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERSDBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERSThe cold cost, useful for sizing the working set, not for tuning
CPU time vs elapsed timealwaysThe ratio tells you whether the plan went parallel and how much it diverged

Never compare logical reads between two runs that returned different row counts without saying so, and never compare a result set of 400,000 rows against one of 12. A fair comparison holds the output constant and varies only the plan. When that is impossible, compare the per row cost, or use a count-only rewrite to isolate the cost of finding rows from the cost of returning them.

6.4 Estimated and actual plans: SET STATISTICS XML and Ctrl+M

An estimated plan is what the optimiser decided before running anything. An actual plan is produced during execution and includes row counts, actual executions, and sometimes rows your application never sees because a later operator stopped reading. When a plan is slow, the actual plan is the only one worth reading.

-- Actual execution plan, returned as a result set of XML documents.
-- One row per statement executed in the batch.
SET STATISTICS XML ON;
GO
SELECT * FROM dbo.Product WHERE SKU = 'SKU-9931';
GO
SET STATISTICS XML OFF;
GO

-- Persist a plan for later comparison against a change, without shipping a
-- file. This is the reliable way to review a plan in a bug report.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = 'N';
GO
GO
SELECT qp.query_plan
FROM sys.dm_exec_query_plan(nsp_handle, 0) AS qp
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_stats(nsp_handle, 0) AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
WHERE st.text LIKE '%Status%';
GO

Shortcuts in SSMS and Azure Data Studio. Ctrl+MCtrl+M includes the actual execution plan with the next statement; Ctrl+ECtrl+E includes the estimated plan, which does not execute. Use estimated for interactive exploration, where you do not want to run a statement that may be expensive, and actual for every performance question, because the estimated plan cannot tell you what went wrong. The XEvent profiler’s sqlserver. event is the production equivalent, and it also carries the Parameteri and ParameterCo attributes that identify a sniffed plan.

Two plan properties are diagnostic on their own. StatementOptEarly Termination Reason on a seek says the optimizer stopped seeking early because the row goal was met, which is normal for T, EXI and M/M and a warning sign anywhere else. Non-Clustered Index Lookup with a large Actual Executions is the Key Lookup of section 5.4, and the estimated number of executions on the same operator is the number to compare it against.

6.5 The operator set and what each one costs

A plan is a tree of operators, read from the bottom up: data flows upward, and the widest node tells you where the work happens. Knowing what each operator does and what it costs in memory is enough to diagnose almost every plan without documentation.

OperatorReadsMemory behaviour
Clustered Index ScanEvery page of the tablePrefetch-friendly, cheapest per page
Clustered Index SeekThe pages from the root to the last qualifying rowCheap when few rows qualify
Index Scan / Index SeekNonclustered pages onlyCheap; can be covering and need no lookup
Key LookupOne clustered page per row returnedRandom reads; the cost of a narrow index that is not covering
Nested LoopsOuter once, inner per outer rowSmall; excellent when the inner side is an indexed seek
Hash Match (Join)Builds a hash of the smaller inputNeeds memory equal to the build side or spills to tempdb
Merge JoinBoth inputs read in orderAlmost no memory; needs sorted inputs
SortIts input, in fullGrant memory, or spill to tempdb with a warning icon
Top / Stream AggregateStops early / runs in one passMinimal; Stream Aggregate avoids hashing a GROUP BYGROUP BY
Parallelism (Gather, Repartition)Aggregates its input on each threadBlocks on the exchange; the Repartition StreamsRepartition Streams operator is a sort-hash join for the parallel case
-- A sort that ran out of memory shows a warning and reports a spill, with the
-- number of times it happened. This is the single most common cause of a
-- query that is fine in test and catastrophic in production.
SELECT ProductID, SKU, Name, UnitPrice
FROM dbo.Product
WHERE IsActive = 1
ORDER BY UnitPrice DESC, Name;
GO

-- 'estimated execution mode: row' on a columnstore scan means the query is
-- running one row at a time and is missing the batch-mode win entirely.
-- Check the compatibility level first: 150 or higher is required.
SELECT compatibility_level, is_read_committed_snapshot_on
FROM sys.databases
WHERE database_id = DB_ID();
GO

Read plans bottom to top and right to left, following the arrow with the thickest border, which is the operator doing most of the rows. Read the tooltip for every operator whose estimated row count is large, not just for the one at the top. The cost of a query is dominated by whichever operator touches the most rows, and that operator is usually not the one in the top right corner where the eye goes first.

6.6 Join algorithm selection

Three algorithms are available and the optimiser picks between them on cost. Understanding the conditions under which each wins is enough to predict the choice and to recognise when a plan is a symptom rather than a decision.

AlgorithmWins whenCosts
Nested LoopsThe outer input is very small, and the inner side has an index that can be sought per outer rowRe-executes the inner side per row; catastrophic when the inner side is a scan
Hash MatchBoth inputs are large, unsorted, and there is no index to seekBuilds a hash of the smaller side; memory equal to the build side, or a spill
Merge JoinBoth inputs arrive already sorted, typically from an indexAlmost free; runs as a merge with no memory grant beyond the buffers

The pattern worth memorising is the one where a small driving table is on one side of the join. A filtered dimension of 300 rows joined to a fact table of 40 million rows is the canonical case: Nested Loops, 300 seeks, no hash build, no memory grant. The same join with the filter removed is Hash Match, and the difference is the entire execution time. Where the small side is a VAL construct or a table variable, the optimiser knows the row count exactly, which is why table variables and inline VAL frequently produce better plans than the equivalent stored procedure with parameters.

-- Nested Loops territory: a 300-row driving set, an indexed inner side.
-- The table variable carries a known row count, so the outer side is
-- estimated exactly.
DECLARE @Target TABLE (CustomerID int PRIMARY KEY);
INSERT @Target (CustomerID)
SELECT CustomerID FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CreditLimit > 50000;

SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount
FROM @Target AS t
JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = t.CustomerID
JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = t.CustomerID
WHERE c.Status = 'Active'
ORDER BY o.OrderDate DESC;
GO

Hash Match beats Nested Loops whenever the outer side is not small. A Nested Loops join whose outer estimate is 20,000 and whose inner side is a scan will read 20,000 pages instead of 40. This appears when an estimate is wrong, and the plan is not the disease. It also appears when the inner side has no usable index, which is an index design problem. If you find yourself adding LOOP JOINLOOP JOIN or HASH JOINHASH JOIN hints to force the choice on a query with a known outer row count, that is a legitimate, documented exception — but on a query where the outer count varies, it is a fix that works on Tuesday and fails in production on Saturday.

Aggregate operators follow the same logic and appear as Hash MatchHash Match with an Aggr label. A GROUP BYGROUP BY on an input that arrives in index order becomes a Stream Aggregate, which needs no memory and no spill; a GROUP BYGROUP BY on an unordered input becomes a hash aggregate, which needs memory equal to the group count. A Stream AggregateStream Aggregate is a free improvement, and the way to get it is an index that delivers the grouping order.

6.7 Reading a plan: two patterns worth memorising

Almost every plan problem a working DBA meets is one of a small number of shapes. Two of them account for the majority, and both are recognisable from a single operator without any knowledge of the domain.

What you seeWhat it meansWhat to do
One Key LookupKey Lookup operator whose Actual Executions equals the number of rows returned, above an Index SeekIndex SeekA covering index is missing; the seek is cheap and the lookup is the costAdd the returned columns to INC on that index (section 5.4), or include the clustering key columns so the seek becomes covering
A Hash MatchHash Match with a small estimated input and a huge actual input, sometimes with a spill warningThe estimate is wrong; the hash was built for 40 rows and received 4 millionFix the estimate (section 6.2), not the join. Consider OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) before adding any index
-- Pattern 1, before and after. The difference is one operator and a large
-- reduction in logical reads.
--
-- BEFORE: Index Seek on IX_SO_Status_Date  -> Key Lookup  (1 execution per row)
-- AFTER:  Index Seek on IX_SO_Status_Date_Total  (no lookup, index is covering)
SET STATISTICS IO ON;
GO
SELECT o.SalesOrderID, c.Name, o.TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = o.CustomerID
WHERE o.Status = 'N'
  AND o.OrderDate >= '2026-01-01';
GO
SET STATISTICS IO OFF;
GO

-- Pattern 2: a hash join with 'Hash Warnings' or a spill to tempdb in the
-- tooltip, and a Cardinality Estimate mismatch in the plan properties.
SELECT c.Name, SUM(o.TotalAmount) AS Total
FROM dbo.Customer AS c
JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
WHERE o.Status = 'N'
GROUP BY c.Name;
GO

Cardinality estimates in the plan XML. Each operator in an actual plan has a Cardinali property holding the optimiser’s own arithmetic, including any adjustment and the reason for it. Reading it tells you why the estimate was what it was, which distinguishes a stale histogram from a genuinely skewed column from a correlated predicate. That distinction decides whether the fix is a statistics refresh, a new statistic, or a parameter change.

6.8 OPTION hints: RECOMPILE, OPTIMIZE FOR and their limits

A hint overrides the optimiser’s decision. That is exactly why a hint fixes a symptom: the optimiser is no longer choosing, and the conditions under which its choice was right have been removed. A hint is the right answer when the input distribution is genuinely outside what the optimiser can know, and the wrong answer when the optimiser knew and was overruled by bad statistics.

HintEffectUse it when
OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE)Discard any cached plan, compile afresh with the real parameter values, execute, then discard the plan againAlways suspect, as a diagnostic: if it fixes the query, the problem is the cached plan, and the fix is to stop caching it deliberately
OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR UNKNOWN)OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR UNKNOWN)Assume an average selectivity for every parameter, ignoring the histogramAfter RECO has shown the parameter is the cause, and the query genuinely needs a plan that works for all values
OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'N'))OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'N'))Use the histogram for this one parameter and the average for the restWhen one parameter is highly skewed and the others are not; the value must be a literal
OPTION (MAXDOP n)OPTION (MAXDOP n)Cap or force the degree of parallelismNever as a first move; see section 6.10
-- RECOMPILE: a real parameter is used at compile time, so the plan is built
-- for the data that exists right now. The cost is one compile per execution.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_GetOpenOrders
    @Status char(1),
    @From   date
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE Status = @Status
      AND OrderDate >= @From
    OPTION (RECOMPILE);
END
GO

-- OPTIMIZE FOR UNKNOWN: one plan that is reasonable for every value of
-- @Status, at the cost of being optimal for none of them.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = @Status
OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'N'));
GO

-- OPTIMIZE FOR with a deliberately unrepresentative value forces the average
-- path without discarding the histogram the optimiser already has.
SELECT SalesOrderID, TotalAmount
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE Status = @Status
OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'ZZ'));
GO

RECO is usually the right answer, and this is worth stating plainly. A parameter-sensitive plan — one where the optimal strategy genuinely differs between a selective value and a broad value — cannot be cached usefully, because the cached plan is good for the value that compiled it and bad for the rest. Forcing a recompile per execution costs a few milliseconds of compilation and gives every execution the plan it needs. For a procedure that is called a few hundred times a minute, that trade is not close. The misconceptions worth discarding are that RECO "always makes it slow" and that it prevents plan reuse; it does the opposite, by making every plan correct for its own parameters.

Two cautions. First, RECO on a statement that runs inside a loop compiles once per iteration, which can be a large hidden cost on a maintenance procedure. Second, a hint cannot repair a missing index: OPTION (FORCESEEK)OPTION (FORCESEEK) on a predicate with no index produces a scan with extra steps, not a seek. When a hint is kept in production code, the commit message that introduced it should say which of the four causes it was compensating for, because in two years nobody will remember, and the cause will have changed.

6.9 Parameter sniffing and the plan cache

A cached plan is compiled with the parameter values of its first execution and then reused by every session that submits the same text. If the first caller passed a selective value, the plan is built for a small result; if the first caller passed a broad value, the plan is built for a large one. Either way, other callers inherit a plan that was not built for them.

-- Both statements below are the same text. The first execution decides the plan.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_GetOpenOrders
    @Status char(1),
    @From   date
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
    FROM dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE Status = @Status
      AND OrderDate >= @From
    -- No hint here: this is the version that is slow for a broad
    -- Status after a narrow Status compiled and cached the plan.
END
GO

-- The cache, with the parameter set each plan was compiled for. A plan
-- for @Status = 'N' alongside a plan for @Status = 'X' is the signature of
-- a parameter-sensitive plan the server has already split.
SELECT cp.plan_handle,
       cp.usecounts,
       cp.size_in_bytes,
       cp.cacheobjtype,
       cp.creation_time,
       cp.last_execution_time
FROM sys.dm_exec_cached_plans AS cp
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(cp.plan_handle) AS qp
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(cp.plan_handle) AS st
WHERE st.text LIKE '%usp_GetOpenOrders%'
ORDER BY cp.last_execution_time DESC;
GO

-- Diagnostic order. Run this first, before touching anything.
--   1. Add OPTION (RECOMPILE). If it fixes the problem, it is the cached plan.
--   2. If not, the problem is elsewhere: missing index, wrong statistics,
--      or an unavoidable cost.

DBCC FREEPROCCACHEDBCC FREEPROCCACHE is a diagnostic and a last resort, not a fix. It empties the entire plan cache for the instance, which means every procedure, every report and every application query is recompiled at its next execution. On a busy instance that is a CPU spike, a burst of compilation waits, and — because the cache repopulates in the order queries arrive — the plan that caused the problem may simply be compiled again from a different first caller. It does not address the cause, and its most common effect is to move the symptom to the next maintenance window. The scoped alternatives are better: DBCC FLUSHPROCINDBDBCC FLUSHPROCINDB for one database, sp_rec for one procedure, and ALTER PROCEDURE ... WITH RECOMPILEALTER PROCEDURE ... WITH RECOMPILE to mark a procedure for recompilation on next execution.

ApproachScopeAppropriate for
OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE)One statementParameter-sensitive plans; the default answer
ALTER PROCEDURE ... WITH RECOMPILEALTER PROCEDURE ... WITH RECOMPILEOne procedureProcedures whose plans are always wrong, and you have not yet found why
sp_recOne procedure or tableAfter a statistics refresh or a schema change
ALTER DATABASE ... SET PARAMETER_SNIFFING = OFFALTER DATABASE ... SET PARAMETER_SNIFFING = OFFWhole database, per queryWhen many queries are affected; use sparingly and measure
DBCC FLUSHPROCINDBDBCC FLUSHPROCINDBOne databaseConfirmed plan cache corruption, or a post-deployment flush
DBCC FREEPROCCACHEDBCC FREEPROCCACHEThe whole instanceAlmost never. Test only

6.10 MAXDOP, parallelism and FORCESEEK

When the estimated cost of a statement exceeds the cost threshold for parallel plan, the engine splits it across threads. Parallelism is usually free performance and occasionally the cause of a problem, and the three knobs that control it are the ones people reach for first and regret most.

MAX is the maximum threads per query. The default is 0, which means "use the configured maximum, up to the number of logical processors and 8, SQL Server 2022 and later". On a 64-core instance that is a great deal of parallelism, and for a set-based operation on a large table it is correct: hash aggregates and columnstore scans get faster with more threads until memory becomes the constraint. For a point lookup it does nothing, because the cost is far below the threshold.

-- Cap parallelism for one statement while diagnosing a plan. A query that
-- gets dramatically worse with MAXDOP 1 was relying on a parallel scan,
-- which is a design statement about missing indexes, not a call to keep
-- the hint forever.
SELECT DATEFROMPARTS(YEAR(OrderDate), MONTH(OrderDate), 1) AS MonthStart,
       COUNT_BIG(*) AS Orders
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE OrderDate >= '2025-01-01'
GROUP BY DATEFROMPARTS(YEAR(OrderDate), MONTH(OrderDate), 1)
OPTION (MAXDOP 1);
GO

-- Capping the whole database, for OLTP where runaway parallelism on a
-- 64-core host is a symptom of missing indexes rather than a solution.
-- ALTER DATABASE SalesDB SET MAXDOP 4;
GO

-- The cost threshold above which a parallel plan is considered, and the
-- instance-level default. Raising the cost threshold makes the optimiser
-- less willing to go parallel; it is the opposite of a MAXDOP hint.
SELECT name, value, value_in_use
FROM sys.configurations
WHERE name IN ('cost threshold for parallelism', 'max degree of parallelism',
                 'max worker threads', 'optimize for ad hoc workloads');
GO

The two algorithm hints are for the exceptional case where the optimiser’s cost model is right about the work and wrong about the shape, and both are used in practice far more often than they should be.

-- HASH GROUP forces a hash aggregate, which streams: it can start returning
-- groups before the input is exhausted. Correct for a report that must
-- return a first page quickly, wrong for one that must not.
SELECT Status, COUNT_BIG(*) AS n
FROM dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
GROUP BY Status
OPTION (HASH GROUP);
GO

-- LOOP JOIN is legitimate when the outer side is a known-small set, for
-- example a table variable with a known row count, and the inner side has
-- a supporting index.
SELECT t.CustomerID, o.SalesOrderID
FROM @Target AS t
JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.CustomerID = t.CustomerID
OPTION (LOOP JOIN, MAXDOP 1);
GO

-- FORCESEEK: forces a seek even when a scan would be cheaper, and can make
-- a query arbitrarily slower. It also fails outright when no index can
-- serve the predicate, in which case SQL Server raises an error rather than
-- falling back to a scan.
SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE OrderDate = '2026-03-01'
OPTION (FORCESEEK);
GO

Use hints as a probe. Add OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE), then OPTION (MAXDOP 1)OPTION (MAXDOP 1), then the algorithm hint, and record logical reads for each. When one of them changes the number dramatically, you have learned where the problem is. Remove the hint and fix the underlying cause — usually an index, a statistic, or a parameter. Keeping a hint is defensible when the optimiser cannot know something you know, such as a cardinality guaranteed by an upstream process; it is not defensible as a way of making a plan shape you have already seen.

Exercises 6

  1. Turn on SET STATISTICS IO, TIME ONSET STATISTICS IO, TIME ON and run the export query from 5.4. Record logical reads, physical reads, CPU time and elapsed time, then run it again and confirm the logical reads are stable while the physical reads fall towards zero.
  2. Capture an actual plan with SET STATISTICS XML ONSET STATISTICS XML ON for a three-table join. For every operator, record estimated rows, actual rows, actual executions and estimated cost, and tabulate them. Identify every operator where the estimate is off by more than an order of magnitude.
  3. Build a query that returns one Key LookupKey Lookup operator per row. Record the logical reads, add the missing columns to INC, re-measure, and confirm the lookup operator has disappeared from the plan entirely.
  4. Force a bad estimate: create a table with a column that is 99 percent one value, join it, and read the actual plan. Locate the Hash MatchHash Match with the large gap between estimated and actual rows, and read the Cardinali property to see the optimiser’s own arithmetic.
  5. Take a query with two join orders available. Force each with OPTION (LOOP JOIN)OPTION (LOOP JOIN) and OPTION (HASH JOIN)OPTION (HASH JOIN) and record the logical reads for both, then remove the hints and confirm the optimiser picks the cheaper one.
  6. Create a stored procedure with a @Status@Status parameter. Call it once with a selective value so the plan caches, then call it with a broad value and record the degradation from sys.dm_exec and the plan. Add OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) and repeat both calls.
  7. For the same procedure, compare three variants: no hint, OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE), and OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'N'))OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@Status = 'N')). Record logical reads for a selective and a broad call under each, and state which variant is correct for which workload shape.
  8. Run a large aggregate four times: unmodified, with MAXDOP 1MAXDOP 1, with OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE), and with both. Record CPU time, elapsed time and logical reads, and identify which of the two overrides explains each change.
  9. Show parameter sniffing from both directions. Force a plan cache flush with DBCC FLUSHPROCINDBDBCC FLUSHPROCINDB (not FREEPR), call the procedure with each value in turn, and record the creati and executi of each cached plan from sys.dm_exec_cached_planssys.dm_exec_cached_plans.
  10. Find the ten most expensive cached statements by total_log from sys.dm_exec, retrieve each plan, and classify each one: missing covering index, wrong estimate, unavoidable scan, or a problem that belongs to the calling application rather than the database.

7 · Transactions, Locking and Isolation

7.1 ACID in SQL Server terms7.2 The transaction log, LSN, checkpoints and truncation7.3 BEGIN TRAN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVE TRAN7.4 Read committed and read-lock behaviour7.5 The isolation levels and read versioning7.6 Lock types, modes and compatibility7.7 Lock escalation and key-range locking7.8 LOCK_TIMEOUT, NOWAIT and WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY7.9 Finding and killing blockers7.10 Deadlocks: reading the graph and preventing them7.11 XACT_ABORT ON and savepoints

Concurrency control in SQL Server is lock-based by default and version-based when the database is configured for it. The statements are the same as in any other engine, and the behaviour is not: this chapter covers what the transaction log actually guarantees, why a plain SEL can block a writer, how escalation turns a row-level problem into a table-level one, and how to find the transaction that is holding everything up without restarting the instance.

7.1 ACID in SQL Server terms

The four letters describe four guarantees, and in SQL Server each one is implemented by a specific mechanism rather than by a general property of transactions. Knowing which mechanism implements which guarantee is what makes the failure modes predictable.

GuaranteeMechanismWhat it actually gives you
AtomicityThe transaction log, with write-ahead loggingA transaction’s changes are all in the log or none are; an interrupted transaction is rolled back by recovery
ConsistencyConstraints: primary key, foreign key, check, uniqueThe database is left in a state that satisfies every declared rule; this is the engine’s part, not yours
IsolationLocks, or row versioning when READ_COMMIT is onConcurrent transactions see a set of rows that is consistent with some serial order
DurabilityThe log record flushed to stable storage before commit returnsA committed transaction survives an instance crash; a flushed log record is never lost

Write-ahead logging is the rule that makes atomicity possible: the log record describing a change is written and flushed before the dirty data page is written. Recovery then works in one direction. If a page is in the log, recovery replays it. If a page was modified but the log record is not there, then by the write-ahead rule the page cannot be on disk, so there is nothing to undo. This is why a rollback of a large transaction can be fast: the pages are not restored, they are marked as needing the log, and recovery or the next checkpoint handles them.

Durability is bounded by the flush, not by the commit. The commit returns when the log records are flushed according to the current recovery modelrecovery model and log flush behaviour, which is why a commit on a saturated log can block for a long time. A commit that has returned has been made durable, and a commit that is still waiting has not.

Log truncation. In SIM recovery the log is truncated at a checkpoint once no transaction needs it, so the log file can stay small. In FU or BULK_ the log is truncated only after a log backup, which is the mechanism that lets a point-in-time restore work. A transaction log that grows without bound is a sign that nothing is advancing the truncation point — a long-running transaction, a replication or CDC slot, or a database in FU recovery with no log backup job.

-- Which mechanism is in force, and how much log is reusable right now.
SELECT name, recovery_model_desc, is_read_committed_snapshot_on,
       is_allow_snapshot_isolation_on, log_reuse_wait_desc
FROM sys.databases
WHERE name = 'SalesDB';
GO

-- Log space, in the units that matter: megabytes of the file and of the
-- portion that is currently available for reuse.
SELECT name,
       CAST(size / 128.0 AS decimal(10,2)) AS SizeMB,
       CAST(space_used / 128.0 AS decimal(10,2)) AS UsedMB
FROM sys.database_files
WHERE type = 1;   -- 0 = data file, 1 = log file
GO

-- Virtual log file layout and where truncation currently stands.
DBCC LOGINFO ('SalesDB');
GO

7.2 The transaction log, LSN, checkpoints and truncation

The transaction log is an ordered sequence of log records, each identified by a log sequence number. It is not a backup of the data and it does not contain pages; it contains the changes, in order, with enough information to redo or undo them. Reading it is the way to answer "what was this transaction doing, and what did it change?"

Checkpoints are the other half of the mechanism. A checkpoint writes every dirty page in the buffer pool to disk and records the oldest log sequence number still needed. Recovery after a clean shutdown does nothing; recovery after a crash replays the log from the last checkpoint to the end, and rolls back any transaction that had no commit record. A CHECK statement forces one, and in SIM recovery automatic checkpoints also advance the truncation point.

-- Active transactions and their log consumption. A transaction open since
-- yesterday is the reason a log file grew to 80 GB overnight.
SELECT s.session_id, s.login_name, s.host_name,
       s.status, s.open_transaction_count,
       xact.transaction_begin_time,
       DBCC.LOGUSED(xact.transaction_id) AS LogUsedBytes
FROM sys.dm_tran_database_transactions AS xact
JOIN sys.dm_tran_session_transactions AS st
  ON st.transaction_id = xact.transaction_id
JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions AS s
  ON s.session_id = st.session_id
CROSS APPLY fn_dblog(NULL, NULL, 0, 0) AS DBCC
ORDER BY xact.transaction_begin_time;
GO

-- The exact statement a session is currently running.
-- sys.dm_exec_requests.sql_text is the batch; the input buffer is the
-- current statement within it.
SELECT r.session_id, r.status, r.command,
       t.text AS BatchText
FROM sys.dm_exec_requests AS r
OUTER APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(r.sql_handle) AS t
WHERE r.session_id <> @@SPID
  AND r.database_id = DB_ID();
GO

-- Force a checkpoint. Costs I/O; advances the truncation point in SIMPLE.
-- CHECKPOINT;
GO

Log truncation blockers. In FU or BULK_ recovery the log cannot be truncated until a log backup is taken, so a missing log backup job is indistinguishable from a stuck transaction when you look at file growth. In SIM recovery, log_reuse names the blocker directly: ACTIVE_TR is a long transaction, AVAILABILI is an Always On or replication slot, CHECK means a checkpoint has not run yet, and LOG_B means the recovery model is FULLFULL. Read that column before anything else when a log file is growing.

7.3 BEGIN TRAN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK and SAVE TRAN

Four statements control a transaction, and two of them have a nested semantics that surprises everybody exactly once. @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT is the number of unmatched BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTION statements in the session, and a COM decrements it rather than committing anything until it reaches zero.

BEGIN TRY
    BEGIN TRANSACTION;

    INSERT dbo.AuditLog (LoggedAt, Message)
    VALUES (SYSDATETIME(), 'Order 104512 placed');

    UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
    SET    Status = 'N',
           TotalAmount = TotalAmount * 1.2
    WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;

    INSERT dbo.Notification (SalesOrderID, SentAt)
    VALUES (104512, NULL);

    -- A savepoint, so a failure in the notification step does not lose
    -- the header update above it.
    SAVE TRANSACTION BeforeNotify;

    INSERT dbo.Notification (SalesOrderID, SentAt)
    VALUES (104512, SYSDATETIME());

    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    -- A rollback to the savepoint keeps the transaction alive; a full ROLLBACK
    -- ends it. The count tells you which is possible.
    IF @@TRANCOUNT > 0 AND XACT_STATE() <> 0
    BEGIN
        ROLLBACK TRANSACTION BeforeNotify;
        INSERT dbo.Notification (SalesOrderID, SentAt, FailedReason)
        VALUES (104512, NULL, ERROR_MESSAGE());
    END
    THROW;
END CATCH
GO
StatementEffect on @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT
BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTIONIncrements by 1
COMMIT TRANSACTIONCOMMIT TRANSACTIONDecrements by 1; nothing is durable until it reaches 0
ROLLBACK TRANSACTIONROLLBACK TRANSACTION (no name)Sets to 0 and rolls back the entire transaction, including any outer nesting
SAVE TRANSACTIONSAVE TRANSACTION nameUnchanged; records a point to roll back to
ROLLBACK TRANSACTIONROLLBACK TRANSACTION nameUnchanged; undoes work after the savepoint, keeping the transaction open

The trap is that a named ROLLBACK TRANSACTIONROLLBACK TRANSACTION rolls back only to the matching savepoint, while an unnamed one rolls back everything — including work an outer caller believed was already committed. In a stored procedure called inside a caller-managed transaction, this is the difference between a partial failure and a lost order. Defensive practice is to check @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT on entry and to never issue an unnamed ROLL from a procedure that did not open the transaction.

Distributed transactions. A transaction that updates two databases through the same instance is local, not distributed. A transaction that spans instances, or that enlists in a distributed transaction through a linked server, uses the Microsoft Distributed Transaction Coordinator, which has its own failure modes, its own performance characteristics and a MS DTCMS DTC service to restart. Distributed transactions are not something to design into an application on SQL Server; partition the data so a transaction stays inside one database.

7.4 Read committed and read-lock behaviour

The default isolation level in SQL Server is read committed, and it is implemented with shared locks that are held until the end of the statement. The consequence surprises people who arrive from engines where a reader does not block a writer: a plain SEL can block, and can be blocked, and can hold up a whole table for the length of a long-running report.

The sequence is short. A reader takes a shared lock on each row or page it needs and releases it when the statement finishes, not when the transaction finishes. A writer wanting to modify the same rows needs an exclusive lock and waits. Two writers needing the same rows both wait. Under read committed, a writer takes an update lock first, so two writers to the same row serialise rather than both converting to exclusive and deadlocking immediately.

-- Session 1: holds shared locks on every SalesOrder row from this date forward
-- for the whole statement, which is slow because it aggregates.
BEGIN TRANSACTION;
SELECT CustomerID, SUM(TotalAmount) AS Total
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE  OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
GROUP BY CustomerID;
-- still open; locks still held even though the SELECT finished
GO

-- Session 2: blocks on the first row it needs, then times out.
BEGIN TRANSACTION;
SET LOCK_TIMEOUT 5000;   -- milliseconds
UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
SET    Status = 'X'
WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;
COMMIT TRANSACTION;
GO

Note that the lock is held until the end of the statement, not the end of the transaction, so a plain SEL outside a transaction releases its locks when it finishes reading. It is the combination of read committed and an explicit transaction that turns a long read into a long block, which is why "our reporting query blocks the application" always turns out to be a query with a BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTION in it, or an INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT that is still reading.

WITH (NOLOCK)WITH (NOLOCK) and READUNC do not fix this, and they introduce a worse problem. They tell the engine to take no shared locks and to ignore the shared locks others hold. The query stops blocking and starts returning rows that another transaction has inserted but not committed, and rows that another transaction has updated but not yet committed, so it can read the same logical row twice or read a value that never existed. For a dashboard that is often acceptable; for anything that computes money, writes it onward, or updates the row it just read, it is a data integrity defect. The correct fix is READ_COMMIT, which is set out in section 7.5.

7.5 The isolation levels and read versioning

SQL Server offers two different mechanisms for the same levels, and the choice between them is made once per database. Lock-based readers block writers and block each other. Version-based readers never block and never block, at the cost of a version store in tem and the row versions that updates must leave behind.

Isolation levelReadsDirty readNon-repeatablePhantom
READ UNCOMMITTEDREAD UNCOMMITTEDUncommitted, blocks nobodyYesYesYes
READ COMMITTEDREAD COMMITTED (default)Shared locks until end of statementNoYesYes
REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READShared locks held to end of transaction, plus key-range locks on seeksNoNoYes
SERIALFull range locks on the rows and keys the predicate could matchNoNoNo
SNAPRow versions; the whole transaction sees one consistent snapshotNoNoNo

Read versioning is configured by two database options, and the order in which they must be enabled is not a detail. READ_COMMIT changes how read committed behaves for every table in the database, so it must be set when there are no user tables. ALLOW_SNAPSH merely makes snapshot isolation available; a transaction must still opt in with SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOTSET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT, and it can be set with tables present.

-- RCSI must be enabled before any table exists in the database. The statement
-- fails with Msg 4132 if a user table is present, which is the single most
-- common error when following a blog post on this topic.
-- ALTER DATABASE SalesDB SET READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
GO

-- This is available with tables present: it only makes the option
-- available, and turns it on. It does not change existing sessions.
-- ALTER DATABASE SalesDB SET ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION ON;
GO

-- The flags as they are now.
SELECT name, is_read_committed_snapshot_on AS RCSIOn,
       is_allow_snapshot_isolation_on AS SnapshotAllowed
FROM sys.databases WHERE name = 'SalesDB';
GO

-- Snapshot isolation for one transaction. The version store lives in
-- tempdb, so a long-running snapshot transaction is a tempdb consumer.
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT;
BEGIN TRANSACTION;
SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;
-- The row is now pinned; an update from another session will be blocked
-- only when that session tries to modify this row, and the tempdb version
-- cannot be cleaned until this transaction ends.
COMMIT TRANSACTION;
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED;
GO

-- Under RCSI the reader takes no shared locks at all.
SELECT CustomerID, SUM(TotalAmount) AS Total
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE  OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
GROUP BY CustomerID;
GO
Lock-based (RCSI off)Version-based (RCSI on)
Reader blocks writerYesNo
Writer blocks readerYesNo
Cost to readersLock manager and waitingRow versions in tempdb
Cost to writersNo extra workEvery update writes a 14-byte version row to tempdb
RiskBlocking chains, deadlocks, escalationtempdb growth from long transactions; blocking on UPD of a pinned row

RCSI is a good default for a mixed OLTP and reporting workload, and it is a poor default for a database that runs a high volume of short updates with a tem under pressure, because every update now costs an extra write and a version row. Decide it from the write volume and the tempdb size, not from a preference for non-blocking reads, and measure both. The blocking that RCSI removes from readers is transferred to writers, where it is less visible and more damaging.

Snapshot isolation and update conflicts. Under snapshot isolation two transactions may read the same row and both try to update it. The first to commit wins; the second gets error 3960, "the transaction could not access the row because it was updated by another transaction", and the application must retry. This is not a deadlock and no deadlock victim is chosen. Any code using snapshot isolation needs a retry loop, and a retry loop needs a limit, or it will spin.

7.6 Lock types, modes and compatibility

Locks are held on resources, and the resource granularity is the unit the engine can wait for: a row, a key, a page, an extent, a table. The modes describe intent, and the intent modes exist so that a table-level lock does not have to be taken and released for every individual row change.

ModeMeaning
SS sharedReading; many readers, no writer
UU updateAbout to modify; takes an XX lock when the read is done. Two UU locks are incompatible with each other
XX exclusiveModifying; incompatible with everything
I intent sharedShared locks will be taken on children of this resource
I intent exclusiveExclusive locks will be taken on children
S shared intent exclusiveShared on this resource, exclusive on children — the mode every UPD on a table with a nonclustered index takes
Sc schema stabilityHeld by every statement that compiles; compatible with everything except Sc
Sc schema modificationHeld by DDL; incompatible with everything, which is why DDL blocks everything
Key-rangeHeld only under SERIAL and REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ, on a seek over a unique index key

The compatibility table is short enough to memorise. SS is compatible with SS and I and nothing else. UU is compatible with SS, and that is all. XX is compatible with nothing. I is compatible with I, I, SS and SIXSIX. The intent modes do not conflict with each other; that is the entire point of them.

-- Every lock in the instance, resolved to its object, index, page and row.
-- request_mode is the mode wanted; granted_mode is the mode held. A row
-- with a non-NULL request_mode and NULL granted_mode is what people
-- are waiting for.
SELECT tl.request_session_id, tl.resource_type,
       tl.resource_database_id, tl.resource_associated_object_id,
       tl.index_id, tl.page_id, tl.row_id,
       tl.request_mode, tl.request_status
FROM sys.dm_tran_locks AS tl
WHERE tl.resource_type <> 'DATABASE'
  AND tl.resource_database_id = DB_ID()
ORDER BY tl.request_session_id, tl.request_mode;
GO

-- Lock counts by mode and type for the whole instance. A sudden column of
-- X locks means escalating updates; a flood of KEY means a SERIALIZABLE query.
SELECT resource_type, request_mode, COUNT_BIG(*) AS LockCount
FROM sys.dm_tran_locks
WHERE request_session_id = @@SPID
GROUP BY resource_type, request_mode
ORDER BY LockCount DESC;
GO

Key-range locking is the mechanism behind most phantoms. Under SERIAL, a predicate on an indexed column takes a lock on the range of key values it could match, not only on the rows it does match, so a concurrent insert of a matching row blocks. The range lock is taken only when the access path is a seek on a unique index. This produces an unfortunate pair of behaviours: a serializable query on a non-unique index gets no key-range protection at all and can still see a phantom, and a serializable query that should use a seek is sometimes forced into a scan to avoid holding range locks on a large portion of a table. If you need real serializable semantics, make sure the predicate is sargable and that a unique index supports the seek.

7.7 Lock escalation and key-range locking

Row and page locks have memory cost, and tracking a large number of them for a single statement is worse than the contention they prevent. When a statement acquires a large number of locks on the same resource, SQL Server converts them all to a single table-level lock. This is lock escalation, and it is the mechanism behind the most common "my update blocked the whole table" report.

The trigger is per page: when a single statement holds more than 2440 locks on one page, the engine attempts to escalate to a table lock. If any other session holds a conflicting lock on the table, escalation cannot proceed, and the statement keeps its row and page locks and continues — which is why the behaviour is intermittent and appears only under load. 2440 is a threshold on locks per page, not on rows: the actual number of rows a page holds depends on row width, so a table with narrow rows escalates at fewer rows than a table with wide ones.

-- An UPDATE with no useful index: one statement, millions of rows, and
-- escalation as soon as the first page crosses the threshold.
UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
SET    Status = 'X'
WHERE Status = 'N';      -- and no index on Status
GO

-- The same update, correctly indexed, takes row locks and escalates never.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SO_Status ON dbo.SalesOrder (Status) INCLUDE (SalesOrderID);
GO

-- Proof of escalation: a single X on the table, granted at the end.
SELECT tl.request_session_id, tl.resource_type,
       tl.resource_associated_object_id,
       tl.request_mode, tl.request_status
FROM sys.dm_tran_locks AS tl
WHERE resource_type IN ('TABLE', 'PAGE', 'ROW', 'KEY')
  AND tl.request_session_id <> @@SPID
  AND tl.granted_mode = 'X';
GO

-- Escalation is reported by an Extended Events event on real systems; the
-- XEvent action that matters is lock_type, which reads TABLE when escalation
-- happened and KEY or RID when it did not.
-- SELECT * FROM sys.dm_xe_map_values WHERE name = 'lock_type' AND object_name = 'lock_type';
GO

Lock escalation cannot be disabled by default and should not be. The trace flag 8452 prevents it, and setting it is a well-known way to turn a moderate blocking problem into an instance-wide lock memory problem, because a statement that would have taken one table lock now takes millions of row locks. The defensible interventions are all of them index or design changes: a supporting index so the statement touches fewer rows, smaller batches so no single statement reaches the threshold, and shorter transactions so the escalated lock is released sooner. SQL Server 2019 and later also support partitioned tables with lock partitioning, where escalation stops at the partition level for operations that can be restricted to one partition, which reduces the blast radius without disabling the behaviour.

Batch the large DML rather than raising a threshold. UPDATE TOP (5000) ... ;UPDATE TOP (5000) ... ; in a loop, or a procedure that processes a work queue in chunks and commits between chunks, keeps every statement below the escalation threshold and keeps each transaction’s locks short-lived. The cost is more log flushes, which is a real but bounded and measurable trade, and it is almost always better than holding a table lock against a live table for the length of a 40-minute update.

7.8 LOCK_TIMEOUT, NOWAIT and WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY

The default behaviour when a session cannot get a lock is to wait forever, which in an application means a thread that never returns and a connection pool that exhausts. There are three ways to bound that wait, and they fail in different ways, so the choice depends on what the application should do next.

MechanismBehaviourFailure mode
SET LOCK_TIMEOUT nSET LOCK_TIMEOUT nWaits at most nn milliseconds, then raises error 1222Breaks the transaction; needs handling in a retry loop
WITH (NOWAIT)WITH (NOWAIT)Returns immediately with error 1222 if the lock is unavailableSame, without the wait; a hint on the statement
WAIT_AT_LOQueues the session behind existing user sessions instead of erroring, with a maximum waitDesigned for index operations and alter-partition switches, not for DML
-- Session-scoped, in milliseconds. -1 is the default, which means wait
-- indefinitely. Setting it once at connection open is the usual practice.
-- SET LOCK_TIMEOUT 5000;
GO

-- Statement-level NOWAIT. Error 1222: "Could not wait for this
-- lock to be granted during the wait time period."
UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder WITH (NOWAIT)
SET    Status = 'X'
WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;
GO

-- Application-side timeout on the command. This stops waiting in the
-- client but does NOT roll back the server-side transaction, which is why
-- the transaction stays open and keeps holding locks after the client has
-- given up. Always issue a ROLLBACK in the error path.
-- CommandTimeout = 30 in ADO.NET; SqlCommand.CommandTimeout = 30;
GO

-- WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY, with an explicit escape so the operation does not
-- wait forever behind long-running readers. See also 5.8.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder
ADD CONSTRAINT FK_SalesOrder_Customer
    FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID) REFERENCES dbo.Customer (CustomerID)
    WITH (ONLINE = ON,
          WAIT_AT_LOW_PRIORITY (MAX_DURATION = 5 MINUTES,
                                       ABORT_AFTER_WAIT = NONE));
GO

A client-side command timeout is not a transaction rollback. When the client cancels, the server may still be executing the batch, and the open transaction holds its locks until it finishes or is killed. This is the mechanism behind the "the application timed out but the lock is still there for ten minutes" class of incident. The cure is on the server side: SET LOCK_TIMEOUTSET LOCK_TIMEOUT so the statement itself gives up, and an unconditional ROLL in the error path of the procedure. A connection that is closed does not release the locks of an open transaction; only a rollback, a commit, or a KI does.

7.9 Finding and killing blockers

A blocked session has a blocking_ that points at the session causing the wait, and following that column up gives the root of the chain. The query below is the first thing to run, and in most incidents it identifies the culprit immediately, because the answer is usually an UPD that opened a transaction and then waited on something else while holding its locks.

-- Everyone currently waiting, and on whom. blocking_session_id = 0 means
-- the session is not blocked, however long its elapsed time.
SELECT r.session_id AS BlockedSession,
       r.blocking_session_id AS BlockingSession,
       r.wait_type, r.wait_time AS WaitMs,
       r.wait_resource,
       r.status, r.command,
       s.login_name, s.host_name, s.program_name,
       txt.text AS BatchText
FROM sys.dm_exec_requests AS r
JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions AS s ON r.session_id = s.session_id
OUTER APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(r.sql_handle) AS txt
WHERE r.session_id <> @@SPID
ORDER BY r.wait_time DESC;
GO

-- The same information from the scheduler's point of view, which also
-- shows sessions that are not currently running a request.
SELECT w.session_id, w.blocking_session_id,
       w.wait_type, w.wait_duration_ms,
       w.wait_resource,
       w.wait_handle AS WaitHandle
FROM sys.dm_os_waiting_tasks AS w
WHERE session_id <> @@SPID
  AND w.wait_type <> 'THREADPOOL_WAIT'
ORDER BY w.wait_duration_ms DESC;
GO

-- The statement a session is on right now, not the whole batch. Run this
-- for the blocking_session_id value from the query above.
DBCC INPUTBUFFER (57);
GO

-- The last resort. Rolls back the blocking transaction; the blocked
-- session then continues. Always capture the input buffer first.
-- KILL 57;
GO

Wait types worth knowing. LCK is waiting for an exclusive lock and is the ordinary "this is blocked". LCK is a reader waiting, which is what RCSI removes. LCK_ is a writer waiting for intent exclusive, usually behind an open transaction. ASYNC_NE means the client stopped reading results, so the server is blocked writing to it — a common cause of blocking with no lock contention at all, and one that is fixed in the application, not the database. THREADP means the instance has no worker threads, which is a capacity problem and not a blocking problem.

Automate the first query rather than running it by hand. A job that samples it every few seconds, records the result when a chain of waits longer than a threshold appears, and includes the DBCC INPUTBUFFERDBCC INPUTBUFFER output for the blocking session, produces an evidence trail that makes the root cause a design problem rather than an argument. Blocking is intermittent by nature and the query that reproduces it is rarely the one that was running when someone noticed.

7.10 Deadlocks: reading the graph and preventing them

A deadlock is two transactions, each holding what the other needs. SQL Server detects the cycle with an internal timer, chooses one participant as the victim by lowest cost, rolls it back with error 1205, and lets the survivor finish. The survivor usually completes within milliseconds, which is why a deadlock is almost never a performance problem in itself; it is a correctness problem, because the application now has to handle a failed transaction that was otherwise valid.

-- The deadlock victim is the transaction with the lowest rollback cost.
-- The survivor is the one with more work to lose, which is the better
-- outcome: the cheapest transaction to redo is the one that is undone.
-- SET DEADLOCK_PRIORITY LOW on a session or statement makes that session the
-- preferred victim; the default is NORMAL, which uses cost and then
-- transaction start time to break ties.
SET DEADLOCK_PRIORITY LOW;
GO

-- Two transactions that deadlock on every run, written in the order
-- that causes it: one updates Customers then SalesOrder, the other the
-- reverse.
-- Session A:                          -- Session B:
-- BEGIN TRAN;                         -- BEGIN TRAN;
-- UPDATE Customer SET ...             -- UPDATE SalesOrder SET ...
--   WHERE CustomerID = 1;              --   WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;
-- UPDATE SalesOrder SET ...           -- UPDATE Customer SET ...
--   WHERE SalesOrderID = 104512;        --   WHERE CustomerID = 1;
-- COMMIT;                             -- COMMIT;
GO

-- The same work with a consistent order. One of the two statements will
-- simply wait, and there is no cycle, so no deadlock is possible.
BEGIN TRY
    BEGIN TRANSACTION;
    -- Always Customer before SalesOrder, in every code path.
    UPDATE dbo.Customer     SET Name = @Name WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;
    UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder  SET Status = 'N' WHERE SalesOrderID = @SalesOrderID;
    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    IF XACT_STATE() <> 0 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
    -- Error 1205 is retryable; anything else is a real failure.
    IF ERROR_NUMBER() = 1205
    THROW 51000, 'Deadlock. Retry the operation.', 1;
    THROW;
END CATCH
GO

The deadlock graph is a text diagram of the two participants, the resources each holds and each waits for, the statement each was running, and the input buffers. It is written to the SQL Server error log and captured by the xml_deadl Extended Events event. Reading it takes practice, and the reading order is fixed: find the two pro nodes, look at waitre on each, and confirm the cycle — the resource process A waits for is held by process B and vice versa. The inpu attribute on each process is where the offending statement is, and it usually names the fix.

PreventionWhat it prevents
Consistent access orderThe cycle itself; the only technique that eliminates the deadlock rather than shifting who loses
Short transactionsThe window during which locks are held, so the cycle is far less likely to form
Supporting indexesRow locks instead of page and table locks, and seeks instead of scans, so two statements do not contend for whole pages
Low lock timeout plus retryTurns the deadlock into a fast failure the application can retry, at the cost of retries
DEADLOCKChooses which transaction is sacrificed; it decides who retries, not whether
RCSIMost reader-writer deadlocks, by removing the reader’s locks entirely. It introduces no new deadlocks between two writers

Two deadlocks deserve different responses. A deadlock between two writers is nearly always an access-order problem, and consistent ordering fixes it. A deadlock between a writer and a reader is nearly always a reader inside an open transaction, and it is fixed by shortening the transaction, moving the SEL out of it, or turning on RCSI. Treating them the same, by adding retry logic to both, leaves the real cause in place and the deadlock log full of reports that a retry fixed, which is a worse outcome than either fix.

7.11 XACT_ABORT ON and savepoints

By default a run-time error — a constraint violation, a deadlock, a divide by zero, a severity 16 error raised by a procedure — rolls back only the statement that caused it, and the transaction stays open. SET XACT_ABORT ONSET XACT_ABORT ON changes that: the entire transaction is terminated and rolled back. For any procedure that performs a multi-statement unit of work, that is the setting that guarantees the unit is all or nothing.

-- The safe default for application procedures. Three properties worth knowing:
--   XACT_ABORT is a session setting and stays ON for the connection.
--   It applies to run-time errors; it does not affect compile errors.
--   THROW and RAISERROR honour it, unlike the older severity 10 form.
-- SET XACT_ABORT ON;
GO

-- XACT_STATE() reports whether the transaction can still be committed.
--   1 = the transaction is active and can be committed
--   0 = no transaction, or already rolled back
--  -1 = doomed: the transaction is uncommittable and must be rolled back
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_AdjustCredit
    @CustomerID int,
    @NewLimit   decimal(12,2)
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;
    BEGIN TRY
        BEGIN TRANSACTION;

        UPDATE dbo.Customer
        SET    CreditLimit = @NewLimit
        WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;

        INSERT dbo.CustomerCreditChange (CustomerID, OldLimit, NewLimit, ChangedAt)
        SELECT CustomerID, CreditLimit, @NewLimit, SYSDATETIME()
        FROM   dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;

        COMMIT TRANSACTION;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        -- With XACT_ABORT ON the state is -1 on most run-time errors, and the
        -- ROLLBACK below is unconditional and always the right call.
        IF XACT_STATE() <> 0
            ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
        THROW;
    END CATCH
END
GO

-- Savepoints: partial rollback inside a still-open transaction. The named
-- ROLLBACK is only legal because @@TRANCOUNT is still above zero.
BEGIN TRY
    BEGIN TRANSACTION;

    UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
    SET    Status = 'N'
    WHERE SalesOrderID = @SalesOrderID;

    SAVE TRANSACTION BeforeLines;

    INSERT dbo.SalesOrderLine (SalesOrderID, LineNo, ProductID, Quantity, UnitPrice)
    VALUES (@SalesOrderID, 1, @ProductID, @Quantity, @UnitPrice);

    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    IF XACT_STATE() <> 0 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
    THROW;
END CATCH
GO

Why TH over RAISERRORRAISERROR. TH re-raises the original error with its number, severity, state and procedure context intact, terminates the batch, and obeys XACT_ABORTXACT_ABORT. RAIS with severity 16 does not terminate the batch, does not roll back, and rewrites the message. In a CA block, TH with no parameters is the correct way to propagate the original error; RAIS there produces a message that looks similar and carries none of the diagnostic value. See section 1.6 for the full error-handling pattern.

Savepoints are for a genuine partial-failure case, and they are less common than they look. A savepoint does not make the retained work safe to commit if the rolled-back part was required for correctness, and a procedure that leaves a transaction open on the way out is a procedure that will eventually be blamed for blocking. The honest alternative for most designs is: commit the header, then process the lines in a separate transaction, and accept that the order can briefly exist without its lines. Which of those two is right depends on whether an order without lines is a valid state, and that is a business decision, not a database one.

Two failure modes close this chapter. A procedure that returns without committing or rolling back leaves a transaction open in the caller’s connection, and the locks stay until the connection is reset — the cause of many "phantom locks" where nothing appears to be running. A T block with no CA swallows the error and, with XACT_ABORT ONXACT_ABORT ON, leaves the transaction already rolled back and the code continuing as if it had succeeded. Always pair them.

Exercises 7

  1. Open two sessions. In the first, BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTION and UPD a single dbo.Sal row. In the second, UPD the same row, and record the wait type and duration from sys.dm_exec_requestssys.dm_exec_requests. Then ROLL the first session and confirm the second completes.
  2. Reproduce a deadlock: two sessions updating dbo.Cu then dbo.Sal in opposite orders, inside explicit transactions. Capture the error 1205 message and read the deadlock graph from the SQL Server error log. Repeat with both sessions using the same order and confirm it no longer occurs.
  3. On a copy of the database, set READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ONREAD_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON with tables present and record the error. Drop the tables, set the option, recreate them, and verify the difference in behaviour between the lock-based and version-based read committed with a blocking test from exercise 1.
  4. Build a REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ transaction that reads a key range on a unique index, and show that a concurrent insert of a matching key blocks. Then show that the same query on a non-unique index does not block, and explain the difference in terms of key-range locking.
  5. Run an unindexed UPD over a large table in one session while sampling sys.dm_t from another. Show the transition from row locks to a single table-level exclusive lock, and record the LOCK_ and lock from the escalation Extended Events event.
  6. Write a procedure with XACT_ABORT ONXACT_ABORT ON that performs three updates and deliberately raises an error on the third. Verify that none of the three persist. Then remove the SET XACT_ABORT ONSET XACT_ABORT ON line and show which changes remain, and explain the difference using XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE() and @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT values printed from the CA block.
  7. Add a savepoint before the line-insert step of the order procedure from 7.11, and force a failure at that step. Verify with a SEL that the header update is retained and the line insert is not, and that the transaction was still open when the savepoint rollback ran.
  8. Set LOCK_TIMEOUT 3000LOCK_TIMEOUT 3000 and reproduce a blocking scenario, then repeat the same test with WITH (NOWAIT)WITH (NOWAIT). Record the error number in each case and confirm that both leave the transaction requiring an explicit rollback.

8 · Stored Procedures, Functions, Views and Triggers

8.1 Stored procedures: creating and executing modules8.2 sp_executesql, parameters and output values8.3 Functions: scalar, inline and multi-statement8.4 Views: ordinary, schemabound and indexed8.5 Triggers: AFTER, INSTEAD OF and the pseudo-tables8.6 OUTPUT, MERGE and module versioning

Everything in this chapter is a module: a named, schema-qualified batch of T-SQL stored in the database, compiled once, and executed by name. The three families of module — procedures, functions and views — differ in what they may return and what they may change; triggers differ again in that they are not invoked directly at all. The chapter works through the creation and execution of each, then through the mechanisms that make them behave in ways their authors do not always intend: the plan cache, the pseudo-tables, the ownership chain, and the fixed cardinality estimate the optimiser applies to a table variable.

8.1 Stored procedures: creating and executing modules

A stored procedure is a batch with a name and a parameter list. The name is what makes it a module: it appears in the plan cache under that name, it participates in ownership chains, it can be granted, encrypted, signed and altered independently of any caller, and it separates compilation from execution.

-- Applies to: SQL Server 2016 SP1 and later. CREATE OR ALTER creates the
-- object the first time and replaces its definition thereafter, in one
-- idempotent statement. GO is required: CREATE PROCEDURE must be the
-- first statement in a batch.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary
    @CustomerID  int,
    @FromDate    date         = NULL,
    @ThroughDate date         = NULL,
    @IncludeVoid bit          = 0,
    @RowCount    int          OUTPUT,
    @FullName    nvarchar(200) OUTPUT
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;

    IF @FromDate IS NOT NULL AND @ThroughDate IS NOT NULL
       AND @FromDate > @ThroughDate
    THROW 50001, 'usp_OrderSummary: @FromDate is after @ThroughDate', 1;

    SELECT @FullName = CONCAT(c.FirstName, ' ', c.LastName)
    FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
    WHERE  c.CustomerID = @CustomerID;

    SELECT @RowCount = COUNT(*)
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE  CustomerID = @CustomerID
      AND  (@FromDate    IS NULL OR OrderDate >= @FromDate)
      AND  (@ThroughDate IS NULL OR OrderDate < @ThroughDate)
      AND  (@IncludeVoid = 1 OR StatusCode <> 9);

    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.StatusCode
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
    WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
      AND  (@FromDate    IS NULL OR o.OrderDate >= @FromDate)
      AND  (@ThroughDate IS NULL OR o.OrderDate < @ThroughDate)
    ORDER BY o.OrderDate, o.SalesOrderID;
END;
GO

-- Execution. Positional and named forms are both valid, and a parameter
-- with a default may be omitted only from the right onwards.
EXEC dbo.usp_OrderSummary 1001;
EXEC dbo.usp_OrderSummary 1001, '2026-01-01', '2026-04-01', 1;
EXEC dbo.usp_OrderSummary @CustomerID = 1001, @IncludeVoid = 1;
EXECUTE dbo.usp_OrderSummary @CustomerID = 1001;
GO
ParameterTypeDirectionDefaultNotes
@CustomerID@CustomerIDiInputNoneNo default means the caller must supply it or the call fails with error 8145
@FromDate@FromDatedaInputNUA NU default makes the parameter optional; the OR @p IS NULLOR @p IS NULL idiom in the body defeats parameter sniffing
@ThroughDate@ThroughDatedaInputNUHalf-open interval: >= @FromDate AND < @ThroughDate>= @FromDate AND < @ThroughDate
@IncludeVoid@IncludeVoidbInput00Not b, which cannot take NU; use tin or i for a tri-state flag
@RowCount@RowCountiOutput—Not affected by SET NOCOUNT ONSET NOCOUNT ON
@FullName@FullNamenvarchar(200)nvarchar(200)Output—Left NU if the customer does not exist; the caller must check
Return valueiReturn00Assigned with EXEC @rc = procEXEC @rc = proc; not an error mechanism
-- The settings that are captured when a module is created, not when it
-- runs. ANSI_NULLS and QUOTED_IDENTIFIER are stored with the module;
-- ARITHABORT, CONCAT_NULL_YIELDS_NULL and NUMERIC_ROUNDABORT are taken
-- from the session that EXECUTES it, which is why a module behaves
-- differently under a client that sets different options.
GO
SET ANSI_NULLS ON;
SET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ON;
SET ARITHABORT ON;
SET CONCAT_NULL_YIELDS_NULL ON;
SET NUMERIC_ROUNDABORT OFF;
GO

-- Read them back for a specific module, from the catalog rather than
-- from the session.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(120), s.name)  AS ModuleName,
       s.uses_ansi_nulls, s.uses_quoted_identifier,
       s.uses_native_compilation,
       s.is_recompiled, s.is_encrypted,
       s.create_date, s.modify_date
FROM   sys.sql_modules AS s
JOIN   sys.objects     AS o ON o.object_id = s.object_id
WHERE o.name = 'usp_OrderSummary';
GO

-- The parameter metadata, including the return value at parameter_id 0.
SELECT p.parameter_id, p.name AS ParameterName,
       TYPE_NAME(p.system_type_id) AS TypeName,
       p.max_length, p.precision, p.scale,
       p.is_output
FROM   sys.parameters AS p
WHERE p.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_OrderSummary')
ORDER BY p.parameter_id;   -- 0 is the return value
GO

-- The shape of the first result set, without executing the module for
-- real. Available from SQL Server 2012.
--
-- EXEC sys.sp_describe_first_result_set N'EXEC dbo.usp_OrderSummary 1001', N'#d', 0;
-- SELECT * FROM #d;
GO
-- Result sets are positional. A procedure that returns three SELECTs must
-- have all three consumed before the caller sees the last, and a caller
-- that reads only the first has left two unread. This is why a procedure
-- that returns a result set should return exactly one.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_ThreeResults
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
