    SELECT 1 AS WhichResultSet;
    SELECT 2 AS WhichResultSet;
    SELECT 3 AS WhichResultSet;
END;
GO

-- The one way to capture all of them without a cursor, and the reason a
-- procedure that returns more than one set is awkward to call.
GO
CREATE TABLE #AllResults (Seq int IDENTITY(1,1), WhichResultSet int);

INSERT INTO #AllResults (WhichResultSet) EXEC dbo.usp_ThreeResults;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS RowsCaptured FROM #AllResults;   -- 1, not 3: only the first set is captured
GO

-- The first-statement rule, restated because it is the most common
-- script failure. Msg 111 for CREATE PROCEDURE, and Msg 156 for a
-- function, a view and a trigger.
GO
-- SET NOCOUNT ON; CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_X AS SELECT 1;
--                      ^ fails; the SET must be inside the procedure body
GO

-- Granting execution. EXECUTE on a module is a distinct permission from
-- any permission on the tables it touches; see 9.3 for the ownership chain
-- that makes it sufficient.
GRANT EXECUTE ON dbo.usp_OrderSummary TO [app_ordering];
DENY  EXECUTE ON dbo.usp_OrderSummary TO [app_reporting];
GO
Module optionEffectCost and caveat
WITH RECOMPILEWITH RECOMPILEThe plan is discarded when the procedure exits and is rebuilt on every executionRemoves parameter-sniffing artefacts and adapts to a fresh histogram; costs one compile per call, which is usually not worth it and is harmful under high concurrency
WITH ENCRYPTIONWITH ENCRYPTIONThe source text is stored obfuscated in sys.sql and is not returned by sp_he or OBJECT_DProtects intellectual property, not secrets. Not a security control, and it cannot be removed without replacing the module
WITH EXECUTE AS OWNERWITH EXECUTE AS OWNERThe procedure always runs with the permissions of its owner, regardless of the callerBreaks the ownership chain deliberately; the standard way to grant indirect access to a table without granting it directly. See section 9.3
WITH NATIVE_COMPILATIONWITH NATIVE_COMPILATIONCompiles to machine code for use by memory-optimised tablesThe module must be memory-optimised; a subset of T-SQL is supported, with no T/CA and no TH
No WI clauseDefault: the plan is cached and reusedThe right default. A bare CREATE PROCEDURECREATE PROCEDURE is deprecated from SQL Server 2016 SP1; use CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER. See 8.2

Caveat. WITH ENCRYPTIONWITH ENCRYPTION is not encryption. The definition is stored in a form that the server can read and the tools cannot display; the key material is the server binary. Anyone who can open a trace, read the data files offline or restore the database elsewhere can defeat it. It also cannot be removed without replacing the module, because ALTER PROCEDUREALTER PROCEDURE cannot clear it. Use a source-control deployment pipeline for the real requirement. Note also that sp_describe_fi and sp_describe_unde cannot describe an encrypted module, which means an encrypted module also removes the metadata a client driver uses to build a strongly-typed data access layer.

-- Modules and the plan cache. An ad-hoc batch is cached under its literal
-- text, so a different literal is a different cache entry and a different
-- plan. A module is cached under its name, so all callers share one entry
-- and one plan shape.
GO
SELECT s.type_desc, s.creation_time, s.last_execution_time,
       s.execution_count, s.size_in_bytes, s.cached_plan_handle
FROM   sys.dm_exec_cached_plans AS p
JOIN   sys.dm_exec_query_stats  AS s
       ON s.cached_plan_handle = p.plan_handle
WHERE s.objectid = OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_OrderSummary')
ORDER BY s.creation_time;
GO

-- Ad-hoc SQL is identifiable by objectid = 0. The count is the measure
-- of how much cache space ad-hoc text is consuming, and it grows without
-- bound when a client concatenates literals.
SELECT type_desc, COUNT(*) AS CacheEntries,
       SUM(size_in_bytes) / 1024 AS Kilobytes,
       SUM(execution_count) AS Executions
FROM   sys.dm_exec_cached_plans
GROUP BY type_desc
ORDER BY CacheEntries DESC;
GO

-- Which literals are in there. The text of an ad-hoc entry is the
-- statement exactly as it was sent, concatenated values included.
GO
SELECT TOP (20) qs.execution_count,
       CONVERT(nvarchar(200), qs.text) AS StatementText
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
WHERE qs.objectid = 0
ORDER BY qs.execution_count DESC;
GO

The sys.dm_exec_ DMV is instance-wide and reflects what is cached right now. A module that has never been executed has no row, and an evicted module reappears on next execution with a new creation_timecreation_time. The distinction between Ad HocAd Hoc and Prep in type is the distinction the optimiser itself makes: ad-hoc batches skip name resolution and are bound to a single result shape, which is cheaper per execution and which is why sp_exe with parameters still compiles once and reuses.

-- WITH RECOMPILE at the statement level, which is the form to reach for when
-- the parameter value is what defeats the cached plan rather than the
-- module itself. Also OPTIMIZE FOR UNKNOWN, which forces the optimiser to
-- use an average density rather than the sniffed value.
GO
SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
OPTION (RECOMPILE);

SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@CustomerID UNKNOWN));
GO

-- The deprecation. In SQL Server 2016 SP1, bare CREATE PROCEDURE (and
-- CREATE FUNCTION, VIEW, TRIGGER, RULE, DEFAULT) begin to emit:
--   Msg 17830: CREATE PROCEDURE statement will be removed in a future
--   version of SQL Server. Replace with CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE.
-- It still executes. The guidance is to migrate every script to CREATE OR
-- ALTER, which is also the only form that is idempotent.
GO
CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_LegacyOnly AS
SELECT 1 AS StillWorks;
GO
DROP PROCEDURE dbo.usp_LegacyOnly;
GO

-- The conditional form this replaces, and why it is worse: it needs a
-- batch boundary in each branch, it is two parses, and the existence
-- check races with any concurrent deployment.
GO
-- IF OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_X', 'P') IS NULL
--     EXEC('CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_X AS SELECT 1');
-- ELSE
--     EXEC('ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_X AS SELECT 2');
GO

Applies to. Every version from SQL Server 2005 onward, with no change in 2016, 2017, 2019, 2022 or 2025. The s and dm_ schema for these DMVs has been stable for that entire period. What did change is the surface around it: sys.dm_exec_qu, new in SQL Server 2019, exposes the last known actual plan for any statement including ad-hoc ones without OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE), and sp_describe_fi is the supported route to a client-side type map for a module rather than a hand-maintained one.

8.2 sp_executesql, parameters and output values

Dynamic SQL executed through EX is a string, so the values inside it must be text. Executed through sp_exe, the statement is still a string but its values are passed out of band as typed parameters, which is what makes the statement both safe and cacheable. Choosing the wrong one is the most consequential single decision in this chapter.

-- WRONG: string-built EXEC. The literal is substituted, so the statement
-- text changes with every value, every text is a separate cache entry, and
-- a quote in the value ends the literal and starts a syntax error.
DECLARE @CustomerID int = 1001;

EXEC ('SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE CustomerID = ' + CAST(@CustomerID AS varchar(20)) + ';');
GO

-- RIGHT: the values never enter the statement text.
-- @params declares the types the statement sees; the call supplies values.
-- The parameter names in @params must match those in @stmt exactly.
GO
DECLARE @sql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, StatusCode
       FROM dbo.SalesOrder
       WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID
         AND OrderDate  >= @FromDate;';

EXEC sys.sp_executesql
     @stmt        = @sql,
     @params      = N'@CustomerID int, @FromDate date',
     @CustomerID  = @CustomerID,
     @FromDate    = '2026-01-01';
GO
-- The injection failure, reproduced. The value is a valid string in the
-- database and a syntax error when concatenated.
GO
-- Fails: Msg 102, Incorrect syntax near 'DROP'.
-- EXEC ('SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = ''' + 'O''Brien''; DROP TABLE dbo.Customer; --' + '''');
--
-- The equivalent parameterised statement cannot be broken by the value,
-- because the value is data and never part of the parse.
GO
-- DECLARE @Name nvarchar(200) = N'O''Brien''; DROP TABLE dbo.Customer; --';
-- DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(4000) = N'SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = @n';
-- EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql, N'@n nvarchar(200)', @Name;   -- returns 0 rows
GO

-- And the plan-reuse consequence, measured rather than asserted.
-- Two different literals through EXEC produce two ad-hoc cache entries;
-- the same two values through sp_executesql produce one prepared entry.
GO
-- SELECT type_desc, COUNT(*) FROM sys.dm_exec_cached_plans GROUP BY type_desc;
GO
-- Capturing a module's result set. INSERT ... EXEC works only when the
-- module returns exactly one result set, and it is the one form that lets
-- a procedure's rows land in a table. The column list must match the
-- module's select list by position and type; a mismatch is Msg 213,
-- 'Column name or number of supplied values does not match table
-- definition'.
GO
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerExport
(
    CustomerID  int          NOT NULL,
    FullName    nvarchar(300) NOT NULL,
    CountryCode char(2)      NOT NULL
);

INSERT INTO dbo.CustomerExport (CustomerID, FullName, CountryCode)
    EXEC dbo.usp_ExportCustomers @Country = 'DE';
GO

-- Through sp_executesql, which is the form to use when the module name
-- comes from a table rather than from source. See 10.1 for QUOTENAME.
GO
-- DECLARE @Proc sysname = N'dbo.usp_ExportCustomers';
-- EXEC sys.sp_executesql N'EXEC ' + QUOTENAME(@Proc) + ' @Country = @c;',
--                            N'@c char(2)', @Country;
GO

-- The failure mode of a module that is not there, and of one that was
-- renamed after the caller was deployed: Msg 2812, 'Could not find
-- stored procedure', with the object name in the message. A renamed module
-- is a breaking change to every caller that used the old name, including
-- callers in other databases.
GO
-- EXEC dbo.usp_RenamedAway;
GO
PropertyEX with a built stringsp_exe
Injection surfaceEvery value is code; a single quote in a value breaks out of the literalValues are bound as parameters and can never become syntax
Cache keyThe full statement text, so one entry per distinct value setStatement text plus the parameter declarations, so one entry per shape
Plan reuseNone across differing literals; cache pressure grows without boundOne plan, parameterised, reused by every caller with different values
Type fidelityWhatever the concatenation produced: string literals, implicit conversion, truncation riskExplicit @params@params definition; the declared type drives conversion and comparison
Return valueAny result set, mixed with the outer batchAny result set, plus OUT parameters and an integer return code
Result shapeUnknown to the caller at compile time, so the client receives a loosely typed resultInspectable with sp_describe_fi at authoring time

Use EXEC(@sql)EXEC(@sql) only for a statement whose text is fixed at authoring time and whose only variable part is a literal you can prove safe. In practice that means DDL whose object name comes from a closed set, and nothing else. For everything else, sp_executesqlsp_executesql. The cost of sp_exe over a plain EX is one extra round trip within the same connection and a slightly longer parse; both are irrelevant next to a recompile.

-- Output parameters and the return code. The OUT declarations belong in the
-- @params string, and the values are passed after it.
GO
DECLARE @sql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT @cnt = COUNT_BIG(*), @tot = ISNULL(SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice), 0)
       FROM dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
       JOIN dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
       WHERE o.CustomerID = @cid
         AND (@from IS NULL OR o.OrderDate >= @from);';

DECLARE @RowCount int, @Total decimal(19,4);

EXEC sys.sp_executesql
     @stmt   = @sql,
     @params = N'@cnt int OUTPUT, @tot decimal(19,4) OUTPUT, @cid int, @from date',
     @cnt    = @RowCount OUTPUT,
     @tot    = @Total    OUTPUT,
     @cid    = 1001,
     @from   = NULL;

SELECT RowCount = @RowCount, Total = @Total;
GO

-- Identifiers cannot be parameterised at all; they are concatenated,
-- which is why QUOTENAME is used. See 10.1 for the full treatment.
GO
-- DECLARE @Qualified nvarchar(517) = QUOTENAME(@SchemaName) + '.' + QUOTENAME(@TableName);
-- SET @sql = N'SELECT @cnt = COUNT_BIG(*) FROM ' + @Qualified + ' WHERE TotalAmount >= @min;';
--
-- EXEC sys.sp_executesql @sql, N'@cnt bigint OUTPUT, @min decimal(12,2)',
--      @cnt = @RowCount OUTPUT, @min = @MinAmount;
GO
-- The procedure form, for contrast. An OUTPUT parameter is a parameter; the
-- return code is a separate int assigned with EXEC @rc = proc.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_GetCustomer
    @CustomerID int,
    @Name       nvarchar(200) OUTPUT,
    @OrderCount int          OUTPUT
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    SELECT @Name = CONCAT(FirstName, ' ', LastName)
    FROM   dbo.Customer
    WHERE  CustomerID = @CustomerID;

    IF @@ROWCOUNT = 0
    BEGIN
        SET @OrderCount = 0;
        RETURN -1;              -- the return code, not an error
    END

    SELECT @OrderCount = COUNT_BIG(*)
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE  CustomerID = @CustomerID;

    RETURN 0;
END;
GO

-- Caller side.
DECLARE @Name nvarchar(200), @Orders int, @rc int;
EXEC @rc = dbo.usp_GetCustomer 1001, @Name OUTPUT, @Orders OUTPUT;
SELECT ReturnCode = @rc, Name = @Name, Orders = @Orders;
GO

Caveat. sp_exe parameter declarations must match the variables used in the statement exactly in name, and the types in @params@params are the types the statement sees. A mismatch is a run-time error inside the dynamic batch, and because the batch runs in its own scope the error surfaces with no reference to the calling line. The OUT keyword belongs in @params@params, not in the statement text. A statement issued through sp_exe cannot modify the caller’s local variables except through OUT parameters, and a TRUNCATE TABLETRUNCATE TABLE or any CRE/DR cannot have any part of it parameterised. Also note that sp_exe runs the statement in a new scope, so a CREATE TABLE #tCREATE TABLE #t inside it is destroyed when the statement finishes and a table variable declared inside it is unreachable from the caller.

8.3 Functions: scalar, inline and multi-statement

Three shapes of user-defined function exist in T-SQL, and the difference between them is a difference in how the optimiser can see inside. An inline table-valued function is a macro: the optimiser pastes its body into the calling query and optimises the result. A scalar function is a black box that, until SQL Server 2019, was invoked once per output row. A multi-statement table-valued function is a black box that builds a result set, and is the slowest of the three.

-- Scalar function. Returns exactly one value, and may not contain a SELECT
-- that returns rows. WITH SCHEMABINDING is required for the SQL Server
-- 2019 scalar inlining feature, and it is best practice regardless, because
-- it makes the function deterministic and bindable to a computed column or
-- an indexed view.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal (@CustomerID int)
RETURNS decimal(19,4)
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
BEGIN
    DECLARE @Total decimal(19,4) =
    (
        SELECT SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice)
        FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
        JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
        WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
    );
    RETURN @Total;
END;
GO

-- Called in a projection, it runs once per output row.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.LastName,
       dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal(c.CustomerID) AS OrderTotal
FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
WHERE  c.Country = 'DE'
ORDER BY OrderTotal DESC;
GO

-- The same number as a derived table, which the optimiser can join to.
-- On a database below compatibility level 150 this is strictly faster;
-- on 2019 and later with inlining active, the two are equivalent.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.LastName, t.OrderTotal
FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
INNER JOIN (
        SELECT o.CustomerID, SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice) AS OrderTotal
        FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
        JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
        GROUP BY o.CustomerID
     ) AS t ON t.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
WHERE  c.Country = 'DE';
GO

Caveat. The scalar-function penalty is structural, not incidental. Before SQL Server 2019 the optimiser treats a scalar UDF as opaque: it cannot push a predicate into it, cannot reorder its work, and the documented behaviour was that a scalar UDF in the select list is evaluated once for every row of the input, so a WH on its result cannot become a seek. On a 100,000-row input with an internal join over three tables, that is 100,000 executions of the join. Moving the expression into a derived table and joining to it is the pre-2019 fix, and it remains the fix on any database that has not been moved to compatibility level 150.

-- New in SQL Server 2019: scalar UDF inlining. When the function is schemabound
-- and satisfies the inlining conditions, the optimiser expands it into an
-- expression tree and it becomes a normal inlined expression that the join
-- and filter machinery can act on.
--
-- Conditions that must ALL hold for inlining:
--   WITH SCHEMABINDING
--   no aggregates, no GROUP BY, no window functions
--   no time-dependent functions (GETDATE, SYSDATETIME, NEWID)
--   no recursive CTE, no table variables, no EXECUTE of dynamic SQL
--   no TRY/CATCH, no non-inlineable table-valued functions
--   the database compatibility level is 150 or higher
SELECT c.CustomerID,
       dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal(c.CustomerID) AS OrderTotal
FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
WHERE  dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal(c.CustomerID) > 10000
ORDER BY OrderTotal DESC
OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO

-- The function that CANNOT be inlined, and the reason is visible in the
-- plan: it contains an aggregate, so it becomes a UDF-evaluate operator
-- rather than being expanded. This is the shape most often written by
-- habit and it is the shape that performs badly.
GO
-- CREATE FUNCTION dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal_Aggregate (@CustomerID int)
-- RETURNS decimal(19,4) WITH SCHEMABINDING AS
-- BEGIN
--     DECLARE @T decimal(19,4) = (SELECT SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice)
--                                FROM dbo.SalesOrder o JOIN dbo.SalesOrderLine l
--                                  ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
--                               WHERE o.CustomerID = @CustomerID);
--     RETURN @T;   -- SUM is not inlineable: the function is called per row
-- END;
GO

-- Verify inlining actually happened. The function is expanded when the
-- plan contains the subexpressions inline rather than a UDF-evaluate node.
SELECT TRY_CAST(TRY_CONVERT(xml, qp.query_plan) AS Plan
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) AS qp
WHERE qs.text LIKE '%fn_CustomerOrderTotal%';
GO
-- Inline table-valued function (iTVF). The body is a single RETURN with a
-- query. The optimiser substitutes it, so the join order, the index
-- choices and the predicate pushdown all apply to it as if it had been
-- written inline. This is the form to prefer for all set-returning
-- functions.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION dbo.fn_OrdersForCustomer (@CustomerID int)
RETURNS TABLE
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
RETURN
(
    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.StatusCode,
           l.ProductID, l.Quantity, l.UnitPrice,
           l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice AS LineTotal
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
    WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
);
GO

-- Used like a table. The optimiser still sees the base tables, so a
-- predicate on the outer query is pushed into the function body.
SELECT SUM(f.LineTotal) AS Total
FROM   dbo.fn_OrdersForCustomer(1001) AS f WHERE f.StatusCode <> 9;
GO

-- The schema-bound metadata for an inline function, which is what the
-- optimiser resolves at bind time and what a schema change must respect.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(120), s.name) AS SchemaName,
       CONVERT(varchar(120), o.name) AS ObjectName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  o.type_desc) AS TypeDesc
FROM       sys.objects AS o
INNER JOIN sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = o.schema_id
WHERE o.type IN ('FN', 'IF', 'TF', 'FS', 'FT')
  AND o.is_ms_shipped = 0
ORDER BY TypeDesc, ObjectName;
GO
-- The third option, and the one to prefer when the function computes a value
-- from the current row: a PERSISTED computed column. It is stored, it is
-- always correct, it is indexable, and it costs nothing at query time. It
-- requires a deterministic expression, which is exactly what
-- WITH SCHEMABINDING on a function establishes.
GO
-- ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine
--     ADD LineTotal AS (Quantity * UnitPrice) PERSISTED;
-- CREATE INDEX IX_SalesOrderLine_LineTotal ON dbo.SalesOrderLine (LineTotal);
GO

-- The determinism question, asked of the catalog rather than guessed at. A
-- function is deterministic only if every function it calls is, and
-- ISNUMERIC and ISDATE are explicitly not.
GO
-- SELECT OBJECTPROPERTY(OBJECT_ID('dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal'), 'IsDeterministic');
-- SELECT OBJECTPROPERTY(OBJECT_ID('dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal'), 'IsSchemaBound');
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), s.name)                              AS ModuleName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  s.type_desc)                         AS TypeDesc,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(s.object_id, 'IsDeterministic')   AS IsDeterministic,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(s.object_id, 'IsSchemaBound')      AS IsSchemaBound,
       OBJECTPROPERTY(s.object_id, 'IsInlineable')        AS IsInlineable
FROM   sys.sql_modules AS s
WHERE s.is_ms_shipped = 0
ORDER BY s.name;
GO
-- Multi-statement table-valued function (mTVF). The body may declare
-- variables, branch, and accumulate into a table variable. It is NOT
-- inlined: the optimiser sees only the declaration of the return columns
-- and applies a fixed cardinality estimate, so the join order above the
-- function is chosen without knowing how many rows will come out of it.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION dbo.fn_OrdersWithTotals (@CustomerID int)
RETURNS @Result TABLE
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
BEGIN
    INSERT @Result (SalesOrderID, OrderDate, LineCount, OrderTotal)
    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate,
           COUNT_BIG(*), SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice)
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
    WHERE  o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
    GROUP BY o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate;

    -- Procedural logic on the accumulated set. This is what an mTVF is
    -- for, and it is the only thing an iTVF cannot express.
    UPDATE @Result
    SET    LineCount = LineCount * 2
    WHERE LineCount > 5;

    DELETE @Result
    WHERE OrderTotal IS NULL;

    RETURN;
END;
GO

-- Interleaved execution, new in SQL Server 2019, recovers part of what the
-- fixed estimate costs: when an mTVF is the inner side of a join, the
-- optimiser defers the join until it knows the function's real row
-- count. It does not make the function visible, and it adds per-row cost.
SELECT SUM(f.OrderTotal) AS Total
FROM   dbo.fn_OrdersWithTotals(1001) AS f
INNER JOIN dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = 1001
-- OPTION (DISABLE_PARALLEL_PLAN_PREFERENCE) is the only hint that affects
-- this feature, and it turns interleaved execution OFF.
OPTION (DISABLE_PARALLEL_PLAN_PREFERENCE OFF);
GO

Requirement. A function, of any of the three kinds, cannot modify the database. INS, UPD and DEL are permitted only against a table variable that is local to the function; any write to a permanent table, any DDL, and any call to a procedure that writes, is rejected at compile time. A function also cannot contain GET and friends if it is to be inlined, and cannot use T/CA or THROWTHROW. There is no OUT clause on a function. A user-defined function also cannot be called from a WH clause of a DEL that modifies a table the function reads, and cannot be the target of a permission check that would require a schema modification. If you need to write, the construct is a procedure.

PropertyInline TVFMulti-statement TVFScalar UDF
Return typeTable, declared RETURNS TABLERETURNS TABLETable, declared RETURNS @t TABLERETURNS @t TABLEOne value of a scalar type
BodyOne RETURN (SELECT ...)RETURN (SELECT ...)Arbitrary statements ending in RETArbitrary statements ending in a single RET
Visible to the optimiserYes, fully inlinedReturn columns onlyReturn type only, unless inlined (2019+)
Cardinality estimateReal, from the underlying tablesFixed and low, from the declared shapeFixed at one row per input row
Predicate pushdownYesNoNo, unless inlined
ParallelismYesYes, from 2019 with interleaved executionYes, when inlined

Choose by shape, not by habit. If the result is a set of rows, write an inline table-valued function and nothing else. If it is a single number computed from a large table for one argument, a scalar function with SCHEMA on a 2019-or-later database is fine and is now inlined; verify with the plan. If it is a single number computed from the current row, use a computed column instead — it is stored, always correct, and costs nothing at query time. Multi-statement table-valued functions exist for procedural logic, not for set logic, and every use of one is a decision to work without optimiser information.

8.4 Views: ordinary, schemabound and indexed

An ordinary view stores nothing. It is a named query that the optimiser expands into the calling statement before optimisation, so a predicate against the view can become a predicate against a base table. Two modifiers change that: WITH SCHEMABINDINGWITH SCHEMABINDING makes the view a promise about the shape of its dependencies, and a clustered index on the view makes it materialised data that must be maintained on every write.

-- Ordinary view. Nothing is stored; this is a query with a name.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_OpenOrders
AS
    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.CustomerID, o.OrderDate,
           SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice) AS OrderTotal
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
    WHERE  o.StatusCode <> 9
    GROUP BY o.SalesOrderID, o.CustomerID, o.OrderDate;
GO

-- The predicate is on a computed column, which the optimiser cannot
-- push into the aggregate, so the whole grouping is computed and then
-- the top 20 taken. A view over a GROUP BY is a common and silent loss.
SELECT TOP (20) SUM(v.OrderTotal) AS SumOfTop20
FROM   dbo.vw_OpenOrders AS v
ORDER BY v.OrderTotal DESC;
GO

-- The same query, written so the filter can be applied before the
-- aggregation. This is why a view is a query and not a contract.
SELECT SUM(x.OrderTotal) AS SumOfTop20
FROM   (SELECT TOP (20)
                    SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice) AS OrderTotal
                FROM dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
                JOIN dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
                WHERE o.StatusCode <> 9
                GROUP BY o.SalesOrderID
                ORDER BY SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice) DESC) AS x;
GO
-- WITH SCHEMABINDING: the view cannot be created unless every object it
-- references already exists, and afterwards you cannot ALTER or DROP
-- those objects, or change a column, without dropping the view first.
-- Msg 4282: 'Schema-bound objects cannot refer to non-schema-bound objects.'
-- It is a deliberate coupling, and it is worth declaring deliberately.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_OrderLineDetail
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
    SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.CustomerID,
           l.ProductID, l.Quantity, l.UnitPrice,
           l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice AS LineTotal
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID;
GO

-- WITH CHECK OPTION: a row may not be inserted or updated to a value the
-- view's WHERE clause would filter out, which is what makes the view's row
-- set a real invariant rather than a filter. It also makes the view updatable.
-- Without it, an UPDATE through the view can leave a row the view hides.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_ActiveCustomer
AS
    SELECT CustomerID, FirstName, LastName, IsActive
    FROM   dbo.Customer
    WHERE  IsActive = 1
WITH CHECK OPTION;
GO

-- What the view actually expanded to: sp_helptext, or the module source.
-- A view is a module, so it appears in sys.sql_modules like a procedure.
GO
-- EXEC sys.sp_helptext N'dbo.vw_OrderLineDetail';
GO
SELECT CONVERT(nvarchar(max), m.definition) AS Definition
FROM   sys.sql_modules AS m
WHERE m.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.vw_OrderLineDetail');
GO
-- Indexed view. Two requirements beyond an ordinary view:
--   1. The view must be created WITH SCHEMABINDING.
--   2. A UNIQUE CLUSTERED index must be created on it.
-- The first UNIQUE CLUSTERED index is what makes it an indexed view; before
-- that it is an unindexed view and nothing is stored.
--
-- Other requirements: the SET options must be the safe ones when the view
-- and the index are created, all expressions must be deterministic, and
-- imprecise columns are permitted only if PERSISTED is not used with them.
GO
SET ANSI_NULLS ON;   SET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ON;
SET ARITHABORT ON;    SET CONCAT_NULL_YIELDS_NULL ON;
SET NUMERIC_ROUNDABORT OFF;
GO

CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
    SELECT o.CustomerID,
           SUM(l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice) AS CustomerTotal,
           COUNT_BIG(*)                       AS LineCount
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
    GROUP BY o.CustomerID;
GO

CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX IX_vw_SalesOrderByCustomer
    ON dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer (CustomerID);
GO

-- Confirm it is indexed, and that the optimiser now prefers it.
SELECT v.name AS ViewName, i.name AS IndexName, i.type_desc
FROM   sys.views AS v
JOIN   sys.indexes AS i ON i.object_id = v.object_id
WHERE v.name = 'vw_SalesOrderByCustomer';
GO

SELECT CustomerID, CustomerTotal
FROM   dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer
WHERE CustomerID = 1001
OPTION (RECOMPILE);   -- makes the optimiser consider the view index
GO

-- Disable and re-enable, without dropping. A disabled index is not
-- maintained and the view behaves as an ordinary view again.
GO
ALTER INDEX IX_vw_SalesOrderByCustomer ON dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer DISABLE;
ALTER INDEX IX_vw_SalesOrderByCustomer ON dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer REBUILD;
GO
-- The write-path cost, measured. This is the check to run before creating an
-- indexed view and the check to run after, because the cost lands on
-- every INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE against every base table in the view.
GO
-- WITH THE INDEX DISABLED
-- ALTER INDEX IX_vw_SalesOrderByCustomer ON dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer DISABLE;
-- SET STATISTICS IO ON;
-- UPDATE TOP (10000) dbo.SalesOrderLine SET Quantity = Quantity WHERE SalesOrderID > 0;
--
-- WITH THE INDEX ENABLED
-- ALTER INDEX IX_vw_SalesOrderByCustomer ON dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer REBUILD;
-- UPDATE TOP (10000) dbo.SalesOrderLine SET Quantity = Quantity WHERE SalesOrderID > 0;
--
-- Compare the logical reads and the elapsed time. Then reverse the test
-- for the read path: the same SELECT against the view with and without.
GO

-- The read path, and the reason the view index is often not used: the
-- optimiser prefers the base tables unless the view's aggregation
-- provably satisfies the predicate. A range predicate on the aggregated
-- column is the shape that makes the difference visible.
SELECT CustomerID, CustomerTotal
FROM   dbo.vw_SalesOrderByCustomer
WHERE CustomerTotal > 10000
OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO

-- The consequences of schema binding on the base tables. This fails:
--   Msg 4753 / 4756, because the column is referenced by a schemabound object.
GO
-- ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrderLine ALTER COLUMN UnitPrice decimal(19,4) NOT NULL;
--
-- The only remedy is to drop the dependent view, alter, recreate the view,
-- and recreate its index. That sequence is the real cost of schema
-- binding, and the reason to enumerate dependents before a deployment
-- rather than discovering them from an error.
GO
-- View metadata: what a view depends on, and whether it is indexed. This is
-- the check to run before a schema change, because a schemabound or indexed
-- view turns an ordinary ALTER into a failing one.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), v.name)                        AS ViewName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  v.is_indexed_view)           AS IsIndexedView,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  v.is_schemabound_view)        AS IsSchemaboundView,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  v.is_updatable)               AS IsUpdatable,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  v.has_check_option)          AS HasCheckOption,
       CONVERT(varchar(128), pr.name)                       AS OwnerName
FROM       sys.views AS v
LEFT JOIN sys.objects      AS o  ON o.object_id    = v.object_id
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals AS pr ON pr.principal_id = o.principal_id
ORDER BY ViewName;
GO

-- What each view references, and what references each view. Both
-- directions are needed: the first to know whether an ALTER is legal, the
-- second to know who breaks if the view is dropped.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), referenced_schema_name + '.' + referenced_entity_name) AS ReferencedObject,
       CONVERT(varchar(128), referencing_schema_name + '.' + referencing_entity_name) AS ReferencingObject
FROM   sys.sql_expression_dependencies
WHERE CONVERT(varchar(128), referenced_schema_name + '.' + referenced_entity_name) = 'dbo.SalesOrder'
   OR CONVERT(varchar(128), referencing_schema_name + '.' + referencing_entity_name) = 'dbo.vw_OpenOrders'
ORDER BY ReferencedObject, ReferencingObject;
GO

Caveat. An indexed view is maintained automatically, on every INS, UPD and DEL against any base table in its definition, and that maintenance is a real cost on the write path. It also constrains the base tables: a column referenced by the view cannot be ALTERALTERed or dropped, and with SCHEMA the base table itself cannot be dropped. Before creating one, measure the write rate against the read rate. A view index is a declaration that reads dominate; on a write-heavy table it makes every write slower in exchange for a benefit nobody uses. The optimiser also only chooses the view index when the predicate is provably selective, which frequently requires OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) and is sometimes not achieved at all.

Applies to. All editions. The requirements listed above are unchanged since SQL Server 2012. New in SQL Server 2016 SP1: TRUST is no longer required for a function that references an indexed view or an indexed computed column, which removed a long-standing obstacle. The NOEX table hint is irrelevant on SQL Server where indexed views participate in normal optimisation; it exists for the legacy SET NOEXECSET NOEXEC and SET FORCEPLANSET FORCEPLAN era and is ignored.

8.5 Triggers: AFTER, INSTEAD OF and the pseudo-tables

A DML trigger fires once per statement, not once per row. Everything about triggers follows from that single fact. The inse and del pseudo-tables contain the whole statement’s effect, so the trigger body is a set-based statement, and the recursive pattern that a row-oriented trigger would use is a performance bug rather than a requirement.

-- AFTER (the default). The statement runs first, all constraints are checked,
-- the rows are written, and only then does the trigger fire. 'inserted'
-- holds the rows the statement added; 'deleted' holds the rows it
-- removed. An UPDATE populates BOTH, and for the same key the row appears
-- in each: inserted has the new values, deleted the old.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER dbo.trg_SalesOrder_Audit
ON   dbo.SalesOrder
AFTER INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    -- Rowcount of the outer statement, captured before anything else
    -- changes @@ROWCOUNT. This is how you detect a multi-row operation.
    DECLARE @Rows int = @@ROWCOUNT;

    -- Set-based. One INSERT handles any number of rows.
    INSERT dbo.SalesOrderAudit
        (SalesOrderID, Action, OldStatus, NewStatus, ChangedAt, ChangedBy)
    SELECT i.SalesOrderID,
           CASE WHEN d.SalesOrderID IS NULL THEN 'INSERT'
                WHEN i.SalesOrderID IS NULL THEN 'DELETE'
                ELSE 'UPDATE' END,
           d.StatusCode, i.StatusCode,
           SYSDATETIME(), ORIGINAL_LOGIN()
    FROM   inserted AS i
    FULL JOIN deleted AS d ON d.SalesOrderID = i.SalesOrderID;

    -- If the statement affected no rows the pseudo-tables are empty and
    -- the INSERT above inserts nothing, so no early exit is required. But
    -- returning early saves a plan lookup and an audit no-op.
    IF @Rows = 0 RETURN;
END;
GO

-- Proof that the body ran once, not once per row. This row count is 1
-- for a 5000-row UPDATE.
--
-- DECLARE @Trace int = 0;
-- UPDATE TOP (5000) dbo.SalesOrder SET StatusCode = StatusCode WHERE StatusCode = 2;
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrderAudit WHERE ChangedAt > DATEADD(MINUTE, -1, SYSDATETIME());
GO
-- The single most common trigger defect: a WHILE loop over inserted. This
-- compiles, runs, produces the correct result, and is one to three orders
-- of magnitude slower than the set-based form. It also holds no lock the
-- set-based form does not, and it fails in a subtle way if the loop body
-- contains another statement that changes @@ROWCOUNT.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER dbo.trg_SalesOrder_Audit_Bad
-- ON dbo.SalesOrder AFTER INSERT AS
-- BEGIN
--     SET NOCOUNT ON;
--     DECLARE @id int, @date date, @st int;
--     DECLARE c CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD FOR
--         SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, StatusCode FROM inserted;
--     OPEN c;
--     FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id, @date, @st;
--     WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
--     BEGIN
--         INSERT dbo.SalesOrderAudit (SalesOrderID, Action, ChangedAt, ChangedBy)
--         VALUES (@id, 'INSERT', SYSDATETIME(), ORIGINAL_LOGIN());
--         FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id, @date, @st;
--     END
--     CLOSE c; DEALLOCATE c;
-- END
GO

-- The same thing with a WHILE over a counter, which is the variant that
-- looks reasonable and is the variant that times out. Note the missing
-- TOP: an UPDATE with no WHERE updates every row, and the trigger then runs
-- the loop once per row in the table.
GO
-- DECLARE @i int = 1;
-- WHILE @i <= (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM inserted)
-- BEGIN
--     INSERT dbo.SalesOrderAudit (SalesOrderID, Action, ChangedAt)
--     SELECT SalesOrderID, 'INSERT', SYSDATETIME() FROM inserted ORDER BY SalesOrderID OFFSET @i - 1 ROWS FETCH NEXT 1 ROW ONLY;
--     SET @i = @i + 1;
-- END
GO

-- A cursor over the pseudo-table, which is the one case where a cursor is
-- not merely slower but wrong: a set-based statement inside a trigger sees
-- the same rows the trigger does, and a cursor sees them one at a time with
-- no isolation guarantee between iterations.
GO
-- Detecting which columns an UPDATE actually touched. UPDATE() returns TRUE
-- when the column appears in the SET list, regardless of whether the value
-- changed; COLUMNS_UPDATED() returns the bitmask of every column named in
-- the SET list, at any nesting level, including columns updated by a
-- trigger deeper in the chain.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER dbo.trg_SalesOrder_RejectVoidClosure
-- ON dbo.SalesOrder AFTER UPDATE AS
-- BEGIN
--     SET NOCOUNT ON;
--     IF NOT UPDATE(StatusCode) RETURN;             -- nothing relevant changed
--     IF COLUMNS_UPDATED() & 0x00000400 = 0 RETURN;   -- TotalAmount bit
--     IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM inserted i JOIN deleted d ON d.SalesOrderID = i.SalesOrderID
--                WHERE d.StatusCode = 9 AND i.TotalAmount <> d.TotalAmount)
--         THROW 50002, 'A void order may not have its total changed.', 1;
-- END
GO

-- The bit positions in sys.columns, which is where the mask is defined.
GO
SELECT c.column_id, c.name AS ColumnName,
       CONVERT(varchar(10), c.column_id) AS BitPosition,
       CONVERT(bigint, POWER(CONVERT(bigint, 2), c.column_id - 1)) AS BitMaskValue
FROM   sys.columns AS c
WHERE c.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder')
ORDER BY c.column_id;
GO
-- INSTEAD OF. The statement is not executed at all; the trigger is, and it is
-- the trigger's job to perform the equivalent work. This exists for two
-- reasons only: a view over multiple base tables that must be updatable,
-- and a view whose structure would otherwise make an UPDATE impossible.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER dbo.trg_CustomerView_InsteadOf
ON dbo.vw_ActiveCustomer
INSTEAD OF INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM deleted)
    BEGIN
        DELETE c
        FROM       dbo.Customer AS c
        INNER JOIN deleted AS d ON d.CustomerID = c.CustomerID;
    END

    IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM inserted)
    BEGIN
        MERGE dbo.Customer AS t
        USING inserted AS s
           ON t.CustomerID = s.CustomerID
        WHEN MATCHED THEN
            UPDATE SET t.FirstName = s.FirstName,
                         t.LastName  = s.LastName,
                         t.IsActive  = 1
        WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET THEN
            INSERT (CustomerID, FirstName, LastName, IsActive)
            VALUES (s.CustomerID, s.FirstName, s.LastName, 1);
    END
END;
GO

-- Nesting and recursion. TRIGGER_NESTLEVEL() is how deep this statement
-- already is; TRIGGER_DEPTH() is the depth within this trigger.
SELECT NestingLevel = TRIGGER_NESTLEVEL(),
       DepthWithinTrigger = TRIGGER_DEPTH(),
       CurrentModule = OBJECT_NAME(@@PROCID);
GO

-- Direct recursion is governed by the RECURSIVE_TRIGGERS database
-- option, OFF by default. Indirect recursion (A triggers B, B triggers A)
-- is always permitted and is bounded at 32 levels.
SELECT is_recursive_triggers_on FROM sys.databases WHERE name = DB_NAME();
GO

-- The guard against self-recursion, for the trigger that must update the
-- table it is defined on. Compare the values, not the nesting level,
-- when a value that differs on the outer statement but not on the inner
-- one is the thing that terminates the loop.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER dbo.trg_Customer_NormaliseName
-- ON dbo.Customer AFTER UPDATE AS
-- BEGIN
--     SET NOCOUNT ON;
--     IF TRIGGER_NESTLEVEL() > 1 RETURN;
--     UPDATE c SET c.NormalisedName = UPPER(LTRIM(RTRIM(c.LastName)))
--     FROM dbo.Customer c JOIN inserted i ON i.CustomerID = c.CustomerID;
-- END
GO
-- The data-modification error. A DML statement with an OUTPUT clause and no
-- INTO clause cannot target a table with any enabled trigger, and raises
-- Msg 334 at compile time. This is the avoidable case; the fix is also the
-- better pattern.
GO
-- Fails: Msg 334, 'The target table of the DML statement cannot have any
--        enabled triggers if the statement contains an OUTPUT clause
--        without INTO clause.'
-- UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder SET StatusCode = 7 OUTPUT deleted.StatusCode WHERE SalesOrderID = 1001;
GO

-- Works: the same statement with an INTO target. The caller now receives
-- no extra result set, which is the better behaviour anyway.
GO
-- DECLARE @Log table (SalesOrderID int, OldStatus int, NewStatus int);
-- UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder SET StatusCode = 7
-- OUTPUT inserted.SalesOrderID, deleted.StatusCode, inserted.StatusCode INTO @Log
-- WHERE SalesOrderID = 1001;
-- SELECT * FROM @Log;
GO

-- The other cause of a trigger-related data-modification error: a trigger
-- on T that deletes or updates a row another trigger's target references.
-- The failure is a foreign key violation, and because the whole outer
-- statement is inside the transaction, the caller's work is lost too.
--
-- Diagnose which trigger is on which table before assuming the statement is
-- at fault. This is the query to run first when a DML statement fails with
-- an error the statement itself would not produce.
--
-- SELECT OBJECT_NAME(parent_id) AS TableName, name AS TriggerName, is_disabled
-- FROM sys.triggers WHERE parent_class = 1;
GO

-- DDL and logon triggers. DDL triggers are scoped to a database, the
-- server or a specific object, and the statement() function returns the
-- DDL text, so a trigger can refuse a specific change.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER TRIGGER trg_ServerNoDropDatabase
-- ON ALL SERVER
-- WITH EXECUTE AS OWNER
-- FOR DROP_DATABASE AS
-- BEGIN
--     IF ORIGINAL_LOGIN() NOT IN ('sa', 'CONTOSO\dbadmin')
--         ROLLBACK;  -- Msg 3609 terminates the batch
-- END
GO
Trigger typeFiresStatement already appliedTypical use
AFTER INSERTAFTER INSERTAfter the rows are written and constraints are checkedYesAudit, denormalised totals, search-vector maintenance
AFTER UPDATEAFTER UPDATEAs above; both pseudo-tables populatedYesAudit of changed columns, cascading to another table
AFTER DELETEAFTER DELETEAs above; inse is emptyYesSoft delete, archive, reject a deletion
INSTEAD OF DMLINSTEAD OF DMLInstead of the statementNoMake a multi-table or aggregate view updatable
DDL triggerAfter a DDL statement in scopeYesPrevent unauthorised schema changes; ON DATABASEON DATABASE, ON ALL SERVERON ALL SERVER, ON SCHEMAON SCHEMA
Logon triggerAfter a successful connectionYesReject a login by application name or host; server-level only

Most things a trigger is used for have a better mechanism. A derived value is a PERS computed column. A row-count rollup is a summary table maintained by the same statement that changes the base rows, or an indexed view. A change log is the OUT clause, which costs nothing because it is part of the statement you were going to run anyway. A notification is Service Broker, which is asynchronous and survives a restart. Use a trigger when the rule genuinely cannot be expressed as a constraint, a computed column or the same statement — and then only as AF, only set-based, and only if you have measured the write path afterwards.

8.6 OUTPUT, MERGE and module versioning

The OUT clause captures what a modification did without a trigger, which makes it the correct answer to most problems a trigger is currently solving badly. ME is the other statement that looks like the answer and is not: it is correct in simple cases and has a documented history of correctness and concurrency defects. The section closes with the statements for changing a module on a live system.

-- OUTPUT. Takes the before and after images of a DML statement and makes
-- them available to the rest of the statement, into a table variable or a
-- base table, or to the client if no INTO is given.
--
-- Rules: one OUTPUT clause per DML statement; the referenced columns must
-- exist in inserted or deleted; INTO targets a table variable or a base
-- table that has no enabled trigger, no CHECK constraint, no enabled rule
-- and no foreign key relationship; and OUTPUT without INTO fails against a
-- table that has an enabled trigger (Msg 334).
GO
DECLARE @History TABLE
(
    SalesOrderID int         NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    OldStatus   int         NOT NULL,
    NewStatus   int         NOT NULL,
    ChangedAt   datetime2(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT SYSUTCDATETIME()
);

UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
SET    StatusCode = 7
OUTPUT inserted.SalesOrderID, deleted.StatusCode, inserted.StatusCode
    INTO @History (SalesOrderID, OldStatus, NewStatus)
WHERE StatusCode = 2;

SELECT COUNT(*) AS RowsChanged FROM @History;
GO

-- The same statement, sending the images to an archive table instead.
-- The target must not have triggers or foreign keys, which is a real
-- constraint on the archive table's design.
GO
DELETE dbo.SalesOrder
OUTPUT deleted.SalesOrderID, deleted.CustomerID, deleted.OrderDate, deleted.TotalAmount
    INTO dbo.SalesOrderArchive (SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount)
WHERE OrderDate < '2000-01-01';
GO

-- On a MERGE, OUTPUT may reference $action, and the FROM clause is
-- required. Without INTO the client receives one result set per action.
GO
-- MERGE dbo.DimProduct AS t
-- USING dbo.StageProduct AS s ON t.SKU = s.SKU
-- WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET t.UnitPrice = s.UnitPrice
-- WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET THEN INSERT (SKU, UnitPrice) VALUES (s.SKU, s.UnitPrice)
-- OUTPUT $action, inserted.SKU, inserted.UnitPrice;
GO

-- The audit trigger from 8.5, replaced by an OUTPUT clause on the same
-- statement. Nothing is faster, because nothing is extra.
GO
-- UPDATE o
-- SET    o.StatusCode = 7
-- OUTPUT inserted.SalesOrderID, 'UPDATE', deleted.StatusCode, inserted.StatusCode,
--        SYSUTCDATETIME(), ORIGINAL_LOGIN()
--   INTO dbo.SalesOrderAudit (SalesOrderID, Action, OldStatus, NewStatus, ChangedAt, ChangedBy)
-- WHERE  o.StatusCode = 2;
GO
-- MERGE: one statement for insert / update / delete against a source set.
-- Applies to SQL Server 2008 and later. Two options govern concurrency
-- behaviour: HOLDLOCK on the target, and which WHEN MATCHED clauses are
-- present.
GO
MERGE [dbo.DimProduct] WITH (HOLDLOCK) AS t
USING OPENJSON(@ProductJson) AS s
       ON t.SKU = s.value
WHEN MATCHED AND t.UnitPrice <> TRY_CAST(s.value AS decimal(9,2))
    THEN UPDATE SET t.UnitPrice = TRY_CAST(s.value AS decimal(9,2))
WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET
    THEN INSERT (SKU, UnitPrice) VALUES (s.value, TRY_CAST(s.value AS decimal(9,2)))
OUTPUT $action, inserted.SKU, deleted.UnitPrice, inserted.UnitPrice;
GO

-- WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE then deletes. The classic misuse: a source
-- that is accidentally incomplete, joined on a non-unique column, or whose
-- filter matches nothing, silently deletes every row in the target.
GO
MERGE dbo.DimProduct AS t
USING dbo.StageProduct AS s ON t.SKU = s.SKU
WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET t.UnitPrice = s.UnitPrice
WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET THEN INSERT (SKU, UnitPrice) VALUES (s.SKU, s.UnitPrice)
WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE THEN DELETE;   -- the clause to think hard about
GO

-- The documented correctness rules that bite in practice:
--   1. The ON clause must be an equality against a UNIQUE key on the
--      target, or a single source row can match several target rows.
--   2. The statement must end with a semicolon; without one the following
--      statement is absorbed and produces Msg 10713.
--   3. HOLDLOCK is the documented recommendation for correct concurrency.
--   4. WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE is evaluated over the whole target,
--      not over the subset matched by any other clause.
GO

-- Validate the source before the MERGE, not after. A count that does not
-- match expectations is the cheap way to avoid the delete.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(20), s.Source) AS Side,
       COUNT_BIG(*) AS Rows
FROM   (SELECT 'target' AS Source, SKU FROM dbo.DimProduct
            UNION ALL
            SELECT 'staging', SKU FROM dbo.StageProduct) AS s
GROUP BY s.Source;
GO
-- The same operation as explicit statements. Longer, and every one of these
-- properties is a reason to prefer it for anything nontrivial:
--   - each statement is independently indexed and independently optimisable
--   - each reports its own row count, so an over-delete is visible
--   - no concurrency defect history
--   - the DELETE can be guarded by an explicit count check
GO
BEGIN TRANSACTION;

-- 1. Update the matched rows.
UPDATE t
SET    t.UnitPrice = s.UnitPrice
FROM   dbo.DimProduct    AS t
INNER JOIN dbo.StageProduct AS s ON s.SKU = t.SKU
WHERE  t.UnitPrice <> s.UnitPrice;
DECLARE @Updated int = @@ROWCOUNT;

-- 2. Insert the new rows.
INSERT dbo.DimProduct (SKU, UnitPrice)
SELECT s.SKU, s.UnitPrice
FROM   dbo.StageProduct AS s
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.DimProduct AS t WHERE t.SKU = s.SKU);
DECLARE @Inserted int = @@ROWCOUNT;

-- 3. The delete, guarded. A count check before the statement is the
--    only thing that makes this clause safe.
DECLARE @WouldDelete int =
(
    SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.DimProduct AS t
    WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.StageProduct AS s WHERE s.SKU = t.SKU)
);

IF @WouldDelete > 0 AND @WouldDelete <= 1000
BEGIN
    DELETE t
    FROM   dbo.DimProduct AS t
    WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.StageProduct AS s WHERE s.SKU = t.SKU);
END
ELSE IF @WouldDelete > 1000
BEGIN
    ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
    THROW 50003, 'Staging would delete more than 1000 rows; the source is incomplete.', 1;
END

COMMIT TRANSACTION;

SELECT Updated = @Updated, Inserted = @Inserted, Deleted = CONVERT(int, @WouldDelete);
GO
-- Module versioning and metadata. These are the statements to use when a schema
-- has to be changed safely on a live system.
GO

-- Read a module's definition. Returns NULL for an encrypted module and for
-- a module the caller has no VIEW DEFINITION permission on.
SELECT OBJECT_DEFINITION(OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_OrderSummary'));
GO

-- Rename. sp_rename updates sys.objects and, for modules, the source in
-- sys.sql_modules. It does not update references in other modules, in CHECK
-- constraints, or in application code, and it does not move the object
-- between schemas (use ALTER SCHEMA for that).
EXEC sys.sp_rename 'dbo.usp_OrderSummary', 'usp_OrderSummary_v2', 'OBJECT';
GO

-- Move between schemas. Module resolution is fully qualified at bind time,
-- so any module that calls the old name breaks until it is altered.
ALTER SCHEMA sales TRANSFER dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v2;
GO

-- Find every module that depends on a given object. Use this before altering
-- or dropping anything, not after. sys.sql_expression_dependencies covers
-- modules, views and computed columns; sys.dm_sql_referenced_entities
-- covers them and also resolves objects referenced dynamically inside
-- sp_executesql text that is visible to the plan.
GO
SELECT DISTINCT CONVERT(nvarchar(300), s.schema_name(m.schema_id) + '.' + s.name) AS DependentModule
FROM   sys.sql_expression_dependencies AS d
JOIN   sys.sql_modules AS m ON m.object_id = d.referencing_id
JOIN   sys.objects      AS s ON s.object_id = m.object_id
WHERE d.referenced_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.SalesOrder');
GO

-- Recompile one module, or everything that depends on a change, without a
-- restart. Needed after a schema change made by a different tool that did
-- not send RECOMPILE.
EXEC sys.sp_recompile 'dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v2';
GO
EXEC sys.sp_recompile 'dbo.SalesOrder';   -- all dependents
GO

-- Before and after a module change: how many cached plans and executions
-- the module currently has. Zero cached plans on a module with a long
-- definition is a module nobody calls, and the deployment risk is then the
-- application rather than the ALTER.
GO
-- SELECT s.cached_plan_handle, s.creation_time, s.execution_count, s.size_in_bytes
-- FROM sys.dm_exec_cached_plans p
-- JOIN sys.dm_exec_query_stats  s ON s.cached_plan_handle = p.plan_handle
-- WHERE s.objectid = OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v2');
GO
-- Three ways to invalidate a plan, and which one to use. Getting this
-- wrong is either too little (stale plans survive a schema change) or
-- too much (DBCC FREEPROCCACHE clears the whole instance, including every
-- unrelated application's cached plans, and forces them all to recompile).
GO
-- 1. The supported, targeted form. Invalidates one module and everything
--    that depends on it.
EXEC sys.sp_recompile 'dbo.SalesOrder';
GO

-- 2. A statement-level hint, for the current execution only.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO

-- 3. The instance-wide hammer. Free plan cache, and then the plan cache
--    is rebuilt one statement at a time as statements arrive. Acceptable in
--    a maintenance window; a denial of service in production, because every
--    concurrent query pays a compile while the cache refills.
DBCC FREEPROCCACHE;
-- DBCC FREEPROCCACHE WITH NO_INFOMSGS;   -- the quieter form
-- DBCC FLUSHPROCCACHE;                -- synonym, same effect
GO

-- What the cache held before and after, which is the number to put in the
-- change record when a free plan cache is used.
GO
-- SELECT type_desc, COUNT(*), SUM(size_in_bytes) / 1024 AS KB
-- FROM sys.dm_exec_cached_plans GROUP BY type_desc;
GO

Requirements for a safe module change. Use CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER so the deployment is idempotent and re-runnable. Deploy through a script that ends with EXEC sp_recompileEXEC sp_recompile on the changed objects. Do not use WITH ENCRYPTIONWITH ENCRYPTION in a source-controlled deployment: the obfuscated definition cannot be diffed and cannot be compared against source, and there is no AL that removes it. Keep modules out of the d schema, because the default schema of a careless login resolves unqualified names into d and because sp_r and ALTER SCHEMAALTER SCHEMA are then the only ways to move one.

Exercises 8

  1. Create a stored procedure with three input parameters, a default on one, an OUT parameter and a return code. Call it positionally, named, and with the default omitted, and record the return code in each case.
  2. Query sys.par for your procedure and confirm that parameter 0 is the return value. Then call the procedure without an optional parameter and confirm the default is used, and without a required parameter and record the error number.
  3. Execute the same SEL three times: as ad-hoc text with one literal, as ad-hoc text with a different literal, and through sp_exe with a parameter. Count the rows in sys.dm_exec_ for type_desc = 'Ad Hoc’type_desc = 'Ad Hoc' and 'Prepared''Prepared' before and after, and explain the difference.
  4. Create a procedure with WITH RECOMPILEWITH RECOMPILE and one without. Execute each fifty times, then query sys.dm_exec for cached_pl and creati, and explain what the numbers show about reuse.
  5. Write the set-based audit trigger from 8.5, then run a single UPD that touches 5000 rows, and confirm 5000 audit rows appear from one statement. Add a row count inside the trigger to prove the body executed once and not 5000 times.
  6. Create a scalar function that returns a customer’s order total, a non-schemabound version and a SCHEMA version, on a database at compatibility level 150 or 170. Compare SET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO for the projection form and the filter form, and identify from the plan which queries inlined.
  7. Create a multi-statement table-valued function and a function that attempts an INS into a permanent table. Record the exact compile error. Then do the same from a procedure and confirm it succeeds.
  8. Create an indexed view over a join and an aggregate, create the UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEXUNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX, then attempt to ALTER COLUMNALTER COLUMN on a base-table column the view references. Record the error, drop the index, and confirm the AL then succeeds.
  9. Time a 10,000-row UPD against a table with an indexed view, with the index present and with it disabled (ALTER INDEX ... DISABLEALTER INDEX ... DISABLE). Report the elapsed time and the logical reads in both cases.
  10. Create a trigger on a table and then attempt UPDATE ... OUTPUT deleted.ColUPDATE ... OUTPUT deleted.Col with no IN clause against that table. Record the error number, add an IN clause to a table variable, and confirm the statement runs.
  11. Write a ME with a WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE THEN DELETEWHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE THEN DELETE clause, run it with a deliberately incomplete source, and count the rows deleted. Then rewrite the same operation as explicit UPD plus INS plus a guarded DEL, and show it cannot over-delete.

9 · Security and Access Control

9.1 Authentication modes and the principal hierarchy9.2 GRANT, DENY, REVOKE and ownership chains9.3 Least privilege: fixed roles and custom roles9.4 Row-Level Security and Dynamic Data Masking9.5 Encryption at rest, in transit and Always Encrypted9.6 Auditing, session context and module signing

SQL Server’s security model has three layers that are easy to confuse: authentication establishes who is connecting, authorisation decides what a connected principal may do, and encryption protects data at three different points with three different mechanisms. The chapter follows that structure, then covers the ownership chain that decides which permissions a module needs, the two row-level mechanisms, the two encryption mechanisms most often confused with each other, and the auditing and signing features that make the rest auditable.

9.1 Authentication modes and the principal hierarchy

An instance supports one or both of the two server authentication modes, and which one it is in determines what a client sends over the wire. Below that sits a two-level principal hierarchy: a login at server level, a user at database level, and a role at either, with pub implicit at both.

ModeHow it worksWhere it is requiredPrincipal cost
Windows authenticationThe client performs the network logon (Kerberos or NTLM) and passes the result to the server; the password never crosses the wire to SQL ServerAlways available; the default mode on a new installationRequires Windows in the domain or a trusted forest; service accounts must be managed by Active Directory
SQL Server authenticationThe client sends a username and password to the instance, which validates them against a hash in master.sysRequired where no domain exists, and for accounts that are not Windows accountsThe password is stored as a salted hash and is subject to the instance password policy; the credential is a database credential, not an operating-system one
Contained database authenticationThe login is a user in the database with a password, and authentication is resolved from within the database rather than by the instanceDatabases that must be restorable to a different instance or moved to Azure SQL DatabaseRequires contained database authenticationcontained database authentication enabled at instance level; the credential travels with the database file
Microsoft Entra ID (Azure AD)The client authenticates to Entra ID and presents a token; the server maps the token to a database userAzure SQL Database, Azure SQL Managed Instance, and SQL Server 2022 and later on-premisesRequires the server or database to be registered with the tenant; the token lifetimes, conditional access and MFA policy are the organisation’s
-- Which mode is this instance in? IsIntegrated = 1 is Windows-only mode,
-- which is the state to aim for. Both settings must be changed together
-- and the service restarted, or SQL Server refuses to start.
--
-- Removing Windows authentication while Windows logins exist locks everyone
-- out, including the renamed sa account. Check before changing.
GO
SELECT WindowsOnly = CONVERT(int, SERVERPROPERTY('IsIntegrated')),
              MixedMode   = CONVERT(int, SERVERPROPERTY('IsIntegrated') = 0),
              ProductVersion = CONVERT(varchar(40), SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion'));
GO

-- A SQL Server login. The password policy is enforced at creation and,
-- with CHECK_EXPIRATION, re-checked at logon.
CREATE LOGIN [app_reporting]
    WITH PASSWORD = 'Str0ng!Passw0rd#2026',
         DEFAULT_DATABASE = Sales,
         CHECK_POLICY = ON,
         CHECK_EXPIRATION = OFF;
GO

-- The metadata. sys.sql_logins holds only the SQL-authenticated subset;
-- sys.server_principals holds every kind. type: S = SQL login,
-- U = Windows login, G = Windows group, R = server role, C = mapped cert.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), name)                          AS LoginName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20), type)                          AS TypeCode,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), type_desc)                     AS TypeDesc,
       CONVERT(varchar(40), authentication_policy_desc) AS AuthPolicy,
       is_policy_checked, is_expiration_checked, is_disabled
FROM   sys.server_principals
WHERE type IN ('S', 'U', 'G')
ORDER BY name;
GO
-- Contained database authentication: the user belongs to the database, not
-- the instance, so the database is portable to another instance.
ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET CONTAINED_DATABASE = ON;
GO

-- Now create a user with a password and no login. No server-level object
-- is created for this principal at all.
CREATE USER [app_reporting] WITH PASSWORD = 'Str0ng!Passw0rd#2026';
GO

-- authentication_type: 0 = NONE, 2 = PASSWORD. The database must be
-- ONLINE for the connection to be routed to it, which is the operational
-- cost of containment.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), name)                AS UserName,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), authentication_type_desc) AS AuthType,
       CONVERT(varchar(20), type_desc)           AS UserType,
       is_disabled, create_date
FROM   sys.database_principals
WHERE name = 'app_reporting';
GO

-- Server-level configuration for contained databases. The option is off
-- by default and requires a restart.
GO
-- EXEC sys.sp_configure 'contained database authentication', 1;
-- RECONFIGURE;
GO

-- A contained database carries its own credential, which is a real
-- consequence for handling the file: anyone holding the .mdf and the
-- password can log in.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), name)       AS DatabaseName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), containment) AS Containment,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), state_desc)   AS State,
              CONVERT(varchar(30), user_access_desc) AS UserAccess
FROM   sys.databases
WHERE CONVERT(varchar(20), containment) <> 'NONE';
GO
-- The two-level hierarchy, created end to end: a login at server level, a
-- user in each database, and role membership in each.
GO
CREATE LOGIN [CONTOSO\app_svc] FROM WINDOWS;
GO

USE [Sales];
GO
CREATE USER [app_svc] FOR LOGIN = [CONTOSO\app_svc];
GO

ALTER ROLE [db_datareader] ADD MEMBER [app_svc];
ALTER ROLE [db_datawriter] ADD MEMBER [app_svc];
GO

-- The whole picture from both catalog views. Note the CONVERT to sysname
-- on the SID: syslogins.sid is varbinary(85) on a mixed-mode instance and
-- sysserverprincipals.sid is varbinary(85) as well, but the two columns do
-- not compare directly on a Windows-only instance.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), sp.name)   AS ServerPrincipal,
              CONVERT(varchar(60), mp.name)   AS DatabaseUser,
              CONVERT(varchar(60), rp.name)   AS RoleName,
              CONVERT(varchar(30), mp.authentication_type_desc) AS AuthType
FROM       sys.server_principals    AS sp
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals  AS mp ON mp.sid = sp.sid
LEFT JOIN sys.database_role_members AS rm ON rm.member_principal_id = mp.principal_id
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals  AS rp ON rp.principal_id = rm.role_principal_id
WHERE sp.type IN ('S', 'U', 'G')
ORDER BY ServerPrincipal, RoleName;
GO
-- public: a role every principal belongs to, at both levels. It cannot be
-- created or dropped, and it is not enumerated in the role-membership
-- tables because the membership is implicit. Anything granted to
-- public applies to every principal in the database, which is why the
-- public grant list is the first thing to audit.
--
-- The two permissions needed before anything else is possible:
--   CONNECT SQL   (server)    the right to attempt a connection
--   CONNECT       (database)  the right to use the database once in it
-- A login with CONNECT SQL but no user in a database cannot use it. A user
-- with CONNECT but no object permission can do nothing but connect.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(30), class_desc)      AS ClassDesc,
              CONVERT(varchar(80), permission_name) AS Permission,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), state_desc)       AS State,
              CONVERT(varchar(60), dp.name)           AS Grantee
FROM       sys.database_permissions AS p
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp ON dp.principal_id = p.grantee_principal_id
WHERE dp.name = 'public'
ORDER BY ClassDesc, Permission;
GO

-- Effective permissions on an object, as SQL Server resolves them right
-- now for the current principal. This is the single most useful
-- diagnostic when a permission is not behaving as expected. Run it AS
-- the affected principal, not AS yourself.
--
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT * FROM sys.fn_my_permissions('dbo.SalesOrder', 'OBJECT');
-- REVERT;
GO

-- Which logins can reach this database at all, and as whom.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), sp.name)  AS LoginName,
              CONVERT(varchar(60), dp.name)  AS UserName,
              CONVERT(varchar(40), sp.type_desc) AS LoginType
FROM       sys.server_principals   AS sp
INNER JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp ON dp.sid = sp.sid
WHERE sp.type IN ('S', 'U', 'G')
ORDER BY LoginName;
GO
-- Microsoft Entra ID authentication. On-premises from SQL Server 2022, and
-- carried forward in 2025 unchanged. The server is registered with the
-- tenant, and a principal from the tenant is mapped to a database user.
-- The authentication itself happens outside SQL Server: the client obtains
-- a token and the server validates it against the tenant metadata.
GO
-- Server-level: a login mapped to an Entra principal.
-- CREATE LOGIN [user@contoso.onmicrosoft.com] FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER;
GO
-- Database-level, for a contained user:
-- USE Sales;
-- CREATE USER [app@contoso.onmicrosoft.com] FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER;
-- ALTER ROLE [db_datareader] ADD MEMBER [app@contoso.onmicrosoft.com];
GO

-- The four token sources, in the order a client normally prefers them:
--   Managed Identity     the credential of the host itself; no secret to
--                        store, and the recommended form on Azure
--   Service Principal     a client id and secret or certificate; the form
--                        for unattended work outside Azure
--   Access Token         a token the application already holds
--   Interactive / Device Code  a human, and the form that supports MFA and
--                        conditional access
GO

-- The connection string for the managed-identity form. The database user
-- name is the part after the @ in the user principal name.
--
--   Server=tcp:sql01.contoso.local,1433;Initial Catalog=Sales;
--   Authentication=Active Directory Managed Identity;
--   User Id=app@contoso.onmicrosoft.com;
--   Encrypt=Mandatory;TrustServerCertificate=False;
GO

-- What an Entra user looks like in the catalog: type S, with a SID in the
-- tenant's format rather than a Windows domain SID. That is why a join on
-- sid between server and database principals is the reliable one.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), sp.name)         AS LoginName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  sp.type_desc)        AS LoginType,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  sp.authentication_policy_desc) AS AuthPolicy,
              CONVERT(varbinary(85), sp.sid)    AS Sid
FROM   sys.server_principals AS sp
WHERE sp.name LIKE '%@contoso.onmicrosoft.com'
   OR sp.name LIKE 'app@%';
GO
PrincipalLevelCreated withNotes
LoginServerCREATE LOGINCREATE LOGINWindows account, Windows group, SQL credential, certificate, asymmetric key
UserDatabaseCREATE USER ... FOR LOGINCREATE USER ... FOR LOGINOne per database per login; the same login has different permissions in each database
RoleServer or databaseCREATE ROLECREATE ROLE, CREATE SERVER ROLECREATE SERVER ROLEFixed roles ship with the product; user-defined roles are database-level only
pubBothCannot be created or droppedEvery principal is an implicit member. Permissions granted to pub apply to everyone
guDatabaseExists in every database, disabled by defaultWhen enabled, any login with CON to the server is mapped to gu; enabling it is almost always a mistake
Application roleDatabaseCREATE APPLICATION ROLECREATE APPLICATION ROLEActivated with SET APPLICATION ROLESET APPLICATION ROLE and a password; used where the connection has no end-user identity at all

Caveat. The gu user exists in every database and is disabled. Enabling it, even in one database, lets any login with CON to the server use that database with whatever permissions gu has. Check it as part of any hardening pass: SELECT name, is_disabled FROM sys.database_principals WHERE name = 'guest’SELECT name, is_disabled FROM sys.database_principals WHERE name = 'guest'. The other common surprise is that a login is a server object and a user is a database object, so removing a login from a server role changes nothing about what it can do inside a database where it holds a role with permissions. And a login with VIEW ANY DATABASEVIEW ANY DATABASE can use every database on the instance regardless of its CON permission in each, which makes that one server permission worth auditing on its own.

9.2 GRANT, DENY, REVOKE and ownership chains

Three statements change permissions, and the order in which they are applied is irrelevant: DE always wins, at any level, from any source. Behind that rule is the ownership chain, which is the mechanism that lets a caller reach a table it holds no permission on — provided a single principal owns every object in the chain.

-- The three statements, and the four levels of securable they apply to.
GO

-- Object-level, column-level, and to a role rather than to a user.
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON dbo.SalesOrder TO [app_svc];
DENY  DELETE ON dbo.SalesOrder TO [app_svc];

-- Column-level. A column grant is an effective DENY on every other column.
GRANT SELECT (CustomerID, FirstName, LastName) ON dbo.Customer TO [app_reporting];

-- Schema-level: covers every current and future object in the schema, which
-- is the form to use for a reporting schema of stored procedures.
GRANT SELECT, EXECUTE ON SCHEMA::[reporting] TO [app_reporting];

-- Database-level, by class and principal type.
GRANT CREATE TABLE, CREATE PROCEDURE TO [app_developer];

-- Server-level.
GRANT VIEW ANY STATE TO [app_dba_monitor];
DENY  ALTER ANY LOGIN TO [app_dba_monitor];

-- REVOKE removes both a GRANT and a DENY. It does not add a DENY. After a
-- REVOKE the permission is neither granted nor denied, and the implicit
-- default is DENY.
REVOKE DELETE ON dbo.SalesOrder FROM [app_svc];
GO
StatementWhat it recordsPrecedenceHow to undo
GRstate = GRANTstate = GRANTLowestREV
DEstate = DENYstate = DENYHighest — always wins, from a role, from pub, from an object owner, or from a column grantREV
REVNo rowNot applicableNot applicable — the absence of a row is the result
-- DENY overrides GRANT. The order of the two statements is irrelevant; the
-- DENY wins whether it comes first or last, whether it is granted to the
-- user directly or inherited from a role, and whether the GRANT came
-- from a role the user belongs to or from public.
GO
CREATE ROLE [SalesReader];
GO
GRANT SELECT ON dbo.Customer TO [SalesReader];
DENY  SELECT (Email, PhoneNumber, TaxId) ON dbo.Customer TO [SalesReader];
ALTER ROLE [SalesReader] ADD MEMBER [app_svc];
GO

-- Verification, run as the affected principal. The columns are not
-- readable; the others are. The two statements are the same either way.
--
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT CustomerID, FirstName, LastName, Email FROM dbo.Customer;  -- Msg 230
-- SELECT Email FROM dbo.Customer;  -- Msg 230, SELECT permission denied
-- REVERT;
GO

-- Audit every explicit DENY in the database. In a system built by accretion
-- this is usually a short and surprising list, and every entry is a place
-- where a future GRANT will silently do nothing.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  p.class_desc)      AS ClassDesc,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), OBJECT_NAME(p.major_id)) AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(120), p.permission_name) AS Permission,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  dp.name)           AS Grantee
FROM       sys.database_permissions AS p
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp ON dp.principal_id = p.grantee_principal_id
WHERE p.state = 2              -- 2 = DENY
ORDER BY ObjectName, Permission;
GO
-- The ownership chain. An unbroken chain: app_svc has EXECUTE on the procedure
-- and nothing on the table. Because the procedure, the view and the table
-- are all owned by the same principal, the chain is unbroken and no
-- table-level permission is required.
GO
GRANT EXECUTE ON dbo.usp_OrderSummary TO [app_ordering];
-- No GRANT SELECT on dbo.SalesOrder. None is needed.
GO

-- Who owns what. Ownership is one principal per object, and the owner is not
-- necessarily a person: it is a role, often db_owner, and often nobody
-- has thought about it because it was never set. A chain is broken by
-- every row here with a different owner name.
SELECT SCHEMA_NAME(o.schema_id)          AS SchemaName,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), o.name)  AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  p.name)  AS OwnerName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  o.type_desc) AS Type
FROM       sys.objects AS o
LEFT JOIN sys.Principals AS p ON p.principal_id = o.principal_id
WHERE o.schema_id = SCHEMA_ID('dbo')
  AND o.type IN ('P', 'V', 'FN', 'TF', 'IF', 'U')
ORDER BY ObjectName;
GO

-- Setting the owner. The AUTHORIZATION clause is the current form;
-- sp_changeobjectowner is the legacy equivalent and additionally rewrites
-- the module source.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v3
    AUTHORIZATION [db_owner]
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS OrderCount FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
END;
GO

-- The three documented conditions that break a chain: a cross-database
-- reference, an EXECUTE AS context change, and objects owned by different
-- principals. When the chain is broken, every object in it is checked, and
-- the caller needs permission on all of them.
GO
-- CALLER is the default and runs the module with the caller's permissions.
-- EXECUTE AS switches the effective principal for the duration. Two forms
-- matter: WITH EXECUTE AS OWNER, declared in the module and permanent, and
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'x', issued in the session until REVERT.
--
-- WITH EXECUTE AS OWNER is the standard way to give indirect access: the
-- caller needs EXECUTE on the module and nothing on the tables.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v4
    WITH EXECUTE AS OWNER
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS OrderCount FROM dbo.SalesOrder;
END;
GO

-- The four identity functions, which together answer "who is connected,"
-- "who is executing," and "who is the server login." Inside a module with
-- EXECUTE AS OWNER, USER_NAME() is the owner and ORIGINAL_LOGIN() is still
-- the human or service account at the other end.
--
-- SELECT ORIGINAL_LOGIN()  AS WhoConnected,
--        USER_NAME()       AS WhoIsExecuting,
--        SUSER_SNAME()      AS ServerLogin,
--        SUSER_NAME()       AS EffectiveLogin;
GO

-- Session-level EXECUTE AS. It is a stack: each REVERT pops one level, and
-- an unmatched REVERT is an error. EXECUTE AS LOGIN switches the server
-- principal too, which is a stronger impersonation and requires the
-- IMPERSONATE permission at server level.
EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
SELECT NowActingAs = USER_NAME();
SELECT TOP(1) * FROM dbo.Customer;   -- checked against app_svc's permissions
REVERT;
SELECT BackTo = USER_NAME();
GO

-- The permanent form, and the one to prefer. The role is a purpose-built
-- principal, not db_owner, so the module grants exactly what its body
-- needs. See 9.6 for the signed-module equivalent.
GO
CREATE ROLE [mod_OrderSummary];
GO
GRANT SELECT ON dbo.SalesOrder     TO [mod_OrderSummary];
GRANT SELECT ON dbo.SalesOrderLine TO [mod_OrderSummary];
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5
    WITH EXECUTE AS [mod_OrderSummary]
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS LineCount FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine;
END;
GO
ContextRuns asChainUse it for
CAL (default)The connected principalUnbroken if owners match, so the caller needs permission only on the outermost objectProcedures that must not be able to exceed the caller’s rights
EXECUTE AS OWNEREXECUTE AS OWNERThe module’s owner, usually db_oAlways broken, so every object is checked against the ownerIndirect access, but at the cost of granting the caller everything the owner can do
EXECUTE AS <role>EXECUTE AS <role>The named database roleBroken, as aboveThe safe form of indirect access: the role holds only what the body needs
EXECUTE AS USER = 'x’EXECUTE AS USER = 'x'The named userBrokenDiagnostics: reproduce a permission problem as the affected principal
