EXECUTE AS LOGIN = 'x’EXECUTE AS LOGIN = 'x'The named login, across databasesBrokenRare; cross-database troubleshooting
Signed module (9.6)The certificate ownerBroken in the same way as EXECUTE AS OWNEREXECUTE AS OWNERIndirect access that is not equivalent to db_o, which is why it exists
-- The four classes of securable, and the permissions that are not object
-- permissions. Getting the class wrong in a GRANT is the second most
-- common cause of a permission that appears to be granted and is not.
GO
--   class 0  DATABASE
--   class 1  OBJECT or COLUMN
--   class 2  SCHEMA
--   class 3  DATABASE_PRINCIPAL   (users and roles)
--   class 4  SERVER_PRINCIPAL     (server roles)
--   class 5  TYPE
--   class 6  XML_SCHEMA_COLLECTION
-- plus the OBJECT class for a module, where EXECUTE is a distinct
-- permission from SELECT or UPDATE.
GO

-- The object permissions that exist, in one query, so that a GRANT can be
-- written against the real list rather than against a remembered one.
SELECT DISTINCT CONVERT(varchar(80), permission_name) AS ObjectPermission
FROM   sys.fn_my_permissions('dbo.SalesOrder', 'OBJECT')
UNION
SELECT DISTINCT CONVERT(varchar(80), permission_name)
FROM   sys.fn_my_permissions('dbo.usp_OrderSummary', 'OBJECT')
ORDER BY ObjectPermission;
GO

-- VIEW DEFINITION is the permission that makes an object visible in the
-- catalog without making it readable. It is the permission to use for
-- a schema whose existence should be knowable but whose contents are not,
-- and it is the one a client-side ORM needs to build its type map.
GO
GRANT VIEW DEFINITION ON SCHEMA::[sales] TO [app_svc];
GO

-- The metadata-visibility consequence, which is a security property and not
-- a bug: an object the caller cannot see produces Msg 297 rather than
-- Msg 229, so the error number itself discloses whether it exists.
--
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.SalesOrder;      -- Msg 229, exists, SELECT denied
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.NoSuchTable;     -- Msg 208, invalid object name
-- DROP TABLE dbo.SalesOrder;        -- Msg 297, denied
-- REVERT;
GO

Caveat. WITH EXECUTE AS OWNERWITH EXECUTE AS OWNER gives the caller everything the owner can do on every object the module touches. When the owner is db_o, that is unrestricted access to the database, and the module becomes a privilege-escalation path: any caller who can EXE it can do whatever the body does, including a body that takes a caller-supplied table name. The safe pattern is the third row of the table above — own the module by a purpose-built role that holds only the permissions the body actually needs. Module signing in 9.6 is the modern equivalent and does not require the EXECUTE ASEXECUTE AS clause at all. A fourth trap: EXECUTE ASEXECUTE AS changes the effective principal but not the ownership of anything, so an object created by a module running as a caller is owned by the schema of that caller, not by the module owner.

9.3 Least privilege: fixed roles and custom roles

SQL Server ships with fixed database roles whose names describe an intent and whose actual contents are a list of permissions that has changed between versions. The practice of assigning an application login to db_o because it is the one that definitely works remains the most common security defect in SQL Server systems, and it is the easiest to detect and the easiest to fix.

-- What the fixed database roles actually contain, on this instance. Read this
-- before trusting a role name: the contents are not fixed across versions
-- and are not the same in every edition. state 1 = GRANT, 0 = DENY.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), r.name)         AS RoleName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), r.is_fixed_role) AS IsFixedRole,
              CONVERT(varchar(80), p.permission_name) AS Permission,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), p.state_desc)    AS State
FROM       sys.database_permissions AS p
INNER JOIN sys.database_principals AS r ON r.principal_id = p.grantee_principal_id
WHERE r.type = 'R' AND r.is_fixed_role = 1
  AND r.name IN ('db_datareader', 'db_datawriter', 'db_owner')
ORDER BY RoleName, Permission;
GO

-- Members of the fixed roles. Any application login in db_owner is the
-- finding this whole section is about. The member may itself be a role,
-- which is why the join is recursive in a larger database.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), r.name)  AS RoleName,
              CONVERT(varchar(80), m.name)  AS MemberName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), m.type_desc) AS MemberType
FROM       sys.database_role_members AS rm
JOIN       sys.database_principals   AS r ON r.principal_id = rm.role_principal_id
JOIN       sys.database_principals   AS m ON m.principal_id = rm.member_principal_id
WHERE r.is_fixed_role = 1 AND r.name IN ('db_owner', 'db_securityadmin', 'db_accessadmin')
ORDER BY RoleName, MemberName;
GO
Fixed roleIntended useWhy it is often wrong for an application
db_datRead every user table and viewReads everything, including payroll, audit and configuration tables the application has no business reading
db_datInsert, update and delete in every user tableWrites everywhere, so a bug in one repository can corrupt any table
db_oThe database owner: full control, including the ability to change ownership, permissions and settingsUnrestricted. Includes the ALTER ANYALTER ANY permissions, so the application can grant itself anything and can drop or alter any object
db_ddCreate, alter and drop objectsCan drop a table another team depends on, and can redefine a procedure to do anything
db_securChange roles and permissionsCan grant itself any permission, which is equivalent to db_o for escalation purposes
db_acceChange database users and their mappingsCan map itself or another login into the database with any rights
db_backuTake a backupReads every row as part of the backup; treat a backup operator as a reader of everything
db_dat + db_datRead and write everything, no DDL, no permission changesA common compromise that is still far wider than necessary for most applications
-- The correct shape: a purpose-built role holding object-level grants, owned by
-- nobody the application can change, and the application user as its only
-- member.
GO
CREATE ROLE [app_ordering];
GO

-- Read and write on the tables the application actually owns.
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON dbo.SalesOrder TO [app_ordering];
GRANT SELECT                    ON dbo.Customer TO [app_ordering];
-- Line items: full DML, because the application deletes a line.
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON dbo.SalesOrderLine TO [app_ordering];
-- No DELETE on the order header: orders are closed, not deleted.
-- No permission on AuditLog, Configuration, UserProfile or anything else.

-- The application cannot create or alter objects, so it cannot change the
-- definitions it depends on. This is the point of the exercise.
DENY ALTER ON SCHEMA::[dbo] TO [app_ordering];
DENY ALTER ANY ON DATABASE TO [app_ordering];

ALTER ROLE [app_ordering] ADD MEMBER [app_svc];
GO

-- The verification harness. Every negative case in one script, run as
-- the application principal so that the answers are the ones that matter.
--
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT TOP 5 * FROM dbo.SalesOrder;             -- permitted
-- DELETE FROM dbo.SalesOrder;                 -- Msg 297, denied
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.AuditLog;                 -- Msg 229, denied
-- DROP TABLE dbo.SalesOrder;                   -- Msg 297, denied
-- CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_Test AS SELECT 1;  -- Msg 297, denied
-- GRANT SELECT ON dbo.SalesOrder TO app_svc;  -- Msg 297, denied
-- REVERT;
GO
-- An application role, for a connection that has no end-user identity at all:
-- an ETL process, a reporting service, a scheduled job. It is activated
-- with a password the client must supply, and the connection has no
-- user of its own while it is active.
GO
CREATE APPLICATION ROLE [etl_reader]
    WITH PASSWORD = 'An0ther!Passw0rd',
         DEFAULT_SCHEMA = [dbo];
GO
GRANT SELECT ON SCHEMA::[reporting] TO [netl_reader];
GO

-- The client side. SET APPLICATION ROLE is a connection-level switch, and
-- the password must be supplied by the application rather than embedded in
-- a script, which is why application roles are usually activated by a
-- connection string or a configuration entry.
GO
-- SET APPLICATION ROLE [netl_reader] WITH PASSWORD = 'An0ther!Passw0rd';
-- SELECT * FROM reporting.vw_DailySales;
-- SET APPLICATION ROLE NONE;
GO

-- An application role has no members and does not appear in the role
-- membership tables, so it must be listed separately in an audit.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), name)     AS PrincipalName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), type_desc) AS TypeDesc,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), is_fixed_role) AS IsFixedRole
FROM   sys.database_principals
WHERE type IN ('A', 'R', 'U', 'G', 'S')
ORDER BY type_desc, name;
GO
-- The cross-database question. A login's blast radius is the sum of what it
-- holds in every database, and no single database's catalog answers it.
-- Run this on every database and aggregate the result.
GO
SELECT DB_NAME()                                             AS DatabaseName,
       CONVERT(varchar(60), sp.name)  AS LoginName,
       CONVERT(varchar(60), r.name)  AS FixedRole,
       CONVERT(int, COUNT(DISTINCT p.permission_name)) AS GrantedPermissions
FROM       sys.server_principals     AS sp
INNER JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp  ON dp.sid = sp.sid
LEFT JOIN  sys.database_role_members AS rm ON rm.member_principal_id = dp.principal_id
LEFT JOIN  sys.database_principals   AS r  ON r.principal_id = rm.role_principal_id AND r.is_fixed_role = 1
LEFT JOIN  sys.database_permissions  AS p  ON p.grantee_principal_id = dp.principal_id
WHERE sp.type IN ('S', 'U')
GROUP BY sp.name, r.name
ORDER BY LoginName, FixedRole;
GO

-- The owner's implicit control, which is not enumerated as a permission:
-- a principal that owns an object can always alter it and grant on it, and
-- therefore needs no object-level grant for it to work.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), p.name)          AS OwnerName,
              COUNT_BIG(*)                            AS ObjectsOwned,
              COUNT_BIG(DISTINCT CONVERT(int, o.type)) AS DistinctObjectTypes
FROM   sys.objects AS o
JOIN   sys.database_principals AS p ON p.principal_id = o.principal_id
WHERE o.is_ms_shipped = 0
GROUP BY p.name
ORDER BY ObjectsOwned DESC;
GO
-- The whole least-privilege workflow for one application, from an empty
-- database to a working role, with the grant list derived from the
-- application's actual statements rather than from a fixed role.
--
-- Step 1: the application owner role. It owns the objects and therefore has
-- full control over them, and it is not a member of the runtime role.
GO
CREATE ROLE [app_ordering_owner];
GO

-- Step 2: the runtime role, holding only the object-level grants, and a
-- DENY on the schema so it cannot alter the definitions it relies on.
GO
CREATE ROLE [app_ordering];
GO
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE  ON dbo.SalesOrder     TO [app_ordering];
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON dbo.SalesOrderLine TO [app_ordering];
GRANT SELECT                     ON dbo.Customer       TO [app_ordering];
GRANT EXECUTE                     ON dbo.usp_OrderSummary TO [app_ordering];
DENY  ALTER ON SCHEMA::[dbo] TO [app_ordering];
GO

-- Step 3: the user, in exactly one role. Note that the login is a service
-- account in Active Directory and holds nothing at server level beyond
-- CONNECT SQL, which it has implicitly through public.
GO
-- USE Sales;
-- CREATE USER [app_svc] FOR LOGIN [CONTOSO\app_svc];
-- ALTER ROLE [app_ordering] ADD MEMBER [app_svc];
GO

-- Step 4: the audit, so the grant list and the actual grant list are the same
-- list. Run this on every database and keep the output.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  dp.name)           AS Grantee,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), OBJECT_NAME(p.major_id)) AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  p.permission_name) AS Permission,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  p.state_desc)      AS State
FROM       sys.database_permissions AS p
INNER JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp ON dp.principal_id = p.grantee_principal_id
WHERE dp.name = 'app_ordering'
   OR dp.name = 'app_svc'
ORDER BY Grantee, ObjectName, Permission;
GO

The two roles worth remembering for most applications are db_dat for a read-only reporting login and a custom role for everything else. db_dat is almost never the right answer for an application, because it grants write access to every table including the ones the application must never touch. If a change to a role definition is needed, that is a deployment event, and the deployment principal should not be the runtime principal.

9.4 Row-Level Security and Dynamic Data Masking

Both features change what a SEL returns without changing the table. They solve different problems and do not compose: Row-Level Security removes rows, Dynamic Data Masking alters the values in the rows that remain. Neither is a substitute for authorisation, and both can be bypassed by a sufficiently privileged principal.

-- Row-Level Security. A security policy binds a predicate function to a table.
-- The function is evaluated per row and must return 1 for a row the caller
-- may see and 0 for a row it may not.
--
-- Requirements: the predicate function must be schema-bound and
-- deterministic, and must not reference another RLS-enabled table, a table
-- in another database, or a linked server. The protected table must have a
-- primary key if the predicate filters on a nullable column.
GO
-- The predicate. A scalar function returning 1 or 0 per row.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION dbo.fn_CanAccessCustomer (@CustomerID int)
RETURNS bit
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
BEGIN
    -- fn_my_permissions returns a row per permission; the existence of a row
    -- is what matters, not its value. This is the standard way to exempt
    -- administrative principals from the policy.
    IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.fn_my_permissions('Customer', 'OBJECT'))
        RETURN 1;   -- unrestricted principals see everything

    -- Otherwise, restrict to rows the caller's claims identify. The
    -- lookup must be indexed or the predicate dominates every query
    -- against the table.
    RETURN CASE WHEN EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.appUserCustomerAccess AS a
                     WHERE a.CustomerID = @CustomerID
                       AND a.UserPrincipalName = USER_NAME())
           THEN 1 ELSE 0 END;
END;
GO

-- The policy. A FILTER PREDICATE affects SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE: a
-- caller who cannot see a row cannot update or delete it.
GO
CREATE SECURITY POLICY [pol_CustomerRLS]
    ADD FILTER PREDICATE dbo.fn_CanAccessCustomer(CustomerID) ON dbo.Customer
    WITH (FILTER_PREDICATE = ON);
GO

-- A BLOCK PREDICATE instead, or as well. It applies to INSERT and UPDATE: a
-- caller cannot insert a row that would fall outside the predicate, and
-- cannot update a row so that it would.
GO
CREATE SECURITY POLICY [pol_CustomerRLS]
    ADD BLOCK PREDICATE dbo.fn_CanAccessCustomer(CustomerID) ON dbo.Customer
    WITH (BLOCK_PREDICATE = ON);
GO

-- Enable, disable, remove. Several predicates may coexist in one policy;
-- all of them are ANDed together.
GO
ALTER SECURITY POLICY [pol_CustomerRLS] WITH (STATE = OFF);   -- defined but not enforced
ALTER SECURITY POLICY [pol_CustomerRLS] WITH (STATE = ON);
DROP SECURITY POLICY [pol_CustomerRLS];
GO
-- The write path, which is where a filter-only policy becomes a data bug.
-- With only a FILTER PREDICATE, a caller with INSERT permission can insert a
-- row for a customer they are not entitled to, cannot then see it, and the
-- data is wrong. A BLOCK PREDICATE closes that.
GO
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
--
-- SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = 9999;   -- 0, hidden
-- UPDATE dbo.Customer SET LastName = 'X' WHERE CustomerID = 9999; -- 0 rows, silent
-- DELETE FROM dbo.Customer WHERE CustomerID = 9999;            -- 0 rows, silent
--
-- With a FILTER PREDICATE only, this SUCCEEDS and writes a row the caller
-- cannot read back, verify or correct:
-- INSERT INTO dbo.Customer (CustomerID, FirstName, LastName, IsActive)
-- VALUES (9999, 'Hidden', 'Row', 1);
--
-- With a BLOCK PREDICATE, the same statement is rejected: Msg 13786,
-- 'Update or insert of view or function ... blocked by policy'.
-- REVERT;
GO

-- The audit view. sys.dm_rowset_security reports, per principal, which rows
-- the predicate filtered and why, and it is the only way to see the policy
-- working rather than merely present.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  r.user_sid)      AS UserSid,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  r.user_name)    AS UserName,
              r.select_filter_or_block,
              r.security_policy_id, r.security_policy_principal_id
FROM   sys.dm_rowset_security AS r
WHERE  r.class_desc = 'OBJECT_OR_COLUMN'
ORDER BY r.user_name;
GO

-- Who is exempt. Membership of the database role BYPASSRLS, or the
-- BYPASSRLS database-scoped configuration, removes the policy entirely.
-- sysadmin and the table owner are NOT automatically exempt; they are
-- subject to the policy unless the predicate function exempts them.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), dp.name)  AS PrincipalName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), dp.type_desc) AS TypeDesc
FROM   sys.database_role_members AS rm
JOIN   sys.database_principals   AS r ON r.principal_id = rm.role_principal_id
JOIN   sys.database_principals   AS dp ON dp.principal_id = rm.member_principal_id
WHERE r.name = 'BYPASSRLS';
GO

SELECT CONVERT(varchar(40), name)                 AS ConfigName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), CONVERT(varchar(20), value)) AS ConfigValue
FROM   sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE name = 'BYPASSRLS';
GO
-- Dynamic Data Masking. Alters what a value looks like on the way out, for
-- unauthorised principals only. The stored value is unchanged and the
-- engine never sees a different value.
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ALTER COLUMN Email nvarchar(320)
    MASKED WITH (FUNCTION = 'email()');
GO

-- The four built-in functions, and the custom-function route.
--   email()                     a***@domain.com
--   partial(6, '*******', 2)     first 6, mask, last 2
--   random(0, 9)                a random digit, stable per row
--   default('Redacted', 'x')    a constant, ignoring the input
-- A custom function must be created in a separate schema and marked with
-- the BLOB, SCHEMABINDING and READONLY SQL properties, which is what
-- prevents a function that reveals the original value.
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Employee ALTER COLUMN NationalInsuranceNumber nvarchar(20)
    MASKED WITH (FUNCTION = 'partial(4, "XXXXXX", 4)');
ALTER TABLE dbo.Employee ALTER COLUMN Salary decimal(12,2)
    MASKED WITH (FUNCTION = dfm_Salary);
GO

-- A custom mask, in its own schema with the three required properties.
GO
-- CREATE SCHEMA masking;
-- CREATE FUNCTION masking.dfn_MaskAccountNumber(@Value varchar(20))
-- RETURNS varchar(20)
-- WITH MASKED, SCHEMABINDING, READONLY
-- AS BEGIN RETURN ('X' + RIGHT(@Value, 4)); END;
-- ALTER TABLE dbo.Account ALTER COLUMN AccountNumber varchar(20)
--     MASKED WITH (FUNCTION = 'masking.dfn_MaskAccountNumber');
GO

-- Remove the mask. It is a column alteration, so it takes a schema lock and
-- is not instant on a large table.
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ALTER COLUMN Email nvarchar(320) MASKED WITH (FUNCTION = NULL);
GO

-- Which columns are masked, and with what. The is_masked column on
-- sys.columns is the per-column flag; the masking catalog view carries
-- the function name.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  s.name)   AS SchemaName,
              CONVERT(varchar(120), t.name)   AS TableName,
              CONVERT(varchar(120), c.name)   AS ColumnName,
              c.is_masked,
              CONVERT(varchar(200), m.masking_function) AS MaskFunction
FROM       sys.columns AS c
JOIN       sys.tables  AS t ON t.object_id = c.object_id
JOIN       sys.schemas AS s ON s.schema_id = t.schema_id
LEFT JOIN  sys.security_masking_columns AS m ON m.object_id = c.object_id AND m.column_id = c.column_id
WHERE  c.is_masked = 1
ORDER BY TableName, ColumnName;
GO
-- Who sees unmasked values: members of the fixed role db_masked, the schema
-- owner, and anyone granted UNMASK directly. A database-level UNMASK on a
-- table unmasks every column in it, and UNMASK cannot be granted at column
-- level, which is why a mask on one column of a table is effectively
-- all-or-nothing.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60), dp.name)  AS PrincipalName,
              CONVERT(varchar(40), dp.type_desc) AS TypeDesc
FROM   sys.database_role_members AS rm
JOIN   sys.database_principals   AS r  ON r.principal_id  = rm.role_principal_id
JOIN   sys.database_principals   AS dp ON dp.principal_id = rm.member_principal_id
WHERE r.name = 'db_masked';
GO

SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  dp.name)          AS Grantee,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), OBJECT_NAME(p.major_id)) AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  p.permission_name) AS Permission,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  p.state_desc)      AS State
FROM       sys.database_permissions AS p
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals AS dp ON dp.principal_id = p.grantee_principal_id
WHERE CONVERT(varchar(40), p.permission_name) = 'UNMASK';
GO

-- The predicate leak, which is the reason a mask is not a control. A
-- masked column is fully usable in a comparison, so a caller can test
-- candidate values and can count distinct masked values, neither of which
-- requires seeing the column. partial() masking narrows the search space
-- rather than eliminating it.
GO
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dbo.Customer WHERE Email = 'known@example.com';   -- 0 or 1
-- SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT Email) FROM dbo.Customer;                     -- a cardinality leak
-- SELECT Email FROM dbo.Customer WHERE SUBSTRING(Email, 2, 1) = 'b';   -- letter by letter
-- REVERT;
GO
-- Row-level security and signed views together. This is the combination that
-- has to be right: a view over an RLS-protected table does NOT carry the
-- policy through to the caller unless the view is signed, because the
-- predicate would otherwise be evaluated against the view's owner. An
-- unsigned view is a data-disclosure bug, not a performance problem, and
-- it produces no error and no warning.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_MyCustomerRows
-- AS SELECT CustomerID, LastName, CreditLimit FROM dbo.Customer;
-- GO
-- ADD SIGNATURE TO OBJECT dbo.vw_MyCustomerRows BY CERTIFICATE = [ModSign_Sales];
GO

-- Detection, without reading any data. An unsigned view over a table with a
-- policy is the query to run: sys.security_policies for the table, and
-- sys.dm_database_referencing_entities for the view that reaches it.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  sp.name)   AS PolicyName,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  sp.schema) AS PolicySchema,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  o.name)   AS ProtectedTable,
              sp.filter_predicate, sp.block_predicate,
              CONVERT(varchar(10),  sp.state)  AS State
FROM       sys.security_policies AS sp
INNER JOIN sys.objects       AS o ON o.object_id = sp.security_policy_id
ORDER BY PolicyName;
GO

-- The predicate functions themselves, with the columns they are bound to.
-- A policy with no predicate rows is a policy that does nothing.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), sp.name)                AS PolicyName,
              CONVERT(varchar(10),   spp.security_policy_predicate_type) AS PredicateType,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), spp.schema_name + '.' + spp.predicate) AS PredicateFunction,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),   spp.filter_predicate)       AS IsFilter
FROM   sys.security_policy_predicates AS spp
JOIN   sys.security_policies           AS sp ON sp.object_id = spp.policy_id
ORDER BY PolicyName;
GO
PredicateSELECTUPDATEDELETEINSERT
FIL onlyRow hidden if predicate is 0Only rows the caller can see are updated; a set-based UPDATE touches zero rows for a hidden keyOnly rows the caller can see are deletedNot applied. A hidden row can be written
BL onlyNot applied. Every row is readableThe resulting row is validated; an update to 0 is rejectedNot appliedRejected if the new row evaluates to 0
BothRow hidden if predicate is 0Only visible rows, and the result must satisfy the blockOnly visible rowsRejected if the new row evaluates to 0

Caveat. Dynamic Data Masking is a display control, and a determined or privileged user defeats it. The value is in the data pages, unmasked; the mask is applied on output to principals without UNMASKUNMASK. A caller with UNM sees the real value, and UNM is granted to db_o, to db_m and to the schema owner by default. A caller with AL on the table can remove the mask outright. And as the last sample shows, the masked value still participates in comparisons and groupings, so it leaks by inference. Treat DDM as protection against a well-meaning colleague or an over-broad ad-hoc query, never as a control against a hostile principal; for that, use Always Encrypted (9.5) or encrypt the value before it is stored.

Applies to. RLS and DDM were both introduced in SQL Server 2016. RLS is available in every edition. DDM is Enterprise and Developer edition on SQL Server 2016 and 2017, and was extended to Standard and Express editions from SQL Server 2019. On SQL Server 2022 and 2025 both are available in all editions. The filter predicate is evaluated once per row and its cost is part of every query against the table, so a predicate function that performs a subquery against a large access table will dominate the query; index the columns the predicate filters on, or denormalise the access mapping onto the protected table. Also note that RLS does not apply to a table accessed through a view or a table-valued function unless that module is signed (9.6), because the predicate would otherwise be evaluated against the module owner rather than the caller.

9.5 Encryption at rest, in transit and Always Encrypted

Two features that are constantly confused protect two different things. Transparent Data Encryption encrypts the database files so that a stolen file or a stolen backup is unreadable without the key; it does nothing to protect the data from anyone who can query the instance. Always Encrypted encrypts individual columns with a key the database engine never holds, so that the engine itself — and anyone with server or database administrator rights — cannot read them.

Transparent Data Encryption (TDE)Always Encrypted
ProtectsThe database file, the transaction log and the backup at restSpecific columns, from the moment the value leaves the client
Key locationA certificate in mas; the data encryption key is in the user databaseThe column master key is outside the database, in the Windows certificate store or a hardware device
Who is stoppedAnyone with the file or the backup and no certificateA SQL Server administrator, a db_o, and anyone who can read the files or a backup
Who is not stoppedAny user with SEL, and any administratorA user who can obtain the plaintext through the client
Query costNone from the application’s point of view; AES-256 costs some CPU on older hardwareNon-trivial: the client must encrypt parameters, and rich queries on encrypted columns require a secure enclave or are restricted
New inSQL Server 2005, Enterprise; Standard and Express from SQL Server 2019SQL Server 2017, all editions; rich queries with secure enclaves from SQL Server 2019
-- Transparent Data Encryption. Three steps: a certificate in master, a data
-- encryption key in the user database protected by that certificate, and SET
-- ENCRYPTION ON. The key must be backed up; without the certificate and its
-- private key the database is permanently unreadable.
GO
USE [master];
GO
CREATE CERTIFICATE [TdeCert_Sales]
    WITH SUBJECT = 'TDE certificate for Sales';
GO

USE [Sales];
GO
CREATE DATABASE ENCRYPTION KEY
    WITH ALGORITHM = AES_256,
         ENCRYPTION BY SERVER CERTIFICATE [TdeCert_Sales];
GO
ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET ENCRYPTION ON;
GO

-- Back up the key, once, and store the password-protected file with the
-- certificate backup. This is the step that is skipped, and it is the
-- reason a restore of a TDE database fails with 'certificate not found'.
GO
USE [master];
GO
BACKUP CERTIFICATE [TdeCert_Sales]
    TO FILE = 'C:\Backup\TdeCert_Sales.cer'
    WITH PRIVATE KEY (
        FILE = 'C:\Backup\TdeCert_Sales.key',
        ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault'
    );
GO

-- State of every encrypted database on the instance. encryption_state:
-- 0 no key, 1 unencrypted, 2 encryption in progress, 3 encrypted,
-- 4 key change in progress, 5 decryption in progress, 6 protection change.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), d.name)            AS DatabaseName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  k.encryption_state)  AS EncryptionState,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  k.key_algorithm)     AS Algorithm,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  k.key_length)        AS KeyLength,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), k.encryptor_thumbprint) AS CertificateThumbprint,
              k.percent_complete
FROM   sys.dm_database_encryption_keys AS k
JOIN   sys.databases AS d ON d.database_id = k.database_id;
GO
-- Restoring a TDE database. The order is not negotiable: restore the
-- certificate and its private key into master on the target instance, then
-- restore the database. The private key must have been exported with a
-- password, not a Windows key protection provider, or it cannot be
-- restored to a different service account.
GO
USE [master];
GO
CREATE CERTIFICATE [TdeCert_Sales]
    FROM FILE = 'C:\Backup\TdeCert_Sales.cer'
    WITH PRIVATE KEY (
        DECRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault'
    );
GO
RESTORE DATABASE [Sales]
    FROM DISK = 'C:\Backup\Sales.bak'
    WITH MOVE 'Sales' TO 'C:\Data\Sales.mdf',
         MOVE 'Sales_log' TO 'C:\Data\Sales_log.ldf',
         REPLACE, RECOVERY;
GO

-- Turn it off. Decryption rewrites every page and the log grows to roughly
-- the size of the database, so this is not a fast operation and the log
-- must have room for it.
GO
ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET ENCRYPTION OFF;
GO

-- Rotate the key. The data encryption key is re-encrypted under a new
-- certificate; the data is not re-encrypted, so this is fast compared with
-- the initial SET ENCRYPTION ON.
GO
-- USE Sales;
-- CREATE CERTIFICATE TdeCert_Sales2 WITH SUBJECT = 'TDE certificate for Sales 2';
-- ALTER DATABASE ENCRYPTION KEY REGENERATE WITH ALGORITHM = AES_256;
-- ALTER DATABASE ENCRYPTION KEY ENCRYPTION BY SERVER CERTIFICATE TdeCert_Sales2;
-- SELECT * FROM sys.dm_database_encryption_keys;
GO
-- Always Encrypted. The column master key (CMK) lives outside the database, in
-- the Windows certificate store or a hardware security module. The column
-- encryption key (CEK) is created by the client and stored in the database,
-- encrypted by the CMK. The database engine holds the encrypted CEK and
-- cannot use it.
--
-- Deterministic encryption preserves equality and ordering on the ciphertext
-- and leaks equality patterns, but permits an equality join and a seek.
-- Randomized encryption protects the value and its distribution but forbids
-- both. Which to use is a documented decision per column, not a default.
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ADD
    Email nvarchar(320) ENCRYPTED BY (
        COLUMN_ENCRYPTION_KEY = [CEK_Customer],
        ENCRYPTION_TYPE = Deterministic_Encryption,
        ALGORITHM = 'AEAD_AES_256_CBC_HMAC_SHA_256'),
    NationalInsuranceNumber nvarchar(20) ENCRYPTED BY (
        COLUMN_ENCRYPTION_KEY = [CEK_Customer],
        ENCRYPTION_TYPE = Randomized_Encryption,
        ALGORITHM = 'AEAD_AES_256_CBC_HMAC_SHA_256');
GO

-- From the engine's side the column is a varbinary. Nothing in the table,
-- the log, the backup or a memory dump contains the plaintext.
GO
SELECT name, TYPE_NAME(type_id) AS EngineType, max_length
FROM   sys.columns
WHERE object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.Customer')
  AND name IN ('Email', 'NationalInsuranceNumber');
GO

-- Encryption metadata held in the database: which columns are encrypted,
-- with which key and which mode. The key itself is present but encrypted.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(120), c.name)               AS ColumnName,
              CONVERT(varchar(120), k.name)               AS ColumnEncryptionKey,
              CONVERT(varchar(30),  e.encryption_type_desc) AS EncryptionType,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  k.algorithm)            AS Algorithm
FROM       sys.always_encryption_columns AS e
JOIN       sys.columns AS c ON c.object_id = e.object_id AND c.column_id = e.column_id
JOIN       sys.column_encryption_keys AS k ON k.column_encryption_key_id = e.column_encryption_key_id;
GO

-- The RESTRICTED_USER mode additionally prevents a user without the key from
-- seeing the ciphertext in a SELECT *.
GO
ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET RESTRICTED_USER ON;
GO

-- The engine's refusal, which is the feature working. These statements are
-- rejected because the engine cannot decrypt and therefore cannot resolve
-- the comparison or the index seek.
GO
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE Email = 'known@example.com';  -- Msg 41322
-- SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE NationalInsuranceNumber LIKE '%12%'; -- Msg 41322
GO
-- Encryption in transit. Three settings, all default-on since SQL Server 2012
-- and all enforced from SQL Server 2019.
--
-- 1. Encrypt: certificates are issued and trusted by both ends.
-- 2. TrustServerCertificate: NO, so the server's certificate name and chain
--    are verified by the client. Setting it to YES is the single most common
--    way to turn encryption into obfuscation against a passive observer.
-- 3. The certificate must match the connected server name, so connecting to
--    a FQDN the certificate was not issued for fails.
GO
EXEC sys.sp_configure 'force encryption', 1;   -- 1 = force encryption for all client connections
RECONFIGURE;
GO

-- The connection string, which is where the decision is really made.
-- Encrypt=Mandatory is SQL Server 2022 and later; Encrypt=True is the
-- 2019-era equivalent and the only value older clients understand.
--
--   Server=sql01.contoso.local;Database=Sales;Integrated Security=True;
--   Encrypt=Mandatory;TrustServerCertificate=False;
GO

-- On Linux, the protocol level and the certificate paths live in
-- mssql.conf under [network] and [network.ssl].
--   [EULA]        accepted = true
--   [network]     tls = strict
--   [network.ssl] certificate = /etc/opt/mssql/mssql.pem
--               key         = /etc/opt/mssql/mssql.key
GO
-- sudo /opt/mssql/bin/mssql-conf set network.tls strict
-- sudo systemctl restart mssql-server
GO

-- Confirm what the client negotiated. A client that silently falls back to
-- an unencrypted connection is the failure this query detects.
GO
-- SELECT session_id, encrypt_option, protocol_type, auth_scheme
-- FROM sys.dm_exec_connections WHERE session_id = @@SPID;
--   encrypt_option: FALSE = not encrypted, TRUE = encrypted, STRICT = required
GO

-- OpenSSL, against the instance, which is the check to script for a
-- certificate-expiry alert.
-- openssl s_client -connect sql01.contoso.local:1433 -servername sql01.contoso.local -showcerts
GO
-- Backup encryption, which is a third mechanism and is frequently confused
-- with the other two. It encrypts the backup file itself, so the backup is
-- protected on the media and on any copy of it, and the source database is
-- unaffected. It is additive to TDE and independent of Always Encrypted.
GO
-- The service master key lives in master and protects the server's other
-- keys. Back it up first; everything else depends on it.
USE [master];
GO
BACKUP SERVICE MASTER KEY TO FILE = 'C:\Backup\SMK.bak'
    ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault';
GO

CREATE CERTIFICATE [Cert_BackupEncrypt]
    WITH SUBJECT = 'Backup encryption certificate',
         EXPIRY_DATE = '2030-12-31';
GO

CREATE MASTER KEY ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault';
GO

-- An asymmetric key: the public key encrypts the backup's symmetric key and
-- the private key decrypts it, so the private key can live on a different
-- machine from the backup.
GO
CREATE ASYMMETRIC KEY [AK_BackupEncrypt]
    ENCRYPTION FOR CERTIFICATE = [Cert_BackupEncrypt]
    ALGORITHM = RSA_2048;
GO
USE [Sales];
GO
CREATE SYMMETRIC KEY [SK_BackupEncrypt]
    ENCRYPTION FOR SERVER CERTIFICATE [Cert_BackupEncrypt],
    ALGORITHM = AES_256;
GO

BACKUP DATABASE [Sales] TO DISK = 'C:\Backup\Sales.bak'
    WITH ENCRYPTION (
        ALGORITHM = AES_256,
        ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault',
        SERVER CERTIFICATE = [Cert_BackupEncrypt]
    ),
    COMPRESSION, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;
GO

-- The deprecated form, which put the password in the backup header and is
-- listed here only so that an older script is recognised.
GO
-- BACKUP DATABASE Sales TO DISK = '...' WITH ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = '...';  -- deprecated
GO

-- Verify that a backup is encrypted, and by what. has_backup_encryption
-- and key_algorithm are the columns to check before trusting a restore
-- procedure written by somebody else.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), b.database_name)      AS DatabaseName,
              CONVERT(varchar(260), b.physical_device_name) AS BackupFile,
              b.has_backup_encryption,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  b.key_algorithm)        AS KeyAlgorithm,
              b.encryptor_thumbprint, b.encryptor_type
FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset AS b
ORDER BY b.backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

Which to choose. TDE answers "someone stole the file or the backup". Always Encrypted answers "someone with full control of the server wants to read one column". They are complementary and both should be on in a system that holds regulated data. The operational cost of Always Encrypted is that the client application must be configured with the column master key location and must retrieve it; a query that touches an encrypted column from a client without the key returns a ciphertext value or an error, and rich operations such as LI, GROUP BYGROUP BY and index seeks are not possible on a deterministically encrypted column without a secure enclave. Always Encrypted with secure enclaves, first shipped in SQL Server 2019, allows computation on encrypted columns inside a trusted enclave and is the only way to make the feature usable for anything beyond equality. Finally, TDE on a log backup chain constrains the restore options: a database or log backup of a TDE database can only be restored to a version of SQL Server that supports TDE, and an encrypted database cannot be attached by an older instance.

9.6 Auditing, session context and module signing

SQL Server Audit writes an audit trail to a file, the Windows Security log, or an Azure Storage location, from specifications that name which events to record. It is the compliance feature; the older trace-based auditing is deprecated. Session context is a place to record who a request claims to be, which is useful and which is not a security control. Module signing is the modern answer to the problem EXECUTE AS OWNEREXECUTE AS OWNER solves badly.

-- SQL Server Audit. Three objects: a server audit (the destination and the
-- options), an audit specification (which events), and optionally a database
-- audit specification attached to it.
GO
-- Create the audit. A file path per instance is the usual destination; the
-- rollover and size settings bound the disk the audit can consume, and
-- RESERVE_DISK_SPACE makes the instance fail to start rather than silently
-- fill the volume.
GO
CREATE SERVER AUDIT [Audit_Security]
TO FILE ('C:\Audit\Security_<year>-<month>-<day>.sqlaudit')
WITH (
    MAX_ROLLOVER_FILES = 30,
    MAX_SIZE = 1024,          -- MB per file
    RESERVE_DISK_SPACE = ON
);
GO

-- Turn it on. Without ALTER SERVER AUDIT STATE = ON it records nothing,
-- and the failure is silent.
ALTER SERVER AUDIT [Audit_Security] WITH (STATE = ON);
GO

-- Server-level object access, including successful and failed attempts.
-- This is the specification to write first: it answers "who tried to do
-- what to which object, and did it work".
GO
CREATE SERVER AUDIT SPECIFICATION [Audit_ServerObjectAccess]
TO [Audit_Security]
ADD (AUDIT_SERVER_OBJECT_ACCESS_EVENT GROUP, WITH (AUDIT_FAILURE, WITH (AUDIT_SUCCESS)))
WITH (STATE = ON);
GO

-- A database-level specification for DDL, which is what a change-control
-- requirement usually asks for.
GO
CREATE DATABASE AUDIT SPECIFICATION [Audit_SalesDDL]
TO [Audit_Security]
ADD (AUDIT_SCHEMA_OBJECT_CHANGE_EVENT_GROUP GROUP)
WITH (STATE = ON);
GO
-- Reading the audit. fn_audit_file is a table-valued function over the file, so
-- it takes a path pattern rather than a filename, and the caller needs
-- ALTER ANY AUDIT or the ALTER permission on the audit object.
GO
SELECT event_time,
              CONVERT(varchar(60),  session_id)            AS SessionId,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), server_principal_name)  AS LoginName,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), database_principal_name) AS DatabaseUser,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), object_name)            AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(80),  statement)              AS StatementText,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  succeeded)             AS Succeeded,
              CONVERT(varchar(40),  permission_bitmask)     AS PermissionBits,
              CONVERT(varchar(30),  client_ip)               AS ClientIp,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), application_name)        AS ApplicationName
FROM   sys.fn_audit_file('C:\Audit\Security_*.sqlaudit', DEFAULT, NULL, NULL)
WHERE  event_time > DATEADD(HOUR, -24, SYSUTCDATETIME())
ORDER BY event_time DESC;
GO

-- Audit status: is it running, is it recording, and how much has it lost.
-- A non-zero not_recorded_count is an audit that silently dropped events.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), a.name)              AS AuditName,
              CONVERT(varchar(10),  s.status_desc)         AS Status,
              s.audit_file_path, s.not_recorded_count,
              s.user_defined_event_count, s.audit_file_size
FROM   sys.dm_server_audit_status AS s
JOIN   sys.server_audits         AS a ON a.audit_id = s.audit_id;
GO

-- What each event group actually contains, so that the specification that
-- was written can be checked against what it will record.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(60),  a.name)        AS AuditAction,
              CONVERT(varchar(120), a.class_desc)   AS ObjectClass,
              CONVERT(varchar(30),  a.major_id_desc) AS ObjectType
FROM   sys.dm_audit_actions AS a
WHERE  CONVERT(varchar(60), a.name) LIKE '%OBJECT%'
ORDER BY AuditAction;
GO
-- CONTEXT_INFO: 128 bytes of nvarchar, per session, entirely ignored by the
-- engine. Deprecated in SQL Server 2012 in favour of SESSION_CONTEXT, which
-- is the same idea done properly: named keys, fifty of them, a typed value.
GO
-- The three failure modes that make it unsuitable as anything but a hint:
--   - a SET that affects no rows leaves the previous value in place, so the
--     context silently describes the previous request
--   - a value longer than 128 characters is truncated silently
--   - any principal that can execute a statement can set it
GO
-- SET CONTEXT_INFO @Context;                     -- the write
-- SELECT CONTEXT_INFO() AS RequestContext;    -- the read
GO

-- SESSION_CONTEXT: the supported mechanism. Named, typed, and NULL when
-- never set, so the stale-value failure cannot occur by accident.
GO
EXEC sys.sp_set_session_context @key = 'RequestId', @read_only = 0, @value = 'A1B2C3D4-0000-0000-0000-000000000001';
EXEC sys.sp_set_session_context @key = 'UserId',    @read_only = 1, @value = 4711;
-- @read_only = 1 makes the value immutable for the rest of the session, which
-- is the right choice for an identity: nothing later in the connection can
-- change it, and a second call to set it raises Msg 3930.
GO

SELECT CONVERT(nvarchar(128), key)                                   AS ContextKey,
              CONVERT(nvarchar(128), CAST(value AS nvarchar(128))) AS ContextValue,
              read_only
FROM   sys.dm_exec_session_contexts AS c
WHERE c.session_id = @@SPID;
GO

-- Reading the immutable one back, which is the value to record in a log
-- table alongside ORIGINAL_LOGIN() and USER_NAME().
SELECT UserId = TRY_CAST(TRY_CONVERT(nvarchar(128), value) AS int)
FROM   sys.dm_exec_session_contexts AS c
WHERE c.session_id = @@SPID AND c.key = 'UserId';
GO

-- Across the instance: which sessions have set a context, and what it says.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(40),   s.session_id)  AS SessionId,
              CONVERT(varchar(128),  s.login_name)  AS LoginName,
              CONVERT(nvarchar(128), c.key)       AS ContextKey,
              CONVERT(nvarchar(128), CAST(c.value AS nvarchar(128))) AS ContextValue
FROM       sys.dm_exec_session_contexts AS c
INNER JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions       AS s ON s.session_id = c.session_id
ORDER BY s.session_id, c.key;
GO
-- Module signing: the answer to "grant indirect access without granting
-- db_owner". A certificate, a key, and a signature on the module. The module
-- runs with the certificate owner's permissions, and the owner is a role
-- that holds only what the body needs.
GO
-- 1. A role with the minimum permissions the module body requires.
GO
CREATE ROLE [mod_OrderSummaryPermissions];
GO
GRANT SELECT ON dbo.SalesOrder     TO [mod_OrderSummaryPermissions];
GRANT SELECT ON dbo.SalesOrderLine TO [mod_OrderSummaryPermissions];
GO

-- 2. A symmetric key protected by a certificate, and the module signed with
--    the key. The certificate is the permission anchor; the key is what the
--    signature is made with.
GO
CREATE CERTIFICATE [ModSign_Sales]
    WITH SUBJECT = 'Module signing certificate for Sales';
GO
CREATE SYMMETRIC KEY [ModSignKey_Sales]
    ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = 'a-long-password-stored-in-a-vault'
    ENCRYPTION FOR CERTIFICATE = [ModSign_Sales];
GO

-- 3. The module. It is not owned by the role and does not need
--    EXECUTE AS: the signature is what binds it to the certificate.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SELECT o.CustomerID, COUNT(*) AS LineCount
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder     AS o
    JOIN   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
    GROUP BY o.CustomerID;
END;
GO

-- 4. Sign it, and tell the engine which certificate to trust. Order
--    matters: sign before the first execution, because signing invalidates
--    the module's cached plans.
GO
ADD SIGNATURE TO OBJECT dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5
    BY CERTIFICATE = [ModSign_Sales];
GO
ALTER SERVER CONFIGURATION ADD SIGNATURE TO OBJECT dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5 BY CERTIFICATE = ModSign_Sales;
GO

-- 5. The caller needs only EXECUTE on the module.
GO
GRANT EXECUTE ON dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5 TO [app_svc];
GO
-- The second reason to sign: a signed view or table-valued function is a
-- separate execution context, so the row-level security predicate is
-- evaluated against the table owner rather than the caller. Without the
-- signature, an RLS policy on the base table is not applied through the
-- view, which is a data-disclosure bug rather than a performance one.
GO
-- CREATE OR ALTER VIEW dbo.vw_CustomerRestricted
-- AS SELECT CustomerID, LastName FROM dbo.Customer;
-- GO
-- ADD SIGNATURE TO OBJECT dbo.vw_CustomerRestricted BY CERTIFICATE = [ModSign_Sales];
GO

-- The third: the describe functions require a signed module, which is what
-- allows an object-level permission check without granting the permission.
GO
-- EXECUTE AS USER = 'app_svc';
-- SELECT * FROM sys.dm_exec_describe_first_result_set_for_object(
--     OBJECT_ID('dbo.usp_OrderSummary_v5'), 0);   -- permitted, signed
-- REVERT;
GO

-- Who is signed, by what, and under whose authority the module then runs.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), o.name)  AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), c.name)  AS CertificateName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  c.type_desc) AS CertType,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), p.name)  AS AuthorityPrincipal,
              pr.thumbprint
FROM       sys.database_principals AS p
LEFT JOIN sys.cryptography_properties AS pr ON pr.thumbprint = p.thumbprint
LEFT JOIN sys.certificates             AS c  ON c.thumbprint = pr.thumbprint
LEFT JOIN sys.objects                AS o  ON o.principal_id = p.principal_id
WHERE pr.thumbprint IS NOT NULL
   AND p.type = 'R'
ORDER BY ObjectName;
GO

-- The signed-object list, which is the query to keep in a change record.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), SCHEMA_NAME(o.schema_id)) AS SchemaName,
              CONVERT(varchar(128), o.name)           AS ObjectName,
              CONVERT(varchar(20),  o.type_desc)      AS TypeDesc
FROM   sys.objects AS o
JOIN   sys.dm_database_referencing_entities AS d ON d.referenced_id = o.object_id
WHERE o.type IN ('P', 'V', 'FN', 'IF', 'TF', 'TR')
ORDER BY SchemaName, ObjectName;
GO

Applies to. SQL Server Audit is available in all editions from SQL Server 2008. Trace Audit, the older mechanism, was deprecated in SQL Server 2017 in favour of SQL Server Audit; the audit levelaudit level trace option is the legacy equivalent of a Common Criteria audit and should be read only to understand an existing configuration. Module signing is available in every edition from SQL Server 2005 and is unchanged in SQL Server 2025. SESSION was introduced in SQL Server 2016 and CONTEX is deprecated from the same version. sp_set_co is the legacy form of sp_set_sess and is deprecated.

Caveat. CONTEX and SESSION are not security controls. Any principal that can execute a statement can set them, and a value written with @read_only = 0@read_only = 0 can be replaced later in the same session by any code sharing that connection — including a connection-pooled client running a different request. A log table that records the context value records what the application said, not what the engine verified. If the value must be trustworthy, bind it at connection time with @read_only = 1@read_only = 1 and record ORIGINAL_LOGIN()ORIGINAL_LOGIN() and USER_NAME()USER_NAME() alongside it. Also note that neither context survives a connection-pool reset unless the pool re-initialises it, which is an argument for treating the value as per-request state even though the mechanism is session-scoped. Finally, an audit specification is not a guarantee: not_recor in sys.dm_server is the number of events the audit failed to write, and it is non-zero on a busy instance with an undersized file target.

Exercises 9

  1. Query SERVERPROPERTY('IsIntegrated')SERVERPROPERTY('IsIntegrated') and list every login in sys.server with its type and authentication policy. Identify any login that should not exist, and report s’s is_di value.
  2. Create a contained database user in a database with CONTAINED_DATABASE = ONCONTAINED_DATABASE = ON, grant it db_dat, connect from a client that knows only the database name, and confirm the connection succeeds without a server login existing.
  3. As a test user, run sys.fn_my_permissions('dbo.SalesOrder', 'OBJECT')sys.fn_my_permissions('dbo.SalesOrder', 'OBJECT') and record the result. Grant the user SEL through a role, re-run it, then add a column-level DE and re-run it a third time. Record all three outputs.
  4. Grant SELECT ON dbo.Customer TO some_roleSELECT ON dbo.Customer TO some_role, add the user to the role, then DENY SELECT ON dbo.Customer TO some_roleDENY SELECT ON dbo.Customer TO some_role. Reverse the order of the two statements in a second test. Confirm the outcome is identical and explain why.

Then run a WHERE Email = '...known value...'WHERE Email = '...known value...' predicate as the masked caller and note what it reveals.

Map each permissio to the permission it represents, and check not_recorded_countnot_recorded_count.

10 · T-SQL Patterns and Data Manipulation

10.1 Batches, the GO separator and dynamic SQL10.2 Temp tables, global temp tables and tempdb10.3 Table variables and the 1,000-row estimate10.4 Cursors, APPLY and PIVOT10.5 Error handling, transactions and the transaction count10.6 Bulk load, batch insert and identifier generation

This chapter is the working syntax of T-SQL: how a script is divided, how a statement is built at run time, where an intermediate result lives, what the optimiser believes about it, how a row-at-a-time loop is written when one is unavoidable, how an error is caught and a transaction unwound correctly, and how rows are moved in bulk and given identifiers. Every section here is a pattern that recurs in production code, and every one of them has a failure mode that is silent rather than loud.

10.1 Batches, the GO separator and dynamic SQL

A batch is one unit of parsing, name resolution, compilation and execution. The T-SQL language has no statement for ending a batch, because a batch is a client concept: the client decides where one ends and sends the text. G is that client instruction, and it is the reason a large number of otherwise-correct scripts fail.

-- The whole of the GO rule. A module must be the first statement in its
-- batch, so a CREATE must be preceded by a batch boundary.
GO
-- Msg 111: 'CREATE PROCEDURE must be the first statement in a query batch.'
DECLARE @x int = 1;
CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_Wrong AS SELECT @x;
GO

-- Correct: the batch ends after the DECLARE.
GO
DECLARE @x int = 1;
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_Right AS SELECT @x AS x;   -- Msg 137, different problem: the variable did not survive GO
GO

-- A module body has no GO: the client never sees inside it.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_TwoStatements
AS
BEGIN
    SELECT 1 AS a;
    SELECT 2 AS b;
    -- GO here would be a syntax error.
END;
GO

The same first-statement rule applies to CREATE VIEWCREATE VIEW, CREATE FUNCTIONCREATE FUNCTION, CREATE TRIGGERCREATE TRIGGER, CREATE PROCEDURECREATE PROCEDURE, CREATE RULECREATE RULE and CREATE DEFAULTCREATE DEFAULT. The AL forms have no such restriction, which is one reason an idempotent deployment script uses CREATE OR ALTERCREATE OR ALTER preceded by a boundary rather than a conditional IF OBJECT_ID ... CREATE ... ELSE ALTER ...IF OBJECT_ID ... CREATE ... ELSE ALTER ... construction, which needs a batch boundary of its own for each branch.

-- Dynamic SQL, three forms. Only the third is safe to use with values.
GO

-- 1. EXEC with a literal. The text is fixed, so the plan is cached once
--    and reused. This is fine.
EXEC ('SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder');
GO

-- 2. EXEC with a concatenated string. A different value produces a
--    different cache entry, and a quote in the value produces an error.
--    There is no reason to write this.
DECLARE @Country char(2) = 'DE';
EXEC ('SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dbo.Customer WHERE Country = ''' + @Country + '''');
GO

-- 3. sp_executesql. Values are parameters and never become syntax.
DECLARE @Country char(2) = 'DE';
DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dbo.Customer WHERE Country = @c';

EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql, N'@c char(2)', @Country;
GO
-- Identifiers cannot be parameterised. A procedure that takes a table
-- name must concatenate it, and must quote it. QUOTENAME is the only
-- safe way: it adds the delimiters and doubles any embedded one, so
-- 'My]Table' becomes '[My]]Table]' and 'a'; DROP' becomes '[a]]; DROP'.
--
-- Note the schema: omitting it means the default schema of the calling
-- context resolves the name, which is a behaviour that changes with the
-- login. Always qualify.
GO
DECLARE @SchemaName sysname = 'sales',
        @TableName  sysname = 'My]Table',
        @Sql        nvarchar(4000);

DECLARE @Qualified nvarchar(517) =
    QUOTENAME(@SchemaName) + '.' + QUOTENAME(@TableName);

SET @Sql = N'SELECT COUNT(*) FROM ' + @Qualified;

EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
GO

-- A value that contains a quote. Never doubled by hand.
GO
DECLARE @Name nvarchar(200) = N'O''Brien and Sons';
DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = @n';
EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql, N'@n nvarchar(200)', @Name;
GO

-- Verify that the identifier resolves to what was intended, before
-- running anything against it.
SELECT Quoted = @Qualified,
       Resolved = OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(object_id),
       ObjectId  = object_id
FROM   sys.objects
WHERE object_id = OBJECT_ID(@Qualified);
GO
-- FORMAT versus CONVERT for dates. FORMAT is CLR-based, culture-aware and
-- slow; it returns nvarchar and is not deterministic in a way that
-- permits it in an index or a persisted computed column. CONVERT
-- with a style code is a native operation, is deterministic, and is
-- the correct choice for anything a machine reads.
GO
DECLARE @d date = '2026-09-28';

-- Native style codes, for machine consumption. Note 23 is ISO 8601.
SELECT ISO_8601      = CONVERT(char(10), @d, 23),      -- 2026-09-28
              ODBC_canonical = CONVERT(char(10), @d, 120),     -- 2026-09-28
              US_slash       = CONVERT(char(10), @d, 101),     -- 09/28/2026
              ISO_8601_time  = CONVERT(char(33), SYSDATETIME(), 126);
GO

-- FORMAT: correct, readable, and the wrong tool in a loop, a view,
-- a WHERE clause, an index expression or an ORDER BY on a large set.
SELECT Human          = FORMAT(@d, 'd MMMM yyyy', 'en-GB'),
              Human_invariant = FORMAT(@d, 'yyyy-MM-dd'),
              With_ticks       = FORMAT(@d, 'yyyyMMddHHmmss');
GO

-- The definitive comparison. FORMAT over 50,000 rows costs orders of
-- magnitude more CPU than CONVERT with style 23, for the same
-- output, because each call resolves the culture.
GO
SET STATISTICS TIME ON;
SELECT SUM(LEN(FORMAT(OrderDate, 'yyyy-MM-dd')))
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder;
SELECT SUM(LEN(CONVERT(char(10), OrderDate, 23)))
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder;
SET STATISTICS TIME OFF;
GO

-- A date that FORMAT can produce and CONVERT cannot: a culture that
-- has no equivalent style code. If a specific human format is
-- required, do it once at the presentation layer, not in the query.
SELECT FORMAT(CAST('2026-09-28' AS date), 'dddd d MMMM yyyy', 'fr-FR') AS FrenchLong;
GO
-- What a batch boundary actually costs, and why the client owns it. The same
-- text sent as one batch is parsed, name-resolved and compiled once; sent as
-- six batches, it is six times. For a 40,000-statement deployment script that
-- ratio decides whether the deployment takes seconds or minutes.
GO
-- One batch: one compile, and the whole script is a single unit of work.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_A AS SELECT 1 AS A;
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_B AS SELECT 2 AS B;
GO

-- Two batches, separated by GO. The client sends the text up to the first
-- GO, waits for the result, then sends the rest. Neither procedure is in the
-- other's scope.
GO
-- sp_executesql N'CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_A AS SELECT 1 AS A;';
-- sp_executesql N'CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_B AS SELECT 2 AS B;';
GO

-- The rule that follows: a module that another statement in the same batch
-- calls needs GO before the call, because the batch is compiled before it
-- runs. This batch fails with Msg 2812 at compile time, before the CREATE
-- has any effect.
GO
-- CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_C AS SELECT 3 AS C;
-- EXEC dbo.usp_C;              -- Msg 2812 in the same batch
GO
-- CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.usp_C AS SELECT 3 AS C;
-- GO
-- EXEC dbo.usp_C;              -- works in the next batch
GO
-- Escaping for dynamic SQL. Three mechanisms, and STRING_ESCAPE is the one
-- to use; the other two are the older answer and are deprecated.
GO
DECLARE @Value nvarchar(200) = N'O''Brien; DROP TABLE dbo.Customer; --';

-- Preferred: STRING_ESCAPE, introduced in SQL Server 2012. json is the
-- only escape type currently supported.
DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = ''' + STRING_ESCAPE(@Value, 'json') + '''';;

-- Deprecated: unicode(). Do not use in new code.
-- SET @Sql = N'SELECT * FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = ''' + unicode(@Value) + '''';
GO

-- Better still: do not build the literal at all. A parameter cannot contain
-- syntax, so the escaping question does not arise. The two forms are not
-- equivalent: the escaped-literal form is one plan, the parameterised form
-- is one plan per shape but is never reparsed for a new value.
GO
DECLARE @ParamSql nvarchar(4000) =
    N'SELECT CustomerID, LastName FROM dbo.Customer WHERE LastName = @name';

EXEC sys.sp_executesql @ParamSql, N'@name nvarchar(200)', @Value;
GO

-- The unicode() form is one reason dynamic SQL is still widely written by
-- concatenation: it predates STRING_ESCAPE by a decade, and its failure
-- mode is a syntax error rather than a wrong answer, so it is noisy when it
-- fails and invisible when it does not.
GO
SELECT StringEscape = STRING_ESCAPE(N'a''b\c
d', 'json');
GO

-- TRY_CONVERT and TRY_CAST, from the same release, are the third member of
-- the family and the one to use when a value may be malformed rather than
-- absent. Both return NULL instead of raising, and neither is usable in a
-- filtered index or a persisted computed column because they are not
-- deterministic.
SELECT BadInt   = TRY_CONVERT(int, 'not a number'),
              GoodInt  = TRY_CONVERT(int, '42'),
              BadDate  = TRY_CONVERT(date, '2026-13-45'),
              GoodDate = TRY_CAST(N'2026-09-28' AS date);
GO

New in SQL Server 2012. FOR and TRY_C and TRY_ and STRING were all introduced in SQL Server 2012 and are available in every edition from that version onward, unchanged in 2025. The unicode()unicode() function, the earlier mechanism for escaping a value for dynamic SQL, is deprecated and should not appear in new code. CON, also 2012, is the other function in this family and is the one to use instead of ++ for string building, because it treats NU as an empty string rather than propagating it — which is either helpful or dangerous depending on whether the propagation was the point.

10.2 Temp tables, global temp tables and tempdb

A temporary table is a real table with a generated name in tempdbtempdb. It has statistics, it can be indexed, it participates in transactions and it holds a log. The name is scoped: ## to the session, #### to the instance, and nothing at all to a table variable.

-- Local temp table. Visible only in this session, discarded when the
-- session ends, and nameable only by its own scope.
GO
CREATE TABLE #Staging
(
    SalesOrderID int            NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    CustomerID   int            NOT NULL,
    OrderDate   date           NOT NULL,
    TotalAmount decimal(19,4)    NOT NULL
);
GO

-- SELECT INTO creates and populates in one statement. The inferred
-- types and the nullability are consequences of the SELECT, not a
-- decision: an expression column arrives NOT NULL, and a nullable
-- source column with no rows produces an int-typed, NOT NULL column.
GO
SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.CustomerID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount
INTO   #Staging2
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
WHERE  o.OrderDate >= '2026-01-01';
GO

-- Index it. This is the point of using a temp table rather than a
-- table variable, and it is the step that is usually forgotten.
CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX IX_#Staging2
    ON #Staging2 (SalesOrderID);
GO

SELECT COUNT(*) AS RowsInStaging FROM #Staging2;
GO

-- The metadata. tempdb is a real database and this is a real catalog.
SELECT t.name AS TempTableName,
       t.create_date, t.row_count
FROM   tempdb.sys.tables AS t
WHERE t.name LIKE '#%' OR t.name LIKE '##%'
ORDER BY t.create_date DESC;
GO
-- Global temp table. Visible to every session on the instance, and dropped
-- when the creating session's last statement referencing it finishes.
-- This lifetime rule is the reason global temp tables are unsafe.
GO
CREATE TABLE ##SharedBatchState
(
    RunId     int          NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    StepName  nvarchar(100) NOT NULL,
    StartedAt datetime2(3)  NOT NULL
);
GO

-- Any session can now read and write it. Nothing in the name binds it
-- to a session, a login, a database or a purpose.
GO
-- SELECT * FROM ##SharedBatchState;   -- from any other session
-- DROP TABLE ##SharedBatchState;        -- from any other session
GO

-- A procedure is the correct owner of a global temp table: it is
-- created and dropped inside a scope the caller cannot reach.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_BatchStep @RunId int, @StepName nvarchar(100)
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    -- IF OBJECT_ID('tempdb..##SharedBatchState') IS NULL -- the check every
    -- caller would otherwise have to write, and which is racy in its
    -- own right.
    INSERT ##SharedBatchState (RunId, StepName, StartedAt)
    VALUES (@RunId, @StepName, SYSUTCDATETIME());
END;
GO

Caveat. A global temp table can be hijacked. Because the name is instance-wide and any principal with CREATE TABLECREATE TABLE in tem can create it, a caller that pre-creates ##SharedBatchState##SharedBatchState before your procedure runs causes your CREATE TABLECREATE TABLE to fail with error 2714, There is already an object named 'SharedBatchState' in the database, or — worse — succeeds against a table the caller created with a different shape, and your procedure writes into it. The same argument applies to the read path: any session can read from a global temp table, so nothing written into one is private. Use a local temp table, or a procedure-scoped #### table whose name is unpredictable, or a table variable, and never treat a global temp table as a private channel.

-- tempdb: what it costs and how to watch it. Every temporary table,
-- every table variable, every sort spill that exceeds memory, every
-- cursor, and the row-version store of every RCSI database land here.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(40), su.session_id)   AS SessionId,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), su.status)     AS Status,
       CONVERT(varchar(128),su.user_objects_alloc_page_count) AS UserObjectsPages,
       CONVERT(varchar(128),su.internal_objects_alloc_page_count) AS InternalObjectsPages
FROM   tempdb.sys.dm_db_session_space_usage AS su
ORDER BY (su.user_objects_alloc_page_count + su.internal_objects_alloc_page_count) DESC;
GO

-- Version store: rows retained for RCSI readers, and the thing that grows
-- tempdb under long-running read transactions. A non-zero count on an
-- otherwise idle instance means a transaction is holding versions it
-- can never release because it has not completed.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS VersionStoreRowCount
FROM   tempdb.sys.dm_tran_version_store;
GO

-- Allocations that could not be satisfied, which is the signal that
-- tempdb is under pressure and that the next allocation will fail.
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(40), type_desc)   AS AllocationType,
       COUNT(*)                          AS UnfulfilledCount
FROM   tempdb.sys.dm_db_file_space_usage AS f
GROUP BY type_desc;
GO

-- The long-running transaction that is pinning the version store.
-- tempdb does not shrink automatically after a spike, so the file
-- size is not a current measurement; the DMV values are.
GO
SELECT TOP(20)
       CONVERT(varchar(40), s.session_id) AS SessionId,
       CONVERT(varchar(128),s.login_name) AS LoginName,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), s.status)   AS Status,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), t.status)   AS TransactionStatus,
       t.transaction_begin_time
FROM       tempdb.sys.dm_tran_active_transactions AS t
INNER JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions            AS s ON s.session_id = t.session_id
WHERE t.transaction_begin_time < DATEADD(MINUTE, -10, SYSUTCDATETIME())
ORDER BY t.transaction_begin_time;
GO
-- Collation inheritance: a temp table created in a database with a
-- non-default collation keeps that collation, and a join or UNION
-- against a table with a different one fails with error 457, 'COLLATION
-- conflict', or silently coerces when one side is a literal.
GO
DECLARE @dbCollation sysname = CONVERT(sysname, DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Collation'));
SELECT DatabaseCollation = @dbCollation;
GO

-- Create the table with an explicit collation, or create it with a
-- column-level collation when the source is a different one.
GO
CREATE TABLE #MixedCollation
(
    Code nvarchar(10) COLLATE SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS NOT NULL
);
GO

-- The reliable pattern for staging heterogeneous input: normalise
-- with an explicit COLLATE on the converting side.
GO
SELECT c.Code COLLATE SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS AS Code_Normalised,
       COUNT(*) AS Rows
FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
GROUP BY c.Code COLLATE SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS;
GO
-- The transaction-rollback difference, which is the one that changed. Before
-- SQL Server 2017, rolling back a transaction did NOT roll back the contents
-- of a table variable: the rows stayed. That made a table variable unusable as
-- the accumulator of a unit of work that can fail, because a retry would see
-- the rows from the failed attempt. From 2017 the contents participate in the
-- transaction, and the two constructs are equivalent in that respect.
GO
DECLARE @t TABLE (Id int PRIMARY KEY, Note nvarchar(100));

BEGIN TRANSACTION;
INSERT @t VALUES (1, 'will be rolled back');
ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;

SELECT COUNT(*) AS RowsAfterRollback FROM @t;   -- 0 on 2017 and later
GO

-- Name resolution differs in the other direction, and it is a compile-time
-- error rather than a run-time one. A temporary table is resolved when the
-- statement executes, so a batch that creates it and then uses it in the
-- same batch works. A table variable is part of the batch that declares it, so
-- a reference to one created later in the same batch is Msg 137.
GO
-- CREATE TABLE #t (Id int);  SELECT * FROM #t;      -- works
-- SELECT * FROM @t;  DECLARE @t TABLE (Id int);          -- Msg 137
GO

-- Truncating and re-creating in a loop. The DROP plus CREATE is the
-- pattern that gives the optimiser the true shape; truncating only leaves
-- a temp table with statistics that describe a different cardinality.
GO
-- WHILE 1 = 1
-- BEGIN
--     IF OBJECT_ID('tempdb..#Staging') IS NOT NULL DROP TABLE #Staging;
--     CREATE TABLE #Staging (Id int NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY, Qty int NOT NULL);
--     INSERT #Staging SELECT ... FROM dbo.SourceTable;
--     -- work against #Staging
--     IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM #Staging) BREAK;
-- END
GO

-- Never assume a temp table exists. A procedure that assumes one was
-- created by its caller is a procedure that fails when the caller is a
-- different procedure, and the failure is on the second call because the
-- first call left the table behind in a session that is still open.
GO
-- IF OBJECT_ID('tempdb..#Staging') IS NULL
--     THROW 50004, '#Staging was not supplied by the caller.', 1;
GO
-- Concurrency on temp tables is a non-issue; concurrency on table variables is
-- a different question. A table variable is scoped to the declaration, so
-- two sessions can each have one with the same name. The risk is a
-- double-hash temp table, whose name is instance-wide.
GO
-- Session A                          |  Session B
-- CREATE TABLE ##BatchState (...)    |  CREATE TABLE ##BatchState (...)
--                                 |  -- Msg 2714: already an object named
--                                 |  -- 'BatchState' in the database
GO

-- Two sessions writing to the same ##table interleave without error. The last
-- COMMIT wins for a conflicting key, and the loser gets Msg 2627.
GO
-- EXECUTE AS LOGIN = 'CONTOSO\app_svc';    -- in session A
-- EXECUTE AS LOGIN = 'CONTOSO\app_etl';    -- in session B
-- Both run: INSERT INTO ##BatchState (RunId, StepName) VALUES (1, 'load');
GO

-- The safe global temp table, for the cases where a procedure genuinely
-- has to share state with other sessions. The name is unpredictable and
-- includes the session id, so it cannot be pre-created by a caller.
GO
-- DECLARE @Name nvarchar(300) = N'##BatchState_' + CONVERT(nvarchar(36), NEWID());
-- DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(max) = N'CREATE TABLE ' + QUOTENAME(@Name) + N' (RunId int NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY, StepName nvarchar(100) NOT NULL);';
-- EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
-- SET @Sql = N'INSERT INTO ' + QUOTENAME(@Name) + N' (RunId, StepName) VALUES (@r, @s);';
-- EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql, N'@r int, @s nvarchar(100)', @RunId, @StepName;
GO

-- Even that is read by any session. Anything sensitive belongs in a table
-- the caller owns, not in ##anything.
GO

Requirements for temp tables. tem should be placed on its own volume, sized to hold the largest expected concurrent staging set, and configured with a small number of data files. It does not shrink: once a spike has grown the file it stays that size, so a file that is 400 GB because of one runaway query is a capacity fact until it is manually resized. The tem version store is separate from the object store, and both are visible through tempdb.sys.dm_db_s; the version store is what a long-running read transaction under READ_COMMIT holds open. A table created by SELECT INTOSELECT INTO without an explicit ORDER BYORDER BY is populated in whatever order the plan produces, which is not a guarantee, and the result carries no statistics until the first statement that needs them creates them.

10.3 Table variables and the 1,000-row estimate

The claim that a table variable lives in memory and therefore performs well is the most persistent myth in T-SQL. A table variable is stored in tem like any other user table. What it actually does is tell the optimiser the exact shape of the data before the plan is built, and apply a fixed estimate of 1,000 rows to it regardless of how many rows it actually holds.

-- A table variable. The definition is part of the statement that uses it,
-- so the optimiser knows the column types and nullability exactly. It
-- is in tempdb. It is not in memory, as a guarantee, and the 2019
-- optimisation that keeps small table variables in memory is
-- conditional on the compiled plan, not a property of the construct.
GO
DECLARE @Filter TABLE
(
    CountryCode char(2)        NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    MinOrderDate date        NOT NULL
);

INSERT @Filter (CountryCode, MinOrderDate)
VALUES ('DE', '2026-01-01'), ('AT', '2025-06-01');
GO

-- The join. The optimiser applies a fixed estimate of 1,000 rows to
-- @Filter; here it actually holds 2. The join order it chooses is based
-- on 1000, and it will usually build a hash or merge join that would
-- have been wrong for 2 rows.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
INNER JOIN @Filter     AS f ON f.CountryCode = o.CountryCode
                          AND o.OrderDate  >= f.MinOrderDate;
GO

-- The mitigation available in every version: recompile, so the plan is
-- built after the table has been populated and the row count is real.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
INNER JOIN @Filter     AS f ON f.CountryCode = o.CountryCode
                          AND o.OrderDate  >= f.MinOrderDate
OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO

-- Confirm the estimate in the plan. Estimated rows on the table
-- variable scan is 1000 regardless of what the INSERT did.
GO
SELECT TRY_CAST(TRY_CONVERT(xml, qp.query_plan) AS Plan
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) AS qp
WHERE qs.text LIKE '%@Filter%';
GO
-- New in SQL Server 2022: deferred compilation for table variables.
-- Requires compatibility level 160 or higher. The optimiser delays
-- building the plan that references a table variable until the first
-- execution, at which point the actual row count is known and the 1,000-row
-- assumption is replaced by the real cardinality.
--
-- It applies only where the table variable is referenced. It does not
-- apply at compatibility levels below 160, and it is disabled by the
-- LEGACY_DEFERRED_COMPILATION database-scoped configuration.
GO
ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL = 170;   -- 170 = SQL Server 2025
GO

SELECT CONVERT(varchar(10), name)                 AS ConfigName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20), CONVERT(varchar(20), value)) AS ConfigValue
FROM   sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE name IN ('LEGACY_DEFERRED_COMPILATION', 'MEMORY_OPTIMIZED_ELEVATE_TO_SNAPSHOT',
              'PARAMETER_SNIFFING', 'LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION',
              'MAXDOP', 'PREVIEW_FEATURES');
GO

-- The dangerous case, unchanged by deferred compilation: a table
-- variable large enough that the 1,000-row assumption is a gross
-- under-estimate. An assumed 1,000 rows may justify a nested loop
-- over a clustered index that is catastrophic at 500,000 rows, and
-- the plan is chosen before the INSERT has run.
GO
DECLARE @Work TABLE (Id int NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY, Qty int NOT NULL);

INSERT @Work (Id, Qty)
SELECT TOP (500000) NEWID() AS Id, Qty
FROM   dbo.SalesOrderLine;
GO

SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   @Work     AS w
INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l ON l.SalesOrderID = w.Id
OPTION (RECOMPILE);   -- or use a temp table, which has real statistics
GO
Table variableLocal temp table
Storagetem, like any user tabletem, like any user table
StatisticsNone. The optimiser knows the schema and applies a fixed 1,000-row estimateReal, auto-created and auto-updated, and updatable with UPDATE STATISTICSUPDATE STATISTICS
Row-count accuracyExact, from deferred compilation at compatibility level 160+Exact, always
IndexesCan be created, but they produce no statistics and do not change the estimateFull range, and the optimiser uses them for costing
Known to the plan at compile timeYes — column names and typesNo — resolved at run time like a permanent table
Transaction rollbackYes, in SQL Server 2017 and later; before 2017 a transaction rollback did not roll back the contentsYes, in every version
Cardinality estimate effect on a planFrequently the wrong one; OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) is the standard remedyNone
Compile-time errorsYes — a reference to a table not yet created in the same batch is a compile errorNo — deferred name resolution applies
-- Reading the estimate, rather than inferring it. The estimated and actual row
-- counts for a table variable, from the last execution. This is the query
-- to run before rewriting a query that "should be fast".
GO
-- SET STATISTICS XML ON;  -- or just run the query and read the plan
--
-- SELECT q.query_plan
-- FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats qs
-- CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) q
-- WHERE qs.text LIKE '%@Filter%' ORDER BY qs.last_execution_time DESC;
GO

-- The same number without a plan, from the cached statement statistics:
-- the estimated rows and the actual rows for the whole statement, and the
-- ratio that says whether the plan was chosen on a wrong premise.
SELECT CONVERT(nvarchar(4000), qs.text)              AS StatementText,
              qs.execution_count, qs.last_rows,
              qs.last_execution_time,
              CONVERT(bigint, qs.total_logical_reads) AS TotalLogicalReads
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
WHERE qs.text LIKE '%@Filter%'
ORDER BY qs.last_execution_time DESC;
GO

-- The statistics question, asked of the catalog. A temp table has real
-- statistics; a table variable has none, and there is nothing to inspect.
-- This difference is the practical reason to prefer a temp table when the
-- row count can be large.
GO
-- SELECT s.name AS StatisticName, sp.stats_blob
-- FROM tempdb.sys.stats AS s
-- CROSS APPLY sys.dm_db_stats_properties(s.object_id, s.stats_id) AS sp
-- WHERE s.object_id = OBJECT_ID('tempdb..#Staging');
GO

-- The mitigation for a temp table that needs a better histogram, and for a
-- table variable that cannot have one: reduce the rows that reach the join.
-- Push the selective predicate into the table variable's own definition, so
-- the estimate applies to a small set, rather than joining a small set to a
-- large one and hoping the optimiser picks the right order.
GO
-- DECLARE @Ids TABLE (CustomerID int NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY);
-- INSERT @Ids (CustomerID)
-- SELECT TOP (100) CustomerID FROM dbo.SalesOrder
-- WHERE StatusCode = 2 ORDER BY SalesOrderID;   -- the TOP is the bound
--
-- SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder o
-- INNER JOIN @Ids i ON i.CustomerID = o.CustomerID
-- OPTION (RECOMPILE);
GO
-- Deferred compilation, configured and verified. The database-scoped
