-- configuration is the switch; the compatibility level is the gate; and a
-- per-statement RECOMPILE hint defeats it for that statement because the plan
-- is built after the first execution either way.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(5), compatibility_level) AS CompatLevel
FROM   sys.databases WHERE name = DB_NAME();
GO

-- 150 = 2019, 160 = 2022, 170 = 2025. A database upgraded in place keeps
-- its old level until ALTER DATABASE ... SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL runs, and
-- changing the level invalidates every cached plan on the database, so do it
-- in a deployment and not at random.
GO
-- ALTER DATABASE [Sales] SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL = 170;
-- EXEC sys.sp_recompile;   -- the optimiser will recompile on its own
GO

-- Turn it off per database, and confirm it took effect.
GO
-- ALTER DATABASE SCOPED CONFIGURATION SET LEGACY_DEFERRED_COMPILATION = ON;
-- SELECT name, value, value_for_secondary FROM sys.database_scoped_configurations
-- WHERE name = 'LEGACY_DEFERRED_COMPILATION';
GO

-- Other database-scoped configurations that interact with estimation and
-- are worth reading at the same time, because a plan that behaves oddly is
-- usually one of these rather than the table variable.
GO
SELECT CONVERT(varchar(50), name)                 AS Configuration,
              CONVERT(varchar(20), CONVERT(varchar(20), value)) AS Value
FROM   sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE name IN ('LEGACY_DEFERRED_COMPILATION',
              'LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION', 'PARAMETER_SNIFFING',
              'QUERY_OPTIMIZER_HOTFIXES',     'BYPASSRLS',
              'MAXDOP',                      'PREVIEW_FEATURES',
              'MEMORY_OPTIMIZED_ELEVATE_TO_SNAPSHOT')
ORDER BY Configuration;
GO

-- The instances where a table variable is the right choice regardless of the
-- estimate: a lookup list of a handful of keys, a small unpivot target, and a
-- recursive CTE's anchor, where the anchor set is small by construction. In
-- all three, 1,000 is close enough to the truth that the plan is fine.
GO
-- WITH CountryCodes AS (SELECT CAST(N'DE' AS char(2)) AS c UNION ALL SELECT 'AT' UNION ALL SELECT 'CH')
-- SELECT o.SalesOrderID FROM dbo.SalesOrder o
-- JOIN CountryCodes cc ON o.CountryCode = cc.c;
-- OPTION (MAXDOP 1);
GO
-- The in-memory question, which the section exists to answer, asked of the
-- engine. There is a real optimisation that keeps small table variables in
-- memory rather than in tempdb, but it is conditional on the compiled plan
-- and it is not a guarantee; the claim that a table variable is "always in
-- memory" is false and the memory grant is the reason it was ever believed.
GO
-- SET STATISTICS IO ON; DECLARE @t TABLE (Id int PRIMARY KEY, Pad nvarchar(200));
-- INSERT @t SELECT TOP (50) NEWID(), REPLICATE('x', 200) FROM sys.all_objects;
-- SELECT * FROM @t a CROSS JOIN @t b WHERE a.Id = b.Id;
--
-- Compare the logical reads against the same join through a temp table. The
-- table variable version reports reads against tempdb; the temp table version
-- reports reads against tempdb as well, and the difference is the overhead
-- of the object rather than of its location.
GO

-- The memory grant, which is the number that decides whether a large table
-- variable is paged to tempdb. serial_required_memory and serial_desired_memory
-- in sys.dm_exec_query_memory_grants are the figures to read for a cached
-- statement that uses one.
SELECT CONVERT(nvarchar(200), qs.text)                       AS StatementText,
              mg.requested_memory_kb, mg.granted_memory_kb,
              mg.used_memory_kb,     mg.ideal_memory_kb,
              mg.max_used_memory_kb
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_memory_grants AS mg
JOIN   sys.dm_exec_query_stats        AS qs
       ON qs.plan_handle = mg.plan_handle
WHERE qs.text LIKE '%@%'
ORDER BY mg.granted_memory_kb DESC;
GO

-- The size at which the fixed estimate stops being a rounding error. Below a few
-- hundred rows the loop plan chosen for 1,000 is the right one; above a few
-- thousand it is usually the wrong one, and the crossover depends on the
-- indexes on the other side of the join. The threshold to remember is not a
-- row count but the presence or absence of a usable index on the joined
-- column: a nested loop over a clustered key is cheap at 1,000 rows and
-- ruinous at 1,000,000.
GO
-- The three-row experiment, on the same data, in the same session:
--   1. join with the table variable, no hint, note the operator and the estimate
--   2. join with the table variable, OPTION (RECOMPILE), note the difference
--   3. join through a temp table with statistics, note the difference
-- If (1) and (2) differ materially, the fixed estimate is the cause and the
-- remedy is one of those two. If (2) and (3) differ, the table variable is not
-- the cause and something else is.
GO

Caveat. The 1,000-row estimate is not a small inaccuracy that evens out. It is a hard number the optimiser uses to choose between algorithms, and a wrong choice between a nested loop and a hash join is not a proportional slowdown — it is orders of magnitude, or a timeout. A table variable whose contents are small and unpredictable is the classic case: a list of identifiers read from a configuration table, joined against a large fact table, where the loop plan is chosen because 1,000 rows was assumed and 1,000,000 are present. The three remedies, in order of preference: use a temp table if the row count can be large; use a table variable with OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) if the row count is bounded and the statement runs once per request; use deferred compilation, which requires compatibility level 160 or higher and is not available on a database that has not been moved up.

Applies to. The 1,000-row estimate has been the documented behaviour since SQL Server 2000 and is unchanged in 2025; it is one of the few long-standing behaviours that has not been replaced. Deferred compilation for table variables is new in SQL Server 2022, is on by default at compatibility level 160 and 170, and can be turned off per database with the LEGACY_DEFERR database-scoped configuration. Transaction rollback of table variable contents is new in SQL Server 2017: before that version, rolling back a transaction left the table variable’s rows in place, which made a table variable unusable as the accumulator of a unit of work that could fail.

10.4 Cursors, APPLY and PIVOT

A cursor turns a set operation into a loop, and the loop re-enters the query engine once per row. It is the correct tool in a small number of cases, all of which share a property: the thing to be done next depends on the row just read. Everything else should be a set-based statement, and where a set-based statement is not obvious, the usual answer is APPLYAPPLY.

-- Cursor anatomy. The option list is a performance contract:
--   LOCAL        visible only in this scope; the default and the right one
--   FAST_FORWARD  FORWARD_ONLY + READ_ONLY + STATIC in one, and the only
--                combination that behaves as a forward-only set
--   STATIC       the result is copied into tempdb, so the base tables
--                are not locked for the life of the cursor
--   READ_ONLY    no UPDATE or DELETE through the cursor
--   SCROLL        enables PRIOR, LAST, ABSOLUTE; each one costs a
--                seek, and together they make the cursor read-write
GO
DECLARE @SalesOrderID int, @OrderDate date, @StatusCode int;

DECLARE OrderCursor CURSOR
    LOCAL FAST_FORWARD
FOR
    SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, StatusCode
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE  StatusCode = 2
    ORDER BY SalesOrderID;   -- deterministic order: required for anything resumable

OPEN OrderCursor;

FETCH NEXT FROM OrderCursor INTO @SalesOrderID, @OrderDate, @StatusCode;

WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
BEGIN
    -- One round trip per row, and the statement inside re-plans every time.
    EXEC sys.sp_executesql
         N'UPDATE dbo.SalesOrderLine SET Quantity = Quantity * 1.05 WHERE SalesOrderID = @id',
         N'@id int', @SalesOrderID;

    FETCH NEXT FROM OrderCursor INTO @SalesOrderID, @OrderDate, @StatusCode;
END

-- CLOSE releases the resources; DEALLOCATE removes the name. A cursor
-- that is CLOSEd and not DEALLOCATEd survives to the end of the batch
-- and reports 'A cursor with the name already exists' on redeclaration.
CLOSE OrderCursor;
DEALLOCATE OrderCursor;
GO

-- @@FETCH_STATUS values other than 0, which are silently ignored by
-- the WHILE @fetch_status = 0 idiom and must be handled explicitly:
--   0  a row was fetched
--  -1  no more rows in the result set
--  -2  the row a positioned FETCH pointed at was deleted
--  -9  the row is no longer in the result set
GO
-- The set-based equivalent of the cursor above: one statement, one plan,
-- no loop, and the optimiser can reorder the work.
GO
UPDATE l
SET    l.Quantity = l.Quantity * 1.05
FROM   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l
INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrder AS o ON o.SalesOrderID = l.SalesOrderID
WHERE o.StatusCode = 2;
GO

-- A cursor over a data modification, which is the only shape the
-- optimiser cannot express: the work depends on a value read from the
-- current row, such as a running total or a recursive walk.
GO
DECLARE @Id int, @ParentId int, @Depth int = 0;

DECLARE TreeCursor CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD
FOR
    SELECT CategoryID, ParentCategoryID
    FROM   dbo.Category
    WHERE  ParentCategoryID IS NULL;

OPEN TreeCursor;
FETCH NEXT FROM TreeCursor INTO @Id, @ParentId;

WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
BEGIN
    INSERT dbo.CategoryPath (CategoryID, Path)
    VALUES (@Id, CONVERT(varchar(500), REPLICATE('/', @Depth + 1) + CAST(@Id AS varchar(10)));

    -- The recursive half. This is the case a recursive CTE is for.
    UPDATE c
    SET    c.Depth = @Depth + 1
    FROM   dbo.Category AS c
    INNER JOIN dbo.CategoryPath AS p ON p.CategoryID = c.ParentCategoryID
    WHERE p.CategoryID = @Id;

    SET @Depth = @Depth + 1;
    FETCH NEXT FROM TreeCursor INTO @Id, @ParentId;
END

-- Hardening every cursor: a cap, so a logic error cannot loop forever,
-- and an OPTION on the SELECT, so the whole cursor runs as one plan shape.
CLOSE TreeCursor;
DEALLOCATE TreeCursor;
GO
-- APPLY: the set-based answer to "for each row on the left, run this".
-- CROSS APPLY keeps rows with no match; OUTER APPLY does not. This is
-- how a correlated subquery in the select list becomes a join the
-- optimiser can reorder and cost.
GO
-- Top 3 products per customer, computed set-based.
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.LastName, r.SalesOrderID,
       r.OrderDate, r.OrderTotal, r.RankInCustomer
FROM       dbo.Customer AS c
CROSS APPLY (
    SELECT TOP (3)
           so.SalesOrderID, so.OrderDate,
           so.TotalAmount AS OrderTotal,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER ( ORDER BY so.TotalAmount DESC, so.SalesOrderID ) AS RankInCustomer
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS so
    WHERE  so.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
    ORDER BY so.TotalAmount DESC, so.SalesOrderID
) AS r
ORDER BY c.CustomerID, r.RankInCustomer;
GO

-- APPLY against a table-valued function, which is the idiomatic pairing.
GO
SELECT c.CustomerID, c.LastName, t.OrderTotal, t.StatusCode
FROM       dbo.Customer AS c
OUTER APPLY (
    SELECT TOP (1) f.OrderTotal, f.StatusCode
    FROM   dbo.fn_OrdersForCustomer(c.CustomerID) AS f
    ORDER BY f.OrderTotal DESC
) AS t;
GO

-- PIVOT: rows to columns. The column list is a literal, not a
-- variable, so the shape of the result must be known at authoring time.
GO
SELECT o.OrderDate, p.[P1], p.[P2], p.[P3]
FROM   (
    SELECT CONVERT(date, l.OrderDate) AS OrderDate,
           N'P' + CONVERT(varchar(3), l.ProductID) AS ProductLabel,
           l.Quantity * l.UnitPrice  AS Amount
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS l
    WHERE  l.ProductID IN (1, 2, 3)
) AS src
PIVOT (
    SUM(src.Amount)
    FOR src.ProductLabel IN ([P1], [P2], [P3])
) AS p
ORDER BY o.OrderDate;
GO

-- UNPIVOT, the reverse, and the reason to prefer a normalised table to
-- a pivoted one in the first place: a pivoted result is not insertable
-- and not safely comparable between periods.
GO
SELECT u.OrderDate, u.ProductLabel, u.Amount
FROM   (
    SELECT OrderDate, [P1] AS [P1], [P2] AS [P2], [P3] AS [P3]
    FROM   dbo.MonthlyProductPivot
) AS piv
UNPIVOT (
    Amount FOR ProductLabel IN ([P1], [P2], [P3])
) AS u;
GO

When a cursor is genuinely required, three things reduce the damage: LOCAL FAST_FORWARDLOCAL FAST_FORWARD rather than the default GLO forward-only form, OPTION (FAST_FORWARD)OPTION (FAST_FORWARD) or an explicit option list on the SEL so the whole cursor uses one plan shape, and a batching loop over TOP (n)TOP (n) instead of a row-at-a-time loop, which reduces a million round trips to a few hundred while keeping the code simple. If the loop is a recursive walk or a running aggregate, a recursive CTE or a window function does it in one statement and there is no performance argument left to make.

-- The cost, measured. This is the comparison to run before accepting a cursor
-- in a review: the same logic as a set-based statement and as a loop, over
-- the same rows, with SET STATISTICS IO and TIME on for both.
GO
-- SET-BASED: one statement, one plan, one scan of the line table.
--
-- SET STATISTICS IO ON; SET STATISTICS TIME ON;
-- UPDATE l SET l.Quantity = l.Quantity * 1.05
-- FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine l
-- INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrder o ON o.SalesOrderID = l.SalesOrderID
-- WHERE o.StatusCode = 2 AND o.OrderDate >= '2026-01-01';
--
-- CURSOR: one plan per iteration, one round trip per row, and the same join
-- executed N times. The logical reads are the number that matters: they grow
-- with the row count even when the elapsed time looks acceptable for a
-- small table.
--
-- DECLARE @id int;
-- DECLARE c CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD FOR
--     SELECT SalesOrderID FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE StatusCode = 2;
-- OPEN c; FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id;
-- WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
-- BEGIN
--     UPDATE l SET l.Quantity = l.Quantity * 1.05
--     FROM dbo.SalesOrderLine l WHERE l.SalesOrderID = @id;
--     FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id;
-- END
-- CLOSE c; DEALLOCATE c;
GO

-- The batching pattern, which is the middle ground and is often the right
-- answer. A loop over TOP (n) rather than over rows: a few hundred iterations
-- instead of a few hundred thousand, and the set-based statement inside is
-- exactly the one that would be written without a loop at all.
GO
-- DECLARE @BatchSize int = 5000, @Rows int = 1;
-- WHILE @Rows > 0
-- BEGIN
--     UPDATE TOP (@BatchSize) l
--     SET    l.Quantity = l.Quantity * 1.05
--     FROM       dbo.SalesOrderLine l
--     INNER JOIN dbo.SalesOrder o ON o.SalesOrderID = l.SalesOrderID
--     WHERE o.StatusCode = 2 AND l.Processed = 0;
--     SET @Rows = @@ROWCOUNT;
-- END
GO

-- A recursion cap on any loop. A cursor over inserted with no cap, or a WHILE
-- whose termination condition depends on data the loop is changing, will run
-- until the log fills or the instance restarts.
GO
-- DECLARE @Iterations int = 0, @MaxIterations int = 1000;
-- DECLARE c CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD FOR SELECT SalesOrderID FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE StatusCode = 2;
-- OPEN c; FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id;
-- WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0 AND @Iterations < @MaxIterations
-- BEGIN
--     SET @Iterations = @Iterations + 1;
--     -- work
--     FETCH NEXT FROM c INTO @id;
-- END
-- IF @Iterations >= @MaxIterations
--     THROW 50005, 'Cursor loop hit the iteration cap; review the termination condition.', 1;
-- CLOSE c; DEALLOCATE c;
GO
-- The set-based aggregation patterns that usually replace a cursor, with the
-- reason each is faster rather than merely shorter.
GO

-- Running total, without a loop. A window function reads the rows once and
-- the frame is evaluated in the same pass; a cursor reads them once per row.
SELECT o.CustomerID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount,
              RunningTotal  = SUM(o.TotalAmount) OVER (
                  PARTITION BY o.CustomerID
                  ORDER BY o.OrderDate, o.SalesOrderID
                  ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW),
              PreviousOrder = LAG(o.TotalAmount) OVER (
                  PARTITION BY o.CustomerID ORDER BY o.OrderDate, o.SalesOrderID)
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder AS o
ORDER BY o.CustomerID, o.OrderDate;
GO

-- Gap-and-islands, which is the canonical "a cursor over a date range" and
-- the reason a calendar table is usually the better answer. Note ROWS, not
-- RANGE: RANGE treats peers as one group and breaks the chain on duplicate
-- dates.
WITH Ordered AS (
    SELECT OrderDate,
              PrevDate = LAG(OrderDate) OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate)
    FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
),
Grouped AS (
    SELECT OrderDate,
              IslandId = SUM(CASE WHEN DATEDIFF(day, PrevDate, OrderDate) <> 1 OR PrevDate IS NULL THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)
                                 OVER (ORDER BY OrderDate ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING)
    FROM   Ordered
)
SELECT IslandId, MIN(OrderDate) AS IslandStart, MAX(OrderDate) AS IslandEnd, COUNT(*) AS DayCount
FROM   Grouped
GROUP BY IslandId
ORDER BY IslandStart;
GO

-- De-duplication without a loop. ROW_NUMBER over a partition, then delete the
-- rows above 1. This is the pattern that replaces "read a list, for each
-- value check whether it already exists".
GO
-- WITH Ranked AS (
--     SELECT SalesOrderID, OrderDate, TotalAmount,
--            rn = ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
--                                       ORDER BY SalesOrderID)
--     FROM dbo.SalesOrderStaging)
-- DELETE FROM Ranked WHERE rn > 1;
--
-- CTE, which is deletable in one statement from SQL Server 2005 onward.
GO

Caveat. SP_C support and the legacy cursor library settings are deprecated: sp_configure 'cursor threshold’sp_configure 'cursor threshold', sp_configure 'cursor options’sp_configure 'cursor options' and the API-level cursor options were deprecated in SQL Server 2012 and are removed from current guidance. The SET CURSOR_DEFAULT GLOBAL|LOCALSET CURSOR_DEFAULT GLOBAL|LOCAL option also changes the default scope of cursors in a way that differs between the old API and ADO.NET, so a procedure that relies on either should declare its scope explicitly. A cursor that is neither closed nor deallocated, and that is declared GLO outside a procedure, survives to the end of the session and holds locks for that whole period.

10.5 Error handling, transactions and the transaction count

The correct error handler in T-SQL is short, and almost all of its length is about one question: is there still a transaction to roll back, and can it be committed? XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE() answers the first part and @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT does not, which is the single most consequential difference between them.

-- The complete pattern. Four requirements, all of them necessary:
--   1. SET XACT_ABORT ON, so a run-time error terminates the transaction
--      instead of rolling back only the failing statement
--   2. The error values captured at the very top of the CATCH, because any
--      statement in the handler can change them
--   3. A conditional rollback driven by XACT_STATE(), not @@TRANCOUNT
--   4. THROW as the last statement, to re-raise with the original number
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_PlaceOrder
    @CustomerID int,
    @Lines     dbo.OrderLineList READONLY
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;              -- (1)

    BEGIN TRY
        BEGIN TRANSACTION;

        INSERT dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate, StatusCode)
        VALUES (@CustomerID, CAST(SYSUTCDATETIME() AS date), 1);

        DECLARE @NewOrderID int = CONVERT(int, SCOPE_IDENTITY());

        INSERT dbo.SalesOrderLine (SalesOrderID, ProductID, Quantity, UnitPrice)
        SELECT @NewOrderID, l.ProductID, l.Quantity, l.UnitPrice
        FROM   @Lines AS l;

        COMMIT TRANSACTION;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        -- (2) Capture before anything else runs.
        DECLARE @ErrNum  int          = ERROR_NUMBER(),
                @Sev     int          = ERROR_SEVERITY(),
                @State   int          = ERROR_STATE(),
                @Line    int          = ERROR_LINE(),
                @Proc    nvarchar(128) = ERROR_PROCEDURE(),
                @Msg     nvarchar(2048) = ERROR_MESSAGE();

        -- (3) Roll back only if there is a transaction to roll back.
        --     XACT_STATE(): 1 = committable, 0 = none or already rolled
        --     back, -1 = doomed, uncommittable, must be rolled back.
        IF XACT_STATE() <> 0
            ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;

        -- Log it. The procedure name, the number and the message are
        -- what a support engineer will need.
        EXEC sys.sp_write_error_log 'OrderFailure',
             @Proc, @Line, @ErrNum, @Sev, @State, @Msg;

        -- (4) Re-raise. THROW with no arguments re-raises the original.
        THROW;
    END CATCH
END;
GO
-- THROW versus RAISERROR. Both raise an error; they differ in what the
-- client receives and in how they interact with TRY/CATCH.
GO
-- RAISERROR: the legacy form. The message may contain substitution
-- parameters, the severity and state are supplied, and the behaviour is
-- governed by the legacy severity table.
DECLARE @CustomerID int = 1001, @Count int = 3;

RAISERROR ('Customer %d has %d overdue orders; contact is required before shipping.',
            16, 1, @CustomerID, @Count) WITH NOWAIT;
GO

-- Severity decides what happens, for RAISERROR only:
--   10  informational; returned as a message, does not enter CATCH
--   11-15 moderate; aborts the statement, caught by CATCH
--   16  run-time error; aborts the statement, caught by CATCH
--   17-19 severe; batch is aborted, connection retained
--   20+ fatal; the connection is terminated and the transaction is doomed
GO

-- WITH LOG adds the error to the SQL Server error log and, with
-- sp_write_error_log, to sp_read_error_log. It raises the cost of the
-- error and is only appropriate for errors that need a record that
-- survives the client.
--
-- RAISERROR ('Fatal condition %s in %s', 16, 1, @Detail, @ProcName)
--     WITH LOG, NOWAIT;
GO

-- THROW. Introduced in SQL Server 2012. The number, message and state
-- are supplied, or nothing is supplied and the original error is re-raised.
-- A THROW must be preceded by a semicolon on the same statement.
-- A THROW with a number >= 50000 is a user error; THROW cannot be used
-- to raise a system error number.
GO
-- The two forms side by side. THROW 50000, '...', 1; raises a user error
-- that a client can distinguish from a system error by its number, which
-- is why the 50000 range is reserved for it.
THROW 50000, 'The order is already closed and cannot be modified.', 1;
GO

-- Re-throwing with a new message, while keeping the original number.
-- This is the operationally correct thing to do in a wrapper procedure.
GO
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_PlaceOrderWrapper @CustomerID int
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    BEGIN TRY
        EXEC dbo.usp_PlaceOrder @CustomerID = @CustomerID;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        DECLARE @Num int = ERROR_NUMBER(), @Msg nvarchar(2048) = ERROR_MESSAGE();
        -- Add context, then hand the client a well-formed error.
        THROW 51000, CONCAT('Order placement failed for customer ', @CustomerID, ': ', @Msg), 1;
    END CATCH
END;
GO
-- Transactions and @@TRANCOUNT. BEGIN TRAN nests: it increments the count
-- without creating a new transaction, and only the matching COMMIT at
-- the outermost level ends it. A nested BEGIN TRAN inside a procedure that
-- is called inside a transaction therefore does NOT commit independently.
GO
-- This is the shape that produces error 266 on COMMIT, and error 3903
-- on COMMIT with no transaction: a procedure that assumes it owns the
-- transaction boundary it does not own.
GO
DECLARE @OwnTransaction bit = 0;

IF @@TRANCOUNT = 0
BEGIN
    BEGIN TRANSACTION;
    SET @OwnTransaction = 1;   -- this procedure is the outermost boundary
END

BEGIN TRY
    UPDATE dbo.Customer
    SET    CreditLimit = CreditLimit * 1.05
    WHERE IsActive = 1;

    -- Commit only if we started it. If the caller already had one,
    -- COMMIT here would decrement the caller's count and the caller
    -- would then commit work this procedure only partially completed.
    IF @OwnTransaction = 1 AND @@TRANCOUNT = 1
        COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    -- Roll back the whole thing regardless of who owns it, or the caller
    -- is left holding a doomed transaction it does not know about.
    -- A procedure that participates in a caller's transaction must
    -- therefore document that it will roll the caller's work back on error.
    IF XACT_STATE() <> 0
        ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
    THROW;
END CATCH
GO

-- Savepoints, for a procedure that must roll back only its own part of
-- a transaction it shares. ROLLBACK TRAN to a savepoint does not change
-- @@TRANCOUNT and does not release the transaction.
GO
BEGIN TRANSACTION;

UPDATE dbo.SalesOrder
SET    StatusCode = 8
WHERE SalesOrderID = 1001;

SAVE TRANSACTION AfterHeader;
DECLARE @TranCountAtSave int = @@TRANCOUNT;   -- unchanged by the savepoint

BEGIN TRY
    INSERT dbo.SalesOrderLine (SalesOrderID, ProductID, Quantity, UnitPrice)
    VALUES (1001, 42, 1, 9.99);
    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END TRY
BEGIN CATCH
    -- Undo only the line insert; the header update survives.
    IF XACT_STATE() = 1 AND @@TRANCOUNT = @TranCountAtSave
    ROLLBACK TRANSACTION AfterHeader;
    IF XACT_STATE() <> 0 AND @@TRANCOUNT = @TranCountAtSave
        COMMIT TRANSACTION;   -- commit what remains
    THROW;
END CATCH
GO
-- The nesting cases, which is where a handler is usually wrong. Three
-- arrangements, and the behaviour of @@TRANCOUNT and XACT_STATE() in each.
GO

-- Case 1: the procedure owns the transaction. COMMIT leaves it with zero
-- open transactions, and an error rolls back its own work entirely.
GO
-- BEGIN TRY BEGIN TRANSACTION;
--     INSERT ...; COMMIT TRANSACTION; END TRY
-- BEGIN CATCH IF XACT_STATE() <> 0 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION; THROW; END CATCH
GO

-- Case 2: the caller owns it and the procedure participates. COMMIT inside
-- decrements the count without ending the transaction, and the caller's work
-- is now only partly done. This is the shape that produces error 266,
-- 'Transaction count after EXECUTE indicates a mismatching number of BEGIN and
-- COMMIT statements', when the counts do not balance at the end of the batch.
GO
-- Caller:   BEGIN TRANSACTION;  EXEC dbo.usp_Participate;  COMMIT;
-- Procedure: BEGIN TRANSACTION;   -- count becomes 2
--            INSERT ...; COMMIT;  -- count becomes 1, transaction still open
-- Caller:   COMMIT;               -- count 0, but see section 7.3 for the trap
GO

-- Case 3: the procedure uses a savepoint inside a caller's transaction, which
-- is the only arrangement in which the procedure can fail without destroying
-- the caller's work. ROLLBACK TRAN to a savepoint does not change @@TRANCOUNT
-- and does not release the transaction.
GO
-- BEGIN TRY
--     SAVE TRANSACTION BeforeProc;
--     EXEC dbo.usp_RiskyStep;
-- END TRY
-- BEGIN CATCH
--     IF XACT_STATE() = 1 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION BeforeProc;  -- caller's work survives
--     THROW;
-- END CATCH
GO

-- The detection, and the rule. A procedure that begins a transaction without
-- first checking @@TRANCOUNT, and commits without checking that it is the
-- outermost level, is unsafe in every arrangement except case 1. The check is
-- one line and it belongs at the top of the procedure.
GO
SELECT TranCount  = @@TRANCOUNT,
              XactState  = XACT_STATE(),
              InTrigger   = CONVERT(int, OBJECTPROPERTY(@@PROCID, 'ExecIsInsertTrigger'));
GO

-- Distributed transactions change the rule. A transaction promoted to two
-- phase commit cannot be rolled back after the prepare phase, so XACT_STATE()
-- becomes -1 and the transaction can only be committed. This is the case where
-- the usual 'roll back on error' handler is unable to do what it says.
GO
-- BEGIN TRY
--     BEGIN DISTRIBUTED TRANSACTION;
--     UPDATE LinkedServer.Sales.dbo.Customer SET ...;
--     COMMIT TRANSACTION;
-- END TRY
-- BEGIN CATCH
--     IF XACT_STATE() = -1
--         COMMIT TRANSACTION;   -- doomed but promotable: commit and fail loudly
--     ELSE IF XACT_STATE() = 1
--         ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
--     THROW;
-- END CATCH
GO
-- The error-number decisions worth branching on, and what each means. This is
-- the small set that appears in almost every real handler.
GO
-- BEGIN CATCH
--     DECLARE @Num int = ERROR_NUMBER();
--
--     IF @Num = 2627 OR @Num = 2601
--     BEGIN   -- duplicate key: a PK or unique violation
--         -- retryable, in the sense that a different key will succeed
--         THROW 50010, CONCAT('Duplicate key rejected: ', ERROR_MESSAGE()), 1;
--     END
--     IF @Num = 1205
--     BEGIN   -- deadlock victim: the correct response is to retry the whole
--             -- transaction, and the standard practice is to cap the retries
--         THROW 50011, 'Deadlock; retry the transaction.', 1;
--     END
--     IF @Num = 4060
--     BEGIN   -- cannot open database: the login is valid, the database is not
--             -- available. Operational, not a bug in the caller.
--         THROW 50012, 'Target database is unavailable.', 1;
--     END
--     IF @Num BETWEEN 22000 AND 22999
--     BEGIN   -- any user-defined error from a THROW or a severity 16
--         THROW;  -- re-raise unchanged: the number is meaningful to the client
--     END
--
--     -- Anything else: log everything and re-raise, because a swallowed error
--     -- in a procedure is the failure mode that costs the most to diagnose.
--     EXEC sys.sp_write_error_log 'Procedure', @Proc, @Line, @Num, @Sev, @State, @Msg;
--     THROW;
-- END CATCH
GO

-- The log, written by a procedure rather than by a trigger on the table, so
-- that the failure is recorded even when the transaction is rolled back. A
-- table written inside a doomed transaction is lost with the rollback; this
-- one is not, because the write happens after it.
GO
-- CREATE TABLE dbo.ErrorLog
-- (
--     ErrorLogId     int IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
--     ErrorDate      datetime2(3)    NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_ErrorLog_Date DEFAULT SYSUTCDATETIME(),
--     ProcedureName  nvarchar(128)  NULL,
--     ErrorNumber    int            NOT NULL,
--     ErrorSeverity  tinyint        NULL,
--     ErrorState     tinyint        NULL,
--     ErrorLine      int            NULL,
--     ErrorMessage   nvarchar(2048) NOT NULL,
--     OriginalLogin  nvarchar(128)  NULL,
--     HostName       nvarchar(128)  NULL,
--     ApplicationName nvarchar(128) NULL,
--     RequestId      uniqueidentifier NULL
-- );
--
-- EXEC sys.sp_write_error_log 'SalesError', @Proc, @Line, @Num, @Sev, @State, @Msg;
GO

-- Reading it, which is the query to put in a runbook. Grouped by procedure and
-- number, so a deployment that introduced a new error is visible as a row.
SELECT CONVERT(nvarchar(128), ProcedureName) AS ProcedureName,
              ErrorNumber, COUNT(*) AS Occurrences,
              MIN(ErrorDate) AS FirstSeen, MAX(ErrorDate) AS LastSeen,
              CONVERT(nvarchar(200), MAX(ErrorMessage)) AS SampleMessage
FROM   dbo.ErrorLog
WHERE  ErrorDate > DATEADD(day, -7, SYSUTCDATETIME())
GROUP BY CONVERT(nvarchar(128), ProcedureName), ErrorNumber
ORDER BY Occurrences DESC;
GO
FunctionReturnsUse it for
XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE()11 active and committable, 00 no transaction or already rolled back, -1-1 doomed and uncommittableDeciding whether to roll back. This is the correct basis for the decision
@@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNTNumber of BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTION statements not yet matched by a COMDetecting nesting. It is not a substitute for XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE(), because it is still 11 on a doomed transaction
ERROR_NUMBER()ERROR_NUMBER()Number of the current errorBranching on a specific error; logging
ERROR_MESSAGE()ERROR_MESSAGE()Text of the current errorLogging and re-raising. It is NU outside a CA block, and it is truncated to 2048 characters by the documented return type
ERROR_SEVERITY()ERROR_SEVERITY(), ERROR_STATE()ERROR_STATE(), ERROR_LINE()ERROR_LINE(), ERROR_PROCEDURE()ERROR_PROCEDURE()The remaining error attributesLogging. ERROR_LINE()ERROR_LINE() reports the line in the batch that failed, not the line in the procedure that called it
@@ERROR@@ERRORNumber of the previous statement’s errorLegacy handlers only. It is overwritten by the next statement and is not set by TH

Caveat. T/CA does not catch everything. It does not catch compile-time errors, because the batch is compiled before execution and a compile error is reported before any statement runs. It does not catch a severity 20 or higher error, because the connection is terminated. It does not catch a KI, a client timeout, or most client-side failures: a client that cancels a query leaves the transaction open on the server, and only a subsequent rollback or a connection close reclaims it. A transaction whose log cannot grow because the log is full is doomed, not committable, and must be rolled back; the error surfaces as 2627's neighbours rather than as a catchable statement error, which is why XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE() is checked rather than @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT. Finally, a TH or RAIS inside a CA block replaces the error context, so the values must be captured into variables before any of them is issued.

10.6 Bulk load, batch insert and identifier generation

Loading rows in batches rather than in one transaction, and generating identifiers with something other than IDEN, are two separate topics that share a cause: the write path of a growing table is the constraint that shapes the schema. The batch size, the log volume, and the fact that IDEN leaves permanent gaps are all consequences of the same decision about how rows arrive.

-- BULK INSERT. The options that matter are the two IGNORE_ flags, and
-- both of them are unsafe in a way that must be understood before use.
GO
-- IGNORE_CONSTRAINTS: constraints are not checked. The default for a bulk
-- load is that they are checked; this turns them off, and any row that
-- violates a CHECK or UNIQUE constraint is silently accepted.
-- IGNORE_TRIGGERS: triggers do not fire. The default is that they do, which
-- is why a bulk load into a table with an audit trigger is slow and why the
-- audit trail is incomplete if IGNORE_TRIGGERS is used.
GO
BULK INSERT dbo.SalesOrderStaging
FROM 'C:\Data\salesorders-2026-09.csv'
WITH (
    FIELDTERMINATOR     = ',',
    ROWTERMINATOR       = '0x0a',
    FIRSTROW            = 2,          -- skip the header line
    CODEPAGE            = '65001',      -- UTF-8, matching the file
    BATCHSIZE           = 100000,
    CHECK_CONSTRAINTS,
    FIRE_TRIGGERS,
    TABLOCK,              -- minimal logging; see below
    ROWS_PER_BATCH       = 10000
);
GO

-- The unmanaged form, which is faster because it bypasses the
-- engine's parsing and uses the file's own sort order.
GO
-- bcp Sales.dbo.SalesOrderStaging in "C:\Data\salesorders.csv" -S sql01.contoso.local
--    -d Sales -u -P * -c -t "," -r "\n" -b 2 -a 65001 -m 50 -h -1
--    -a 65001    code page 65001 = UTF-8; without it, non-ASCII is
--               converted to the client code page and corrupted
--    -b 2        batch size, in rows, and also the commit frequency
--    -m 50       max errors before bcp aborts
--    -h -1       column names on the first row
--    -C          case-sensitive matching of the column names
--    -b and -r interact: each batch is one transaction
GO

-- Verify a file before loading it. bcp can read a file and write to
-- stdout, which is how you discover that a column count is wrong
-- before the load fails halfway through.
GO
-- bcp Sales.dbo.SalesOrder in "C:\Data\salesorders.csv" out NUL -S sql01
--    -c -t "," -r "\n" -a 65001 -b 1 -m 50
GO
-- Minimal logging. TABLOCK, or TABLOCKX on a partitioned table, is what makes
-- a bulk operation minimally logged. In SIMPLE or BULK_LOGGED recovery
-- that means the log does not record the row images and the table is
-- not locked for the duration.
--
-- The three requirements, all of which must hold:
--   1. The recovery model is SIMPLE or BULK_LOGGED (not FULL)
--   2. TABLOCK is specified, or the table is empty and the load is not a
--      parallel bulk insert
--   3. The operation is INSERT SELECT with a bulk insert method, or
--      BULK INSERT
--
-- Check it with the undocumented but reliable function, and confirm that
-- the transaction count is what you expect afterwards.
GO
-- SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sys.fn_dblog(NULL, NULL) WHERE [Transaction ID] = XACTID;
-- The supported equivalent: compare the log record counts before and
-- after for the same operation, with and without TABLOCK.
GO

-- Batch insert. The correct pattern for a large load: commit per batch,
-- never inside a procedure that can be rolled back as a whole, and always
-- with an idempotent re-run.
GO
DECLARE @BatchSize int = 5000, @Total int, @Done int = 0;

SELECT @Total = COUNT(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrderStaging;
-- Re-running from the beginning is safe only because the target is emptied
-- first and the load is inside a transaction. See the note below.
TRUNCATE TABLE dbo.SalesOrder;

WHILE @Done < @Total
BEGIN
    BEGIN TRANSACTION;

    INSERT dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate, StatusCode, TotalAmount)
    SELECT TOP (@BatchSize) s.CustomerID, s.OrderDate, s.StatusCode, s.TotalAmount
    FROM       dbo.SalesOrderStaging AS s
    ORDER BY    s.SalesOrderID;

    SET @Done = @Done + @@ROWCOUNT;

    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END
GO

-- The batch size is a trade: a larger batch is faster and produces fewer
-- log flushes, and a larger batch holds more locks and more undo for
-- longer, and any failure loses more work. 5,000 to 50,000 is the range in
-- which most loads sit.
GO
-- The identifier decision, and the two failure modes it avoids. Neither a
-- clustered GUID nor a sequential IDENTITY column is universally right; the
-- question is the write rate, the page-fill behaviour, and whether the key is
-- ever exposed to a client.
GO
-- The GUID measurement. Insert a million rows in random key order and report
-- the page count, against the same rows inserted in order.
--
-- CREATE TABLE t (Id uniqueidentifier NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY);
-- INSERT t (Id) SELECT NEWID() FROM sys.all_objects a CROSS JOIN sys.all_objects b;
-- SELECT SUM(reserved_page_count) * 8 / 1024 AS SizeMB
-- FROM sys.dm_db_partition_stats WHERE object_id = OBJECT_ID('t') AND index_id = 1;
--
-- Random keys split every insert across the whole B-tree, so most inserts dirty
-- a page that is not already in the buffer pool. The uniqueidentifier_newsequentialid
-- type, new in SQL Server 2005, is the mitigation: time-ordered GUIDs that insert
-- sequentially. It is incompatible with a ROWGUIDCOL column or a filtered
-- unique index on one.
GO

-- Business keys, which is what an application actually wants to show, and the
-- two ways to derive one without a round trip. A sequence gives a value before the
-- row exists; a computed value is derived from the row itself.
GO
-- ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder ADD OrderNumber AS CONVERT(bigint,
--     CONVERT(varbinary(8), REVERSE(NEWSEQUENTIALID()))) PERSISTED;
--
-- A PERSISTED computed column is stored, deterministic and indexable, and it
-- requires a UNIQUE index to guarantee uniqueness: the computed value alone does
-- not stop two rows from producing the same number.
--
-- CREATE UNIQUE INDEX UX_SalesOrder_OrderNumber ON dbo.SalesOrder (OrderNumber);
GO

-- The gap-free alternative, and why it is usually a mistake. A maintenance job
-- that rewrites the counter to MAX(id) + 1 produces contiguous keys and costs a
-- full scan of the clustered index on every run, breaks the insert performance the
-- counter exists to provide, and is undone by the next failed transaction.
-- Contiguity is not a property a key column should have.
GO
-- DECLARE @Max bigint = (SELECT MAX(SalesOrderID) FROM dbo.SalesOrder WITH (TABLOCK));
-- IF @Max IS NOT NULL
--     DBCC CHECKIDENT (dbo.SalesOrder, RESEED, @Max) WITH NO_INFOMSGS;
GO

-- The distributed case, which is the strongest argument for SEQUENCE over
-- IDENTITY: a sharded or multi-node load where each node must generate from a
-- disjoint range. A single-node sequence cannot do this alone, but the
-- arithmetic is explicit and the ranges are auditable.
GO
-- CREATE SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumberByRegion AS bigint
--     START WITH 1 INCREMENT BY 1000000 NO CYCLE;   -- one row per region
-- -- region 1 uses ... NEXT VALUE FOR dbo.OrderNumberByRegion, + 0
-- -- region 2 uses ... NEXT VALUE FOR dbo.OrderNumberByRegion, + 1
-- -- region 99 uses ... NEXT VALUE FOR dbo.OrderNumberByRegion, + 99
GO
-- IDENTITY gaps are permanent and by design. A failed transaction, a
-- rolled-back insert, an IGNORE_DUP_KEY insert, a TRUNCATE that is not
-- followed by a reseed, and a multi-row insert that fails its unique
-- constraint all consume values that no row will ever hold.
--
-- Consequences to design around: a value from an IDENTITY is not a
-- count, a gap in a clustered key is wasted page space, and reseeding is
-- a documented operation with a documented restriction.
GO
-- Restart the counter. Three forms, and what each one does.
GO
-- 1. Report only. Does not change the value; returns the current identity
--    and the current seed. The one to run before reseeding.
DBCC CHECKIDENT (dbo.SalesOrder);
GO
-- 2. Reseed. The next value inserted is 5000 + 1 = 5001. Only legal on a
--    column that is also a PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE CONSTRAINT, and it
--    requires INSERT permission on the table.
DBCC CHECKIDENT (dbo.SalesOrder, RESEED, 5000) WITH RESEED;
GO

-- SCOPE_IDENTITY versus IDENT_CURRENT. SCOPE_IDENTITY returns the value
-- generated in the current scope, which is what you want after an INSERT;
-- @@IDENTITY is the deprecated equivalent and returns the last value
-- across all scopes, so a trigger that inserts breaks it. IDENT_CURRENT
-- returns the last value for a table regardless of scope, which is a
-- diagnostic tool and nothing else.
GO
SELECT ScopeIdentity  = CONVERT(bigint, SCOPE_IDENTITY()),
              AtIdentity      = CONVERT(bigint, @@IDENTITY),
              IdentCurrent   = CONVERT(bigint, IDENT_CURRENT('SalesOrder'));
GO
-- SEQUENCE. Introduced in SQL Server 2012. A standalone object, not part of a
-- table, with no cache-row table, no dependency on a column and no
-- requirement that the value be used. Gaps are documented and normal.
--
-- Properties that matter: CACHE (values pre-generated, for throughput,
-- at the cost of gaps on a crash), CYCLE (wrap and restart, which makes the
-- value useless as an identity), NO CYCLE (the default, and the correct
-- choice for a key), and the data type, which for a key should be
-- bigint and non-cycling.
GO
-- Application allocated: an order number the client needs before the
-- row is inserted, which is the usual reason to reach for a sequence.
GO
-- CREATE SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber
--     AS bigint
--     START WITH 1000000
--     INCREMENT BY 1
--     MINVALUE 1
--     MAXVALUE 9223372036854775807   -- bigint max
--     NO CYCLE;
GO

-- Table bound: attached to an IDENTITY column so that a migrated table
-- keeps producing the same values, and used with NEXT VALUE FOR. The
-- value is still not transactional and still gaps.
GO
-- ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder
--     ADD OrderNumberSeq AS SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber;
GO

CREATE OR ALTER SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber
    AS bigint
    START WITH 1000000
    INCREMENT BY 1
    MINVALUE 1
    MAXVALUE 9223372036854775807
    NO CYCLE;
GO

-- NEXT VALUE FOR. The one statement the whole feature exists for.
-- Note that it is a value expression, so it can appear in SELECT, INSERT,
-- DEFAULT, UPDATE and a CHECK constraint's right-hand side.
GO
DECLARE @OrderNumber bigint = NEXT VALUE FOR dbo.OrderNumber;

INSERT dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate, StatusCode, OrderNumber)
VALUES (1001, CAST(SYSUTCDATETIME() AS date), 1, @OrderNumber);
GO

-- A set of values in one statement, which is how a sequence is used for a
-- batch insert without a loop.
GO
INSERT dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate, StatusCode, OrderNumber)
SELECT c.CustomerID, CAST(SYSUTCDATETIME() AS date), 1, v.n
FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
CROSS JOIN (SELECT TOP(1000) ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY (SELECT NULL)) AS n
             FROM sys.all_objects) AS v;
GO

-- State and administration. RESTART is a requirement, not a repair: it
-- sets the next value, and it consumes nothing.
GO
EXEC sys.sp_sequence_get_range @sequence_name = 'dbo.OrderNumber',
     @range_size = 100, @sequence_value = 1000 OUTPUT;
GO
ALTER SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber RESTART WITH 1;
GO
-- ALTER SEQUENCE dbo.OrderNumber NO CYCLE;   -- required before MAXVALUE can
--                                           be changed on a cycling object
GO

SELECT CONVERT(varchar(128), s.name)      AS SequenceName,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  s.type)       AS DataType,
       s.start_value, s.minimum_value, s.maximum_value,
       s.increment, s.is_cycling, s.is_cached, s.cache_size,
       CONVERT(varchar(20),  s.current_value) AS CurrentValue
FROM   sys.sequences AS s
ORDER BY s.name;
GO
IDENSEQU
ScopeA column on one tableA standalone object; any number of tables, and none
Value retrievalSCOPE_IDENTITY()SCOPE_IDENTITY() after the insert, or an IDEN value exposed by the tableNEXT VALUE FORNEXT VALUE FOR anywhere a value expression is allowed, before or during the insert
ConcurrencyInternally serialised by the tableInternally serialised by the sequence; with CA it is lock-free across a cache per session
GapsYes, on rollback, failed inserts, IGNORE_Yes, on rollback, and up to CACHE more on a server crash
Reuse across serversNoYes, with a configured start and increment — which is how a merge or a sharded load avoids collisions
PermissionRequires INS on the tableRequires SEL or EXE on the sequence in addition to the table permission, so a grant can be made separately
New inSQL Server 2005 as IDEN; earlier forms are deprecatedSQL Server 2012

Requirements and caveats for bulk operations. The staging table should have no indexes, no foreign keys, no check constraints and no triggers until after the load, because each of them is a per-row cost during the insert and a validation cost after it; the validations belong in the statements that move the rows from staging into the target. A bulk load into a table in FU recovery is fully logged and the log grows in proportion to the data; a bulk load into the same table in SIM with TAB is minimally logged and the log grows by a constant. The difference is often the difference between a load that fits in the log and one that does not. Never use IGNORE_CO on a table with a foreign key that other tables depend on — the load succeeds and the database is left inconsistent; if the load is wrong, fix the data, do not disable the check.

For a large load the sequence is: create the staging table with the target’s column types and no constraints; load it; validate it with a set-based query that must return zero rows; then move the rows in batches with the constraints in place; then create the indexes; then update statistics with UPDATE STATISTICS ... WITH FULLSCANUPDATE STATISTICS ... WITH FULLSCAN on anything new, because the statistics that exist for a freshly populated table describe a table that was empty. The last step is the one that is skipped, and it is the one that produces a bad plan on the first query of the morning.

Exercises 10

  1. Write a script with three batches where the third references a procedure created in the second, and confirm it runs. Move the G to remove the boundary and record the exact error number and text.
  2. Build a procedure that takes a table name and row count as parameters, and run it three times with a value containing a single quote, a closing bracket and a semicolon. Use QUOT and confirm each case is handled. Then remove the QUOT and record the failure.
  3. Compare FOR and CON over 50,000 rows with SET STATISTICS TIME ONSET STATISTICS TIME ON, and record both CPU times. Repeat with 500,000 rows and confirm the ratio is not constant.
  4. Create a ## temp table, populate it, index it, and query tempdb.s and tempdb.sy to confirm both. Drop it and confirm it is gone. Then create a #### table from two sessions and confirm each can read the other’s rows.
  5. Populate a table variable with 2 rows, join it to a large table, and read the estimated row count on the table variable scan in the actual plan. Repeat with 50,000 rows and with OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE), and record the three estimates and the three plans.
  6. Set the database to compatibility level 160, repeat exercise 5 without the recompile hint, and confirm the estimate now reflects the real row count. Then set LEGACY_DEFERR to O and confirm it reverts to the fixed estimate.
  7. Write the cursor version and the AP version of "top 3 orders per customer", measure both with SET STATISTICS TIMESET STATISTICS TIME and SET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO, and record the difference in round trips, logical reads and CPU.
  8. Write a procedure containing BEGIN TRANSACTIONBEGIN TRANSACTION unconditionally, call it from a batch that already has a transaction, and then call COM in the outer batch. Record what happens to @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT and to the committed data. Then rewrite the procedure to detect and respect an existing transaction.
  9. Force a constraint violation inside a transaction in a procedure, and in the CA block print XACT_STATE()XACT_STATE(), @@TRANCOUNT@@TRANCOUNT, ERROR_NUMBER()ERROR_NUMBER() and ERROR_MESSAGE()ERROR_MESSAGE() before and after a THROWTHROW. Explain which value changes and which does not.
  10. Add a savepoint before a second step inside a procedure’s transaction, fail that step, roll back to the savepoint, and commit. Verify with a SEL that the first step’s change is present and the second’s is not.
  11. Bulk load 200,000 rows into a staging table with and without TAB, in SIM and in FU recovery, and record the log size and elapsed time for each of the four cases.
  12. Create a table with an IDEN column, insert rows, roll a transaction back, and record the gap. Do the same with a SEQU with CACHE 1000CACHE 1000, restart the service mid-way, and record the additional gap. Explain which identifier is suitable for a key and which is not.

11 · Performance Tuning Methodology

11.1 The measurement discipline: baseline, one change, re-measure11.2 Wait statistics: deltas, not cumulative counters11.3 Extended Events, traces and the deadlock graph11.4 Query statistics, cached plans and parameter sniffing11.5 Index suggestions and the maintenance schedule11.6 Memory, parallelism, checkpoints and the pre-production checklist

Performance work is an experiment, and an experiment has controls. This chapter is the method rather than the catalogue: how to establish a number that can be trusted, how to change exactly one thing and change it again to see whether the number moved, how to read the three diagnostic sources the engine provides (waits, query statistics, and events), and where the tuning effort actually belongs when the answer is that nothing is wrong with the query. Section 11.6 collects the pre-production checklist that the rest of the chapter exists to justify.

Three claims frame everything that follows. First, a performance problem is a symptom with several possible causes, and the symptom does not identify the cause; the wait tells you which resource, not which statement, and only the statement’s statistics connect the two. Second, the great majority of production performance incidents are not query defects at all but capacity and design defects: an unindexed foreign key, a scalar function in a predicate, a table that grew a factor of a hundred without a plan ever being re-evaluated, or a server whose memory setting has been wrong for two years. Third, tuning is only defensible against a measurement, and a measurement made once on a development machine with four rows is not a measurement.

Applies to. Every DMV, procedure and option in this chapter is documented for SQL Server 2025 (17.x) and behaves the same in 2022 (16.x) and 2019 (15.x) unless stated. The wait descriptions, the DMV column names and the BAC and RES grammars are stable across those four versions; the preview-only features listed in the Orientation chapter are explicitly not production inputs and are not used as tuning levers here.

11.1 The measurement discipline: baseline, one change, re-measure

A baseline is a recorded number with enough context to be reproduced: the statement, the data distribution, the plan, the elapsed time, the logical reads and the CPU time. Change one variable, re-measure, and keep both numbers. Everything else in this chapter is a technique for producing one of the columns of that record.

MeasureProduced byWhat it tells youCommonly misread as
Elapsed timeSET STATISTICS TIME ONSET STATISTICS TIME ON, DMV total_elaWhat the user waits for, including time spent blocked and time spent not scheduledThe cost of the query. It is not: elapsed time includes waits
Logical readsSET STATISTICS IO ONSET STATISTICS IO ON, DMV total_logWork done against the buffer pool; scales with row count and index design, and is the figure that predicts how the statement will behave as data growsDisk activity. Logical reads are satisfied from memory in almost all cases
Physical readsSET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO log column, DMV total_physActual I/O to the data or log files; a cold-cache figure, meaningful only on a cold cacheA storage problem. It is usually a warm-up artefact
CPU timeDMV total_wo (microseconds, summed over all workers)CPU consumed; on a parallel plan it exceeds elapsed time by the degree of parallelismElapsed time. Divide by DOP before comparing them
Row counts and plan hashActual execution plan, DMV query and query_pWhether the statement you measured is the statement that runs in production, and whether the plan is the one you readMetadata. It is the control that makes the other four comparable

The reason to record all five rather than elapsed time alone is that elapsed time is the least reproducible of the five. It moves with the plan cache, with the buffer pool, with lock conflicts, with other queries, and with the phase of any statistics update. Logical reads are deterministic for a given plan on a given data set, which makes them the right figure to compare before and after a change, and the wrong figure to optimise for in isolation. The standard working rule is: use logical reads to decide whether a change helped, and elapsed time to decide whether the change was worth deploying.

-- Step one of any tuning exercise: the number you will compare against. Run the
-- statement twice. The first execution populates the plan cache and pulls the
-- pages into the buffer pool; the second is the one whose figures are stable.
SET STATISTICS IO ON;
SET STATISTICS TIME ON;
GO
SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE  Status = 'Open'
  AND    OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
ORDER BY OrderDate DESC;
GO
-- Repeat the identical statement now, and record the second pair of figures.
SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
FROM   dbo.SalesOrder
WHERE  Status = 'Open'
  AND    OrderDate >= '2026-01-01'
ORDER BY OrderDate DESC;
GO
SET STATISTICS IO OFF;
SET STATISTICS TIME OFF;
GO

The two SET STATISTICSSET STATISTICS statements write to the client message stream, not to the result set, so they are placed outside the batch being measured wherever the client makes that possible. SET STATISTICS TIMESET STATISTICS TIME reports CPU and elapsed in milliseconds for the preceding statement; SET STATISTICS IOSET STATISTICS IO reports reads, writes and physical reads per table, with the scan count and a hash or lob count where applicable. For a repeatable record rather than a screen, capture the numbers into a table with the harness below.

-- A record of measurements, so that a claim such as "the index helped" is a
-- comparison between two rows rather than between two memories.
CREATE TABLE dbo.PerfBaseline
(
    BaselineID    int IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_PerfBaseline PRIMARY KEY,
    CapturedAt    datetime2(3)    NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_PerfBaseline_CapturedAt DEFAULT (SYSUTCDATETIME()),
    Scenario      sysname         NOT NULL,
    Iteration     int             NOT NULL,
    ElapsedMs     int             NOT NULL,
    RowsReturned  bigint          NOT NULL,
    StatementText nvarchar(2000)  NOT NULL,
    Notes         nvarchar(400)   NULL
);
GO

-- The harness. sp_executesql is used so the statement text is a parameter
-- rather than syntax, and so the procedure itself compiles once.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_RecordBaseline
    @Scenario   sysname,
    @Iteration  int          = 1,
    @Statement  nvarchar(2000),
    @Notes      nvarchar(400) = NULL
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    DECLARE @Start datetime2(3) = SYSDATETIME();

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Statement;

    -- @@ROWCOUNT and the clock are read immediately: any intervening statement
    -- overwrites both of them.
    DECLARE @Rows    bigint = @@ROWCOUNT,
            @Elapsed int    = DATEDIFF_BIG(millisecond, @Start, SYSDATETIME());

    INSERT dbo.PerfBaseline (Scenario, Iteration, ElapsedMs, RowsReturned, StatementText, Notes)
    VALUES (@Scenario, @Iteration, @Elapsed, @Rows, @Statement, @Notes);
END;
GO

DECLARE @Warm sql = N'SELECT SalesOrderID, CustomerID, OrderDate, TotalAmount
                     FROM dbo.SalesOrder
                     WHERE Status = ''Open'' AND OrderDate >= ''2026-01-01''
                     ORDER BY OrderDate DESC;';

-- Discard the first run: it is measuring a cold plan and a cold buffer pool.
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline @Scenario = N'ch11-openorders', @Iteration = 0, @Statement = @Warm;
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline @Scenario = N'ch11-openorders', @Iteration = 1, @Statement = @Warm;
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline @Scenario = N'ch11-openorders', @Iteration = 2, @Statement = @Warm;
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline @Scenario = N'ch11-openorders', @Iteration = 3, @Statement = @Warm;
GO

The Iteration = 0Iteration = 0 row is kept deliberately: recording the cold run and the warm runs in the same table makes the difference between them visible, and a change that helps only the cold path has to say so. A baseline taken at a different time of day, against a table with a different row count, or with a different index set, is not a baseline for the same experiment, which is why Statem and the timestamp are stored rather than being assumed.

-- The comparison. Two scenarios, same data, same server, one difference between
-- them. Report the median rather than the mean: a single blocking episode in
-- one of five runs moves a mean and does not move a median.
SELECT  Scenario,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                                                   AS Runs,
        CAST(AVG(CAST(ElapsedMs AS decimal(9,2))) AS decimal(9,2))     AS AvgMs,
        MIN(ElapsedMs)                                                 AS MinMs,
        MAX(ElapsedMs)                                                 AS MaxMs,
        CAST(PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY ElapsedMs)
             OVER (PARTITION BY Scenario) AS decimal(9,2))            AS MedianMs
FROM    dbo.PerfBaseline
WHERE   CapturedAt >= DATEADD(hour, -24, SYSDATETIME())
  AND   Scenario IN (N'ch11-openorders', N'ch11-openorders-indexed')
GROUP BY Scenario
ORDER BY Scenario;
GO

-- The mean and the median disagreeing by a wide margin is itself a finding:
-- it means one run met contention the others did not, and the statement is
-- not the only variable in the room.
SELECT  Scenario,
        CAST(AVG(CAST(ElapsedMs AS decimal(9,2))) AS decimal(9,2))     AS AvgMs,
        CAST(PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY ElapsedMs)
             OVER (PARTITION BY Scenario) AS decimal(9,2))            AS MedianMs,
        CAST(100.0 * (AVG(CAST(ElapsedMs AS decimal(9,2)))
            - PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY ElapsedMs)
              OVER (PARTITION BY Scenario))
            / NULLIF(AVG(CAST(ElapsedMs AS decimal(9,2))), 0)
            AS decimal(5,1))                                          AS PctSkew
FROM    dbo.PerfBaseline
WHERE   CapturedAt >= DATEADD(hour, -24, SYSDATETIME())
  AND   Scenario IN (N'ch11-openorders', N'ch11-openorders-indexed')
GROUP BY Scenario;
GO

Caveats. A measurement taken on a development database with a thousand rows tells you nothing about a production database with a hundred million, because the plan the optimiser chooses changes with cardinality. Equally, a measurement taken on production while the buffer pool is cold, while statistics are being updated, or while another team is running a report measures those conditions rather than the statement. If the goal is a defensible before-and-after, the two measurements must be taken on the same data, with the same statistics, in the same cache state, and the second must be labelled with all three. The one measurement that is never valid is the one taken once, informally, in a query window, and remembered.

-- A cold-cache measurement is a different measurement and has to be asked for
-- explicitly. These two commands are privileged, instance-wide and
-- destructive to performance for their duration: they are for a maintenance
-- window or a representative copy, never for business hours.
DBCC FREEPROCCACHE;      -- empties the plan cache; every plan is recompiled
DBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERS;   -- empties the buffer pool; every read becomes physical
GO
-- Measure the first execution after DROPCLEANBUFFERS, and record it as the
-- cold figure. Then measure again for the warm figure. Both belong in
-- the record; neither substitutes for the other.
GO

-- The safe alternative on a production instance: measure the warm path, and
-- estimate the cold path from the physical-read counters over a real interval.
SELECT  instance_name,
        cntr_name,
        cntr_value
FROM    sys.dm_os_performance_counters
WHERE   object_name  = N'SQLServer:Buffer Manager'
  AND   instance_name = N'_Total'
  AND   cntr_name IN (N'Buffer cache hit ratio',
                      N'Page reads/sec',
                      N'Page writes/sec',
                      N'Page life expectancy',
                      N'Free list stalls/sec',
                      N'Read-ahead/sec',
                      N'Read-ahead pages/sec');
GO

The buffer cache hit ratio is a cumulative instance-wide percentage with no memory of recent behaviour, which makes it close to useless as an alert and useful only as a historical sanity check. Page life expectancy is the number that reflects recent behaviour: on a machine with 64 GB of buffer pool and stable memory, a value in the tens of thousands means pages are surviving; a value in the hundreds means the working set does not fit and every request is a physical read. The threshold scales with the buffer pool size, which is why no single number is correct for all instances.

-- The third column of the baseline record: the data the estimate is made
-- against. Without this, an estimate-versus-actual comparison two weeks later
-- is not comparable, because the distribution has moved.
SELECT  s.name                                       AS SchemaName,
        t.name                                       AS TableName,
        SUM(CASE WHEN p.index_id IN (0, 1) THEN p.rows ELSE 0 END) AS RowsHeapOrClustered,
        SUM(p.rows)                                  AS RowsAllIndexes,
        CAST(SUM(a.total_pages) * 8.0 / 1024 AS decimal(12,1)) AS SizeMB,
        MIN(a.creation_time)                         AS CreatedAt,
        MAX(a.modified_date)                         AS LastModified
FROM    sys.tables      AS t
JOIN    sys.schemas     AS s  ON s.schema_id = t.schema_id
JOIN    sys.indexes     AS i  ON i.object_id = t.object_id
JOIN    sys.partitions  AS p  ON p.object_id = i.object_id AND p.index_id = i.index_id
JOIN    sys.allocation_units AS a ON a.container_id = p.partition_id
GROUP BY s.name, t.name
ORDER BY SUM(p.rows) DESC;
GO

-- Statistics freshness. A histogram describing yesterday's data is a silent
-- failure: nothing errors, the plan is simply chosen for a table that no
-- longer exists.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(s.object_id)  AS SchemaName,
        OBJECT_NAME(s.object_id)         AS TableName,
        s.name                           AS StatName,
        s.auto_created,
        sp.rows,
        sp.rows_sampled,
        sp.last_updated,
        DATEDIFF(day, sp.last_updated, SYSDATETIME()) AS DaysSinceUpdate
FROM    sys.stats AS s
JOIN    sys.dm_db_stats_properties(s.object_id, s.stats_id) AS sp
WHERE   sp.rows > 0
  AND   (sp.last_updated IS NULL
         OR DATEDIFF(day, sp.last_updated, SYSDATETIME()) > 7)
ORDER BY sp.rows DESC;
GO

11.2 Wait statistics: deltas, not cumulative counters

A wait is a queue at a resource, and the queue names itself. The sys.dm_os_ view holds one row per wait type with the accumulated time threads have spent in that state since the instance started, so a single reading tells you about the whole life of the instance and about nothing in particular. The only meaningful reading is a difference between two readings, taken at the start and end of a window in which you know what the workload was doing.

Wait typeMeaningWhat it is waiting forFirst actions
PAGEIOLA shared latch on a data page that is not in the buffer pool, while the read completesStorage latency or a working set larger than memoryRead sys.dm_io_virt latency; check page life expectancy; check whether the index used is the one intended; consider memory before adding indexes
CXPAA parallel worker is synchronising with the rest of the queryCoordination between threads, not a resourceDivide CPU by DOP; check for repeated recompiles; check whether the parallelism earns its cost; consider a MAX of 1 for a small hot query
LATCExclusive access to a short-lived in-memory structure such as a log file, an allocation block or a metadata cacheContention on internal structures, frequently a symptom of high write volume or of many concurrent compilesCheck for long-running transactions holding the log; check for a scalar UDF in a predicate forcing serial plans; check the tem version store
WRITWaiting for the write-ahead log to be flushed to diskTransaction log write latencyMeasure log flush latency on the volume holding the log; check for a log file sharing a volume with a data file; check commit frequency on durable tables in memory-optimised containers
SOS_SCHEDA runnable worker gave up its quantum because another runnable worker was waitingCPU oversubscription: more runnable workers than schedulersCheck sys.dm_os_ runnable-task counts against scheduler count; check the worker-thread settings; the fix is fewer concurrent requests, not more threads
THREADPOO, RESOURCE_SEMAPHORequests cannot start because no worker thread is freeThread starvationCheck max worker threadsmax worker threads; check for a large number of blocked requests each consuming a thread; reduce concurrency at the application

A wait type is evidence, not a diagnosis. PAGEIOL at the top of the list is what a memory-starved instance looks like and also what a cold buffer pool on a perfectly sized instance looks like immediately after a restart. Distinguishing them requires the delta of the buffer manager counters over the same window, which is why the two snapshots below are taken together rather than separately.

-- Take the first snapshot. A temp table is the right container: it is local to
-- the session, so two engineers taking snapshots do not overwrite each other.
IF OBJECT_ID('tempdb..#WaitBase') IS NOT NULL DROP TABLE #WaitBase;

SELECT wait_type, waiting_tasks_count, wait_time_ms, max_wait_time_ms, signal_wait_time_ms
INTO   #WaitBase
FROM   sys.dm_os_wait_stats;
GO

-- ... the workload being investigated runs here ...

-- The second snapshot and the delta. Note the ORDER BY: rank by the wait time
-- accumulated in the window, not by the lifetime total, which is dominated by
-- whatever the instance did in its first month.
SELECT TOP (20)
       b.wait_type,
       w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count             AS waits_delta,
       w.wait_time_ms         - b.wait_time_ms                   AS total_wait_ms,
       w.signal_wait_time_ms  - b.signal_wait_time_ms            AS total_signal_ms,
       CASE WHEN w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count > 0
            THEN (w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms)
               / (w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count)
       END                                                       AS avg_wait_ms
FROM       sys.dm_os_wait_stats AS w
INNER JOIN #WaitBase           AS b ON b.wait_type = w.wait_type
WHERE      w.waiting_tasks_count > b.waiting_tasks_count
ORDER BY   w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms DESC;
GO

signal_wa is the part of the wait in which the thread was runnable and waiting for a CPU to be assigned. A wait with a large wait_t and a small signal_wa is a resource problem; a wait with large signal time is a CPU problem, and the resource named in the wait type is where the CPU is being spent. max_wait is the worst single occurrence since the instance started, which is the figure to look at when diagnosing an intermittent incident rather than an average one.

-- The same delta with the wait types that are always present and never mean
-- anything removed. This list is long because the list of background waits is
-- long, and a long list is the honest answer: do not filter by guesswork, and
-- do not persist a filtered view as the only record.
SELECT TOP (20)
       b.wait_type,
       w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count             AS waits_delta,
       w.wait_time_ms         - b.wait_time_ms                   AS total_wait_ms,
       w.wait_time_ms         - b.wait_time_ms
           - (w.signal_wait_time_ms - b.signal_wait_time_ms)      AS total_resource_ms
FROM       sys.dm_os_wait_stats AS w
INNER JOIN #WaitBase           AS b ON b.wait_type = w.wait_type
WHERE      w.waiting_tasks_count > b.waiting_tasks_count
  AND      b.wait_type NOT IN (N'SLEEP_TASK', N'WAITFOR', N'XE_DISPATCHER_WAIT',
                               N'LAZYWRITER_SLEEP', N'CHECKPOINT_QUEUE',
                               N'HADR_CLUSAPI_VERSION_CHECK_EVENT',
                               N'HADR_WORKER_IDLE', N'HADR_SYNC_COMMIT',
                               N'BROKER_RECEIVE_WAITFOR', N'BROKER_TASK_STOP',
                               N'BROKER_TO_FLUSH', N'BROKER_EVENTHANDLER',
                               N'ONDEMAND_TASK_QUEUE', N'PARALLEL_REDO_DRAIN_WORKER',
                               N'PVS_WAIT_FOR_CLEANUP', N'MEMORY_ALLOCATION_EXT',
                               N'RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE_QUERY_COMPILE',
                               N'SQLTRACE_BUFFER_FLUSH', N'SQLTRACE_INCREMENTAL_FLUSH_SLEEP',
                               N'TRACEWRITE', N'WAIT_FOR_RESULTS')
ORDER BY   w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms DESC;
GO

-- The scheduler view behind SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD. If the runnable count is
-- persistently at or above the number of schedulers, the instance has more
-- work than it has cores and the only honest fixes are fewer requests,
-- cheaper requests, or more cores.
SELECT  s.status,
          s.scheduler_id,
          s.cpu_count,
          s.runnable_tasks_count,
          s.current_tasks_count,
          s.work_queue_count,
          s.pending_disk_io_count,
          s.scheduler_type
FROM      sys.dm_os_schedulers AS s
ORDER BY  s.runnable_tasks_count DESC;
GO

Sample the waits and the buffer manager counters in the same window, and read them together. The combination that identifies memory pressure is rising PAGEIOL delta, a low Page life expectancyPage life expectancy, a high Page reads/secPage reads/sec and a runnable task count at or above the scheduler count. The combination that identifies a slow volume is the same PAGEIOL delta with a normal Page life expectancyPage life expectancy and a high sys.dm_io_virt read latency. Neither is visible from the other, and both present to the user as "the database is slow".

-- Storage latency, per database file. stdevdev_read_latency above roughly 10 ms
-- on a local SSD volume is worth investigating; the same number on a
-- network-attached file is a different conversation entirely.
SELECT  DB_NAME(fs.database_id)                              AS DatabaseName,
        mf.name                                               AS LogicalName,
        mf.type_desc                                          AS FileType,
        CAST(fs.num_of_reads          AS bigint)              AS Reads,
        CAST(fs.num_of_writes         AS bigint)              AS Writes,
        CAST(fs.stdevdev_read_latency  AS decimal(9,2))       AS ReadLatencyMs,
        CAST(fs.stdevdev_write_latency AS decimal(9,2))       AS WriteLatencyMs,
        CAST(fs.num_of_bytes_read     AS decimal(18,0))       AS BytesRead,
        CAST(fs.num_of_bytes_written  AS decimal(18,0))       AS BytesWritten
FROM    sys.dm_io_virtual_file_stats(NULL, NULL) AS fs
JOIN    sys.master_files   AS mf ON mf.database_id = fs.database_id
                             AND mf.file_id    = fs.file_id
WHERE   fs.database_id <> 2      -- exclude tempdb unless that is the subject
ORDER BY fs.stdevdev_read_latency DESC;
GO

-- Log flush behaviour. WRITELOG is the wait this query explains, and the
-- counters that explain it are per-database rather than instance-wide.
SELECT  instance_name                                   AS DatabaseName,
        cntr_name                                      AS CounterName,
        cntr_value                                     AS Value
FROM    sys.dm_os_performance_counters
WHERE   object_name = N'SQLServer:Databases'
  AND   cntr_name IN (N'Log Flushes/sec',
                      N'Log Flush Wait Time',
                      N'Log Flushes Outstanding',
                      N'Log Write Completion',
                      N'Log Flush Size',
                      N'Transaction Logs Size',
                      N'Transaction Log Flushes')
  AND   instance_name <> N'_Total'
ORDER BY instance_name, cntr_name;
GO
-- Waiting-wait aggregation by database. wait_stats is instance-wide, so a
-- busy reporting database on one instance hides everything on the others.
-- sys.dm_exec_requests carries the database id of the session that is waiting,
-- and that is the join that makes the view per-database.
SELECT  DB_NAME(r.database_id)                                   AS DatabaseName,
        w.wait_type,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                                            AS Events,
        SUM(CAST(w.wait_duration AS bigint)) / 1000             AS TotalWaitMs
FROM    sys.dm_os_waiting_tasks  AS w
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_requests     AS r ON r.session_id = w.session_id
WHERE   r.database_id <> 0
GROUP BY DB_NAME(r.database_id), w.wait_type
ORDER BY TotalWaitMs DESC;
GO

-- Waiting right now, which is a different question from what has accumulated.
-- This is the query to run during an incident, and it is cheap: it reads
-- in-memory structures only.
SELECT  r.session_id,
        r.status,
        r.command,
        w.wait_type,
        w.wait_duration,
        w.waiting_tasks_count,
        w.wait_queue_address,
        r.blocking_session_id,
        s.login_name,
        s.program_name,
        t.text                                             AS BatchText
FROM    sys.dm_os_waiting_tasks AS w
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_requests   AS r ON r.session_id = w.session_id
LEFT JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions AS s ON s.session_id = r.session_id
OUTER APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(r.sql_handle) AS t
WHERE   r.session_id <> @@SPID
ORDER BY w.wait_duration DESC;
GO

-- Thread starvation, which is the failure mode that looks like a hang.
-- THREADPOOL_STEPPED means a request exists that has no worker thread to run
-- on, and the number of OS threads the instance is allowed is in
-- sys.dm_os_sys_info.
SELECT  r.wait_type,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                                              AS Requests,
        SUM(CAST(r.worker_time AS bigint)) / 1000                 AS TotalWorkerMs
FROM    sys.dm_exec_requests AS r
WHERE   r.wait_type IN (N'THREADPOOL_STEPPED', N'THREADPOOL_WAIT'
                        OR r.status = N'pending')
GROUP BY r.wait_type;
GO

SELECT  sqlserver_max_worker_threads,
        sqlserver_max_dop,
        scheduler_count
FROM    sys.dm_os_sys_info;
GO

-- A long-running read transaction on a snapshot-enabled database, which is
-- the usual reason the version store fills tempdb. The transaction that
-- started earliest is the one holding the oldest row versions.
SELECT  s.session_id,
        s.login_name,
        s.host_name,
        at.transaction_begin_time,
        DATEDIFF(minute, at.transaction_begin_time, SYSDATETIME()) AS AgeMinutes,
        s.open_transaction_count
FROM    sys.dm_tran_active_transactions  AS at
JOIN    sys.dm_tran_session_transactions AS st ON st.transaction_id = at.transaction_id
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_sessions             AS s  ON s.session_id       = st.session_id
WHERE   s.is_user_process = 1
ORDER BY at.transaction_begin_time;
GO
-- The five most common waits, read as a rate rather than a total, which is the
-- form that survives being put on a dashboard. The rate is meaningless without
-- the window, so the window is part of the row.
DECLARE @WindowSeconds int = 300;

SELECT TOP (5)
    b.wait_type,
    (w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms) / @WindowSeconds          AS WaitMsPerSecond,
    (w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count)              AS WaitsInWindow,
    CASE WHEN (w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count) > 0
         THEN (w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms)
              / (w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count)
    END                                                          AS AvgWaitMs,
    (w.signal_wait_time_ms - b.signal_wait_time_ms)              AS SignalMsInWindow
FROM       sys.dm_os_wait_stats AS w
INNER JOIN #WaitBase            AS b ON b.wait_type = w.wait_type
WHERE      w.waiting_tasks_count > b.waiting_tasks_count
ORDER BY   w.wait_time_ms - b.wait_time_ms DESC;
GO

-- A persisted baseline, so the wait profile can be compared between two
-- periods. This is the same idea as PerfBaseline from 11.1 applied to the
-- resource picture rather than the query picture.
CREATE TABLE dbo.WaitBaseline
(
    WaitBaselineID  int IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_WaitBaseline PRIMARY KEY,
    CapturedAt      datetime2(3) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_WaitBaseline_CapturedAt DEFAULT (SYSUTCDATETIME()),
    Scenario        sysname      NOT NULL,
    WaitType        nvarchar(80) NOT NULL,
    WaitsInWindow   bigint       NOT NULL,
    WaitMsInWindow  bigint       NOT NULL,
    SignalMsInWindow bigint      NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT UQ_WaitBaseline_Scenario_WaitType UNIQUE (Scenario, WaitType, CapturedAt)
);
GO

INSERT INTO dbo.WaitBaseline (Scenario, WaitType, WaitsInWindow, WaitMsInWindow, SignalMsInWindow)
SELECT  N'peak-2026-09-28',
        b.wait_type,
        w.waiting_tasks_count - b.waiting_tasks_count,
        w.wait_time_ms         - b.wait_time_ms,
        w.signal_wait_time_ms  - b.signal_wait_time_ms
FROM       sys.dm_os_wait_stats AS w
INNER JOIN #WaitBase            AS b ON b.wait_type = w.wait_type
WHERE      w.waiting_tasks_count > b.waiting_tasks_count;

SELECT TOP (10)
    WaitType,
    SUM(WaitMsInWindow) / 1000                AS TotalWaitSeconds,
    SUM(WaitsInWindow)                        AS TotalWaits,
    CAST(SUM(WaitMsInWindow) / NULLIF(SUM(WaitsInWindow), 0) AS decimal(18,1)) AS AvgWaitMs,
    CAST(SUM(SignalMsInWindow) * 100.0 / NULLIF(SUM(WaitMsInWindow), 0) AS decimal(5,1)) AS SignalPct
FROM    dbo.WaitBaseline
GROUP BY WaitType
ORDER BY TotalWaitSeconds DESC;
GO

11.3 Extended Events, traces and the deadlock graph
