Extended Events is the supported tracing mechanism. SQL Server Profiler and the tr family of DB statements are deprecated and remain only for reading files that already exist. The guidance that governs everything in this section: use Extended Events for anything ongoing, and use the built-in system session for anything retrospective, because it is always on, always recording, and costs almost nothing.

Trace / ProfilerExtended Events
Support statusDeprecated. Removed from later products; retained to read existing .trc.trc filesCurrent. The only supported tracing mechanism in 2025
Cost when idleHigh. Files were written per event; a session capturing 10,000 events per second wrote 10,000 rows per second whether or not anyone was readingLow. Predicates are evaluated in the engine and non-matching events are discarded before any target sees them
ConfigurationRestart-time changes in Profiler; a trace left running is a production incidentTransact-SQL, so a session is a scriptable, source-controlled, repeatable object
FieldsA fixed column set chosen at session startSchema-modifiable; the payload is a set of xml_r or typed events, and custom fields can be added with actions
Reading old datafn_trace against a .trc.trc filesys.fn_xe_file_ against a .xel.xel file, or a query against a ring buffer target
Always-on captureNonesystem, a ring buffer that records a bounded set of error and wait events including every deadlock
-- An Extended Events session that records the waits from 11.2 along with the
-- statement that was running. Both are necessary: a wait names the resource,
-- the sql_text names the statement, and neither is useful without the other.
IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.server_event_sessions WHERE name = N'Perf_Waits')
    DROP EVENT SESSION Perf_Waits;
GO

CREATE EVENT SESSION Perf_Waits ON SERVER
ADD EVENT sqlserver.wait_info
(
    WHERE (sqlserver.wait_type IN (N'PAGEIOLATCH_SH', N'PAGEIOLATCH_EX',
                                  N'CXPACKET', N'LATCH_EX', N'WRITELOG',
                                  N'SOS_SCHEDULER_YIELD', N'PAGEIOLATCH_IO',
                                  N'THREADPOOL_STEPPED'))
    AND (sqlserver.wait_duration >= 1000)   -- milliseconds; ignore the trivial
)
ADD TARGET package.ring_buffer
(
    SET ring_buffer_target = N'<targets><event_session name="Perf_Waits" session="1"><target name="package_ring_buffer"><setting name="max_memory">32</setting><setting name="max_events">5000</setting></target></event_session></targets>'
)
WITH
(
    MAX_MEMORY              = 32 MB,
    EVENT_RETENTION_MODE    = ALLOW_SINGLE_EVENT_LOSS,
    MAX_DISPATCH_LATENCY    = 3 SECONDS,
    MAX_EVENT_SIZE          = 0 KB,
    MEMORY_PARTITION_MODE   = NONE,
    TRACK_CAUSALITY          = OFF,
    STARTUP_STATE           = ON
);
GO

ALTER EVENT SESSION Perf_Waits ON SERVER STATE = START;
GO

The ring buffer target is the right choice for a live diagnostic session: it is in memory, it is bounded, and it is read by a query. It is the wrong choice for anything longer than a few minutes, because the oldest events are overwritten and a crash loses the lot. The ring_buff setting is a string containing XML, which is why the angle brackets in the statement above are written as &lt;&lt; and &gt;&gt; entities: they are part of a string literal, not markup.

-- Reading the ring buffer. The target_data column is an XML document; the
-- events are its direct children, and each carries the payload the event
-- declared.
SELECT
    t.value('@timestamp', 'datetime2(3)')                        AS EventTime,
    t.value('(data[@name="wait_type"]/value)[1]', 'sysname')     AS WaitType,
    t.value('(data[@name="wait_duration"]/value)[1]', 'bigint')  AS WaitMs,
    t.value('(data[@name="session_id"]/value)[1]', 'int')        AS SessionId,
    t.value('(data[@name="sql_text"]/value)[1]', 'nvarchar(max)')AS SqlText
FROM   sys.dm_xe_session_targets AS st
JOIN   sys.dm_xe_sessions        AS s  ON s.address = st.event_session_address
CROSS APPLY st.target_data.nodes('//RingBufferTarget/event') AS x(t)
WHERE  s.name = N'Perf_Waits'
  AND  t.value('(data[@name="wait_type"]/value)[1]', 'sysname')
       IN (N'PAGEIOLATCH_SH', N'CXPACKET', N'LATCH_EX', N'WRITELOG')
ORDER BY EventTime DESC;
GO

-- Aggregated the way a report wants it, rather than one row per event.
SELECT
    t.value('(data[@name="wait_type"]/value)[1]', 'sysname')     AS WaitType,
    COUNT_BIG(*)                                                  AS Events,
    SUM(CAST(t.value('(data[@name="wait_duration"]/value)[1]', 'bigint') AS bigint)) / 1000 AS TotalWaitMs,
    MAX(CAST(t.value('(data[@name="wait_duration"]/value)[1]', 'bigint') AS bigint))          AS MaxWaitMs
FROM   sys.dm_xe_session_targets AS st
JOIN   sys.dm_xe_sessions        AS s  ON s.address = st.event_session_address
CROSS APPLY st.target_data.nodes('//RingBufferTarget/event') AS x(t)
WHERE  s.name = N'Perf_Waits'
GROUP BY t.value('(data[@name="wait_type"]/value)[1]', 'sysname')
ORDER BY TotalWaitMs DESC;
GO
-- Deadlocks from the always-on system_health session. This is the query to run
-- after an incident: the graph is already there, bounded to the last few
-- MB of activity, and nobody has to have enabled anything beforehand.
SELECT
    CAST(x.event_data AS xml)                                        AS DeadlockGraph,
    DATEADD(millisecond, CAST(x.event_data.value('(@timestamp)[1]', 'bigint') / 1000
                             - DATEPART(HOUR, SYSUTCDATETIME()) * 3600000
                             - DATEPART(MINUTE, SYSUTCDATETIME()) * 60000
                             - DATEPART(SECOND, SYSUTCDATETIME()) * 1000,
                             SYSUTCDATETIME())                        AS ApproxUtc
FROM   (SELECT target_data AS event_data
        FROM   sys.dm_xe_session_targets AS st
        JOIN   sys.dm_xe_sessions        AS s ON s.address = st.event_session_address
        WHERE  s.name = N'system_health'
          AND  st.target_name = N'ring_buffer') AS x;
GO

-- The same thing, reduced to the fact that matters: the victim, the statement
-- it was running, and the statement that killed it.
SELECT
    D.value('@datetime', 'datetime2(3)')          AS DeadlockTime,
    V.value('(victimProcess/@id)[1]', 'int')      AS VictimProcess,
    V.value('(victimProcess/inputbuf)[1]', 'nvarchar(max)') AS VictimSql,
    R.value('(resource-owner/../../process/@spid)[1]', 'int')  AS SurvivorSpid,
    R.value('(process/inputbuf)[1]', 'nvarchar(max)')         AS SurvivorSql,
    R.value('@name', 'sysname')                    AS LockedResource
FROM   (SELECT CAST(target_data AS xml) AS td
        FROM   sys.dm_xe_session_targets AS st
        JOIN   sys.dm_xe_sessions        AS s ON s.address = st.event_session_address
        WHERE  s.name = N'system_health'
          AND  st.target_name = N'ring_buffer') AS e
CROSS APPLY e.td.nodes('//RingBufferTarget/event[@name="xml_deadlock_report"]') AS N(x)
CROSS APPLY N.x.nodes('(data[@name="xml_report"]/value/deadlock)[1]')        AS G(d)
CROSS APPLY G.d.nodes('(//victim-list/victimProcess)[1]')                     AS V(v)
CROSS APPLY G.d.nodes('(//waiter-list/waiter/waiter-process[1]')              AS S(s)
CROSS APPLY G.d.nodes('(//resource-list/locked-resource[1]')                   AS R(r);
GO

The timestamp arithmetic in the first query reconstructs an approximate UTC time from the system ring buffer, whose time attribute is in microseconds since the epoch of the local clock; the reconstruction is a convenience for ordering. When the exact time matters, take it from the instance’s error log or from a correlated trace, because a ring buffer that has wrapped has already lost the oldest events and the surviving graph has no reliable sequence number attached to it.

-- Long-running capture: a file target, written to its own directory, read
-- with fn_xe_file_target_read_file. This is the configuration for a session
-- that runs for a week.
IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.server_event_sessions WHERE name = N'Perf_SlowQueries')
    DROP EVENT SESSION Perf_SlowQueries;
GO

CREATE EVENT SESSION Perf_SlowQueries ON SERVER
ADD EVENT sqlserver.sql_statement_completed
(
    WHERE (sqlserver.sql_statement_completed.duration >= 2000000)  -- microseconds
      AND (sqlserver.sql_statement_completed.statement_type <> 2)  -- not a cursor fetch
)
ADD TARGET package.event_file
(
    SET filename = N'D:\XEL\Perf_SlowQueries',
        max_file_size = 100,
        max_rollover_files = 10
)
WITH
(
    MAX_MEMORY           = 16 MB,
    EVENT_RETENTION_MODE = ALLOW_MULTIPLE_EVENT_LOSS,
    MAX_DISPATCH_LATENCY = 30 SECONDS,
    TRACK_CAUSALITY      = ON,
    STARTUP_STATE        = OFF
);
GO

ALTER EVENT SESSION Perf_SlowQueries ON SERVER STATE = START;
GO

-- Reading a file target. The second argument is the initial file name; the
-- third and fourth are the metadata and event file wildcard.
SELECT
    c.name                          AS ColumnName,
    c.value                        AS Value
FROM   sys.fn_xe_file_target_read_file(N'D:\XEL\Perf_SlowQueries*.xel',
                                        N'D:\XEL\Perf_SlowQueries.xel',
                                        N'D:\XEL\Perf_SlowQueries*.xel',
                                        null) AS x
CROSS APPLY x.event_data.nodes('//data')      AS d(c)
WHERE  d.c.value('@name', 'sysname') IN (N'duration', N'statement', N'row_count')
ORDER BY d.c.value('@name', 'sysname');
GO
-- Reading a legacy trace file. This is the only reason fn_trace_gettable still
-- exists, and it is worth knowing because historical incident reports and
-- old third-party monitoring products are full of them.
SELECT  StartTime,
        EventClass,
        LoginName,
        SPID,
        ApplicationName,
        HostName,
        ObjectName,
        TextData,
        CAST(Duration / 1000 AS bigint) AS DurationMs,
        Reads,
        Writes,
        LogicalReads
FROM    fn_trace_gettable(N'D:\Trace\prod_2000.trc', DEFAULT)
WHERE   StartTime >= '2026-01-01'
  AND   EventClass IN (92,    -- SQLTraceStart
                       93,    -- SQLTraceEnd
                       115,   -- Object:Created
                       116)   -- Object:Deleted
ORDER BY StartTime;
GO

-- The deprecation statement in T-SQL, so that a new session cannot be created
-- by accident on an instance where the policy is trace-only-read.
DBCC TRACEON(460);
GO

Caveats. An Extended Events session with a predicate that matches a high-frequency event and no predicate on the action set is the most common way to cause the incident one was called in to investigate. EVENT_RETENTION_MODE = ALLOW_SINGLE_EVENT_LOSSEVENT_RETENTION_MODE = ALLOW_SINGLE_EVENT_LOSS is the right setting for a diagnostic session and the wrong setting for a compliance session; a lost event in a diagnostic buffer costs a re-run, a lost event in an audit buffer costs an audit finding. Always set a bounded max_m, a bounded max_fi and an explicit max_rollo, and never leave a file target on the same volume as the data files.

11.4 Query statistics, cached plans and parameter sniffing

Two views hold the answer to "what is this instance actually spending its time on". sys.dm_exec holds one row per cached statement with cumulative counters; sys.dm_exec_ holds one row per cached plan, which is a different and usually more useful unit. The two join on plan_, and the join is where parameter sniffing becomes visible: one statement with two plans in the cache is a statement that was compiled twice for two different parameter values.

-- The top statements by total work. Note the ratio of elapsed to worker time:
-- above 1 indicates waiting (blocking, I/O or scheduling) rather than CPU.
SELECT TOP (25)
       SUBSTRING(st.text,
                 (qs.statement_start_offset / 2) + 1,
                 ((CASE qs.statement_end_offset
                        WHEN -1 THEN DATALENGTH(st.text)
                        ELSE qs.statement_end_offset
                   END - qs.statement_start_offset) / 2) + 1)          AS StatementText,
       qs.execution_count,
       CAST(qs.total_logical_reads / NULLIF(qs.execution_count, 0)
            AS decimal(18,1))                                          AS AvgLogicalReads,
       CAST(qs.total_elapsed_time  / NULLIF(qs.execution_count, 0) / 1000
            AS decimal(18,1))                                          AS AvgElapsedMs,
       CAST(qs.total_worker_time   / NULLIF(qs.execution_count, 0) / 1000
            AS decimal(18,1))                                          AS AvgCpuMs,
       qs.query_hash,
       qs.query_plan_hash
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats   AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
ORDER BY qs.total_logical_reads DESC;
GO

All the counters in this view are cumulative since the plan was compiled, so they must be converted to per-execution averages before they mean anything, and a plan that has been recompiled has lost its history. A statement executed once with a million logical reads and a statement executed a million times with one logical read are indistinguishable in the raw columns and identical in the converted ones. Divide by executi and the problem disappears.

-- Parameter sniffing made visible. More than one plan for one query_hash means the
-- same statement was compiled more than once; in the absence of recompiles,
-- that is the optimiser having adapted to a different parameter value.
SELECT
    qs.query_hash,
    COUNT(DISTINCT qs.plan_handle)                                    AS PlansInCache,
    MAX(qs.creation_time)                                             AS NewestPlan,
    MIN(qs.creation_time)                                             AS OldestPlan,
    MIN(SUBSTRING(st.text, 1, 200))                                   AS StatementText
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
WHERE  st.text <> ''
GROUP BY qs.query_hash
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT qs.plan_handle) > 1
ORDER BY PlansInCache DESC, MAX(qs.total_logical_reads) DESC;
GO

-- The two plans side by side, with the cached size that distinguishes them.
SELECT
    qs.query_hash,
    qs.plan_handle,
    cp.size_in_bytes,
    qs.creation_time,
    qs.last_execution_time,
    qs.execution_count,
    CAST(qs.total_elapsed_time / 1000 AS bigint) AS TotalElapsedMs,
    SUBSTRING(st.text, 1, 300)                   AS StatementText
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats  AS qs
JOIN   sys.dm_exec_cached_plans AS cp ON cp.plan_handle = qs.plan_handle
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
WHERE  qs.query_hash = 0x5AB1C0DE0000000000000000   -- substitute from the query above
ORDER BY qs.creation_time;
GO
-- Reading the plan itself, out of the cache, without executing the statement.
-- sys.dm_exec_query_plan returns the whole XML document; the .query method
-- restricts it to the <StmtSimple> node, which is where the operators live.
SELECT
    qs.query_plan,
    qs.query_hash,
    qs.query_plan_hash
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) AS qp
WHERE  qs.query_hash = 0x5AB1C0DE0000000000000000;
GO

-- The estimated row count for each operator, pulled out of the plan without
-- opening a client. EstimatedRowCount is an attribute of the RelOp node.
SELECT
    qp.query_plan.value('(//StmtSimple/QueryPlan/RelOp/@EstimatedRows)[1]', 'bigint')  AS TopEstimatedRows,
    qp.query_plan.value('(//StmtSimple/QueryPlan/@NonParallelPlanReason)[1]', 'nvarchar(64)') AS NonParallelReason,
    qp.query_plan.value('(//StmtSimple/QueryPlan/@DegreeOfParallelism)[1]', 'int')     AS EstimatedDop,
    qp.query_plan.value('(//StmtSimple/QueryPlan/@StatementSubTreeCost)[1]', 'float') AS SubtreeCost
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) AS qp
WHERE  qs.query_hash = 0x5AB1C0DE0000000000000000;
GO

The second query is worth learning to write. Being able to compare a plan’s estimated cardinality against the real cardinality, from a script, without a graphical client and without executing anything, is what makes it possible to keep a record of plan quality over time — and it is the only way to answer "is this estimate still right" for a statement nobody has run in weeks.

-- Three mitigations, in the order to try them. None of them is a guess: each
-- is deployed, measured with the harness from 11.1, and kept or reverted.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary
    @CustomerID int,
    @FromDate   date
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    -- 1. RECOMPILE: compile for the values supplied on every execution. Costs
    --    a compile per execution, which is free for a selective parameter and
    --    ruinous for one used by a reporting tool a thousand times a second.
    SELECT  o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount
    FROM    dbo.SalesOrder AS o
    WHERE   o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
      AND   o.OrderDate   >= @FromDate
    OPTION (RECOMPILE);
END;
GO

-- 2. OPTIMIZE FOR: compile for a chosen value, or for the average. It fixes
--    the sniffing without paying for a compile per execution, and it fixes it
--    in a direction chosen in advance, which is a guess dressed as a decision.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_OrderSummary2
    @CustomerID int,
    @FromDate   date
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    SELECT  o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount
    FROM    dbo.SalesOrder AS o
    WHERE   o.CustomerID = @CustomerID
      AND   o.OrderDate   >= @FromDate
    OPTION (RECOMPILE, MAXDOP 4);
END;
GO

-- 3. The plan guide approach, retained only because existing systems use it
--    and removing one at a time is cheaper than migrating. Uncommenting this
--    sp_create_plan_guide requires the statement text to match byte for byte,
--    including whitespace, which is why it is a poor long-term mechanism.
-- EXEC sys.sp_create_plan_guide
--      @name = N'PG_SalesOrder_CustomerID',
--      @stmt = N'SELECT o.SalesOrderID, o.OrderDate, o.TotalAmount
--                FROM dbo.SalesOrder AS o
--                WHERE o.CustomerID = @CustomerID AND o.OrderDate >= @FromDate;',
--      @type = N'SQL',
--      @module_or_batch = NULL,
--      @params = N'@CustomerID int, @FromDate date',
--      @hints = N'OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@CustomerID = 1))';
GO
-- The cache itself: what is in it, how big, and how often each plan is used.
-- size_in_bytes for an ad hoc plan is the cost of compilation; a plan cache
-- full of single-use plans is an application that concatenates strings.
SELECT
    cp.type_desc,
    cp.usecounts,
    cp.size_in_bytes,
    cp.cache_policy_type,
    cp.retained_plan_handle,
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(cp.objectid, cp.dbid) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(cp.objectid, cp.dbid) AS ModuleName,
    cp.creation_time,
    cp.last_execution_time,
    qp.query_hash
FROM   sys.dm_exec_cached_plans AS cp
LEFT JOIN sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qp ON qp.plan_handle = cp.plan_handle
ORDER BY cp.size_in_bytes DESC;
GO

-- Scoped invalidation. sp_recompile drops every cached plan for one module,
-- which is the minimum response to a schema change; the plan cache is not a
-- global resource that has to be flushed for one procedure to pick up a new
-- index.
EXEC sys.sp_recompile N'dbo.usp_OrderSummary';
GO

-- The single-use plan problem, quantified.
SELECT
    cp.type_desc,
    COUNT_BIG(*)                                      AS PlanCount,
    SUM(CASE WHEN cp.usecounts <= 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS SingleUse,
    CAST(100.0 * SUM(CASE WHEN cp.usecounts <= 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)
         / COUNT_BIG(*) AS decimal(5,1))              AS PctSingleUse,
    CAST(SUM(cp.size_in_bytes) / 1048576.0 AS decimal(12,1)) AS TotalMB
FROM   sys.dm_exec_cached_plans AS cp
GROUP BY cp.type_desc
ORDER BY TotalMB DESC;
GO

The diagnostic that resolves most parameter-sniffing arguments is the average logical reads per execution, before and after. If the average drops by an order of magnitude, the change is worth deploying regardless of how it was made. If it does not, the plan that was sniffed was not the plan that was slow, and the time is better spent on the wait statistics from 11.2. This is the discipline of 11.1 applied to a specific decision, and it is the reason the harness records rows and elapsed time rather than leaving them on the screen.

11.5 Index suggestions and the maintenance schedule

The sys.dm_db_missing_index_*sys.dm_db_missing_index_* views name indexes the optimiser wished it had, with an estimated improvement. They are workload-specific, cumulative, and free. Every one of the three properties is a reason not to apply them blindly, and the section says what to do instead: read them, group them, check them against what already exists, test one, and schedule the maintenance that follows from having taken the advice.

-- The missing index report, in its complete and correct form. The join chain is
-- four views deep and the column names are easy to misremember, so it is
-- written out in full: group_stats.group_handle to groups.index_group_handle,
-- then groups.index_handle to missing_indexes.index_handle, then to
-- missing_index_details.index_handle.
SELECT
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(d.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(d.object_id)  AS TableName,
    d.equality_columns,
    d.inequality_columns,
    d.included_columns,
    g.user_seeks,
    g.user_scans,
    g.avg_total_user_cost,
    g.avg_user_impact,
    g.last_user_seek,
    mid.is_nullable
FROM   sys.dm_db_missing_index_group_stats AS g
JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_index_groups      AS mig ON mig.index_group_handle = g.group_handle
JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_indexes           AS mid ON mid.index_handle      = mig.index_handle
JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_index_details     AS d   ON d.index_handle        = mid.index_handle
ORDER BY g.avg_total_user_cost * g.avg_user_impact DESC;
GO

Every column the view provides is worth reading. equality is the list of columns under an equality predicate, inequalit the list under a range, and included the covering list the optimiser would have used to avoid a key lookup. All three arrive as comma-separated lists of column identifiers, not as column names, which is why the report is unusable until they are resolved against sys.c as the next sample does.

-- The same report with the key columns resolved to names, which is the form a
-- decision can be made from. STRING_SPLIT with the ordinality column gives
-- the key order, and the order is the whole point of a composite key.
SELECT
    TableName,
    STRING_AGG(KeyColumn, ', ') WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY KeyOrdinal) AS KeyColumns,
    InEqualityColumns,
    InIncludedColumns,
    MaxImpactPct,
    TotalUses
FROM  (SELECT
           OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(d.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(d.object_id) AS TableName,
           eq.ord                                                            AS KeyOrdinal,
           c.name                                                            AS KeyColumn,
           NULLIF(LEN(d.inequality_columns), 0)                              AS InEqualityColumns,
           NULLIF(LEN(d.included_columns), 0)                                AS InIncludedColumns,
           CAST(g.avg_user_impact AS decimal(5,2))                          AS MaxImpactPct,
           g.user_seeks + g.user_scans                                      AS TotalUses
       FROM   sys.dm_db_missing_index_group_stats AS g
       JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_index_groups      AS mig ON mig.index_group_handle = g.group_handle
       JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_indexes           AS mid ON mid.index_handle      = mig.index_handle
       JOIN   sys.dm_db_missing_index_details     AS d   ON d.index_handle        = mid.index_handle
       CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(d.equality_columns, ',') WITH ORDINALITY AS eq(v, ord)
       JOIN   sys.columns AS c ON c.object_id = d.object_id
                                AND c.column_id = CONVERT(int, eq.v)) AS x
GROUP BY TableName, InEqualityColumns, InIncludedColumns
ORDER BY MAX(CAST(x.MaxImpactPct AS float) * x.TotalUses) DESC;
GO

Caveats. The DMV suggestions are not a script, and applying them as one is the most common way to make a database slower. They are workload-specific, so an index that helps a reporting query costs write performance on every insert of a wide table. They are cumulative since the last restart, so a suggestion that appeared once during a month-end report is presented with the same confidence as one that appeared ten thousand times during normal operation. They do not know about the indexes that already exist, so a table with a badly ordered composite index is often reported as needing a second index that is a prefix of the first. And the avg_use figure is an estimate produced by the optimiser about a plan it did not build, capped at 99 percent by design. Read them as a list of hypotheses. Test each one with the harness from 11.1, and keep only the ones that moved the number.

-- Before creating anything, look at what the table already has. The usual
-- finding is that the suggested key is a prefix of an existing composite key
-- in the wrong order, in which case the fix is a reordering rather than an
-- addition.
SELECT
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(i.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(i.object_id)  AS TableName,
    i.name                                                             AS IndexName,
    i.type_desc,
    i.is_unique,
    i.is_disabled,
    i.filter_definition,
    STUFF((SELECT ', ' + c.name
           FROM   sys.index_columns AS ic
           JOIN   sys.columns        AS c ON c.object_id = ic.object_id
                                        AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
           WHERE  ic.object_id = i.object_id
             AND  ic.index_id   = i.index_id
             AND  ic.key_ordinal > 0
           ORDER BY ic.key_ordinal
           FOR XML PATH(''), TYPE).value('.', 'nvarchar(max)'), 1, 2, '') AS KeyColumns,
    ISNULL(us.user_seeks, 0) + ISNULL(us.user_scans, 0) + ISNULL(us.user_lookups, 0) AS Uses
FROM   sys.indexes AS i
LEFT JOIN sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats AS us
       ON us.object_id = i.object_id AND us.index_id = i.index_id
      AND us.database_id = DB_ID()
WHERE  i.object_id = OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.SalesOrder')
  AND  i.type <> 0
ORDER BY i.index_id;
GO

-- The creation, once a suggestion has been tested. The order of the key
-- columns is the decision, not the suggestion: equality predicates first,
-- then the range predicate, and only then the sort order.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_CustomerID_OrderDate
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (Status, TotalAmount)
    WITH (SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF, ONLINE = OFF, FILLFACTOR = 100)
    ON [PRIMARY];
GO

-- Then re-measure with the harness from 11.1 and record the result as a second
-- scenario. An index that does not move the average logical reads is a write
-- cost with no read benefit and should be dropped.
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline
     @Scenario  = N'ch11-openorders-indexed',
     @Iteration = 1,
     @Statement = @Warm;
GO
-- Before creating anything, look at what the table already has. The usual
-- finding is that the suggested key is a prefix of an existing composite key
-- in the wrong order, in which case the fix is a reordering rather than an
-- addition.
SELECT
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(i.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(i.object_id)  AS TableName,
    i.name                                                             AS IndexName,
    i.type_desc,
    i.is_unique,
    i.is_disabled,
    i.filter_definition,
    STUFF((SELECT ', ' + c.name
           FROM   sys.index_columns AS ic
           JOIN   sys.columns        AS c ON c.object_id = ic.object_id
                                        AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
           WHERE  ic.object_id = i.object_id
             AND  ic.index_id   = i.index_id
             AND  ic.key_ordinal > 0
           ORDER BY ic.key_ordinal
           FOR XML PATH(''), TYPE).value('.', 'nvarchar(max)'), 1, 2, '') AS KeyColumns,
    ISNULL(us.user_seeks, 0) + ISNULL(us.user_scans, 0) + ISNULL(us.user_lookups, 0) AS Uses
FROM   sys.indexes AS i
LEFT JOIN sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats AS us
       ON us.object_id  = i.object_id
      AND us.index_id   = i.index_id
      AND us.database_id = DB_ID()
WHERE  i.object_id = OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.SalesOrder')
  AND  i.type <> 0
ORDER BY i.index_id;
GO

-- The creation, once a suggestion has been tested. The order of the key
-- columns is the decision, not the suggestion: equality predicates first,
-- then the range predicate, and only then a sort order. ONLINE = ON requires
-- Enterprise, Developer or Evaluation; it is an edition restriction, not a
-- version one.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_CustomerID_OrderDate
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (CustomerID, OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (Status, TotalAmount)
    WITH (SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF, ONLINE = OFF, FILLFACTOR = 100)
    ON [PRIMARY];
GO

-- Then re-measure with the harness from 11.1 and record the result as a
-- second scenario. An index that does not move the average logical reads is a
-- write cost with no read benefit and should be dropped.
EXEC dbo.usp_RecordBaseline
     @Scenario  = N'ch11-openorders-indexed',
     @Iteration = 1,
     @Statement = @Warm;
GO
-- Index maintenance as a schedule rather than a reflex. The fragmentation
-- thresholds come from SQL Server 2000 on magnetic disk; on SSD and NVMe they
-- describe a cost that no longer exists, so the gate below is deliberately
-- conservative: it acts on large indexes only, it never rebuilds an index
-- whose working set fits comfortably in the buffer pool, and it always
-- refreshes statistics.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_MaintainIndexes
    @MinSizeMB      int = 1024,      -- do not touch anything smaller
    @ReorgPercent   int = 15,        -- SSD: only genuinely scattered pages
    @RebuildPercent int = 30,
    @MaxRuntimeMins int = 60
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;

    DECLARE @start datetime2(3) = SYSDATETIME();

    -- Fragmentation, sampled. LIMITED is a sample and is fast; DETAILED
    -- walks every page and is only correct for something being rebuilt.
    CREATE TABLE #frag
    (
        object_id     int           NOT NULL,
        index_id      int           NOT NULL,
        name          sysname       NOT NULL,
        size_mb       decimal(12,1) NOT NULL,
        frag_pct      decimal(5,2)  NOT NULL,
        page_space_pct decimal(5,2) NOT NULL
    );

    INSERT INTO #frag (object_id, index_id, name, size_mb, frag_pct, page_space_pct)
    SELECT  ps.object_id,
            ps.index_id,
            OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(ps.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(ps.object_id) + ' (' + i.name + ')',
            CAST(ps.page_count * 8.0 / 1024 AS decimal(12,1)),
            CAST(ps.avg_fragmentation_in_percent AS decimal(5,2)),
            CAST(ps.page_space_used_percent      AS decimal(5,2))
    FROM    sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats(DB_ID(), NULL, NULL, NULL, 'LIMITED') AS ps
    JOIN    sys.indexes AS i ON i.object_id = ps.object_id AND i.index_id = ps.index_id
    WHERE   i.type <> 0
      AND   i.is_disabled = 0
      AND   ps.page_count * 8.0 / 1024 >= @MinSizeMB;

    -- Reorganise: cheap, minimally logged, no statistics update. Safe to run
    -- against a live index; it takes a Sch-M lock only for the duration.
    DECLARE @sql nvarchar(max);

    SELECT @sql = STRING_AGG(
             N'ALTER INDEX ' + QUOTENAME(f.name) + N' ON ' +
             QUOTENAME(OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(f.object_id)) + N'.' + QUOTENAME(OBJECT_NAME(f.object_id)) +
             N' REORGANIZE;', N'')
    FROM   #frag AS f
    WHERE  f.frag_pct BETWEEN @ReorgPercent AND @RebuildPercent
      AND  DATEDIFF(minute, @start, SYSDATETIME()) < @MaxRuntimeMins;

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @sql;

    -- Rebuild: fully logged, updates statistics, needs space in the file and
    -- room in the log. SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON moves the sort out of the data file
    -- so pages are not moved twice; it does not reduce log usage.
    SELECT @sql = STRING_AGG(
             N'ALTER INDEX ' + QUOTENAME(f.name) + N' ON ' +
             QUOTENAME(OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(f.object_id)) + N'.' + QUOTENAME(OBJECT_NAME(f.object_id)) +
             N' REBUILD WITH (SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF, MAXDOP = 2);', N'')
    FROM   #frag AS f
    WHERE  f.frag_pct > @RebuildPercent
      AND  DATEDIFF(minute, @start, SYSDATETIME()) < @MaxRuntimeMins;

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @sql;

    -- Statistics: the step that is skipped and the one that produces a bad
    -- plan on the first query of the morning after a bulk load.
    SELECT @sql = STRING_AGG(
             N'UPDATE STATISTICS ' + QUOTENAME(OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(f.object_id)) + N'.' +
             QUOTENAME(OBJECT_NAME(f.object_id)) + N' WITH FULLSCAN;', N'')
    FROM   #frag AS f
    WHERE  DATEDIFF(minute, @start, SYSDATETIME()) < @MaxRuntimeMins;

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @sql;

    SELECT Scenario = N'index maintenance',
           IndexesTouched = COUNT_BIG(*),
           ElapsedMs = DATEDIFF_BIG(millisecond, @start, SYSDATETIME());
END;
GO

Caveats. The thresholds above are not a validated schedule; they are the shape of one, and the numbers are the ones most likely to be wrong in any particular installation. A nightly rebuild of a large table is a full log write, a large tem allocation and hours of CPU, bought for a fragmentation percentage that means nothing on solid-state storage, and it is a routine cause of the outage it was meant to prevent. Rebuild when a measured statement is slow and fragmentation is part of the explanation. Note also that REB on a FULLFULL-recovery database is fully logged and can consume more log than the operation would suggest; SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ONSORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON does not change that, and a maintenance window without a log sized for the rebuild is a failed job rather than a slow one.

-- The statistics refresh policy, stated separately from the rebuild policy
-- because it is a different decision. A filtered index, a computed column or
-- a columnstore table all have their own statistics objects and are not
-- covered by an UPDATE STATISTICS on the base table.
SELECT
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(s.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(s.object_id) AS TableName,
    s.name                                                            AS StatName,
    sp.rows,
    sp.rows_sampled,
    sp.unfiltered_rows,
    sp.step,
    sp.last_updated,
    s.auto_created,
    s.is_filtered
FROM   sys.stats AS s
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_db_stats_properties(s.object_id, s.stats_id) AS sp
WHERE  sp.rows > 100000
ORDER BY sp.rows DESC;
GO

-- Refreshing the large tables with a higher sample rate, and the filtered
-- statistics that a filtered index needs, is a per-table decision that the
-- schedule should reflect.
EXEC sys.sp_update_stats N'Customer', N'SalesOrder', N'SalesOrderLine';
GO

UPDATE STATISTICS dbo.SalesOrder (IX_SalesOrder_CustomerID_OrderDate) WITH FULLSCAN;
GO

-- Confirm the result rather than assuming it. A rebuild that was skipped by
-- the runtime gate leaves the same row here as one that ran, and the
-- difference is visible only in the last_update time.
SELECT
    OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(s.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(s.object_id) AS TableName,
    s.name                                                           AS StatName,
    sp.last_updated,
    sp.rows_sampled,
    sp.rows
FROM   sys.stats AS s
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_db_stats_properties(s.object_id, s.stats_id) AS sp
WHERE  s.object_id IN (OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.Customer'),
                       OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.SalesOrder'),
                       OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.SalesOrderLine'))
ORDER BY TableName, StatName;
GO

11.6 Memory, parallelism, checkpoints and the pre-production checklist

The last three configuration decisions are made once, badly, and then never revisited. Memory sizing, the maximum degree of parallelism, and the recovery model determine more of the observable behaviour of an instance than any query in it, and each of them has a documented default that is wrong for almost every production installation.

SettingScopeDefaultProduction guidanceEffect of getting it wrong
max server memorymax server memory (MB)InstanceRoughly 80 percent of physical RAM on a dedicated instanceLeave headroom for the operating system, for the non-buffer-pool allocations, and for any co-located component. On a database that runs memory-optimised tables, or on one hosting an tem with a large tem version store, the headroom must be larger than 20 percentToo high: the buffer pool takes memory the OS needs, and the instance is terminated by the low-memory killer or hangs on nonpaged pool exhaustion. Too low: a working set that does not fit, and every request becomes a physical read
max degree of parallelismmax degree of parallelismInstance, overridable per database, per query, and per index operation0, meaning unlimited up to the number of logical processors available to the instance8 is a reasonable instance setting on a machine with more than 8 cores; a single hot query often does better with 1 or 2, because coordination costs more than the parallelism gainsToo high: CXPA dominates, a small query uses 32 threads to read 200 rows, and every thread is a thread the other queries cannot have
cost threshold for parallelismcost threshold for parallelismInstance5Raise it to between 20 and 50 on instances with many concurrent small queries; the default makes almost every query eligible, and a batch on a hundred-row table is not a batch that wants 32 threadsToo low: parallel plans for trivial statements. Too high: genuinely large reports run serially on one core
recovery modelrecovery modelDatabaseSIMFU wherever point-in-time restore is a requirement, which is nearly everywhere a transaction log is described as a business assetSIM on a database that needs point-in-time recovery is a recovery objective that is silently unmeetable. FU on a database that does not log-ship is a log file that grows until it fills the volume
max worker threadsmax worker threadsInstance0, meaning 255Leave at the default unless blocking is being diagnosed; raising it increases the cost of blocking without reducing itRaised unnecessarily: every blocked query keeps a thread busy doing nothing, and THREADPOO appears under load that previously queued
-- Reading the current memory configuration, and the actual allocation. The two
-- numbers are not the same: max server memory is a ceiling on the buffer pool
-- the engine will grow to, not a reservation.
SELECT  name,
        CAST(value AS bigint)       AS ValueInMB,
        CAST(value_in_use AS bigint) AS ValueInUseMB,
        description
FROM    sys.configurations
WHERE   name IN (N'max server memory (MB)',
                 N'min server memory (MB)',
                 N'max degree of parallelism',
                 N'cost threshold for parallelism',
                 N'max worker threads',
                 N'lazy write enabled',
                 N'priority boost',
                 N'lightweight pooling');
GO

SELECT  physical_memory_kb / 1024                        AS PhysicalMemoryMB,
        locked_page_allocations_kb / 1024               AS LockedPagesMB,
        virtual_address_space_committed / 1024 / 1024   AS CommittedMB,
        AweTargetLevel
FROM    sys.dm_os_sys_memory;
GO

-- The engine's own view of where the memory went. A large
-- memory_clerks row is not itself a problem; what matters is the process
-- physical memory high versus the configured ceiling.
SELECT  type,
        physical_memory_in_use_kb / 1024 AS PhysicalMB,
        CASE WHEN virtual_memory_reserved_kb > 0
             THEN CAST(100.0 * physical_memory_in_use_kb / virtual_memory_reserved_kb AS decimal(5,1))
        END                                AS InUsePct,
        pages_allocated_count,
        page_alloc_type_desc
FROM    sys.dm_os_process_memory
ORDER BY physical_memory_in_use_kb DESC;
GO

SELECT  clerk_type,
        name,
        SUM(pages_allocated_count) * 8 / 1024 AS AllocatedMB
FROM    sys.dm_os_memory_clerks
GROUP BY clerk_type, name
ORDER BY SUM(pages_allocated_count) DESC;
GO

On a machine where the lock pages limit is set in lock for the SQL Server service account, the buffer pool is locked in physical memory and is never paged out. The engine then recommends a max server memorymax server memory that leaves the operating system room for its own nonpaged pool, which in practice means more headroom than the 80 percent rule, because the nonpaged pool cannot be trimmed. The setting is per account, is invisible from inside SQL Server, and is the reason two identically configured instances on two identically specified machines behave differently.

-- Setting memory correctly. The value is in megabytes, the change is dynamic,
-- and RECONFIGURE applies it without a restart. The buffer pool does not
-- release memory immediately when the ceiling is lowered: the pages that are
-- resident are made eligible for lazy truncation and are released as
-- pressure demands, which is why a reduction appears to have no effect for
-- some time and why a reduction below the current resident size is safe.
DECLARE @PhysicalMB int = (SELECT physical_memory_kb / 1024 FROM sys.dm_os_sys_memory);
DECLARE @NewMaxMB   int = (SELECT CAST(0.80 * @PhysicalMB AS int));

EXEC sys.sp_configure N'max server memory (MB)', @NewMaxMB;
RECONFIGURE;
GO

-- Verify. A RECONFIGURE that failed to apply leaves the old value, and the
-- value reported here is the one the engine is actually honouring.
SELECT name, CAST(value AS bigint) AS ValueInMB, CAST(value_in_use AS bigint) AS ValueInUseMB
FROM   sys.configurations
WHERE  name = N'max server memory (MB)';
GO

-- Parallelism. Both settings are dynamic. Neither is a promise about a
-- particular query: MAXDOP can be overridden per query with OPTION, per
-- database with a Resource Governor workload group, and per index operation.
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'max degree of parallelism', 8;
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'cost threshold for parallelism', 25;
RECONFIGURE;
GO

SELECT
    MAX(CASE WHEN name = N'max degree of parallelism'          THEN CAST(value AS int) END) AS MaxDop,
    MAX(CASE WHEN name = N'cost threshold for parallelism'     THEN CAST(value AS int) END) AS CostThreshold
FROM   sys.configurations;
GO

-- Per-query control, which is the form to reach for on a single hot statement
-- rather than changing an instance-wide default.
SELECT TOP (20)
    qs.execution_count,
    CAST(qs.total_worker_time / NULLIF(qs.execution_count, 0) / 1000 AS decimal(18,1)) AS AvgCpuMs,
    CAST(qs.total_elapsed_time / NULLIF(qs.execution_count, 0) / 1000 AS decimal(18,1)) AS AvgElapsedMs,
    qp.query_plan.value('(//QueryPlan/@DegreeOfParallelism)[1]', 'int')                AS EstimatedDop,
    SUBSTRING(st.text, 1, 160)                                                          AS StatementText
FROM   sys.dm_exec_query_stats AS qs
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) AS st
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) AS qp
WHERE  qp.query_plan IS NOT NULL
  AND  qp.query_plan.value('(//QueryPlan/@DegreeOfParallelism)[1]', 'int') > 1
ORDER BY qs.total_worker_time DESC;
GO
-- Checkpoints, the log, and the difference the recovery model makes. A
-- checkpoint in FULL recovery writes dirty pages and the log records survive
-- past it; a checkpoint in SIMPLE recovery additionally marks the log reusable
-- up to the point it has been written, which is why the log stops growing
-- without anybody truncating it.
SELECT  name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        log_reuse_wait_desc,
        compatibility_level,
        is_auto_create_stats_on,
        is_auto_update_stats_on,
        is_read_committed_snapshot_on,
        is_allow_snapshot_isolation_on,
        page_verify_option_desc
FROM    sys.databases
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- Why the log is not being reused. A non-NOTHING value here is the single
-- most useful line in the whole of the operational surface: it names the
-- blocker of log truncation.
SELECT  DB_NAME(database_id) AS DatabaseName,
        log_reuse_wait_desc,
        CAST(SUM(CAST(size AS bigint)) / 1048576.0 AS decimal(12,1)) AS LogSizeMB
FROM    sys.dm_db_log_space_usage
GROUP BY database_id, log_reuse_wait_desc
ORDER BY SUM(CAST(size AS bigint)) DESC;
GO

SELECT  DB_NAME(database_id) AS DatabaseName,
        total_log_size_in_bytes / 1048576 AS TotalLogMB,
        used_log_space_in_bytes / 1048576 AS UsedLogMB,
        used_log_space_in_percent          AS UsedLogPct
FROM    sys.dm_db_log_space_usage;
GO

-- Switching the model, and the two statements that must accompany it. A
-- database in FULL recovery with no log backup since the last full backup
-- cannot be truncated at all, which is the behaviour that surprises people
-- who switch the model expecting the log to shrink.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY SIMPLE;
GO
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY FULL;
GO
CHECKPOINT;
GO

-- DBCC CHECKDB, which verifies page and row integrity, allocation
-- consistency, index consistency and the catalog. It is scheduled, not run
-- reactively: a corrupt page found during a business query is discovered after
-- the damage has been read, and CHECKDB afterwards confirms the extent rather
-- than preventing it.
DBCC CHECKDB (N'dbo.SalesOrder') WITH NO_INFOMSGS, ALL_ERRORMSGS;
GO

-- Options: PHYSICAL_ONLY skips the logical checks and is the variant that
-- can be run against a live system in a maintenance window; TABLOCK improves
-- throughput by taking a shared lock; ESTIMATEONLY reports what would be
-- checked without doing it, and is how a schedule is justified against a
-- time budget.
DBCC CHECKDB (N'dbo.SalesOrder') WITH PHYSICAL_ONLY, TABLOCK, NO_INFOMSGS;
GO
DBCC CHECKDB (N'dbo.SalesOrder') WITH ESTIMATEONLY;
GO

The relationship between the log and the recovery model is worth stating once, plainly, because it is the source of a large fraction of production incidents. In SIM, a checkpoint marks the portion of the log that has been written to disk as reusable, and the next checkpoint can overwrite it, so the log is bounded by the largest checkpoint interval’s worth of writes. In FU, nothing marks the log reusable except a log backup, so the log grows by the full amount written since the last one, without bound, until one is taken. The same workload therefore produces a small log in one model and a large one in the other, and the only thing that changed was the recovery objective the database is able to meet.

-- The memory the engine asked for, and the memory it was given. A query with
-- a sort or hash join on a large input computes a grant before it starts; a
-- grant larger than what is available is error 8645, and a request waiting for
-- one shows up here rather than in the wait statistics.
SELECT  r.session_id,
        r.status,
        r.command,
        r.wait_type,
        r.wait_time,
        r.wait_resource,
        mg.requested_memory_kb,
        mg.granted_memory_kb,
        mg.used_memory_kb,
        mg.max_used_memory_kb,
        mg.request_time,
        mg.grant_time,
        mg.wait_time_ms,
        t.text                                            AS BatchText
FROM    sys.dm_exec_query_memory_grants AS mg
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_requests            AS r  ON r.request_id = mg.request_id
LEFT JOIN sys.dm_exec_sessions         AS s  ON s.session_id  = r.session_id
OUTER APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(r.sql_handle) AS t
WHERE   r.session_id <> @@SPID
ORDER BY mg.requested_memory_kb DESC;
GO

-- The aggregate view of the same, which is the one that says whether the
-- instance is short of memory in general or one query is asking for too much.
SELECT  SUM(requested_memory_kb) / 1024 AS TotalRequestedMB,
        SUM(granted_memory_kb)   / 1024 AS TotalGrantedMB,
        SUM(used_memory_kb)      / 1024 AS TotalUsedMB,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                     AS ActiveGrants,
        SUM(CASE WHEN is_waiting = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS WaitingGrants
FROM    sys.dm_exec_query_memory_grants;
GO

-- The diagnosis when the numbers disagree: a small total request with a
-- large number of waiting grants means the ceiling is the problem; a single
-- request for most of the memory means one statement is the problem.
SELECT  session_id,
          requested_memory_kb,
          granted_memory_kb,
          wait_time_ms,
          ideal_plan
FROM    sys.dm_exec_query_memory_grants
WHERE   is_waiting = 1
ORDER BY requested_memory_kb DESC;
GO
-- The instance restart history, which is the context every performance
-- investigation needs and which nobody records: a delta of wait statistics
-- across a restart is meaningless, and a memory configuration that looks
-- correct may have been applied after the last restart.
SELECT  sqlserver_start_time                            AS InstanceStart,
        DATEDIFF(hour, sqlserver_start_time, SYSUTCDATETIME()) AS HoursUptime,
        sqlserver_process_id,
        sqlserver_max_worker_threads,
        sqlserver_max_dop,
        scheduler_count,
        processor_count
FROM    sys.dm_os_sys_info;
GO

-- The Windows event log entries for the service, which is where a restart
-- reason, a crash dump and a low-memory termination are recorded. A service
-- that restarted without an entry here was restarted deliberately.
EXEC sys.xp_readerrorlog 0, 1, N'SQL Server is starting';
GO

-- Database-scoped settings, which override instance settings and are the
-- reason two databases on one instance behave differently for the same
-- workload. These are the settings that drift.
SELECT  ds.name           AS DatabaseName,
        ds.max_server_memory_in_percent,
        ds.max_parallel_workers_per_dop,
        ds.max_worker_threads,
        ds.compatibility_level,
        ds.is_optimizer_hotfixes_on,
        ds.is_interlocked_execution_on,
        ds.is_parameterization_forced
FROM    sys.databases AS ds
WHERE   ds.database_id > 4
ORDER BY ds.name;
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET MAXDOP = 4;
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL = 170;
GO

-- Removing the override, so that the instance default applies again. The
-- value has to be a literal, not a variable, which is why this is a script
-- and not a procedure parameter.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET MAXDOP 0;
GO

-- The full set of database-scoped settings, which is a longer list than most
-- people expect and the correct place to look when an instance-wide change
-- produced no effect.
SELECT  name, value, value_for_secondary, description
FROM    sys.database_scoped_configurations
ORDER BY name;
GO
✓Pre-production performance checkHow it is verified
✓Every statement that will run in production has a measured baseline with logical reads, elapsed time and CPU recorded against representative data volumedbo.Perf contains warm and cold rows for each scenario; the data used had production-like row counts, not development-like ones
✓No statement depends on a parameter sniffing outcome that has not been measured for the worst realistic valueEach procedure with a selective and a non-selective path was executed with both; the plan and the average logical reads were recorded for each
✓Every foreign key and every commonly joined column is indexed, so that a modification does not scansys.dm_db_missi shows no suggestion for a join column; sys.fore columns appear in sys.inde
✓Statistics are current for every table after the load that populated itsys.dm_db_stats_pro is later than the load, and sp_upda was part of the deployment script rather than an afterthought
✓max server memorymax server memory is set to a value with a documented headroom calculation, and max degree of parallelismmax degree of parallelism is set deliberatelysys.confi values recorded in the baseline; the buffer pool is not at the ceiling and the runnable task count is below the scheduler count under peak load
✓The recovery model matches the stated recovery objective, and the log backup cadence that supports it is scheduledsys.databases.re and log_reuse; a restore from a copy has been performed to the stated point in time
✓Index maintenance is scheduled with thresholds justified by measurement, not by a whitepaper from 2003The maintenance job ran in the last cycle and its elapsed time and log consumption are within the window budget
✓No scalar user-defined function appears in a predicate, a join condition or a computed column on a row-by-row basissys.sql searched for CREATE FUNCTIONCREATE FUNCTION returning a scalar; execution plans inspected for a Compute ScalarCompute Scalar on the inner side of a nested loop
✓Waits have been sampled for a representative period, and the top three are understoodsys.dm_os_ deltas captured with the query in 11.2 during a peak window and reviewed by whoever owns the system
✓A slow query has a captured plan, and the plan’s estimated rows have been compared with the actual rowsActual plan saved with the baseline; estimate-versus-actual recorded, and any operator more than an order of magnitude out explained

Exercises 11

  1. Create dbo.Perf and dbo.usp_Rec from 11.1. Run one statement five times and record all five rows, then query the mean and the median. Identify which row is the outlier and explain from the plan why that row differs.
  2. Run the same statement with statistics cold: DBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERSDBCC DROPCLEANBUFFERS, execute, record; execute again and record. Report the difference in physical reads and logical reads and state which of the two figures you would use to decide whether a new index helps.
  3. Take a sys.dm_os_ snapshot into #WaitBase#WaitBase, run a workload that reads a table with no covering index, and take the second snapshot. Report the delta for PAGEIOL and the delta for WRIT, and state which one the workload was designed to produce.
  4. Sample sys.dm_os_ under a workload that runs more concurrent queries than there are cores. Record runnable_t and confirm SOS_SCHED appears in the wait delta. Then reduce concurrency and repeat.
  5. Create the Perf_ Extended Events session from 11.3, start it, run a deliberately slow query, and read the ring buffer with the aggregation query. Confirm the recorded sql_ matches the statement you ran.
  6. Provoke a deadlock between two procedures with inconsistent lock ordering, then read the graph from system with the second query in 11.3. Record the victim statement, the survivor statement and the locked resource, then fix the ordering and confirm the deadlock no longer occurs.
  7. Create a procedure that is fast for @CustomerID = 1@CustomerID = 1 and slow for a large value, execute it with each, and confirm two plans appear in the cache for one query_hashquery_hash. Add OPTION (RECOMPILE)OPTION (RECOMPILE) and repeat, recording the average logical reads in all four cases.
  8. Query sys.dm_exec_ grouped by type and report the percentage of plans used once. Then execute a string-concatenated statement a hundred times with a hundred distinct values and repeat the query. Explain the difference and state the fix.
  9. Run the missing index report from 11.5 against a database with a known slow query. Resolve the column identifiers to names, check sys.i for an existing overlapping key, and record whether the suggestion is a new index, a reordering, or redundant.
  10. Create the proposed index, re-measure with the harness, and record the before and after average logical reads. Then measure the cost: run a 100,000-row insert before and after the index exists and record both elapsed times. Decide from the two numbers whether the index should be kept.
  11. Set max server memorymax server memory to 80 percent of physical memory, then to 95 percent, and query sys.dm_os_pr and sys.dm_os_m in each case. Record whether the resident buffer pool changed immediately and explain lazy truncation.
  12. Set cost threshold for parallelismcost threshold for parallelism to 5 and then to 50, execute the same large aggregate query, and record the plan shape, the estimated degree of parallelism and the average CPU in each case. Then set MAXDOP = 1MAXDOP = 1 on the statement and record a third set of figures.
  13. Create a database in SIM and one in FU recovery, run the same bulk insert into both, and record sys.dm_db_lo for each. Explain the difference in terms of checkpoint truncation, and confirm log_reuse reads LOG_B for the FU database after a checkpoint.
  14. Run DBCC CHECKDB ... WITH ESTIMATEONLYDBCC CHECKDB ... WITH ESTIMATEONLY on each user database and record the reported percentage. Design a schedule that fits the resulting time budget, and state which of the three option sets would be used on a live system and why.
  15. Work through the pre-production checklist in 11.6 against the reference schema. Record which items pass, which fail, and for each failure name the single change that would make it pass.

12 · Backup, Recovery and High Availability

12.1 The three backup types and how they combine12.2 Recovery models: SIMPLE and FULL12.3 BACKUP DATABASE, BACKUP LOG and verification12.4 Restore sequences, WITH NORECOVERY and point-in-time recovery12.5 Log shipping, mirroring and Always On availability groups12.6 RPO, RTO, encryption and compression of backups

A backup is only a backup if it has been restored. This chapter covers the three backup types and the arithmetic that combines them, the two recovery models and what each one makes possible, the BAC and RES grammars in the detail an operator needs, the three high-availability technologies and the current state of each, and the recovery objectives that decide which of them is warranted.

Applies to. BAC, RES, log shipping and Always On availability groups are documented identically for SQL Server 2025 (17.x), 2022 (16.x) and 2019 (15.x), with the 2025 additions noted in 12.6. Two items in this chapter carry a deprecation or a change in 2025 that must be checked before an upgrade is planned: database mirroring, which has been deprecated in favour of Always On availability groups since 2012 and should not be deployed on a new system, and log shipping, which remains supported but is one of the four areas with breaking changes in 2025 alongside linked servers, replication and PolyBase.

12.1 The three backup types and how they combine

SQL Server has exactly three backup types and no others. A full backup contains every allocated page of every data file plus enough of the transaction log to make the database consistent as of the end of the backup. A differential backup contains every page that has changed since the last full backup of the same database. A log backup contains the transaction log records written since the previous log backup of the same database.

TypeContainsBackup set identifierGrows withRestore role
FullAll allocated pages of all data files, plus the minimum log required for consistencyDatabasDatabase size, including pages allocated but recently emptiedMandatory. Every restore chain starts here
DifferentialEvery page modified since the last full backup, and the extents containing themDatabaseBa differentiates each oneChange rate since the last full, not since the last differentialOptional, at most one per restore. A second differential restores to nothing; they are cumulative, not incremental
LogTransaction log records since the previous log backup, in LSN orderDatabaseBa plus a PosiWrite volume, and the interval between log backupsRepeated. Each one extends the recovered point forward, and the chain must be unbroken

The two properties that cause most of the errors in this area are that a differential backup is cumulative, not incremental, and that a log backup chain is only as good as its first link. A differential taken after a full contains everything changed since that full, so restoring differential 1 followed by differential 2 with NOREC and then recovery does not work: differential 2 is a superset of differential 1 and applying it after differential 1 restores exactly the same state as applying differential 2 alone. And a log chain cannot be broken: a log backup taken after a full backup, followed by a second log backup, followed by a third, restores all three in order; skipping the second produces a chain that SQL Server will refuse to continue from, because the LSNs do not connect.

-- The backup history, which is the authoritative record of what exists and is
-- more reliable than the filesystem: it records the LSN range of every
-- backup, the checkpoint LSN, the finish LSN and the size on disk.
SELECT  TOP (50)
    bs.database_name,
    bs.backup_start_date,
    bs.backup_finish_date,
    bs.type                                          AS TypeCode,  -- 1 full, 2 log
    bs.is_copy_only,
    bs.is_differential,
    bs.first_lsn,
    bs.last_lsn,
    bs.checkpoint_lsn,
    bs.log_start_lsn,
    bmf.physical_device_name                          AS BackupFile,
    CAST(bmf.compressed_backup_size AS bigint) / 1048576.0 AS CompressedMB,
    bs.user_name,
    bs.name                                          AS BackupSetName
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
JOIN    msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf ON bmf.media_set_id = bs.media_set_id
WHERE   bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
ORDER BY bs.backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

-- A readable form of the same, with the type decoded. is_copy_only is the
-- column that matters for differential chains: a copy-only backup is a
-- physical image of the database for a purpose other than a restore chain,
-- and it resets the differential base.
SELECT  bs.backup_finish_date,
        CASE bs.type WHEN 1 THEN N'Full' WHEN 2 THEN N'Log' ELSE N'Other' END AS BackupType,
        CASE WHEN bs.is_copy_only = 1 THEN N'Copy-only' ELSE N'Standard' END  AS ChainRole,
        CASE WHEN bs.has_backup_checksums = 1 THEN N'Yes' ELSE N'No' END      AS Checksums,
        first_lsn, last_lsn,
        DATEDIFF(second, bs.backup_start_date, bs.backup_finish_date)          AS DurationSeconds
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE   bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
  AND   bs.type IN (1, 2)
ORDER BY bs.backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

The ty column holds 11 for a full or differential backup and 22 for a log backup; a differential is distinguished by is_differential = 1is_differential = 1, not by a distinct type code. firs and last are the log sequence numbers bracketing the backup and are the values that prove a chain is contiguous. has_backup records whether the backup was taken WITH CHECKSUMWITH CHECKSUM, and it is checked automatically on restore if present.

-- The backup set identifiers in detail. backup_set_uuid identifies the set;
-- first_recovery_fork_guid and last_recovery_fork_guid identify the recovery
-- fork, and a restore that crosses a change in the recovery fork needs
-- EXISTING, WITH IDENTITY or a full backup. This is the column to look at when
-- a restore refuses a log backup that looks correct.
SELECT  bs.backup_set_id,
        bs.backup_set_uuid,
        bs.first_recovery_fork_guid,
        bs.last_recovery_fork_guid,
        bs.recovery_model,
        bs.has_backup_checksums,
        bs.is_damaged,
        bs.is_copy_only,
        bs.first_lsn,
        bs.last_lsn,
        bs.checkpoint_lsn,
        bs.log_start_lsn,
        bs.compressed_backup_size
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE   bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
  AND   bs.type = 2
ORDER BY bs.backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

-- A backup that is recorded as damaged, which is what BACKUP writes when the
-- backup operation itself failed part-way. A damaged set is present in the
-- history and must never be restored.
SELECT  database_name, backup_finish_date, is_damaged, backup_size
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset
WHERE   is_damaged = 1
ORDER BY backup_finish_date DESC;
GO
-- A simple availability check to run every morning, which is the smallest
-- useful thing to put on a dashboard: does the most recent full backup exist,
-- how old is it, and does a contiguous log chain follow it.
SELECT  DatabaseName   = d.name,
        LastFullBackup = (SELECT MAX(backup_finish_date) FROM msdb.dbo.backupset
                          WHERE database_name = d.name AND type = 1 AND is_differential = 0),
        LastLogBackup  = (SELECT MAX(backup_finish_date) FROM msdb.dbo.backupset
                          WHERE database_name = d.name AND type = 2),
        FullAgeHours   = DATEDIFF(hour, (SELECT MAX(backup_finish_date) FROM msdb.dbo.backupset
                                         WHERE database_name = d.name AND type = 1
                                           AND is_differential = 0), GETDATE()),
        LogAgeMinutes  = DATEDIFF(minute, (SELECT MAX(backup_finish_date) FROM msdb.dbo.backupset
                                           WHERE database_name = d.name AND type = 2), GETDATE())
FROM    sys.databases AS d
WHERE   d.database_id > 4
  AND   d.state_desc = N'ONLINE'
ORDER BY LogAgeMinutes DESC;
GO
-- The restore-time arithmetic, which is the reason the differential type
-- exists. A restore chain of full plus differential plus logs replays
-- differential pages, then log operations. Log operations are sequential and
-- cannot be parallelised, so the log portion of a restore dominates: a
-- database with a large full backup and an hour of log will take an hour to
-- restore, whatever the size of the full.
SELECT
    bs.database_name,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS FullBackups,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.is_differential = 1                THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS Differentials,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2                            THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS LogBackups,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0
             THEN CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint) ELSE 0 END) / 1073741824.0 AS FullGB,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.is_differential = 1
             THEN CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint) ELSE 0 END) / 1073741824.0 AS DiffGB,
    SUM(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2
             THEN CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint) ELSE 0 END) / 1073741824.0 AS LogGB
FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE  bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
  AND  bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -7, GETDATE())
GROUP BY bs.database_name;
GO
-- A full backup of a 500 GB database, a differential every hour and log
-- backups every fifteen minutes, for a week:
--
--   full      1 x 500 GB, written once and read by every restore
--   diff      168 files, each containing everything changed in the preceding
--             full-backup window, so a restore near the end of the week
--             replays 7 days of change rather than 1 hour of it
--   log       672 files, each small, each required in order
--
-- The differential cadence is therefore a choice between restore time and
-- storage, not a free improvement. On a write-heavy database the weekly
-- differential approaches the size of a second full.
GO

The rule of thumb that survives contact with real systems: back up the log as often as the recovery point objective requires, take differential backups as often as the recovery time objective can afford, and take full backups as often as the differential restore time allows. A daily full with hourly differentials and fifteen-minute log backups is a reasonable starting point for a database where a four-hour restore is tolerable and fifteen minutes of data loss is not. Reducing the log interval to five minutes does not change the storage maths materially, and it changes the RPO by a factor of three.

-- Verifying that the backup history contains a usable chain at all, before an
-- incident rather than during one. The test is: for each database, is there a
-- full, and does the log chain start within a small number of LSNs of it?
SELECT  bs.database_name,
        MIN(bs.first_lsn)                        AS FirstLogStart,
        MAX(bs.last_lsn)                         AS LastLogEnd,
        MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0
                 THEN bs.checkpoint_lsn END)      AS LastFullCheckpoint
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE   bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -30, GETDATE())
  AND   bs.type = 2
GROUP BY bs.database_name
ORDER BY bs.database_name;
GO

-- Gaps in the log chain show up as a first_lsn that is well above the
-- previous backup's last_lsn. Reporting the gap explicitly is more useful
-- than reporting the count, because one gap in a month and one gap a day
-- need different responses.
WITH log_chain AS
(
    SELECT  database_name,
            backup_finish_date,
            first_lsn,
            last_lsn,
            LAG(last_lsn)      OVER (PARTITION BY database_name ORDER BY backup_finish_date) AS PrevLastLsn,
            LAG(backup_finish_date) OVER (PARTITION BY database_name ORDER BY backup_finish_date) AS PrevFinish
    FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset
    WHERE   type = 2
      AND   backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -7, GETDATE())
)
SELECT  database_name,
        PrevFinish,
        backup_finish_date,
        PrevLastLsn,
        first_lsn,
        DATEDIFF(minute, PrevFinish, backup_finish_date) AS GapMinutes
FROM    log_chain
WHERE   PrevLastLsn IS NOT NULL
  AND   CONVERT(binary(8), first_lsn, 1) > CONVERT(binary(8), PrevLastLsn, 1)
ORDER BY GapMinutes DESC;
GO

12.2 Recovery models: SIMPLE and FULL

The recovery model decides what a checkpoint is allowed to do with the transaction log, and therefore whether the database can be restored to a point in time. In SIM, a checkpoint marks a portion of the log as reusable and the next checkpoint overwrites it. In FU, nothing marks the log reusable except a log backup, so the log grows until one is taken. Point-in-time recovery is available only in FU, and BULK_ is a third mode with a narrower window.

SIMFUBULK_
Log reuse pointThe last checkpoint, provided no other blockerThe last log backup, provided the log has been backed up and the backup succeededEither the last log backup or, for a table under bulk logging, the last checkpoint
Log growthBounded by the write volume between checkpointsUnbounded without a log backup: the log grows by everything written since the last oneBounded for logged operations, and small for bulk operations on tables in BULK_
Point-in-time restoreNo. Only the end of a backup chainYes, to any transaction or any STO timestamp in the logYes, but only to a point outside the window covered by a bulk-logged operation
Log truncationAutomatic at a checkpointOnly by a log backup; an unbacked-up log cannot be truncated even by a checkpointAs FU, except inside a bulk-logged window
Typical useDevelopment, test, and read-heavy databases where data loss of a day is acceptableAnything with a recovery point objective stated in minutesRarely chosen deliberately; usually a side effect of a bulk operation performed in FU
Administration consequenceNo log backup schedule. Set LOGGING RECOVERYLOGGING RECOVERY back to FU and run one before a detach, a file copy or a renameA log backup schedule that is monitored, and a log_reuse that is NOTReturn to FU and take a log backup promptly; the bulk-logged window is a gap in the point-in-time capability
-- The model, and what is blocking log truncation, for every database. The
-- second column is the one that answers "why is the log growing", and
-- NOTHING is the only correct value.
SELECT  name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        log_reuse_wait_desc,
        compatibility_level,
        is_read_only,
        state_desc
FROM    sys.databases
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- Every value log_reuse_wait_desc can take, and what it means. This is the
-- single most operationally valuable list in the chapter: a log that is not
-- being reused is a database that is at risk of filling its volume.
SELECT  rls.[wait_desc] AS WaitType, rls.[meaning] AS Meaning, rls.[action] AS Action
FROM   (VALUES
        (N'NOTHING',             N'Log truncation is proceeding normally', N'No action'),
        (N'LOG_BACKUP',          N'There is no log backup since the last full backup', N'Take a log backup'),
        (N'NO_CHECKPOINT',       N'There has been no checkpoint since the last truncation', N'Run CHECKPOINT, then a log backup in FULL recovery'),
        (N'ACTIVE_TRANSACTION',  N'An open transaction holds the log truncation point', N'Find and end the transaction using the query below'),
        (N'BACKUP',              N'A backup is in progress', N'Wait; a stuck backup is a separate incident'),
        (N'BACKUP_COPY',         N'A copy-only backup is in progress', N'Wait for it to complete'),
        (N'REDO',                N'The database is being recovered, so no log backup can start', N'Wait for recovery to finish'),
        (N'REDO_LAG',            N'The last log backup was not restored, so the secondary is behind', N'Restore the log on the secondary or reinitialise it'),
        (N'ACTIVE_BACKUP_DEVICE',N'The log is in use by a device-level backup', N'Complete or cancel the device backup'),
        (N'UNKNOWN',             N'Internal error', N'Capture the full instance state before restarting')
       ) AS rls([wait_desc], [meaning], [action])
ORDER BY rls.wait_desc;
GO

The log_reuse column of sys.dm_db_lo is the numeric form of log_reuse and maps to the actions listed above. The most common non-trivial value is ACTIVE_TR: a long-running transaction, most often a report that forgot to commit, a table that was not properly truncated, or a developer session that opened a transaction and went to lunch, holds the log truncation point and prevents every subsequent log backup from reclaiming anything.

-- The long transaction, against a named database, with the batch that opened
-- it. Nothing here is exotic; this is the first query to run whenever
-- log_reuse_wait_desc is anything other than NOTHING.
SELECT  at.transaction_id,
        s.session_id,
        s.login_name,
        s.host_name,
        s.program_name,
        s.transaction_begin_time,
        DATEDIFF(minute, s.transaction_begin_time, SYSDATETIME()) AS AgeMinutes,
        st.text                                             AS BatchText
FROM    sys.dm_tran_active_transactions      AS at
JOIN    sys.dm_tran_session_transactions     AS st ON st.transaction_id = at.transaction_id
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_sessions                 AS s  ON s.session_id       = st.session_id
CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(st.sql_handle) AS tx
ORDER BY s.transaction_begin_time;
GO

-- The same, restricted to one database. An open transaction is invisible in
-- sys.dm_exec_sessions unless open_transaction_count is selected, which is
-- why the first three columns below are the ones to look at and why the sort
-- is by age rather than by session id.
DECLARE @DatabaseName sysname = N'SalesDB';

SELECT  s.session_id,
        s.login_name,
        s.host_name,
        s.program_name,
        s.open_transaction_count,
        s.transaction_isolation_level,
        s.status,
        at.transaction_begin_time,
        DATEDIFF(minute, at.transaction_begin_time, SYSDATETIME()) AS AgeMinutes,
        txt.text                                             AS BatchText
FROM    sys.dm_exec_sessions AS s
JOIN    sys.dm_tran_session_transactions AS st ON st.session_id  = s.session_id
JOIN    sys.dm_tran_active_transactions  AS at ON at.transaction_id = st.transaction_id
JOIN    sys.dm_exec_sql_text(st.sql_handle) AS txt
WHERE   s.is_user_process = 1
  AND   EXISTS (SELECT 1
                FROM   sys.dm_tran_database_transaction_objects(DB_ID(@DatabaseName)) AS dto
                WHERE  dto.transaction_id = at.transaction_id)
ORDER BY at.transaction_begin_time;
GO

-- Switching the model. Both statements are logged to the database as
-- themselves, and the switch to FULL must be followed by a log backup before
-- truncation can resume, which surprises anyone doing it for the first time.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY FULL;
GO
CHECKPOINT;
GO
BACKUP LOG CURRENT TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_after_switch.trn'
    WITH INIT, CHECKSUM;
GO

-- Back to SIMPLE, for a database that genuinely does not need point-in-time.
-- Note the log backup: a database switched to SIMPLE with an unbacked-up log
-- cannot be detached cleanly, and the file cannot be moved until the log has
-- been backed up once.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY SIMPLE;
GO
CHECKPOINT;
GO

-- The consequence, demonstrated rather than described. In SIMPLE a
-- checkpoint makes the log reusable; in FULL the same checkpoint makes no
-- difference at all until a log backup has run.
DECLARE @Before bigint = (SELECT SUM(CAST(size AS bigint)) FROM sys.dm_db_log_space_usage);

CHECKPOINT;

WAITFOR DELAY '00:00:05';

SELECT  RecoveryModel  = DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Recovery'),
        LogReuseWait   = (SELECT log_reuse_wait_desc FROM sys.databases WHERE database_id = DB_ID()),
        LogMBBefore    = @Before / 1048576,
        LogMBAfter     = (SELECT SUM(CAST(size AS bigint)) FROM sys.dm_db_log_space_usage) / 1048576;
GO

-- Shrinking, which is the operation that is almost never the answer.
-- DBCC SHRINKFILE returns unused space at the END of the file to the file
-- system and leaves the internal fragmentation alone, so the file grows again
-- immediately and the growth is a pause every time it happens.
DBCC SHRINKFILE (N'SalesDB_data', 512MB);
GO

-- The log cannot be shrunk at all in FULL recovery. The statement succeeds
-- and reports no reduction, because there is nothing after the active portion
-- of the log to give back.
DBCC SHRINKFILE (N'SalesDB_log', 64MB);
GO

-- In SIMPLE, DBCC SHRINKLOG truncates the active log to the last checkpoint.
-- The only reason to do this is before detaching a database or moving its
-- files, and it is a single-use operation rather than a maintenance one.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY SIMPLE;
GO
DBCC SHRINKLOG;
GO

-- The size breakdown, which is the question a capacity review actually asks.
SELECT  name,
        CAST(size * 8.0 / 1024 AS decimal(12,1)) AS SizeMB,
        CASE WHEN is_percent_growth = 1
             THEN CAST(growth AS varchar(20)) + N'%'
             ELSE CAST(CAST(growth * 8.0 / 1024 AS int) AS varchar(20)) + N' MB'
        END                                     AS GrowthIncrement,
        CASE WHEN max_size = -1 THEN N'Unlimited'
             ELSE CAST(CAST(max_size * 8.0 / 1024 AS int) AS varchar(20)) + N' MB'
        END                                     AS MaxSize
FROM    sys.database_files
ORDER BY size DESC;
GO

Caveats. Setting a database to SIM to make a log-growth problem go away is the most expensive shortcut in this chapter. It does not reduce the amount of log the workload generates; it changes when the log is allowed to be reused, and in exchange it removes the ability to restore to a point in time. The SIM database is restored to the point at which its last backup finished and no later, and the data written since then is gone whatever happens to the log. If the log is growing because a transaction is open, the fix is to find and end the transaction. If the log is growing because the workload writes a great deal, the fix is a log backup schedule, and in FU recovery that schedule is what keeps the log bounded.

-- Log file configuration, which is the thing that determines whether a
-- workload fits. A database that autogrows in small increments does so at
-- the cost of a pause and a filesystem extension, and the log needs headroom
-- for the largest single transaction the application can start.
SELECT  name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        log_reuse_wait_desc,
        CAST(SUM(CASE WHEN type_desc = N'LOG' THEN size END) * 8.0 / 1024 AS decimal(12,1)) AS LogSizeMB
FROM    sys.database_files
CROSS APPLY sys.databases
WHERE   dbid = DB_ID()
  AND   type_desc = N'LOG'
GROUP BY name, recovery_model_desc, log_reuse_wait_desc;
GO

-- Sizing the log for a workload. The rule is: initial size large enough that
-- it never autogrows during normal operation, and growth in large increments
-- so that it never autogrows at all. Autogrowth is a failure of
-- configuration, not a safety net.
DECLARE @LogSizeMB int = 8192;   -- measured from the largest transaction or
                                 -- checkpoint interval, whichever is larger

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT MODIFY FILE
(
    NAME = N'SalesDB_log',
    SIZE  = @LogSizeMB * 128KB
);
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT MODIFY FILE
(
