    NAME = N'SalesDB_log',
    MAXSIZE = 65536MB,
    GROWTH = 512MB
);
GO

-- The log growth events, which are an operational alert rather than a
-- curiosity. This is the log_growth event in the default trace, and the
-- Extended Events equivalent is sqlserver.file_growth.
SELECT  StartTime,
        EventClass,
        Duration,
        TextData          AS FileName,
        IntegerData       AS SizeMB
FROM    fn_trace_gettable(N'D:\Trace\default.trc', DEFAULT)
WHERE   EventClass = 94           -- FileGrowth
ORDER BY StartTime DESC;
GO

-- A log that has filled and recovered, which is the evidence of a near-miss.
-- Auto-growth events and the 9002 / 9003 errors in the log tell the story.
EXEC xp_readerrorlog 0, 1, N'The log or the differential backup file has been truncated';
GO
-- Accelerated Database Recovery, which changes the log-truncation behaviour
-- described above without changing the recovery model. It is the answer to a
-- database whose log grows without bound in SIMPLE recovery, and the answer to
-- a recovery time measured in hours.
SELECT  name,
        is_accelerated_database_recovery_on,
        is_earlier_database_rollback_on,
        is_delayed_durability_on,
        log_reuse_wait_desc
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   database_id > 4
ORDER BY name;
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET ACCELERATED_DATABASE_RECOVERY = ON;
GO

-- The version store, which is where a persistent version store lives, and the
-- reason ADR needs headroom in the log: the store is part of the log, and the
-- log is what has to be big enough to hold it.
SELECT  DatabaseName  = DB_NAME(vs.database_id),
        CurrentVersionStoreSizeKB,
        MinVersionStoreSizeKB,
        MaxVersionStoreSizeKB,
        VersionStoreCleanupState
FROM    (SELECT DB_NAME(database_id) AS DatabaseName,
                SUM(current_version_store_size_kb) AS CurrentVersionStoreSizeKB,
                MIN(min_version_store_size_kb)     AS MinVersionStoreSizeKB,
                MAX(max_version_store_size_kb)     AS MaxVersionStoreSizeKB,
                MAX(version_store_cleanup_state)   AS VersionStoreCleanupState
         FROM   sys.dm_db_version_store_db_info
         GROUP BY database_id) AS vs
ORDER BY vs.CurrentVersionStoreSizeKB DESC;
GO

-- The transaction-log backup history against the recovery model, which is the
-- evidence that the schedule is doing what the RPO statement says it is.
SELECT  bs.database_name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        COUNT_BIG(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2 THEN 1 END)                        AS LogBackups,
        COUNT_BIG(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0 THEN 1 END) AS FullBackups,
        MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2 THEN bs.backup_finish_date END)          AS LastLogBackup,
        CAST(AVG(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2
                      THEN DATEDIFF(second, bs.backup_start_date, bs.backup_finish_date)
                 END) AS decimal(12,1))                                    AS AvgLogSeconds
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
JOIN    sys.databases      AS d ON d.name = bs.database_name
WHERE   bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -1, GETDATE())
GROUP BY bs.database_name, recovery_model_desc
ORDER BY bs.database_name;
GO
-- Accelerated Database Recovery in a database that is in FULL recovery and
-- log shipping: ADR is compatible with both, and the combination is the
-- normal answer on a busy OLTP database. The version store is cleaned up
-- aggressively, so a long reader is the thing to alert on.
SELECT  r.session_id,
        r.status,
        r.command,
        r.wait_type,
        at.transaction_begin_time,
        DATEDIFF(minute, at.transaction_begin_time, SYSDATETIME()) AS AgeMinutes
FROM    sys.dm_exec_requests              AS r
JOIN    sys.dm_tran_active_transactions   AS at ON at.transaction_id =
              (SELECT MIN(transaction_id) FROM sys.dm_tran_session_transactions
               WHERE session_id = r.session_id)
WHERE   r.session_id <> @@SPID
  AND   r.open_transaction_count > 0
ORDER BY at.transaction_begin_time;
GO

-- The one transition that is not reversible by a statement: going from FULL
-- to SIMPLE while a backup is running is refused, and the error is 3902. The
-- order of operations is: take a log backup, stop the log shipping or
-- availability group, then change the model.
SELECT  name, recovery_model_desc, is_accelerated_database_recovery_on, log_reuse_wait_desc
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   database_id = DB_ID();
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET RECOVERY SIMPLE WITH NO_WAIT;
GO

12.3 BACKUP DATABASE, BACKUP LOG and verification

The BAC statement has two forms and a shared option set. The forms differ in what they read: BACKUP DATABASEBACKUP DATABASE reads data pages and the log, BACKUP LOGBACKUP LOG reads only the log. The option set is the same, and the options that matter in production are IN, CHEC, ST, COMPR, MAXTRAN, INIT, MIR and NOWAITNOWAIT.

OptionEffectUse it whenFailure mode if omitted
INOverwrites the backup medium, or writes a new backup set to the beginning of the file when a file is used with SKIPAlways, unless deliberately appending to a fileWithout it, writing to an existing file appends a new backup set, and restoring from the file may pick the wrong one. The file grows without bound
SKIPSkips the existing backup sets in the file and writes at the first free space, leaving them intactWhen a file holds several backup sets and a new one must be added without disturbing themUsed with IN it overwrites the existing sets; used with SKIP without IN it writes past them
WITH FORMATWITH FORMATEquivalent to IN with SKIP: writes a new header and overwrites the media, discarding prior setsExplicitly reformatting a tape or a file that is being recycledAmbiguity at restore time about which set a file holds
CHECWrites a checksum per page during the backup; the checksum is verified automatically on restoreAlwaysA corrupted backup is discovered only when it is needed, which is the worst time
STATS = nSTATS = nReports progress every nn percentAlways, for any backup that takes more than a few secondsA backup that appears to have hung and gets killed by an operator
COMPRESSION = ONCOMPRESSION = ONCompresses the backup. Default depends on edition and on the backup compression defaultbackup compression default configurationAlways for a full backup; almost always for a log backup, which compresses extremely wellSeveral times the storage and several times the elapsed time, for no benefit
MAXTRANSFERSIZE = nMAXTRANSFERSIZE = nBuffer size for the backup, in bytes; larger values can improve throughput on fast mediaWhen tuning a backup job; 4 MB to 64 MB is a reasonable rangeDefault is 1 MB, which is slow on high-bandwidth storage
NOWFails immediately if the backup cannot obtain the required database locks rather than waitingIn a backup schedule, to fail fast and retry rather than queue behind a long transactionThe backup waits indefinitely behind a blocking session and the schedule silently slips
MIRROR TOMIRROR TOWrites a second copy to another device. The MIR clause of ALTER DATABASEALTER DATABASE, a different feature entirely, is a deprecated mirroring partnerRarely. A physically separate copy managed by different tooling is more robust than MIRROR TOMIRROR TONot applicable; a failure on the second device fails the whole backup
-- A full backup, written the way a production job should write it.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak'
WITH
(
    INIT,
    CHECKSUM,
    STATS            = 10,
    COMPRESSION      = ON,
    MAXTRANSFERSIZE  = 4194304,   -- 4 MB
    DESCRIPTION      = N'Weekly full, SalesDB, 2026-09-28',
    EXPIREDATE       = '2027-09-28',
    RETAINDAYS       = 365,
    NAME             = N'SalesDB-Full-20260928',
    EXECUTE AS       'backup_operator'
);
GO

-- A differential. The chain base is the last full, so the file name must say
-- which full it belongs to or a restore will pick the wrong one.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_diff_20260928_01.bak'
WITH DIFFERENTIAL, INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON,
     NAME = N'SalesDB-Diff-20260928-01';
GO

-- A log backup. NOCHECKPOINT is the option that preserves the point-in-time
-- capability: it forces a checkpoint and then does not write the checkpoint
-- record, so the log backup contains everything back to the previous one. If
-- this option is omitted, a log backup truncates the portion of the log it
-- covers, and a restore cannot rewind to before that backup.
BACKUP LOG [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn'
WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON,
     NOCHECKPOINT,
     NAME = N'SalesDB-Log-20260928-0130';
GO

The difference between BACKUP DATABASEBACKUP DATABASE and BACKUP LOGBACKUP LOG is not only in what is read. A full or differential backup performs a checkpoint by default, which in FU recovery records the LSN up to which the log may be reused. A log backup also checkpoints by default, which is the mechanism that lets the log be truncated after the backup completes. NOCHEC removes that behaviour, which is what allows a log backup chain to contain a consistent, gapless history for a point-in-time restore.

-- COPY_ONLY. A copy-only backup is a physical image of the database for a
-- purpose other than participating in a restore chain, and it is the correct
-- way to take a backup that must not disturb the differential base. It is
-- also the correct way to take a backup before a risky change such as a
-- service pack, a feature enablement or a bulk data load: the chain is
-- untouched and a rollback restores from the ordinary backups.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_prechange_copyonly.bak'
WITH COPY_ONLY, INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON;
GO

-- A backup to a URL, which is Azure Blob storage or a page blob on a storage
-- account. The syntax differs from a file only in the target and in the
-- credential, which must be a SAS token or a storage access key.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO URL = N'https://acct.blob.core.windows.net/backups/SalesDB_full.bak'
WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON,
     CREDENTIAL = N'https://acct.blob.core.windows.net/backups/sas_container';
GO

-- Verification. RESTORE VERIFYONLY reads every block of the backup and checks
-- its structure and, where CHECKSUM was used, its per-page checksums. It does
-- not restore the data anywhere. This is the check that must be in the backup
-- job, because a backup that has never been verified is a hypothesis.
RESTORE VERIFYONLY FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak'
WITH CHECKSUM;
GO

RESTORE VERIFYONLY FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn'
WITH CHECKSUM;
GO

RESTORE VERIFYONLYRESTORE VERIFYONLY is not a substitute for a restore, and the distinction matters at the worst possible moment. Verification confirms that the file is readable, that its structure is intact and that the checksums match. It does not confirm that the database in the backup is logically correct, that the log chain is contiguous from the full backup, or that the files can be moved to the paths a restore would use. The only test that answers all three is a periodic restore to a scratch database, and it should be scheduled as automatically as the backup itself, with the result reported rather than logged and forgotten.

-- The backup job. Complete, runnable, and written so that a failure is a
-- failure rather than a message in a job history nobody reads.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_BackupDatabase
    @DatabaseName     sysname,
    @BackupType       char(1)      = N'F',      -- F full, D differential, L log
    @BackupDirectory  nvarchar(400) = N'D:\Backup',
    @RetentionDays    int          = 14
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;

    DECLARE @Stamp      nvarchar(20)  = CONVERT(nvarchar(20), SYSDATETIME(), 112),
            @FileName   nvarchar(260),
            @Ext        nvarchar(6),
            @Sql        nvarchar(max);

    SET @Ext = CASE @BackupType
                   WHEN N'F' THEN N'_full_'
                   WHEN N'D' THEN N'_diff_'
                   WHEN N'L' THEN N'_log_'
                   ELSE NULL
               END;

    IF @Ext IS NULL
    BEGIN
        RAISERROR (N'@BackupType must be F, D or L.', 16, 1);
        RETURN 1;
    END

    SET @FileName = @BackupDirectory + N'\' + @DatabaseName + @Ext + @Stamp
                    + CASE @BackupType WHEN N'L' THEN N'.trn' ELSE N'.bak' END;

    SET @Sql =
        N'BACKUP DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@DatabaseName) +
        N' TO DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(@FileName) +
        CASE @BackupType
             WHEN N'F' THEN N''
             WHEN N'D' THEN N' WITH DIFFERENTIAL,'
             WHEN N'L' THEN CASE WHEN DATABASEPROPERTYEX(@DatabaseName, 'Recovery')
                                       COLLATE DATABASE_DEFAULT = 'FULL'
                                  THEN N'' ELSE N' WITH NOCHECKPOINT,' END
        END
        + N' WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON, MAXTRANSFERSIZE = 4194304;'
        + N' RESTORE VERIFYONLY FROM DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(@FileName) + N' WITH CHECKSUM;';

    -- A log backup of a SIMPLE database is meaningless: there is no log chain
    -- to extend, and taking one resets the differential base. Refuse rather
    -- than produce a file that looks like a backup and is not one.
    IF @BackupType = N'L'
       AND DATABASEPROPERTYEX(@DatabaseName, 'Recovery') COLLATE DATABASE_DEFAULT = N'SIMPLE'
    BEGIN
        RAISERROR (N'Cannot take a log backup of a database in SIMPLE recovery.', 16, 1);
        RETURN 1;
    END

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;

    -- Record it in msdb by hand is unnecessary: BACKUP already does. Report
    -- what it wrote, so the job's output is a fact rather than a status.
    SELECT  DatabaseName  = @DatabaseName,
            BackupType    = @BackupType,
            FileName      = @FileName,
            VerifiedAt    = SYSUTCDATETIME();

    RETURN 0;
END;
GO

EXEC dbo.usp_BackupDatabase @DatabaseName = N'SalesDB', @BackupType = N'F';
GO
-- The backup file inventory: what is in each file, and whether it verifies.
-- This is the query to run before an incident, because it is the difference
-- between knowing a backup exists and knowing it is restorable.
SELECT  bmf.physical_device_name                                AS BackupFile,
        bs.database_name,
        bs.type                                                AS TypeCode,  -- 1 full or diff, 2 log
        CASE WHEN bs.is_copy_only = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END        AS IsCopyOnly,
        CASE WHEN bs.is_differential = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END     AS IsDifferential,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                                          AS SetsInFile,
        MAX(bs.backup_finish_date)                             AS LatestBackup,
        CAST(SUM(CAST(bmf.compressed_backup_size AS bigint)) / 1048576.0 AS decimal(12,1)) AS FileMB
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf
JOIN    msdb.dbo.backupset         AS bs ON bs.media_set_id = bmf.media_set_id
GROUP BY bmf.physical_device_name, bs.database_name, bs.type,
         CASE WHEN bs.is_copy_only = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END,
         CASE WHEN bs.is_differential = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END
ORDER BY LatestBackup DESC;
GO

-- The verification sweep. A backup that was never verified is a hypothesis,
-- and this is the cheapest way to convert the whole set into evidence.
-- The cursor exists because RESTORE VERIFYONLY is a statement, not a set
-- operation, and dynamic SQL with an identifier needs a cursor to iterate.
DECLARE @BackupSetId int;

DECLARE backup_cursor CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD FOR
    SELECT DISTINCT bs.media_set_id
    FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
    JOIN   msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf ON bmf.media_set_id = bs.media_set_id
    WHERE  bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
      AND  bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -7, GETDATE());

DECLARE @File nvarchar(260), @Sql nvarchar(max), @Failures int = 0;

OPEN backup_cursor;
FETCH NEXT FROM backup_cursor INTO @BackupSetId;

WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
BEGIN
    SELECT @File = bmf.physical_device_name
    FROM   msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf
    WHERE  bmf.media_set_id = @BackupSetId;

    SET @Sql = N'RESTORE VERIFYONLY FROM DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(@File) + N' WITH CHECKSUM;';

    BEGIN TRY
        EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
        PRINT N'VErified: ' + @File;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        SET @Failures += 1;
        PRINT N'FAILED:   ' + @File + N' -- ' + ERROR_MESSAGE();
    END CATCH;

    FETCH NEXT FROM backup_cursor INTO @BackupSetId;
END

CLOSE backup_cursor;
DEALLOCATE backup_cursor;

SELECT BackupsChecked = COUNT_BIG(*), Failures = @Failures
FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset
WHERE  database_name = N'SalesDB'
  AND  backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -7, GETDATE());
GO
-- A backup to a URL with a shared access signature, and the credential object
-- the signature needs. Storing the SAS in the catalog is a decision with a
-- cost: it is readable by sysadmin and it expires, so it has to be in the
-- rotation.
CREATE CREDENTIAL [https://acct.blob.core.windows.net/backups/sas_container]
WITH IDENTITY = N'SAS Token',
     SECRET   = N'?sv=<version>&ss=b&srt=sco&sp=rwdlacupitfx&se=<expiry>&sr=c&sig=<signature>';
GO

-- Reading a credential's metadata without exposing the secret. The secret
-- column exists and is readable by sysadmin; there is no permission that
-- hides it from a sysadmin, which is why a credential is not a security
-- boundary.
SELECT  name, credential_id, credential_type_desc, create_date
FROM    sys.credentials
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- Azure blob or a file share, both of which are writable with BACKUP TO URL.
-- A file share is simpler to reason about and has no per-request cost; blob
-- storage is cheaper at rest and is the usual target for retention.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO URL = N'https://acct.blob.core.windows.net/backups/SalesDB_diff.bak',
       URL = N'https://acct.blob.core.windows.net/backups/SalesDB_diff_copy.bak'
WITH DIFFERENTIAL, INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON;
GO

-- Backing up several databases in one statement. This is the form to use for
-- a very large estate, because it is one connection and one transaction log
-- for the operation rather than N.
BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB], [CustomerDB], [ProductDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\All_20260928.bak'
WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;
GO

-- Backing up every user database, generated rather than hand-maintained, with
-- the same options on all of them. Hand-maintained backup jobs drift, and a
-- new database that was never added to the job is the reason a recovery test
-- failed.
DECLARE @name sysname, @sql nvarchar(max) = N'';

SELECT @sql = STRING_AGG(
             N'BACKUP DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(name) +
             N' TO DISK = N''D:\Backup\' + name + N'_full.bak''
               WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10, COMPRESSION = ON, MAXTRANSFERSIZE = 4194304;',
             N' ' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10))
             FROM   sys.databases
             WHERE  database_id > 4
               AND  state_desc = N'ONLINE'
               AND  is_read_only = 0
               AND  is_distributor = 0;

EXEC sys.sp_executesql @sql;
GO

Requirements and caveats for the backup job. The job runs as a login that is a member of sysa or of the db_dat and db_o roles on the database, or that holds the BACKUP DATABASEBACKUP DATABASE and BACKUP LOGBACKUP LOG permissions explicitly. It writes to a path the SQL Server service account can write to, on storage that is not the same storage as the data files. It checks that there is space before it starts, because a full backup that fills a volume takes the instance down with it when the log cannot grow. And it verifies what it wrote, because the cost of verification is a fraction of the cost of the backup and the value of it is the whole point.

12.4 Restore sequences, WITH NORECOVERY and point-in-time recovery

A restore replays backup sets in a fixed order: the most recent full, then at most one differential taken after that full, then every log backup in sequence. Every set except the last is applied WITH NORECOVERYWITH NORECOVERY, which leaves the database in REST state and permits more sets to be applied; the last is applied with recovery, which is what makes the database available and what replays the rolled-back transactions.

-- Before restoring anything, read the backup. RESTORE HEADERONLY reads only
-- the header of a backup set; RESTORE FILELISTONLY reads the file list. Both
-- are non-destructive and both are the step that determines whether the
-- restore below will work.
RESTORE HEADERONLY FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak';
GO

RESTORE FILELISTONLY FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak';
GO

-- The full restore. MOVE is required whenever the target instance does not
-- have the original file paths, which is almost always: the path recorded in
-- the backup is the path on the machine where the backup was taken.
-- REPLACE is required when a database of that name already exists.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_restored]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_diff_20260928_01.bak',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0145.trn'
WITH
    MOVE N'SalesDB'      TO N'D:\Restore\SalesDB.mdf',
    MOVE N'SalesDB_log'  TO N'D:\Restore\SalesDB_log.ldf',
    REPLACE,
    RECOVERY,
    STATS = 10,
    CHECKSUM;
GO

The list of DI clauses is read left to right and applied in that order, so the order of the clauses is the restore sequence. A differential cannot be applied before its full, and a log backup whose LSN does not follow the previous set produces error 4305, 4309 or 4310 rather than a partial restore. STATS = 10STATS = 10 on a restore is not a nicety: a restore of a large database reports progress percentages that correspond to nothing a human can influence, but they are the only evidence that it is still running.

-- The chained form, which is what a point-in-time restore always looks like.
-- The full and the differential are restored NORECOVERY so the database stays
-- in RESTORING and the log backups can be applied to it; the final log backup
-- is applied with RECOVERY, which replays the incomplete transactions and
-- brings the database online.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak'
WITH MOVE N'SalesDB'     TO N'D:\Restore\PITR.mdf',
     MOVE N'SalesDB_log' TO N'D:\Restore\PITR_log.ldf',
     NORECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_diff_20260928_01.bak'
WITH NORECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn'
WITH NORECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0145.trn'
WITH NORECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

-- The final step. STANDBY instead of RECOVERY leaves the database readable
-- and undoable: every reader gets a consistent view, and each reader's
-- uncommitted work is rolled back when it disconnects, so the database can
-- then be rolled forward by another RESTORE LOG with STANDBY.
--
-- A database in STANDBY is not available for writes, cannot be modified, and
-- holds a shared lock on the log chain. It is the correct state for a
-- forensic examination or a read-only report, and the wrong state for
-- anything that needs to be modified.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0145.trn'
WITH STANDBY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

-- Roll it forward to the next log backup, still in STANDBY.
RESTORE LOG [SalesDB_pitr]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0200.trn'
WITH STANDBY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

-- Commit the standby state when the examination is finished, which makes the
-- database available and is irreversible.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_pitr] WITH RECOVERY;
GO
-- Point-in-time restore with STOPAT. The timestamp is interpreted in the
-- server's local time, not UTC, which is the detail that produces an
-- off-by-one-day restore. The target must fall strictly inside the LSN range
-- of the final log backup; a target after the end of that backup is error
-- 4335, and a target before the start of the chain is error 4305.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_asof]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260928.bak',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0145.trn',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0200.trn'
WITH
    MOVE N'SalesDB'     TO N'D:\Restore\ASOF.mdf',
    MOVE N'SalesDB_log' TO N'D:\Restore\ASOF_log.ldf',
    REPLACE,
    NORECOVERY,
    STATS = 10,
    CHECKSUM;
GO

-- Confirm where the chain currently stands before choosing the next file.
SELECT  TOP (20)
    bs.backup_finish_date,
    bs.first_lsn,
    bs.last_lsn,
    bs.first_recovery_fork_guid,
    DATEDIFF(second, bs.backup_start_date, bs.backup_finish_date) AS Seconds
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE   bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
  AND   bs.type = 2
ORDER BY bs.backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

RESTORE LOG [SalesDB_asof]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0200.trn'
WITH STOPAT = '2026-09-28 14:32:17', RECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

-- To stop immediately before a named transaction, which is more precise than a
-- timestamp because the transaction name is recorded in the log. A batch that
-- wrote millions of rows in one transaction is either wholly present or
-- wholly absent; there is no such thing as half a transaction at a point in
-- time.
RESTORE LOG [SalesDB_asof]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0215.trn'
WITH STOPATMARK = N'NightlyPriceUpdate', RECOVERY, STATS = 10, CHECKSUM;
GO

Caveats. Three failure modes account for nearly all unsuccessful restores. Path mismatch: the restore fails with error 3201 if MO is omitted and the original paths do not exist on the target instance, which is the normal case for a restore to a different machine. Broken log chain: error 4305 or 4309 if a log backup is missing, out of order, or belongs to a different backup set GUID, and the only remedy is to start again from a point at which the chain is contiguous. File already exists: error 1839 or 3701 if a file of that name is present and REP was not specified. Beyond these, note that a restore with STA cannot be the destination of a differential backup or of a log backup, so the standby chain must be committed before the database can be used as a source again.

-- Reading a restored database. Every one of these is a check that the restore
-- produced what it was supposed to produce, rather than a database that
-- merely came online.
SELECT  name, state_desc, recovery_model_desc, log_reuse_wait_desc
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   name = N'SalesDB_pitr';
GO

-- MS_foregenumber and the differential base confirm the chain was applied in
-- the right order. fg_start_lsn advancing means the differential was applied.
SELECT  differential_base_lsn        AS DiffBaseLsn,
        differential_base_guid       AS DiffBaseGuid,
        differential_base_file_name  AS DiffBaseName
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset
WHERE   database_name = N'SalesDB_pitr'
  AND   is_differential = 0
ORDER BY backup_finish_date DESC;
GO

-- Business-level verification. A restore that succeeds and contains the wrong
-- data is a worse outcome than a failed restore, because it is believed.
SELECT  COUNT_BIG(*)      AS OrderCount,
        MIN(OrderDate)    AS EarliestOrder,
        MAX(OrderDate)    AS LatestOrder,
        SUM(TotalAmount)  AS TotalValue
FROM    dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- The same, against the source at the time of the backup, which is the only
-- comparison that proves the point in time was the one intended.
SELECT  @ExpectedCount = COUNT_BIG(*), @ExpectedTotal = SUM(TotalAmount)
FROM    SalesDB.dbo.SalesOrder;
GO
-- The restore script, generated from the backup history rather than written by
-- hand. This is the object that makes a point-in-time restore repeatable, and
-- it is the object to keep in source control next to the schema.
DECLARE @TargetName     sysname = N'SalesDB_restore',
        @TargetMdf      nvarchar(260) = N'D:\Restore\SalesDB_restore.mdf',
        @TargetLdf      nvarchar(260) = N'D:\Restore\SalesDB_restore_log.ldf',
        @StopAt         datetime = '2026-09-28 14:32:17';

SELECT N'RESTORE DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@TargetName) + N' FROM' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + STUFF((SELECT N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10) + N'     DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(bmf.physical_device_name)
              FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset         AS bs
              JOIN   msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf ON bmf.media_set_id = bs.media_set_id
              WHERE  bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
                AND  bs.backup_finish_date <= @StopAt
                AND  bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0
                AND  bs.is_copy_only = 0
              ORDER  BY bs.backup_finish_date DESC
              OFFSET 0 ROWS FETCH NEXT 1 ROW ONLY
              FOR XML PATH('')), 1, 1, N'')
     + N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + STUFF((SELECT N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10) + N'     DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(bmf.physical_device_name)
              FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset         AS bs
              JOIN   msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf ON bmf.media_set_id = bs.media_set_id
              WHERE  bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
                AND  bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 1
                AND  bs.backup_finish_date = (SELECT MAX(backup_finish_date)
                                              FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset
                                              WHERE  database_name = N'SalesDB'
                                                 AND  type = 1 AND is_differential = 1
                                                 AND  is_copy_only = 0)
                AND  bs.is_copy_only = 0
              FOR XML PATH('')), 1, 1, N'')
     + N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + STUFF((SELECT N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10) + N'     DISK = ' + QUOTENAME(bmf.physical_device_name)
              FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset         AS bs
              JOIN   msdb.dbo.backupmediafamily AS bmf ON bmf.media_set_id = bs.media_set_id
              WHERE  bs.database_name = N'SalesDB'
                AND  bs.type = 2
                AND  bs.is_copy_only = 0
                AND  bs.backup_finish_date <= @StopAt
              ORDER  BY bs.first_lsn
              FOR XML PATH('')), 1, 1, N'')
     + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + N'WITH MOVE ' + QUOTENAME(N'SalesDB')     + N' TO ' + QUOTENAME(@TargetMdf) + N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + N'     MOVE ' + QUOTENAME(N'SalesDB_log') + N' TO ' + QUOTENAME(@TargetLdf) + N',' + CHAR(13) + CHAR(10)
     + N'     REPLACE, NORECOVERY, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;'
     AS GeneratedRestoreScript,
       @StopAt AS StopAt;
GO

-- The three things that make a generated restore script worth generating:
-- the differential base is the most recent differential AFTER the full, the
-- log chain is ordered by first_lsn and not by finish date, and copy-only
-- backups are excluded from both because they are not part of a chain.
GO

12.5 Log shipping, mirroring and Always On availability groups

Three technologies move or duplicate a database. They are not versions of one another and they solve different problems. Log shipping copies the transaction log to a secondary at intervals, is asynchronous, has a measurable lag, and has no automatic failover. Database mirroring duplicates a database to a partner synchronously or asynchronously, has automatic failover, and has been deprecated since SQL Server 2012. Always On availability groups duplicate to one or more secondaries with automatic failover, a readable secondary, and read-scale routing; it is the current answer.

Log shippingDatabase mirroring (deprecated)Always On availability group
Copy modelBackup and restore of the log, on a scheduleFull log shipping performed synchronouslyLog shipping performed by the engine, synchronously or asynchronously per replica
AvailabilityRead-only on the secondary until failover; primary is not failed over automaticallyPrimary or mirror, with automatic or manual failoverOne primary, up to five secondaries; automatic failover with a synchronous commit replica
Data loss windowWhatever the log backup interval is, plus the copy time: minutes typicallyZero with a synchronous mirrorZero with a synchronous commit secondary; one transaction with asynchronous
Readable secondaryYes, with STA, which holds a shared lock on the log chainNoYes, always, without affecting the primary
Read scaleNot applicableNot applicableYes: secondaries can serve read-only queries, and all replicas can serve read-scale routing when enabled
Number of secondariesOne, with monitoring; multiple is a manual arrangementOneUp to five
Status in 2025Supported. One of the four areas with breaking changes in 2025, so validate before an upgradeDeprecated. Do not deploy on a new system; ALTER DATABASE ... SET PARTNERALTER DATABASE ... SET PARTNER is not a 2025 featureCurrent. The recommended technology, and the only one that survives a technology review in 2026
Contained databaseNot supported in a contained database on the primaryNot supportedNot supported as a contained database; the availability group owns the databases rather than the instance
-- Log shipping, configured from the primary. The three parts are the backup
-- job, the copy of the log file, and the restore of it on the secondary,
-- each on its own schedule and each monitored separately.
EXEC master.dbo.sp_add_log_shipping_primary
     @primary_server_name    = N'PRIMARY01\PRIMARY',
     @backup_directory       = N'D:\LogShip\Backup',
     @backup_share           = N'\\PRIMARY01\LogShipBackup$',
     @backup_retention_days  = 14,
     @backup_job_name        = N'LSBackup_SalesDB',
     @backup_job_owner       = N'sa',
     @backup_job_schedule    = N'Sun 23:00',
     @backup_alert_threshold = 1440;
GO

EXEC master.dbo.sp_add_log_shipping_secondary
     @secondary_server_name   = N'SECONDARY01\SECONDARY',
     @primary_server_name     = N'PRIMARY01\PRIMARY',
     @backup_directory        = N'D:\LogShip\Backup',
     @backup_share            = N'\\PRIMARY01\LogShipBackup$',
     @secondary_database_name = N'SalesDB',
     @restore_directory       = N'D:\LogShip\Restore',
     @restore_job_name        = N'LSRestore_SalesDB',
     @restore_job_owner       = N'sa',
     @restore_job_schedule    = N'Sun 23:00',
     @restore_retention_days  = 14,
     @monitor_server_name     = N'MONITOR01',
     @monitor_server_security_mode = 1,   -- Windows authentication
     @monitor_job_name        = N'LSMonitor_SalesDB',
     @monitor_job_owner       = N'sa',
     @monitor_job_schedule    = N'Sun 23:00',
     @monitor_level           = N'VERBOSE';
GO

-- The lag, which is the number that matters. It is the age of the last
-- restored log backup, and it is the data loss window in a failure.
SELECT  p.primary_server                                       AS PrimaryServer,
        s.secondary_server_name                                AS SecondaryServer,
        d.secondary_database_name                               AS SecondaryDatabase,
        d.last_copy_file_name                                  AS LastCopy,
        d.last_copy_finish_date                                AS LastCopyFinish,
        d.last_restore_file_name                               AS LastRestored,
        d.last_restore_finish_date                             AS LastRestoreFinish,
        s.restore_delay                                        AS RestoreDelayMinutes,
        DATEDIFF(minute, d.last_restore_finish_date, GETDATE()) AS MinutesBehind
FROM    msdb.dbo.log_shipping_monitor_primary   AS p
JOIN    msdb.dbo.log_shipping_monitor_secondary AS s ON s.primary_id = p.primary_id
JOIN    msdb.dbo.log_shipping_monitor_databases AS d ON d.primary_id = s.primary_id
                                                  AND d.secondary_id = s.secondary_id
ORDER BY MinutesBehind DESC;
GO

-- Enforcing the log backup job. Without this, a database taken out of FULL
-- recovery silently stops the log shipping chain without any error.
EXEC master.dbo.sp_add_log_shipping_alert_job
     @primary_server_name = N'PRIMARY01\PRIMARY',
     @backup_threshold    = 60,
     @alert_level         = 1;
GO
-- An availability group. The primary replica is the current writer; secondary
-- replicas are copies. Synchronous commit means the primary waits for a
-- synchronous secondary to acknowledge before reporting success, which gives
-- zero data loss and a transaction latency cost; asynchronous commit does not
-- wait, which gives a low latency and a data loss window of one transaction.
CREATE AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales]
FOR DATABASE [SalesDB]
WITH
(
    AUTOMATIC_FAILOVER                   = ON,
    FAILOVER_MODE                        = FULL,
    CONTAINED_DATABASE_AUTHENTICATION    = OFF,
    AVAILABILITY_MODE                    = SYNCHRONOUS_COMMIT,
    REQUIRED_SECONDARY_SYNCHRONOUS_COMPUTERS_TO_COMMIT = 1
);
GO

ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] ADD SECONDARY [SECONDARY01\SECONDARY];
GO

-- A read-only routing target. Clients connecting to the secondary's listener
-- with ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly are redirected to this replica.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales]
    WITH (READ_ONLY_ROUTING_LIST = (N'SECONDARY01\SECONDARY2'));
GO

ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] ADD SECONDARY [SECONDARY01\SECONDARY2]
    WITH (AVAILABILITY_MODE = SYNCHRONOUS_COMMIT,
           SECONDARY_ROLE = SECONDARY);
GO

-- The databases join the group from each instance.
ALTER DATABASE [SalesDB] SET AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales];
GO
ALTER DATABASE [SalesDB_SETAG] SET AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales];
GO

-- Bring the secondaries online.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] OFFLINE DROP PRIMARY;
GO

-- What the engine believes the group is doing. A group whose synchronisation
-- state is not SYNCHRONIZING is not providing the guarantee it was configured
-- for, and this is the query that says so.
SELECT  ag.name                                            AS AvailabilityGroup,
        ags.synchronization_state_desc                   AS SyncState,
        ags.synchronization_health_desc                 AS SyncHealth,
        ar.replica_server_name                           AS Replica,
        ar.availability_mode_desc                        AS CommitMode,
        ar.failover_mode_desc                            AS FailoverMode,
        ars.connected_state_desc                         AS Connected,
        ars.operational_state_desc                       AS OperationalState,
        ars.role_desc                                     AS Role
FROM    sys.availability_groups                       AS ag
JOIN    sys.availability_replicas                      AS ar  ON ar.group_id = ag.group_id
JOIN    sys.dm_hadr_availability_replica_states       AS ars ON ars.replica_id = ar.replica_id
                                                      AND ars.group_id    = ag.group_id
ORDER BY ars.role_desc, ar.replica_server_name;
GO

The listener is the connection point, not the instance name. A client that connects to a specific replica breaks when that replica is failed over, which defeats the purpose of the group; a client that connects to the listener is moved to whatever is currently primary, which is the point of the group. ApplicationIntent=ReadOnlyApplicationIntent=ReadOnly sends the read-only routing connection to a secondary, and that connection follows the primary as the roles change.

-- A read-scale replica differs from an ordinary secondary in one specific way:
-- it serves read-scale routing without being a candidate for automatic
-- failover and without the synchronous commit cost. It is intended for
-- reporting workloads that would otherwise run against the primary.
-- The instance-level setting is required on every replica.
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'hadr enable', 1;
RECONFIGURE;
GO

ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] ADD SECONDARY [SECONDARY01\SCALE];
GO

-- The read-scale routing URL is the name the reporting clients connect to.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales]
    WITH (READ_SCALE_ROUTING_URL = N'TCP://SECONDARY01\AG_Sales_ROS');
GO

ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales]
    WITH (READ_SCALE_ROUTING_LIST = (N'SECONDARY01\SCALE'));
GO

-- Monitoring the health of the whole group, which is the check to schedule.
SELECT  g.name                                       AS AvailabilityGroup,
        g.is_clustered,
        g.is_automated_failover_available,
        MAX(CASE WHEN r.availability_mode = 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS HasSynchronousCommit,
        MAX(CASE WHEN r.failover_mode = 1          THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS HasAutomaticFailover,
        MAX(CASE WHEN r.connected_state = 1        THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS ReplicasConnected,
        COUNT_BIG(*)                                AS ReplicaCount
FROM    sys.availability_groups AS g
JOIN    sys.availability_replicas AS r ON r.group_id = g.group_id
GROUP BY g.name, g.is_clustered, g.is_automated_failover_available
ORDER BY g.name;
GO

-- Suspending data movement is a legitimate operation and is frequently used
-- during a maintenance window; a replica that stays suspended is a silent
-- data loss exposure.
SELECT  g.name                        AS AvailabilityGroup,
        r.replica_server_name,
        rs.suspend_reason_desc
FROM    sys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states AS drs
JOIN    sys.availability_replicas           AS r  ON r.replica_id = drs.replica_id
JOIN    sys.availability_groups             AS g  ON g.group_id   = drs.group_id
WHERE   drs.is_suspended = 1;
GO
-- The availability group, on the second instance. Joining a secondary requires
-- the database to exist there first, in the restoring state, which is created
-- by a restore WITH NORECOVERY, and it requires the same database name on both
-- instances. The sequence is: restore NORECOVERY on the secondary, join the
-- group, and the engine brings the database online itself.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] JOIN;
GO

-- Leave the group, from the primary: the database becomes a normal database
-- again. Leave from a secondary: the replica is removed.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] REMOVE SECONDARY [SECONDARY01\SECONDARY];
GO

-- The listener. This is the connection point a client uses, and creating it
-- is what makes the group usable: a client that connects to an instance name
-- rather than to the listener is not using the group at all.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales]
    ADD LISTENER [AG_Sales_Listener] (IPADDRESS = '10.20.30.40', PORT = 1433);
GO

-- Automatic failover. With a synchronous commit secondary and
-- AUTOMATIC_FAILOVER = ON, the cluster fails the group over when the primary
-- becomes unavailable, without data loss. Without the synchronous replica the
-- setting is rejected: there is nothing to fail over to.
SELECT  ag.name,
        ag.is_automated_failover_available,
        ar.replica_server_name,
        ar.availability_mode_desc,
        ar.failover_mode_desc,
        ars.synchronization_state_desc,
        ars.synchronization_health_desc,
        ars.connected_state_desc
FROM    sys.availability_groups               AS ag
JOIN    sys.availability_replicas              AS ar  ON ar.group_id = ag.group_id
JOIN    sys.dm_hadr_availability_replica_states AS ars ON ars.replica_id = ar.replica_id
                                                     AND ars.group_id    = ag.group_id
ORDER BY ag.name, ar.replica_server_name;
GO

-- A synthetic failover, for the rehearsal. Planned failovers are safe; the
-- rehearsal is the point at which the application’s reconnect behaviour is
-- discovered to be untested, which is why it is rehearsed in advance and not
-- discovered.
-- ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP [AG_Sales] FAILOVER;
-- GO

-- After a failover, the roles have swapped. The database is still the same
-- database and the listener is still the connection point; only the instance
-- behind each role has changed.
SELECT  DB_NAME(database_id)   AS DatabaseName,
        is_read_only,
        synchronization_state_desc,
        synchronization_health_desc,
        is_joined_to_availability_group
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   is_joined_to_availability_group = 1
ORDER BY DatabaseName;
GO
-- A contained database. The point of containment is that the database can be
-- moved to a different instance, or to Azure SQL Database, without an
-- external dependency on a server-level login: the user travels with the
-- database file.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET CONTAINMENT = PARTIAL;
GO

-- Contained database authentication has to be enabled at instance level
-- first, and it is a server-wide setting that permits a database-scoped
-- credential. It is not a security improvement; it is a portability one.
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'contained database authentication', 1;
RECONFIGURE;
GO

-- The contained user, with a password rather than a Windows or Entra mapping.
CREATE USER [app_reader] WITH PASSWORD = N'<a strong generated password, held in a secret store>',
     DEFAULT_SCHEMA = [dbo];
GO

-- Roles resolve without a server principal behind them, which is what makes
-- the restore portable: after the database is restored on another instance,
-- app_reader exists and its membership is intact.
ALTER ROLE [db_datareader] ADD MEMBER [app_reader];
GO

SELECT  u.name                                AS UserName,
        u.type_desc                           AS UserType,
        u.authentication_type_desc            AS AuthenticationType,
        r.name                                AS RoleName,
        su.name                               AS ServerPrincipal
FROM    sys.database_principals AS u
LEFT JOIN sys.database_role_members AS drm ON drm.member_principal_id = u.principal_id
LEFT JOIN sys.database_principals AS r  ON r.principal_id = drm.role_principal_id
LEFT JOIN sys.server_principals  AS su ON su.sid = u.sid
WHERE   u.name = N'app_reader';
GO

-- The restrictions. Everything below is refused inside a contained database,
-- and each of them is a design decision that has to be made elsewhere.
--
--   * Three-part names and cross-database queries
--   * xp_cmdshell and any other extended stored procedure
--   * sp_configure, and therefore any instance-level setting
--   * Linked servers
--   * SQL Server Agent
--   * CLR, in a partially contained database that is not a trusted one
--   * CHANGE_TRACKING = AUTO_CLEANUP at database level is unaffected, but
--     the change tracking feature requires the setting to be on
--
-- The useful question is not "can this be a contained database" but "which
-- of the above does this database use", and the answer is a query.
SELECT  Feature = N'Cross-database references',
        InUse   = CASE WHEN EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.sql_modules
                                     WHERE definition LIKE N'%.dbo.%'
                                        OR definition LIKE N'%;%') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END
UNION ALL
SELECT N'Extended stored procedures',
       CASE WHEN EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.sql_modules
                          WHERE definition LIKE N'%xp[_]%') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END
UNION ALL
SELECT N'Dynamic SQL with an instance-level object',
       CASE WHEN EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.sql_modules
                          WHERE definition LIKE N'%msdb%') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END
ORDER BY InUse DESC, Feature;
GO

-- The state of the database, which is the one-line answer to "is this
-- contained, and how".
SELECT  name,
        containment_desc,
        collation_name,
        is_contained_database_authentication_enabled
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   database_id = DB_ID();
GO

Requirements. Always On availability groups require the Windows Failover Cluster, or a SQL Server on a Linux cluster, and the same hadr enabledhadr enabled setting on every instance. The databases must be in FU recovery. A secondary replica must be on a separate instance; two replicas on one instance provide no availability, because the instance is the single point of failure. An availability group does not replace backups: it replicates the database, and it cannot restore a database to a point in time before the group existed or before a mistake was made on the primary. Every design that uses an availability group still takes full backups on a schedule.

12.6 RPO, RTO, encryption and compression of backups

Two numbers determine the technology. The recovery point objective is how much data loss is acceptable, and it is set by the log backup interval and by the commit mode of the replicas. The recovery time objective is how long the service may be unavailable, and it is set by how fast a restore or a failover completes. Neither is a property of the technology; both are properties of the schedule and the size of the data.

Objective pairMechanismRPO achievedRTO achievedCost
24 hours / 4 hoursDaily full, hourly log backups, no failoverUp to one hourThe restore time: hours for a large database on ordinary storageLow. A nightly job and storage
1 hour / 1 hourFull plus differential plus log every 15 minutes, restored to a standby copy on a schedule15 minutesThe time to fail over to the standby copy, or to restoreModerate. A second copy of the data and a second set of jobs
15 minutes / 15 minutesAlways On with a synchronous commit secondary and automatic failoverZero: a transaction is not acknowledged until the secondary has itSeconds to tens of seconds, subject to the cluster failing over within the timeoutHigh. A second instance, cluster, storage and a licence for it
Zero / 60 secondsAlways On with two synchronous commit secondaries in different fault domainsZero, and the availability survives the loss of a nodeThe cluster failover timeHighest. Three instances, or a cloud equivalent
-- The arithmetic, made explicit. RPO is a property of the schedule: the log
-- backup interval plus the copy time, unless synchronous commit makes it zero.
-- RTO is a property of the size of the data and the speed of the storage.
SELECT
    DatabaseName          = bs.database_name,
    LogBackupIntervalMins = 15,
    SecondsPerFullRestorePerGB = 45,      -- measured, not assumed
    FullBackupGB         = 500.0,
    ExpectedRpoMins      = CASE WHEN SynchronousCommit = 1 THEN 0 ELSE 15 END,
    ExpectedRtoMins      = CAST(500.0 * 45 / 60 AS int) + 10   -- plus failover
FROM   (SELECT DISTINCT database_name FROM msdb.dbo.backupset WHERE type = 1) AS bs
CROSS JOIN (SELECT 1 AS SynchronousCommit) AS s;
GO

-- Measuring the restore rate rather than assuming it. This is the figure that
-- should come from the periodic restore test in 12.3, and it is the only
-- input to the RTO column that anybody can defend.
SELECT
    DatabaseName = bs.database_name,
    FullBackups  = COUNT_BIG(*),
    AvgDurationSeconds = CAST(AVG(DATEDIFF(second, bs.backup_start_date, bs.backup_finish_date)) AS decimal(12,1)),
    AvgSizeGB    = CAST(AVG(CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint)) / 1073741828.0 AS decimal(12,2))
FROM   msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE  bs.type = 1
  AND  bs.is_differential = 0
  AND  bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(month, -3, GETDATE())
GROUP BY bs.database_name
ORDER BY AvgSizeGB DESC;
GO

-- The schedule as a script, so that the RPO is enforced rather than intended.
-- A log backup every fifteen minutes on every database in FULL recovery, with
-- the reuse wait checked afterwards, is the smallest thing that makes an RPO
-- statement true.
DECLARE @db sysname;

DECLARE db_cursor CURSOR LOCAL FAST_FORWARD FOR
    SELECT name FROM sys.databases
    WHERE  database_id > 4
      AND  state_desc = N'ONLINE'
      AND  recovery_model_desc = N'FULL'
      AND  is_read_only = 0;

OPEN db_cursor;
FETCH NEXT FROM db_cursor INTO @db;

WHILE @@FETCH_STATUS = 0
BEGIN
    DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(max) =
        N'BACKUP LOG ' + QUOTENAME(@db) +
        N' TO DISK = N''D:\Backup\' + @db + N'_log.trn''
          WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, COMPRESSION = ON, NOCHECKPOINT, STATS = 5;';

    BEGIN TRY
        EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        -- A log backup that fails is an RPO that is no longer being met, and
        -- the failure has to reach the operator rather than the job history.
        EXEC msdb.dbo.sp_send_dbmail
             @profile_name = N'DBAAlerts',
             @recipients   = N'dba@contoso.example',
             @subject      = N'Log backup failed',
             @body         = N'Database: ' + @db + NCHAR(13) + NCHAR(10) + ERROR_MESSAGE();
    END CATCH;

    FETCH NEXT FROM db_cursor INTO @db;
END

CLOSE db_cursor;
DEALLOCATE db_cursor;
GO

-- Confirm afterwards, which is the check the runbook requires. Anything other
-- than NOTHING here means the chain is not advancing and the RPO is not met.
SELECT  name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        log_reuse_wait_desc,
        CAST(SUM(CAST(size AS bigint)) / 1048576.0 AS decimal(12,1)) AS LogMB
FROM    sys.dm_db_log_space_usage
JOIN    sys.databases ON databases.database_id = dm_db_log_space_usage.database_id
WHERE   databases.database_id > 4
  AND   databases.recovery_model_desc = N'FULL'
ORDER BY LogMB DESC;
GO
-- Encrypting a backup. SQL Server can encrypt a backup file with a
-- certificate, an asymmetric key, or a database master key protected by one of
-- those. The certificate is the practical choice because the key can then be
-- backed up and restored with it.
USE master;
GO

CREATE CERTIFICATE [Cert_BackupEncryption]
WITH SUBJECT = N'Backup encryption certificate';
GO

CREATE MASTER KEY ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = N'<a strong password held in a secret store, not in source control>';
GO

BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_encrypted.bak'
WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, COMPRESSION = ON, STATS = 10,
     ENCRYPTION BY PASSWORD = N'<the restore password, held separately from the backup>';
GO

-- Certificate-based encryption. The private key must be available on the
-- instance that restores, and the certificate must be restored from its own
-- backup first or the restore fails with error 3314.
BACKUP CERTIFICATE [Cert_BackupEncryption]
TO FILE = N'D:\Backup\Cert_BackupEncryption.cer';
GO

BACKUP DATABASE [SalesDB]
TO DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_cert_encrypted.bak'
WITH INIT, CHECKSUM, COMPRESSION = ON, STATS = 10,
     ENCRYPTION BY CERTIFICATE [Cert_BackupEncryption];
GO

-- Transparent Data Encryption encrypts the data files but not the log, and a
-- TDE-encrypted database needs a certificate for its log backup. The
-- certificate is the thing that must be backed up and stored off the
-- instance; without it the database is readable and its log backup is not.
SELECT  DB_NAME(database_id) AS DatabaseName,
        encryptor_type_desc,
        key_length,
        key_algorithm,
        encryptor_name
FROM    sys.dm_database_encryption_keys;
GO

SELECT  name, pvt_key_encryption_type_desc, thumbprint, pvt_key_valid_days
FROM    sys.certificates
WHERE   name = N'Cert_BackupEncryption';
GO

New in SQL Server 2025: ZSTD backup compression. SQL Server 2025 adds the Zstandard algorithm to the backup compression option set, alongside the existing LZ-family default. It is selected on the BAC statement with the COMPR option in the 2025 syntax, and it is not available on SQL Server 2022 or earlier, so a script that uses it must be version-gated. ZSTD is generally faster than the LZ default at comparable ratios on data that does not compress extremely well, and it is expected to be the better choice for log backups and for wide tables containing free text. It is a change to a physical attribute of the backup file, not to its logical content, so a backup written with it restores on any supported version. Confirm the ratio empirically with RESTORE HEADERONLYRESTORE HEADERONLY on a representative database before changing a production schedule, because the ratio is a property of the data and not of the algorithm.

-- Compression, on 2025 and before. The keyword differs by version; the
-- comparison is the point.
--
--   SQL Server 2022 and earlier:
--       WITH (COMPRESSION = ON)      -- the LZ-family default
--       WITH (COMPRESSION = OFF)
--
--   SQL Server 2025:
--       WITH (COMPRESSION = ZSTD)    -- the new algorithm, 2025 only
--       WITH (COMPRESSION = ON)      -- unchanged, still the LZ default
--
-- A script that must run on both has to branch, because the parser rejects an
-- unknown COMPRESSION value outright rather than ignoring it.
DECLARE @Sql nvarchar(max) =
    CASE
        WHEN CAST(SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMajorVersion') AS int) >= 17
        THEN N'BACKUP DATABASE dbo.SalesDB TO DISK = N''D:\Backup\c.bak''
               WITH INIT, COMPRESSION = ZSTD, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;'
        ELSE N'BACKUP DATABASE dbo.SalesDB TO DISK = N''D:\Backup\c.bak''
               WITH INIT, COMPRESSION = ON, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;'
    END;

EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
GO

-- The result, from the header, which reports the physical size of each file
-- in the backup. Compare this before and after to see what the algorithm did
-- on this data rather than on a benchmark.
RESTORE HEADERONLY FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\c.bak';
GO

The decision that is most often made in the wrong order is the compression one. Compression is a property of the data, not of the algorithm, and the correct procedure is to take the same backup twice with the two settings, read the physical size of each with RESTORE HEADERONLYRESTORE HEADERONLY, and read the elapsed time from backupsetbackupset. On a database of mostly fixed-width numeric columns the ratio may be under 2:1 and the CPU cost of attempting more is not repaid; on a database with a lot of nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max) or XML the ratio can exceed 10:1 and the CPU cost is repaid many times over.

-- The RPO and RTO as a scheduled check rather than a document. A recovery
-- objective that is not measured is a recovery objective that has quietly
-- stopped being true, and the measurement costs one query.
DECLARE @RpoTargetMinutes  int = 15,
        @RtoTargetMinutes  int = 60,
        @RestoreSecondsPerGB decimal(9,2) = 45.0;   -- measured by the drill

SELECT  bs.database_name,
        LastLogBackup       = MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2 THEN bs.backup_finish_date END),
        ActualRpoMinutes    = DATEDIFF(minute, MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2 THEN bs.backup_finish_date END),
                                                 GETDATE()),
        TargetRpoMinutes    = @RpoTargetMinutes,
        RpoMet              = CASE WHEN DATEDIFF(minute, MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 2
                                                                 THEN bs.backup_finish_date END),
                                                            GETDATE()) <= @RpoTargetMinutes
                                  THEN 1 ELSE 0 END,
        FullSizeGB          = CAST(MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0
                                           THEN CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint) END)
                                    / 1073741824.0 AS decimal(12,2)),
        EstimatedRtoMinutes = CAST(MAX(CASE WHEN bs.type = 1 AND bs.is_differential = 0
                                             THEN CAST(bs.backup_size AS bigint) END)
                                   / 1073741824.0 * @RestoreSecondsPerGB / 60
                                   + 10 AS int),
        TargetRtoMinutes    = @RtoTargetMinutes
FROM    msdb.dbo.backupset AS bs
WHERE   bs.backup_finish_date >= DATEADD(day, -7, GETDATE())
  AND   bs.is_copy_only = 0
GROUP BY bs.database_name
ORDER BY ActualRpoMinutes DESC;
GO

-- The technology the objective forces, stated as a decision. This is the
-- query a review actually looks at, because it converts a sentence in a design
-- document into a number.
SELECT  Requirement = N'RPO <= 15 minutes',
        SatisfiedBy = N'Log backups every 10 minutes, or an availability group with synchronous commit',
        Rpo         = N'10 minutes / zero'
UNION ALL
SELECT N'RTO <= 5 minutes', N'Availability group, automatic failover, synchronous commit',
       N'10 to 60 seconds'
UNION ALL
SELECT N'RTO <= 60 minutes', N'Full plus differential restore to a scratch instance',
       N'Minutes per GB times the database size, plus the failover'
UNION ALL
SELECT N'RPO <= 24 hours',   N'Nightly full backup with no log backup',
       N'Up to 24 hours';
GO

-- A restore drill, reduced to the form that can be scheduled. The database is
-- restored to a scratch name, verified at the business level, and dropped. The
-- scratch name matters: restoring over the production name on the production
-- instance is the failure mode this procedure exists to prevent.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_RestoreDrill
    @SourceDatabase   sysname,
    @ScratchDatabase  sysname,
    @TargetMdf        nvarchar(260),
    @TargetLdf        nvarchar(260),
    @ExpectedRows     bigint
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;

    DECLARE @Sql  nvarchar(max),
            @Rows bigint;

    IF DB_ID(@ScratchDatabase) IS NOT NULL
    BEGIN
        SET @Sql = N'ALTER DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@ScratchDatabase) + N' SET SINGLE_USER WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;';
        EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;
        RETURN 1;
    END

    SET @Sql = N'RESTORE DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@ScratchDatabase) +
               N' FROM DISK = N''D:\Backup\' + @SourceDatabase + N'_full.bak''
                 WITH MOVE N''' + @SourceDatabase + N''' TO ' + QUOTENAME(@TargetMdf) + N',
                      MOVE N''' + @SourceDatabase + N'_log'' TO ' + QUOTENAME(@TargetLdf) + N',
                      REPLACE, RECOVERY, CHECKSUM, STATS = 10;';

    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;

    SET @Sql = N'SELECT @n = COUNT_BIG(*) FROM ' + QUOTENAME(@ScratchDatabase) + N'.dbo.SalesOrder;';
    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql, N'@n bigint OUTPUT', @Rows = @Rows OUTPUT;

    SELECT  SourceDatabase    = @SourceDatabase,
            ScratchDatabase   = @ScratchDatabase,
            RestoredAt        = SYSUTCDATETIME(),
            ExpectedRows      = @ExpectedRows,
            ActualRows        = @Rows,
            DrillPassed       = CASE WHEN @Rows >= @ExpectedRows THEN 1 ELSE 0 END;

    -- The scratch database is dropped, not left behind: a drill that leaves
    -- several hundred gigabytes on the instance is a drill that stops being
    -- scheduled.
    SET @Sql = N'ALTER DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@ScratchDatabase) + N' SET SINGLE_USER WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
                 DROP DATABASE ' + QUOTENAME(@ScratchDatabase) + N';';
    EXEC sys.sp_executesql @Sql;

    RETURN 0;
END;
GO

Exercises 12

  1. Create a database, insert enough rows for the log to be measurable, and take a full, two differential and three log backups. Query msdb.dbo. for each and record ty, is_diff, firs and last_lsnlast_lsn. Confirm that the second differential contains everything the first did by comparing sizes.
  2. Restore the database from the full plus the second differential plus the three log backups. Record the error number. Then restore from the full plus the first differential, apply the second with NOREC, and record the error. Explain from the LSNs why neither works.
  3. Set a database to SIM and perform a large insert, then check sys.dm_db_lo and log_reuse_wait_desclog_reuse_wait_desc. Repeat in FU without a log backup and record the log growth. Take a log backup and confirm the value becomes NOT or LOG_BACKUPLOG_BACKUP.
  4. Open a transaction in one session, leave it open, and in a second session confirm log_reuse becomes ACTIVE_TRANSACTIONACTIVE_TRANSACTION. Run the long-transaction query from 12.2 to find it, close it, and confirm the value returns to NOTHINGNOTHING.
  5. Take a full backup with and without CHEC, and query has_backup in ms for both. Restore each with VERIFYONLY WITH CHECKSUMVERIFYONLY WITH CHECKSUM and record both results, then explain what verification does and does not establish.
  6. Perform a point-in-time restore using STO to a timestamp inside the final log backup, and record the value of MAX(OrderDate)MAX(OrderDate) in the restored database. Then repeat with STOPA against a named transaction and record the same value. Explain why the two answers differ.
  7. Restore a database to STA, query it, then apply the next log backup with STA again, and confirm the newer rows appear. Record the error produced by attempting a BACKUP DATABASEBACKUP DATABASE from a database in STA, and then commit with WITH RECOVERYWITH RECOVERY.
  8. Take a COPY full backup and confirm from ms that is_copy_only = 1is_copy_only = 1 and that it did not become the differential base. Take a normal full backup afterwards and confirm the differential base moved to the new one.
  9. Configure log shipping between two instances, deliberately delay the copy job, and record the value from the log shipping monitor query. Fail over manually and confirm the secondary is writable, then fail back.
  10. Create an availability group with one synchronous and one asynchronous secondary. Record the synchronisation state of each from sys.dm_hadr_availab, suspend the asynchronous secondary, and confirm the query in 12.5 reports a suspension reason.
  11. Take the same database backup twice, once with COMPRESSION = ONCOMPRESSION = ON and once with COMPRESSION = ZSTDCOMPRESSION = ZSTD, on a build where both are available. Record the physical size and the elapsed time of each from back, and state which to schedule and why.
  12. Encrypt a backup with a certificate, restore the certificate onto a second instance, and restore the database there. Record the error produced when the certificate is absent, and explain what has to exist on the restoring instance before the database is readable.
  13. Work out the RPO and RTO for a database of 800 GB with a daily full, hourly differential and log backups every ten minutes, on storage that restores at 60 seconds per GB. Then state what changes if the RTO must be under an hour, and which technology that forces.

13 · Platform Features and Administration

13.1 Temporal tables13.2 Change Tracking and Change Data Capture13.3 Service Broker, CLR integration and the deprecated surface13.4 JSON: FOR JSON, OPENJSON and JSON indexes13.5 String aggregation, splitting, spatial and graph13.6 Instance configuration, memory-optimised tables and the runbook

The remaining platform features are grouped here by what they are for, and each is stated with the limitation that decides whether to use it. Several are excellent; several are deprecated; and the most common error in this area is adopting a feature for a problem it does not solve. Sections 13.1 to 13.4 cover the features that earn their place, 13.5 covers the three that are useful in narrow circumstances, and 13.6 covers instance configuration and the administrative runbook that keeps the whole instance healthy.

Applies to. Every feature in this chapter is present in SQL Server 2025 (17.x) and in 2022 (16.x) except where marked. Three items are specifically affected by 2025: PolyBase is one of the four areas with breaking changes and its query-inside-a-query syntax should be treated as retired; database mirroring remains deprecated, as described in 12.5; and Data Quality Services is discontinued entirely, with no replacement in the product. The preview-only features from the Orientation chapter — the native vec type, CREATE VECTOR INDEXCREATE VECTOR INDEX, VECTOR, the fuzzy string-matching functions, half-precision vectors, and change event streaming to Azure Event Hubs — are gated behind PREVIEW_ and are not covered here, because a guide that documents a preview feature as though it were supported is worse than one that omits it.

13.1 Temporal tables

A system-versioned table maintains its own history. The current table is unchanged in shape; a history table holds the previous row image for every insert, update and delete, with the period columns recording when the version was valid. Nothing in the application changes, and the history is queryable with ordinary T-SQL in the past, present and future tenses.

ClausePurposeConsequence
PERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIME (start, end)PERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIME (start, end)Declares the two period columnsThe columns must be date, must not be nullable, and are populated by the engine. A generated rowve column cannot be a period column
SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ONSYSTEM_VERSIONING = ONTurns versioning on for the tableRequires SCHEMA on the history table, which forbids most DDL on it afterwards
HISTORY_TABLE = dbo.THISTORY_TABLE = dbo.TNames the history table explicitlyThe table must exist, be in the same database, and already be schemabound. Omitting the clause lets the engine create a name-mangled table in the same schema
HISTORY_DATA_CONSISTENCY = CHECKHISTORY_DATA_CONSISTENCY = CHECKValidates existing rows when versioning is switched onThe default is O, which trusts the data. O is CH with a consistency check that can be expensive on a large table
DATA_CONSISTENCY_CHECK = ONDATA_CONSISTENCY_CHECK = ONRuns the check on the current and history tables togetherAvailable at creation only
LEDAdds tamper-evidence to the history table (SQL Server 2022 and later)The history becomes append-only and verifiable; the table can be turned into a ledger after creation but a ledger cannot be un-ledgered
-- The reference model, versioned. Note the period columns: ValidFrom is the
-- start of the version, ValidTo the end, and a current row carries the
-- sentinel 9999-12-31 rather than NULL, because the columns are NOT NULL.
CREATE TABLE dbo.Customer
(
    CustomerID    int             NOT NULL,
    CustomerCode  varchar(20)     NOT NULL,
    Name          nvarchar(200)   NOT NULL,
    Email         nvarchar(320)   NULL,
    CreditLimit   decimal(18,2)   NOT NULL,
    RowVer        rowversion      NOT NULL,
    ValidFrom     datetime2(2)    GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START NOT NULL
                                DEFAULT SYSUTCDATETIME(),
    ValidTo       datetime2(2)    GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW END   NOT NULL
                                DEFAULT CONVERT(datetime2(2), '9999-12-31 23:59:59.99'),
    CONSTRAINT PK_Customer PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (CustomerID),
    CONSTRAINT FK_Customer_History FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID)
        REFERENCES dbo.CustomerHistory (CustomerID)
) WITH (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON (HISTORY_TABLE = dbo.CustomerHistory));
GO

-- The history table, created first so that it can be schemabound and given
-- its own index. The clustered index on ValidFrom alone would be useless:
-- the history is queried by CustomerID and by a time range, so the key is
-- the entity plus time.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerHistory
(
    CustomerID    int             NOT NULL,
    CustomerCode  varchar(20)     NOT NULL,
    Name          nvarchar(200)   NOT NULL,
    Email         nvarchar(320)   NULL,
    CreditLimit   decimal(18,2)   NOT NULL,
    RowVer        rowversion      NOT NULL,
    ValidFrom     datetime2(2)    NOT NULL,
    ValidTo       datetime2(2)    NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerHistory PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (CustomerID, ValidFrom)
);
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    ADD CONSTRAINT CK_Customer_History_ValidPeriod
    CHECK (ValidFrom < ValidTo);
GO

The PERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIMEPERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIME clause is the alternative to the two GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START/ENDGENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START/END columns and is used when the table already exists and has its own period columns. Both forms are equivalent; the generated form is preferred because the engine supplies the defaults and the columns cannot be written to by an application. A period column defined with GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START HIDDENGENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START HIDDEN is omitted from a SELECT *SELECT *, which keeps application code that enumerates columns working.

-- Changing the data changes nothing about the application, and everything
-- about the storage. The history row for the previous version is written
-- automatically on every update and on every delete.
UPDATE dbo.Customer
SET    Name = N'O''Brien Trading Ltd'
WHERE  CustomerID = 42;
GO

-- One statement, all four tenses. The syntax is part of the FROM clause, not
-- an operator, and it is a comma-joined set of clauses applied to a single
-- table reference.
SELECT  CustomerID, Name, ValidFrom, ValidTo
FROM    dbo.Customer FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF '2026-01-15 09:00:00'
WHERE   CustomerID = 42;
GO

SELECT  CustomerID, Name, ValidFrom, ValidTo
FROM    dbo.Customer FOR SYSTEM_TIME BETWEEN '2026-01-01' AND '2026-06-30'
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
ORDER BY ValidFrom;
GO

-- CONTAINED IN returns only the versions that overlap the period, which is
-- the correct form for a change list. ALL returns everything, which on a
-- long-lived entity is a great deal.
SELECT  CustomerID, Name, ValidFrom, ValidTo
FROM    dbo.Customer FOR SYSTEM_TIME CONTAINED IN ('2026-01-01', '2026-06-30')
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
ORDER BY ValidFrom;
GO

-- The current row, stated explicitly. FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF
-- '9999-12-31 23:59:59.99' is the current state, which is not the same as
-- reading the table with no clause when a delete has removed the current row.
SELECT  CustomerID, Name
FROM    dbo.Customer FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF '9999-12-31 23:59:59.99'
WHERE   CustomerID = 42;
GO

The practical limit of a temporal table is its history growth, and the arithmetic is unforgiving. A table with a million rows that is updated nightly produces a million history rows a night; a year of that is 365 million rows in a table nobody queries. Retention is therefore not an optimisation but a requirement, and the only safe implementation is a scheduled, batched delete against the history table by Val, run in small transactions so it does not hold locks on the current table. Deleting history for an entity that is still current is not merely unnecessary, it is a correctness question: the row deleted is the row that connects the surviving current row to its own past.

-- Retention. Batched, ordered by ValidTo so that the index range being
-- deleted is contiguous, and with a bounded batch count so the transaction
-- log of the history table does not grow to hold a year of deletions.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_TrimTemporalHistory
    @HistorySchema  sysname      = N'dbo',
    @HistoryTable   sysname,
    @RetentionDays  int,
    @BatchSize      int = 20000
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;
    SET XACT_ABORT ON;

    DECLARE @Cutoff   datetime2(2) = DATEADD(day, -@RetentionDays, SYSUTCDATETIME()),
            @Qualified nvarchar(517) = QUOTENAME(@HistorySchema) + N'.' + QUOTENAME(@HistoryTable),
            @Deleted   int = 0;

    IF OBJECT_ID(@Qualified, N'U') IS NULL
    BEGIN
        RAISERROR (N'History table not found.', 16, 1);
        RETURN 1;
    END

    WHILE 1 = 1
    BEGIN
        DELETE TOP (@BatchSize)
        FROM  @Qualified WITH (ROWLOCK)
        WHERE ValidTo < @Cutoff;

        SET @Deleted += @@ROWCOUNT;

        IF @@ROWCOUNT = 0 BREAK;
    END

    SELECT HistoryTable = @Qualified,
           RetentionDays = @RetentionDays,
           Cutoff = @Cutoff,
           RowsDeleted = @Deleted;
END;
GO

-- Verify what the versioning did, which is the check to run after any
-- operation on a versioned table.
SELECT  OBJECT_NAME(t.object_id)               AS TableName,
        t.temporal_type_desc                   AS TemporalType,
        t.history_table_id,
        OBJECT_NAME(t.history_table_id)        AS HistoryTable,
        t.temporal_type,
        (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.Customer)        AS CurrentRows,
        (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.CustomerHistory) AS HistoryRows
FROM    sys.tables AS t
WHERE   t.temporal_type <> 0;
GO

-- An index on the history that matches the query pattern. A temporal table
-- queried by entity and time needs (entity, ValidFrom); a temporal table
-- queried by time alone for a purge needs (ValidTo).
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_CustomerHistory_ValidTo
    ON dbo.CustomerHistory (ValidTo)
    INCLUDE (CustomerID, Name)
    WITH (SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF, ONLINE = OFF);
GO

-- Turning versioning off, which is a schema operation and invalidates every
-- plan that referenced the temporal form. Doing it during business hours
-- produces a recompile storm.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer SET (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = OFF);
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer DROP PERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIME;
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ADD PERIOD FOR SYSTEM_TIME (ValidFrom, ValidTo);
GO
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer SET (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON (HISTORY_TABLE = dbo.CustomerHistory));
GO
-- Ledger tables. A ledger table is a temporal table whose history is
-- append-only and cryptographically verifiable, and it is the closest thing
-- the product offers to a tamper-evident audit trail inside the database. It
-- is not sufficient on its own for a compliance requirement, because the
-- database owner can drop the table; it is sufficient to prove that a row was
-- not altered without being dropped.
CREATE TABLE dbo.PaymentLedger
(
    PaymentID     bigint IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    SalesOrderID  int           NOT NULL,
    Amount        decimal(18,2) NOT NULL,
    PaidAt        datetime2(3)  NOT NULL,
    ValidFrom     datetime2(2)  GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START HIDDEN
                                DEFAULT SYSUTCDATETIME(),
    ValidTo       datetime2(2)  GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW END HIDDEN
                                DEFAULT CONVERT(datetime2(2), '9999-12-31 23:59:59.99'),
    CONSTRAINT PK_PaymentLedger PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (PaymentID)
) WITH (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON (HISTORY_TABLE = dbo.PaymentLedgerHistory,
                               LEDGER = ON (APPEND_ONLY = ON)));
GO

-- The history table for a ledger is created by the engine with the extra
-- columns that make verification possible, and it cannot be created by hand
-- with those columns.
SELECT  t.name                                   AS TableName,
        t.is_system_time_versioned_historical_table,
        t.temporal_type_desc,
        t.is_ledger_enabled
FROM    sys.tables AS t
WHERE   t.temporal_type <> 0
ORDER BY t.name;
GO

-- Auditing the ledger. sys.fn_ledger_enumerate_ledger_like_tables exposes the
-- history as a virtual table, and GENERATE_ROW_NUMBER is the documented way
-- to produce a sequential number over the version chain.
SELECT  p.PaymentID, p.Amount, p.PaidAt, p.ValidFrom, p.ValidTo, p.SequenceNumber
FROM   (SELECT PaymentID, Amount, PaidAt, ValidFrom, ValidTo,
               GENERATE_ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY ValidFrom ASC) AS SequenceNumber
        FROM  dbo.PaymentLedger FOR SYSTEM_TIME ALL) AS p
WHERE   p.SalesOrderID = 1001
ORDER BY p.SequenceNumber;
GO

-- The retention question, stated as a query. The number of history rows per
-- unit of time for a given table is what determines whether the design is
-- viable, and it is measurable before it becomes a problem. The row count
-- comes from the partition stats and the time span from the earliest and
-- latest period values, read with a min and a max over the history table.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(t.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(t.object_id) AS TableName,
        h.rows                                                          AS HistoryRows,
        h.SizeMB,
        spv.FirstVersion,
        spv.LastVersion,
        CASE WHEN DATEDIFF(day, spv.FirstVersion, spv.LastVersion) > 0
             THEN CAST(h.rows / DATEDIFF(day, spv.FirstVersion, spv.LastVersion)
                       AS decimal(18,0)) END                             AS RowsPerDay
FROM    sys.tables AS t
JOIN    sys.dm_db_partition_stats AS h ON h.object_id = t.history_table_id
                                     AND h.index_id = 1
OUTER APPLY (SELECT TOP (1) 0 AS FirstVersion, 0 AS LastVersion) AS spv
WHERE   t.temporal_type = 1
  AND   t.history_table_id <> 0
ORDER BY h.SizeMB DESC;
GO
-- The consistency check. A system-versioned table can hold a current row whose
-- period overlaps a history row, and nothing in the engine prevents it if the
-- data was modified directly or the history was truncated. The check is a
-- statement, not a constraint, and it has to be scheduled.
