SELECT  c.CustomerID,
        c.ValidFrom                                       AS CurrentValidFrom,
        h.ValidFrom                                       AS HistoryValidFrom,
        h.ValidTo                                         AS HistoryValidTo
FROM    dbo.Customer      AS c
JOIN    dbo.CustomerHistory AS h ON h.CustomerID = c.CustomerID
                               AND h.ValidFrom < c.ValidFrom
                               AND h.ValidTo   > c.ValidFrom;   -- overlaps
GO

-- The two anomalies that matter: a history row that overlaps its successor,
-- and a current row whose ValidTo is not the sentinel.
SELECT  Anomaly = N'History overlap',
        Rows    = COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM  (SELECT h.CustomerID
       FROM   dbo.CustomerHistory AS h
       WHERE  h.ValidFrom < h.ValidTo
       INTERSECT
       SELECT h2.CustomerID
       FROM   dbo.CustomerHistory AS h1
       JOIN   dbo.CustomerHistory AS h2 ON h2.CustomerID = h1.CustomerID
                                     AND h2.ValidFrom = h1.ValidTo
       WHERE  h1.ValidFrom < h1.ValidTo) AS x
UNION ALL
SELECT N'Current row with a non-sentinel ValidTo',
       COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   dbo.Customer
WHERE  ValidTo <> CONVERT(datetime2(2), '9999-12-31 23:59:59.99');
GO

-- The end-to-end consistency check that ALTER SYSTEM_VERSIONING itself
-- performs, run on demand. ON is the default and it is a full scan of both
-- tables, so it is a maintenance-window operation on a large pair.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    SET (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON (HISTORY_TABLE = dbo.CustomerHistory,
                                DATA_CONSISTENCY_CHECK = ON));
GO

-- The performance characteristics, which decide the design. A versioned table
-- costs a write to the history table for every insert, update and delete, and
-- a foreign key from the current table to the history table is what enforces
-- that. On a high-churn table that is the dominant cost of the operation, and
-- it is the reason to choose the history table's columns deliberately rather
-- than by CREATE TABLE ... AS SELECT.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(t.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(t.object_id) AS TableName,
        t.temporal_type_desc,
        h.rows                                                          AS HistoryRows,
        h.SizeMB,
        CASE WHEN DATEDIFF(day, h.MinVersion, h.MaxVersion) > 0
             THEN CAST(h.rows / DATEDIFF(day, h.MinVersion, h.MaxVersion) AS decimal(18,0))
        END                                                             AS RowsPerDay
FROM    sys.tables AS t
JOIN    sys.dm_db_partition_stats AS h ON h.object_id = t.history_table_id
                                     AND h.index_id = 1
CROSS APPLY (SELECT 0 AS MinVersion, 0 AS MaxVersion) AS h2
WHERE   t.temporal_type = 1
ORDER BY h.SizeMB DESC;
GO

13.2 Change Tracking and Change Data Capture

Two features answer the same question — what changed, and when — and they are not interchangeable. Change Tracking records only the fact that a row changed, plus the columns that were configured to be tracked, and is designed for a single-table read. Change Data Capture records the full row image of every change into a capture instance and is designed for a change stream into a warehouse.

Change TrackingChange Data Capture
GranularityOne table, one row per change with the primary key and the tracked columnsThe database, full before-and-after images of every insert, update and delete in every enabled table
Change informationSYNC_TRAN_ID (commit order), SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION, and the changed column valuesAll columns before and after, plus __$operation__$operation, __$start_lsn__$start_lsn and __$seq__$seq
Reading itCHANG with a VER or CHANG function, returning a table-valued resultThe capture instance tables, queried with cdc.fn_cdc_ge and its variants
CleanupCHANGETABLE ... WITH (REMOVE_OLD_DATA = true)CHANGETABLE ... WITH (REMOVE_OLD_DATA = true) sets a retention period; the engine purges on a scheduleThe capture job and the cleanup job in SQL Server Agent, both of which must be running
Overhead on the source tableVery low: one column per table, no triggers on the table itself for the common case, and the change table holds only what is configuredHigher: the log is read, every enabled table writes to the capture instance, and the transaction log is not trimmable until the capture job has advanced
Use it forSynchronising a single table to a cache, a search index or a reporting copyFeeding a data warehouse, an audit store, or a replication target that needs the values
Compatibility level90 and above90 and above; the capture instance requires the database to be in FU or READ_COMMIT capable configurations
-- Change Tracking: the smallest useful configuration. One column on the
-- table, and a read that returns what changed since a version the caller
-- already has.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    ADD  IsChanged bit NOT NULL
        CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_IsChanged DEFAULT (0);
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer ENABLE CHANGE_TRACKING
    WITH (TRACK_COLUMNS_UPDATED = OFF);
GO

-- Enable it for the database as a whole. The table-level option is what
-- actually starts capture; the database option sets the default for tables
-- enabled later.
ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET CHANGE_TRACKING = ON
    (AUTO_CLEANUP = ON, RETENTION_PERIOD = 2 DAYS);
GO

-- A modification, then a read. CHANGETABLE is a set-returning function
-- applied with a table alias and requires a join predicate on the primary key.
UPDATE dbo.Customer
SET    Email = N'billing@obrientrading.example'
WHERE  CustomerID = 42;
GO

DECLARE @Baseline bigint = (SELECT CHANGE_TRACKING_VERSION() - 1);

SELECT  ct.CustomerID, ct.SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION, ct.IsChanged
FROM    CHANGETABLE (CHANGES_CUSTOMER, @Baseline) AS ct
WHERE   ct.CustomerID = 42;
GO

-- Tracking the updated columns, which costs a little more and turns a
-- "something changed" answer into a "this changed" answer.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    ADD  CreditLimitChanged bit NOT NULL
        CONSTRAINT DF_Customer_CreditLimitChanged DEFAULT (0);
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    CHANGE_TRACKING TRACK COLUMNS UPDATED (
        Name, Email, CreditLimit
    );
GO

DECLARE @Baseline2 bigint = (SELECT CHANGE_TRACKING_VERSION() - 1);

SELECT  ct.CustomerID, ct.SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION, ct.Name, ct.CreditLimitChanged
FROM    CHANGETABLE (CHANGES_CUSTOMER, @Baseline2) AS ct
WHERE   ct.CustomerID = 42;
GO

-- Disabling. The tracking column remains; the change table is emptied and the
-- column no longer changes.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer DISABLE CHANGE_TRACKING;
GO

GO
-- Tracking a table that already exists and already has data. The rows present
-- when tracking is enabled are the baseline, and CHANGE_TRACKING_VERSION()
-- taken immediately after the ALTER is the version that corresponds to that
-- baseline. A consumer that reads from version zero gets every row as an
-- insert, which is correct but expensive.
DECLARE @Baseline bigint;

BEGIN TRANSACTION;

ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder ENABLE CHANGE_TRACKING
    WITH (TRACK_COLUMNS_UPDATED = OFF);

SET @Baseline = CHANGE_TRACKING_VERSION();

COMMIT TRANSACTION;

SELECT BaselineVersion = @Baseline;
GO

-- Reading the tracked change set without joining the base table, which is
-- what a consumer that only needs the keys does. CHANGETABLE can be the
-- outer query, and it needs a join predicate on the primary key, which the
-- system supplies.
DECLARE @From bigint = 0;

SELECT  ct.SalesOrderID,
        ct.SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION,
        ct.SYS_CHANGE_VERSION
FROM    CHANGETABLE (CHANGES_SALESORDER, @From) AS ct
WHERE   ct.SalesOrderID BETWEEN 1000 AND 1010
ORDER BY ct.SalesOrderID;
GO

-- Removing the feature from a table, which is the operation to plan for
-- before dropping a table: change tracking metadata prevents a DROP TABLE
-- until it is disabled, and the failure is error 22113.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder DISABLE CHANGE_TRACKING;
GO
DROP TABLE dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- The two retention mechanisms, because they behave differently. Change
-- Tracking purges automatically on its own schedule, controlled by
-- AUTO_CLEANUP and RETENTION_PERIOD at the database level. Change Data
-- Capture purges only when the cleanup job runs, and the job is a SQL Agent
-- job that somebody has to have created or enabled.
SELECT  name, value, value_for_secondary, description
FROM    sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE   name IN (N'CHANGE_TRACKING',
                 N'CHANGE_TRACKING_AUTO_CLEANUP',
                 N'CHANGE_TRACKING_RETENTION_PERIOD');
GO

SELECT  j.name, j.enabled,
        (SELECT MAX(h.run_date) FROM msdb.dbo.sysjobhistory h WHERE h.job_id = j.job_id) AS LastRun
FROM    msdb.dbo.sysjobs AS j
WHERE   j.name LIKE N'cdc.%'
ORDER BY j.name;
GO
-- Change Data Capture. Four statements, and the last two are jobs that must be
-- running for the feature to function at all.
EXEC sys.sp_cdc_enable_db;
GO

EXEC sys.sp_cdc_enable_table
     @source_schema = N'dbo',
     @source_name   = N'SalesOrder',
     @role_name     = NULL,
     @capture_instance = N'dbo_SalesOrder',
     @supports_net_change = 1,
     @captured_column_list = N'CustomerID,OrderDate,Status,TotalAmount',
     @capture_type = N'log';
GO

-- What is captured and where. capture_type = 'log' means the change is read
-- from the transaction log; 'all' means the full row image is written for
-- every column, including large objects, at a much greater cost.
SELECT  capture_instance,
        source_schema,
        source_table,
        capture_type,
        supports_net_changes,
        start_lsn,
        index_name
FROM    cdc.captured_columns_view_vu
UNION ALL
SELECT  capture_instance, source_schema, source_table, capture_type,
        supports_net_changes, NULL, NULL
FROM    cdc.captured_tables
ORDER BY capture_instance;
GO

-- The mapping between the source table and the change table, without which
-- neither can be reconstructed.
SELECT  object_id, capture_instance, start_lsn, end_lsn, index_name
FROM    cdc.map_parameters_tb
ORDER BY capture_instance;
GO

-- Reading the changes. The LSN range is mandatory and the three functions
-- differ only in what they filter on.
DECLARE @from_lsn binary(10) = (SELECT TOP (1) start_lsn FROM cdc.lsn_time_mapping
                                ORDER BY start_lsn DESC),
        @to_lsn   binary(10) = (SELECT MAX(start_lsn) FROM cdc.lsn_time_mapping);

SELECT  __$operation                       AS Operation,  -- 1 insert, 2 update, 3 delete
        __$start_lsn,
        __$seqval,
        CustomerID,
        OrderDate,
        Status,
        TotalAmount
FROM    cdc.fn_cdc_get_all_changes_dbo_SalesOrder(@from_lsn, @to_lsn, N'all update old')
ORDER BY __$start_lsn, __$seqval;
GO

-- Net changes collapses the update history to the final image of each row,
-- which is what a synchronisation consumer usually wants and which a
-- warehouse usually does not.
SELECT  CustomerID,
        MAX(Operation)      AS FinalOperation,
        MAX(OrderDate)      AS OrderDate,
        MAX(Status)         AS Status
FROM   (SELECT __$operation AS Operation,
               CustomerID,
               OrderDate,
               Status
        FROM   cdc.fn_cdc_get_net_changes_dbo_SalesOrder(@from_lsn, @to_lsn, N'all')) AS x
GROUP BY CustomerID
ORDER BY CustomerID;
GO

-- The capture instance tables have fixed column names: the same names as the
-- source columns, plus __$operation, __$start_lsn, __$seqval and __$update_mask.
SELECT  c.name          AS ColumnName,
        c.column_id,
        t.name          AS SystemType,
        c.is_nullable
FROM    cdc.captured_columns AS c
JOIN    cdc.captured_tables  AS t ON t.object_id = c.object_id
ORDER BY c.column_id;
GO

-- The jobs. Neither runs unless SQL Server Agent is running, and a system
-- with CDC enabled and the agent stopped fills its transaction log until it
-- runs out of space.
SELECT  job_id, name, enabled FROM msdb.dbo.sysjobs
WHERE   name LIKE N'cdc.%'
ORDER BY name;
GO
-- The lag, which is the number that decides whether a warehouse is current.
-- lsn_time_mapping records when each LSN was captured, so the age of the
-- newest captured change is the end-to-end latency of the pipeline.
SELECT  TOP (5)
    start_lsn,
    tran_id,
    begin_time,
    commit_time,
    DATEDIFF(second, commit_time, SYSUTCDATETIME()) AS CommitAgeSeconds
FROM    cdc.lsn_time_mapping
ORDER BY start_lsn DESC;
GO

-- The capture job itself, when it is behind or has stopped. A process named
-- cdc% that is waiting is the reason the consumer is seeing stale data.
SELECT  session_id,
        status,
        command,
        wait_type,
        wait_time,
        total_elapsed_time
FROM    sys.dm_exec_requests
WHERE   program_name LIKE N'cdc%'
   OR   program_name LIKE N'%capture%';
GO

-- Change Tracking, with the baseline discipline that makes it usable. A
-- consumer stores the version it last processed, and the next read takes
-- everything since. Storing the version is the whole pattern, and a consumer
-- that stores the maximum key instead is not reading a change stream.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerSyncState
(
    ConsumerName  sysname      NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerSyncState PRIMARY KEY,
    LastVersion   bigint       NOT NULL,
    LastRunAt     datetime2(3) NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_CustomerSyncState_LastRunAt DEFAULT (SYSUTCDATETIME())
);
GO

CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_SyncCustomer
    @Consumer sysname
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    DECLARE @LastVersion bigint =
            (SELECT LastVersion FROM dbo.CustomerSyncState WHERE ConsumerName = @Consumer);

    IF @LastVersion IS NULL
        SET @LastVersion = 0;

    -- The change set. The version is read AFTER the rows, so that a failure
    -- between the two re-reads the same changes rather than skipping them.
    SELECT  c.CustomerID,
            c.CustomerCode,
            c.Name,
            c.Email,
            c.CreditLimit
    FROM    CHANGETABLE (CHANGES_CUSTOMER, @LastVersion) AS ct
    JOIN    dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = ct.CustomerID
    ORDER BY ct.CustomerID;

    MERGE dbo.CustomerSyncState AS tgt
    USING (SELECT @Consumer AS ConsumerName, CHANGE_TRACKING_VERSION() AS NewVersion) AS src
       ON tgt.ConsumerName = src.ConsumerName
    WHEN MATCHED THEN
        UPDATE SET LastVersion = src.NewVersion, LastRunAt = SYSUTCDATETIME()
    WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN
        INSERT (ConsumerName, LastVersion, LastRunAt)
        VALUES (src.ConsumerName, src.NewVersion, SYSUTCDATETIME());
END;
GO

-- The two features side by side. This is the comparison that decides which
-- to use, and it comes down to whether the consumer needs the values or only
-- the fact that something changed.
SELECT N'Change Tracking'     AS Feature,
       N'One table'          AS Scope,
       N'Tracked columns plus SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION' AS Payload,
       N'Automatic, on a schedule'                  AS CleanupMechanism
UNION ALL
SELECT N'Change Data Capture', N'The database', N'Full before-and-after images',
       N'The capture and cleanup jobs in SQL Server Agent';
GO
-- Change Tracking, the read-only view. CHANGETABLE_CURRENT_VERSION returns
-- only the current version of each changed row rather than the whole change
-- history, which is the form a synchronisation consumer usually wants and the
-- one that does not have to walk the chain.
DECLARE @FromVersion bigint = 0;

SELECT  ct.CustomerID,
        ct.SYS_CHANGE_OPERATION,
        c.CustomerCode,
        c.Name,
        c.Email,
        c.CreditLimit
FROM    CHANGETABLE (CHANGES_CUSTOMER, @FromVersion,
                     CHANGETABLE_CURRENT_VERSION) AS ct
JOIN    dbo.Customer AS c ON c.CustomerID = ct.CustomerID
ORDER BY ct.CustomerID;
GO

-- Disable on a table, which is the step that is missed. The metadata prevents
-- the table from being dropped until it is disabled, and the tracking column
-- remains in the table afterwards. Both facts surprise people the first time.
SELECT  t.name                                        AS TableName,
        ct.is_track_columns_updated_on,
        ct.tracking_type_desc
FROM    sys.change_tracking_tables      AS ct
JOIN    sys.tables                       AS t ON t.object_id = ct.object_id
ORDER BY t.name;
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer DISABLE CHANGE_TRACKING;
GO

-- Change Data Capture, the rest of the read surface. The three functions
-- differ in the mode argument and that is all: all changes preserves the
-- update history, net changes collapses it, and all update old returns the
-- before-image as well as the after-image.
DECLARE @FromLsn binary(10) = (SELECT MIN(start_lsn) FROM cdc.lsn_time_mapping),
        @ToLsn   binary(10) = (SELECT MAX(start_lsn) FROM cdc.lsn_time_mapping);

SELECT  Operation = __$operation,
        Counted   = COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM    cdc.fn_cdc_get_all_changes_dbo_SalesOrder(@FromLsn, @ToLsn, N'all')
GROUP BY __$operation
ORDER BY Operation;
GO

-- The cleanup, in its two forms. sp_cdc_cleanup truncates a capture instance
-- up to a given LSN; the cleanup job does the same on a schedule. A capture
-- instance that is never cleaned is a table that grows without bound inside
-- the source database.
EXEC msdb.dbo.sp_cdc_cleanup
     @capture_instance = N'dbo_SalesOrder',
     @keep_last_transaction = 1,
     @cleanup_jobname  = N'cdc_SalesDB_cleanup',
     @schedule         = N'Sun 23:30';
GO

-- The change tables, whose column names are the source column names plus four
-- fixed ones. Knowing the four is enough to write a consumer without
-- discovering them.
SELECT  COLUMN_NAME, DATA_TYPE, IS_NULLABLE, ORDINAL_POSITION
FROM    INFORMATION_SCHEMA.COLUMNS
WHERE   TABLE_SCHEMA = N'cdc' AND TABLE_NAME = N'dbo_SalesOrder_CT'
ORDER BY ORDINAL_POSITION;
GO

-- Both features, and the decision between them, from the catalog rather than
-- from a document. This is the query to run when somebody asks whether
-- Change Tracking can be used instead of Change Data Capture for a particular
-- table, because it shows what the engine has actually been told to do.
SELECT  TableName    = t.name,
        ChangeTracking = ct.object_id IS NOT NULL,
        CapturedByCDC  = cdc.object_id IS NOT NULL,
        Temporal      = t.temporal_type_desc
FROM    sys.tables AS t
LEFT JOIN sys.change_tracking_tables        AS ct  ON ct.object_id  = t.object_id
LEFT JOIN cdc.captured_tables                AS cdc ON cdc.object_id = t.object_id
ORDER BY t.name;
GO

Caveats. Change Data Capture is not a backup, an audit trail or a compliance record, and it should not be described as one. The capture instance is stored in the same database, is truncated by a cleanup job with a retention period, and can be disabled by anyone holding ALTER ANY DATABASERALTER ANY DATABASER-class permissions. An audit requirement needs an append-only store outside the database and a mechanism that survives a restore. Change Tracking has the opposite problem: it is cheap and adequate, but it answers only for tables it has been enabled on, and a table added to the synchronisation scope without ENABLE CHANGE_TRACKINGENABLE CHANGE_TRACKING fails silently at read time with an empty result rather than an error.

13.3 Service Broker, CLR integration and the deprecated surface

Service Broker is SQL Server’s built-in asynchronous messaging. Conversations, queues and dialogs are database objects, and the engine guarantees exactly-once delivery in order, with no external broker and no polling of a message table. It is also the feature that most often turns out to be the wrong answer, because a queue in the same database on the same instance is not asynchronous. Two other subjects belong here: CLR integration, which extends the database with user-defined types and functions written in managed code, and the deprecations that change what a 2025 instance will accept.

-- Service Broker: the five objects. A queue holds messages; a service owns
-- queues and is the endpoint conversations refer to; a dialog is one
-- conversation; a message type declares the validation; and the route table
-- says where remote endpoints are.
CREATE MESSAGE TYPE [OrderNotification]
    VALIDATION = WELL_FORMED_XML;
GO

CREATE CONTRACT [OrderContract]
(
    [OrderNotification] SENT BY INITIATOR,
    [OrderNotification] SENT BY ANY
);
GO

CREATE QUEUE [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue];
GO

CREATE SERVICE [OrderNotificationService]
    ON QUEUE [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue]
    ([OrderContract]);
GO

-- Sending. The conversation handle is the handle, and it is the only
-- identifier the receiving side needs in order to reply.
DECLARE @handle uniqueidentifier;

BEGIN DIALOGUE CONVERSATION @handle
    FROM SERVICE [OrderNotificationService]
    TO SERVICE   N'OrderService'         -- resolved from the route table
    ON CONTRACT  [OrderContract]
    WITH ENCRYPTION;

SEND ON CONVERSATION @handle
    (N'<OrderNotification><OrderId>1001</OrderId></OrderNotification>');

COMMIT TRANSACTION;
GO

-- Receiving. RECEIVE takes the oldest message, in order, exactly once, and
-- removes it from the queue. END CONVERSATION is required; a conversation
-- left open accumulates state. The body of a well-formed XML message is
-- written to a file stream by the session, so the code below reads the
-- message body and parses it directly, which works for a well-formed XML
-- message type.
DECLARE @msg_body     varbinary(max),
        @conv_handle  uniqueidentifier,
        @msg_type     sysname,
        @xml          xml;

RECEIVE TOP (1)
    @msg_body    = message_body,
    @conv_handle = conversation_handle,
    @msg_type    = message_type_name
FROM    [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue];

IF @@ROWCOUNT > 0
BEGIN
    SET @xml = TRY_CAST(@msg_body AS xml);

    SELECT ConversationHandle = @conv_handle,
           MessageType         = @msg_type,
           OrderId             = @xml.value('(/OrderNotification/OrderId)[1]', 'int');

    END CONVERSATION @conv_handle;
    COMMIT TRANSACTION;
END;
GO

-- Route table, required for anything cross-instance. A Service Broker
-- conversation between two databases on the same instance needs no route; one
-- that crosses an instance boundary does, and a missing route is a message
-- that sits in the outgoing queue until the retention period expires.
CREATE ROUTE [SalesToWarehouseRoute]
    WITH (SERVICE_NAME = N'OrderService',
          BROKER_INSTANCE = N'SalesInstance',
          ADDRESS         = N'TCP://WAREHOUSE01:1433;');
GO

SELECT  ce.conversation_handle,
        ce.conversation_state,
        ce.start_time,
        ce.end_time,
        OBJECT_NAME(ce.conversation_group_id) AS ServiceName
FROM    sys.conversation_endpoints AS ce
WHERE   ce.conversation_state <> 0;
GO

-- Queued messages: the backlog, and the error that stops delivery.
SELECT  conversation_handle,
        message_sequence_number,
        CAST(message_body AS nvarchar(max)) AS MessageBody,
        error_number,
        error_state,
        error_text
FROM    [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue] WITH (NOLOCK)
ORDER BY message_sequence_number;
GO
-- CLR integration. The setting is off by default on a new installation, and it
-- is instance-wide, which is the first reason to think twice.
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'clr enabled', 1;
RECONFIGURE;
GO

-- 'clr strict security' is the 2022-and-later setting and is on by default.
-- It requires the assembly to be signed with a strong name and the signing key
-- to be registered, so that a component cannot be dropped into the database
-- and executed under the service account's privileges. Turning it off
-- disables the unsigned fast path and is not required by any legitimate use.
SELECT name, value, value_in_use, description
FROM   sys.configurations
WHERE  name IN (N'clr enabled', N'clr strict security', N'clr strict security verified');
GO

CREATE ASSEMBLY [OrderValidation]
    FROM N'D:\Assemblies\OrderValidation.dll'
    WITH PERMISSION_SET = UNSAFE_ACCESS_ONLY;
GO

CREATE TYPE dbo.OrderValidationResult
    EXTERNAL NAME [OrderValidation].[OrderValidationResult];
GO

CREATE FUNCTION dbo.ValidateOrder (@payload varbinary(max))
RETURNS dbo.OrderValidationResult
    WITH NATIVE_COMPILATION, SCHEMABINDING, EXECUTE AS CALLER
AS EXTERNAL NAME [OrderValidation].[ValidateOrder];
GO

-- The security surface. PERMISSION_SET = SAFE is the default and restricts
-- the assembly to a small set of allowed operations; UNSAFE_ACCESS_ONLY
-- permits unmanaged code, file and network access, and the assembly then runs
-- with the full privileges of the service account.
SELECT  name,
        permission_set_desc,
        is_modifiable,
        create_date
FROM    sys.assemblies
ORDER BY name;
GO

SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(object_id) AS CLRObject,
        type
FROM    sys.sql_modules
WHERE   object_id IN (SELECT object_id FROM sys.assemblies)
ORDER BY CLRObject;
GO

-- Removing it. An assembly cannot be dropped while anything depends on it, and
-- the dependency is not always obvious: a scalar UDF used in a check
-- constraint keeps the assembly alive.
DROP FUNCTION dbo.ValidateOrder;
GO
DROP ASSEMBLY [OrderValidation];
GO

GO
-- Migrating a deprecated LOB type. text, ntext and image are the only way to
-- store data over 8000 bytes in a way that cannot be indexed, cannot be
-- compared with = and cannot be passed to most functions. The max variants are
-- the same data with none of those restrictions.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(c.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(c.object_id) AS TableName,
        c.name                                                        AS ColumnName,
        ty.name                                                       AS CurrentType,
        c.max_length,
        CASE ty.name WHEN N'text'  THEN N'varchar(max)'
                     WHEN N'ntext' THEN N'nvarchar(max)'
                     WHEN N'image' THEN N'varbinary(max)'
        END                                                           AS ReplacementType
FROM    sys.columns      AS c
JOIN    sys.types        AS ty ON ty.user_type_id = c.user_type_id
WHERE   ty.name IN (N'text', N'ntext', N'image')
ORDER BY TableName, ColumnName;
GO

-- The migration, in the order that keeps the data readable throughout. The
-- new column is added, populated, and the application is switched, and the
-- old column is dropped afterwards rather than in the same deployment: a
-- deployment that drops the old column and the application change in the same
-- window is a deployment with no rollback.
ALTER TABLE dbo.LegacyNote
    ADD NoteText nvarchar(max) NULL;
GO

-- A bounded batch, because updating every row of a large table in one
-- statement is a single large log write and a long lock hold.
DECLARE @Batch int = 5000, @Rows int = 1;

WHILE @Rows > 0
BEGIN
    UPDATE TOP (@Batch)
        dbo.LegacyNote
    SET    NoteText = CONVERT(nvarchar(max), NoteBody)
    WHERE  NoteText IS NULL
      AND  NoteBody IS NOT NULL;

    SET @Rows = @@ROWCOUNT;
END;
GO

ALTER TABLE dbo.LegacyNote ALTER COLUMN NoteText nvarchar(max) NOT NULL;
GO

-- Verify before the old column is dropped. Row counts and a length
-- comparison are enough; a hash comparison of the whole column is the
-- version to run when the column is large or the data is financial.
SELECT  (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.LegacyNote)                     AS TotalRows,
        (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.LegacyNote WHERE NoteText IS NULL) AS UnmigratedRows,
        (SELECT SUM(DATALENGTH(NoteBody))  FROM dbo.LegacyNote)       AS OldBytes,
        (SELECT SUM(DATALENGTH(NoteText))  FROM dbo.LegacyNote)       AS NewBytes;
GO

-- Only after that check passes.
ALTER TABLE dbo.LegacyNote DROP COLUMN NoteBody;
GO
-- Conversation bookkeeping. A dialog lives until both ends end it, and a dialog
-- left open holds a conversation group, a transaction context and a
-- conversation key in the routing tables. The count of open conversations by
-- endpoint is the number to alert on.
SELECT  ce.conversation_handle,
        ce.conversation_group_id,
        ce.is_initiator,
        ce.conversation_state,
        ce.state_desc,
        ce.start_time,
        DATEDIFF(minute, ce.start_time, GETDATE()) AS AgeMinutes,
        OBJECT_NAME(ce.conversation_group_id)      AS ServiceName
FROM    sys.conversation_endpoints AS ce
ORDER BY ce.start_time;
GO

-- The conversation states, which are the four that matter: 0 is the
-- conversation group exists, 1 is started, 2 is the assigned target
-- responded, 3 is the conversation started on the target side, 4 is the
-- group ended, 5 is the conversation ended, 6 is the assigned target
-- responded but has not been told which endpoint to route to.
SELECT  conversation_state,
        state_desc,
        COUNT_BIG(*) AS Conversations
FROM    sys.conversation_endpoints
GROUP BY conversation_state, state_desc
ORDER BY conversation_state;
GO

-- The queues and how deep they are, which is the only number that says
-- whether the consumer is keeping up.
SELECT  q.name                                       AS QueueName,
        q.is_receive_only,
        q.is_message_encrypted,
        q.max_forward_recipients,
      (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.OrderNotificationQueue) AS Messages,
      (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.OrderNotificationQueue WITH (NOLOCK)
        WHERE error_number <> 0)                  AS FailedMessages
FROM    sys.service_queues AS q
WHERE   q.name IN (N'OrderNotificationQueue')
ORDER BY q.name;
GO

-- Ending a conversation from the target side, which is what a consumer does
-- when it has finished with a message. A conversation that is never ended
-- accumulates a conversation group forever.
-- END CONVERSATION @conv_handle;
-- END CONVERSATION @conv_handle WITH CLEANUP;   -- removes the endpoint
GO

-- Activation: a queue that processes itself when a message arrives, without
-- an external poller. This is the feature that makes Service Broker
-- genuinely asynchronous within one instance, and it is the one that turns
-- the example into a real system.
CREATE QUEUE [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue] WITH STATUS = ON,
    ACTIVATION (STATUS = ON,
                MAX_QUEUE_READERS = 4,
                PROCEDURE_NAME = [dbo.usp_ProcessOrderNotifications]);
GO

-- Changing activation on an existing queue: it has to be disabled first, and
-- the queue is drained in the process, so this is a scheduled operation.
ALTER QUEUE [dbo.OrderNotificationQueue] WITH STATUS = OFF;
GO

-- Dialog cleanup. Conversations that have ended leave entries behind until
-- the cleanup runs, and an unmaintained system accumulates them.
EXEC sys.sp_broker_cleanup_conversations @ConversationGroupId = NULL;
GO

-- A message that cannot be delivered, which is the failure that is easiest to
-- miss. A conversation to a service that does not exist on the target stays in
-- the outgoing queue until the retention period expires, and nothing errors on
-- the sending side.
SELECT  conversation_handle,
        to_service_name,
        service_broker_guid,
        error_number,
        error_text,
        CONVERT(nvarchar(20), next_message_time, 126) AS NextAttempt
FROM    sys.transmission_queue;
GO
-- When Service Broker is the wrong answer, stated as a decision rather than as
-- a preference. A queue in the same database on the same instance is not
-- asynchronous in any sense that matters: a failure takes the queue with it,
-- a slow consumer blocks the sender's transaction if the conversation is in
-- the same transaction, and the operational tooling is the same tooling.
--
-- The feature earns its place in three situations and loses in the rest:
--
--   Wins: an asynchronous boundary between components on the SAME instance,
--         where polling a status column is worse. Activation makes it
--         genuinely event-driven.
--   Wins: a cross-instance or cross-database contract, where Service Broker
--         provides exactly-once in-order delivery with no external broker.
--   Wins: a queue that must be transactional with the data that produced it.
--
--   Loses: a queue that has to survive the loss of the instance. It does not;
--         a disk-based queue in another database does.
--   Loses: cross-machine messaging to a non-SQL-Server consumer. There is no
--         protocol, and the supported answer is a broker, not Service Broker.
--   Loses: anything where the number of active conversations reaches tens of
--         thousands; conversation cleanup becomes the dominant cost.

-- The conversation count, which is the number that decides whether the
-- feature is still appropriate. A system with 400,000 open conversations is
-- spending its time on dialog cleanup.
SELECT  OpenConversations = COUNT_BIG(*),
        OldestOpenDays     = DATEDIFF(day, MIN(start_time), GETDATE())
FROM    sys.conversation_endpoints
WHERE   conversation_state NOT IN (4, 5);   -- 4 ended group, 5 ended conversation
GO

-- The cleanup, which is the operation that keeps the catalogue tables from
-- growing. Conversations that have ended leave rows in four system tables
-- until this runs, and a system that never runs it degrades steadily.
EXEC sys.sp_broker_cleanup_conversations @ConversationGroupId = NULL;
GO

-- The three system tables that matter, and their row counts.
SELECT  'sys.conversation_endpoints' AS TableName, COUNT_BIG(*) AS Rows FROM sys.conversation_endpoints
UNION ALL
SELECT 'sys.conversation_groups',     COUNT_BIG(*) FROM sys.conversation_groups
UNION ALL
SELECT 'sys.conversation_priorities', COUNT_BIG(*) FROM sys.conversation_priorities
UNION ALL
SELECT 'sys.transmission_queue',      COUNT_BIG(*) FROM sys.transmission_queue;
GO

-- Encryption on a conversation. FOR ENCRYPTION means the message bodies are
-- encrypted in transit between the two endpoints; it is off by default and it
-- is a decision about the network as much as about the data.
DECLARE @handle uniqueidentifier;

BEGIN DIALOGUE CONVERSATION @handle
    FROM SERVICE [OrderNotificationService]
    TO SERVICE   N'OrderService'
    ON CONTRACT  [OrderContract]
    WITH ENCRYPTION;
GO

SELECT  c.name,
        c.is_encrypted,
        c.dm_authentication_level_desc
FROM    sys.conversation_endpoints AS c
ORDER BY c.start_time DESC;
GO

-- The retention and poison-message settings, which are the two that turn a
-- message-processing failure into a support incident. Without a poison queue
-- and a retention period, a message that cannot be processed blocks the
-- conversation forever.
SELECT  q.name,
       q.max_forward_recipients,
       q.max_forward_size,
       q.is_receive_only,
       q.is_message_encrypted
FROM    sys.service_queues AS q
ORDER BY q.name;
GO

CREATE QUEUE [dbo.OrderPoisonQueue] WITH STATUS = ON;
GO

-- A conversation with a retry and a dead-letter conversation, which is the
-- form that survives a message the consumer cannot handle.
DECLARE @retry_handle uniqueidentifier;

BEGIN DIALOGUE CONVERSATION @retry_handle
    FROM SERVICE [OrderNotificationService]
    TO SERVICE   N'OrderService'
    ON CONTRACT  [OrderContract]
    WITH ENCRYPTION;
GO
