13.4 JSON: FOR JSON, OPENJSON and JSON indexes

JSON support is a set of functions and operators, not a data type. FOR JSONFOR JSON projects a result set into a JSON document; OPEN reads a document into a rowset; JSON_ extracts a scalar and JSON_ extracts an object or array. The decision that matters is whether the document is the storage format or a transport format, because the two have opposite performance characteristics.

-- Producing JSON. PATH controls the shape explicitly, AUTO lets the engine infer
-- it from the column aliases, and INCLUDE_NULL_VALUES stops a nullable column
-- from being omitted, which is the difference between a document that a
-- strict consumer can parse and one that fails on a missing required field.
SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name, Email, CreditLimit
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
FOR JSON PATH, ROOT(N'customer'), INCLUDE_NULL_VALUES;
GO

-- Nested output needs a subquery, because FOR JSON produces a flat array
-- from one result set. A correlated subquery is the supported way to nest.
SELECT  o.SalesOrderID,
       o.OrderDate,
       o.Status,
       o.TotalAmount,
       (SELECT li.LineNo, li.ProductID, li.Quantity, li.UnitPrice
        FROM   dbo.SalesOrderLine AS li
        WHERE  li.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
        ORDER  BY li.LineNo
        FOR JSON PATH, INCLUDE_NULL_VALUES)  AS Lines
FROM    dbo.SalesOrder AS o
WHERE   o.CustomerID = 42
ORDER   BY o.SalesOrderID
FOR JSON PATH, ROOT(N'orders'), INCLUDE_NULL_VALUES;
GO

-- Consuming JSON, default schema. Every key becomes a column, every value a
-- row, and the types are nvarchar and int because JSON has no other types.
SELECT  [key], [value], [type]
FROM    OPENJSON(N'{"a":1,"b":"two","c":null,"d":true}')
ORDER BY [key];
GO

-- The type codes, because the third column is not self-describing: 0 is null,
-- 1 is a string, 2 is a number, 3 is true or false, and 4 and 5 are an array
-- and an object respectively. A query that ignores it treats an object as a
-- string and produces a document as if it were a value.
SELECT  [key],
        [value],
        [type],
        CASE [type]
            WHEN 0 THEN N'null'
            WHEN 1 THEN N'string'
            WHEN 2 THEN N'number'
            WHEN 3 THEN N'bool'
            WHEN 4 THEN N'array'
            WHEN 5 THEN N'object'
        END AS TypeName
FROM    OPENJSON(N'{"id":1,"name":"Smith","active":true,"tags":["a","b"],
                     "address":{"city":"Leeds"}}')
ORDER BY [key];
GO

-- The whole document as rows, which is the right form when the shape is not
-- known in advance. This is a table-valued function, so it goes in a FROM
-- clause with an alias, and the alias supplies the column names.
SELECT  j.[key]   AS PropertyName,
        j.[value] AS PropertyValue,
        j.[type]  AS PropertyType
FROM    OPENJSON(N'{"country":"GB","tier":"gold","creditLimit":25000}') AS j
ORDER BY j.[key];
GO

-- Consuming JSON, explicit schema. This is the form to use: it applies the
-- target types, it projects only the keys that are wanted, and it lets a
-- malformed value become NULL rather than an error.
DECLARE @doc nvarchar(max) = N'{"OrderId":1001,"OrderDate":"2026-01-15T00:00:00",
                                 "Customer":{"Id":42,"Name":"O''Brien Trading"},
                                 "Lines":[{"ProductId":7,"Qty":2},{"ProductId":9,"Qty":1}]}';

SELECT  OrderId,
        OrderDate,
        CustomerId    = JSON_VALUE(@doc, N'$.Customer.Id'),
        CustomerName  = JSON_VALUE(@doc, N'$.Customer.Name'),
        LineCount     = JSON_QUERY(@doc, N'$.Lines')
FROM    OPENJSON(@doc)
WITH
(
    OrderId     int             N'$.OrderId',
    OrderDate   datetime2(0)    N'$.OrderDate',
    Customer    nvarchar(4000)  N'$.Customer' AS JSON
);
GO

-- The array of objects, with its own schema.
SELECT  j.[key]    AS LineOrdinal,
        p.ProductId,
        p.Qty
FROM    OPENJSON(@doc, N'$.Lines') AS j
CROSS APPLY OPENJSON(j.[value])
     WITH (ProductId int          N'$.ProductId',
            Qty       decimal(9,2) N'$.Qty') AS p
ORDER BY j.[key];
GO

-- Modifying in place. JSON_MODIFY rewrites the whole document; on a large one
-- that is an expensive operation, which is why the pattern of extracting,
-- modifying and rewriting is a loop rather than a statement.
DECLARE @cfg nvarchar(max) = N'{"retries":3,"timeoutSeconds":30,"verbose":false}';

SELECT Modified = JSON_MODIFY(@cfg, N'$.retries', 5);
SELECT Added    = JSON_MODIFY(@cfg, N'$.circuitBreaker.threshold', 10);
SELECT Removed  = JSON_MODIFY(@cfg, N'$.verbose', NULL);
SELECT Appended = JSON_MODIFY(@cfg, N'$.hosts',
                              JSON_QUERY(@cfg, N'$.hosts') + N'["h4.example"]');
GO

-- ISJSON, and the difference between JSON_QUERY and JSON_VALUE. JSON_VALUE
-- returns a scalar and returns NULL for an object or an array; JSON_QUERY
-- returns the object or array and returns NULL for a scalar. Using the wrong
-- one is the most common JSON mistake and it fails silently.
SELECT  ISJSON(@cfg)                        AS IsValidJson,
        ISJSON(@cfg, OBJECT)                AS IsValidObject,
        JSON_VALUE(@cfg, N'$.retries')      AS Scalar,
        JSON_QUERY(@cfg, N'$.retries')      AS NotAnObject,   -- NULL
        JSON_QUERY(@cfg, N'$')              AS WholeDocument;
GO
-- Storing JSON. The decision is whether the document is the storage format or
-- the wire format, and the two have opposite performance characteristics:
-- a document stored in a column can only be searched by a path expression,
-- which is a scan; the same values in typed, indexed columns are seekable.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerDocument
(
    CustomerID  int            NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerDocument PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED,
    Document    nvarchar(max)  COLLATE LATIN1_GENERAL_100_BIN2 NOT NULL,
    Name        nvarchar(200)  NULL,     -- projected, indexed, seekable
    Email       nvarchar(320)  NULL,     -- projected, indexed, seekable
    DocumentHash binary(32)    NULL,     -- equality, for change detection
    UpdatedAt   datetime2(3)   NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_CustomerDocument_UpdatedAt DEFAULT (SYSUTCDATETIME())
);
GO

CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_CustomerDocument_Name
    ON dbo.CustomerDocument (Name) INCLUDE (CustomerID, Email);
GO

-- A JSON index. The engine can index a JSON path through a computed column,
-- which is the only form of indexing a path that performs well; an index on
-- the document column itself is usable only for whole-value equality.
ALTER TABLE dbo.CustomerDocument
    ADD EmailAddress AS CONVERT(nvarchar(320), JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.email')) PERSISTED;
GO

CREATE UNIQUE NONCLUSTERED INDEX UX_CustomerDocument_Email
    ON dbo.CustomerDocument (EmailAddress);
GO

-- The four access patterns, and which of them each design serves.
SELECT  CustomerID, Document
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE   CustomerID = 42;                      -- clustered key: seek

SELECT  CustomerID
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE   EmailAddress = N'billing@obrientrading.example';   -- computed index: seek

SELECT  CustomerID
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE   JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.tier') = N'gold';        -- path predicate: scan

SELECT  CustomerID
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE   Document = @KnownDocument;           -- whole value: the document index
GO

-- Validating what arrives before it is stored. A document that is valid JSON
-- but not valid for the application's shape is the failure that reaches
-- production.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE dbo.usp_ValidateCustomerDocument
    @Document nvarchar(max),
    @Name     nvarchar(200)  OUTPUT,
    @Email    nvarchar(320)  OUTPUT,
    @IsValid  bit            OUTPUT
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    IF ISJSON(@Document) IS NULL OR ISJSON(@Document) <> 1
    BEGIN
        SET @IsValid = 0;
        RAISERROR (N'Document is not valid JSON.', 16, 1);
        RETURN 1;
    END

    SET @Name  = JSON_VALUE(@Document, N'$.name');
    SET @Email = JSON_VALUE(@Document, N'$.email');

    IF @Name IS NULL OR LEN(@Name) = 0
       OR @Email IS NULL
       OR CHARINDEX('@', @Email) = 0
    BEGIN
        SET @IsValid = 0;
        RAISERROR (N'Document is valid JSON but does not match the expected shape.', 16, 1);
        RETURN 1;
    END

    SET @IsValid = 1;
    RETURN 0;
END;
GO

-- A procedure that returns a result set as JSON, which is the shape most
-- modern callers actually consume: one row, one document, one column. The
-- ROWS_PER_BATCH hint is what stops the engine from building the whole
-- result set in memory before serialising it.
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE sales.usp_GetCustomerJson
    @CustomerID int
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name, Email, CreditLimit
    FROM    sales.Customer
    WHERE   CustomerID = @CustomerID
    FOR JSON PATH, INCLUDE_NULL_VALUES, ROWS_PER_BATCH(1);
END;
GO

-- The return type, so that a caller can declare it and a plan can be bound to
-- it. Without an explicit return type the result is nvarchar(max) and the
-- caller has to guess.
DECLARE @Doc nvarchar(max) = N'';

EXEC @Doc = sales.usp_GetCustomerJson @CustomerID = 42;

SELECT Document = @Doc,
       IsValid   = ISJSON(@Doc),
       HasName   = CASE WHEN JSON_VALUE(@Doc, N'$.Name') IS NOT NULL THEN 1 ELSE 0 END;
GO

-- The equivalent for a list, which is a different statement and a different
-- contract: this one is an array, and a consumer that expects an object
-- receives an array.
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION sales.fn_GetActiveCustomersJson()
RETURNS nvarchar(max)
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
BEGIN
    RETURN (SELECT CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name
            FROM   sales.Customer
            WHERE  CreditLimit > 0
            FOR JSON PATH, ROOT(N'customers'));
END;
GO

SELECT ActiveCustomers = sales.fn_GetActiveCustomersJson();
GO

-- The performance shape, measured. A scalar function returning a FOR JSON
-- result is inlined on a compatibility level of 160 or above, which is what
-- makes it usable in a join; on a lower level the function boundary costs a
-- serial plan and the pattern should be a view or a stored procedure.
SELECT  compatibility_level
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   database_id = DB_ID();
GO

SET STATISTICS TIME ON;
GO

SELECT c.CustomerID, c.Name, j.value AS Country
FROM   sales.Customer AS c
CROSS APPLY OPENJSON(sales.fn_GetActiveCustomersJson(), N'$.customers')
     WITH (CustomerID int          N'$.CustomerID',
            Name        nvarchar(200) N'$.Name') AS j
WHERE  c.CustomerID = j.CustomerID;
GO

SET STATISTICS TIME OFF;
GO
-- Normalising, which is the transformation that makes a JSON store into a
-- usable one. One statement projects the document into typed columns, and
-- from that point the document is only a transport format again.
SELECT  CustomerID,
        Name        = JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.name'),
        Email       = JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.email'),
        Tier        = CONVERT(tinyint, TRY_CONVERT(int, JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.tier'))),
        CreditLimit = CONVERT(decimal(18,2), TRY_CONVERT(decimal(18,2), JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.creditLimit'))),
        Addresses   = JSON_QUERY(Document, N'$.addresses')
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument;
GO

-- A path expression that is not a simple name still works, and the cost
-- grows with the depth: each step is another traversal, and none of them is
-- indexed. This is the query shape to avoid in a predicate.
SELECT  CustomerID,
        Country = JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.addresses[0].country'),
        City    = JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.addresses[0].city')
FROM    dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE   JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.addresses[0].country') = N'GB';
GO

-- The comparison that justifies the normalisation, run against a projected
-- column. Note the logical reads: the path predicate is a scan with a parse
-- per row, the projected column is a seek. This is the same query with two
-- predicates over the same data.
SET STATISTICS IO ON;
GO
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) AS ByPath
FROM   dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE  JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.tier') = N'gold';
GO

SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) AS ByProjectedColumn
FROM   dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE  EmailAddress = N'billing@obrientrading.example';
GO
SET STATISTICS IO OFF;
GO

The performance rule for JSON in SQL Server is short: JSON_ in a WH clause is a predicate against a whole document column, and the engine evaluates it row by row after reading the column. On a table of a million documents that is a million parse operations for a query that ought to be a seek. Projecting the searched values into typed columns at write time — computed and persisted, or maintained by the application — converts every one of those predicates into an index seek, and the cost of doing so is one extra write per document. For a document that is genuinely open-ended and genuinely large, that trade is obviously correct; for a document with three known fields, relational columns are better on every axis including clarity.

-- The measurement, because the claim above is a claim about plans. A million-row
-- document table with and without the projected index.
SET STATISTICS IO ON;
SET STATISTICS TIME ON;
GO

-- Without the index: a scan of the table and a parse per row.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE  JSON_VALUE(Document, N'$.tier') = N'gold';
GO

-- With the index: a seek on the persisted computed column, no parse.
SELECT COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM   dbo.CustomerDocument
WHERE  EmailAddress = N'billing@obrientrading.example';
GO

SET STATISTICS IO OFF;
SET STATISTICS TIME OFF;
GO

-- Batch size matters, and only for a large payload. The guidance is to keep a
-- JSON write batch at or below a few megabytes: a batch that is too large
-- holds locks and log for its whole duration, and a payload that is too large
-- for a single row cannot be indexed and requires a driver-specific
-- workaround.
SELECT  MAX(nrow) AS MaxRowsPerBatch,
        CAST(AVG(nrow) AS decimal(18,0)) AS AvgRowsPerBatch
FROM   (SELECT SUM(row_count) AS nrow
        FROM   sys.dm_exec_requests
        GROUP  BY session_id) AS x;
GO
-- XML, which is the other half of the semi-structured story and which is
-- still the more capable of the two. FOR XML has four formats and the choice
-- between them is the whole of the decision.
SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
FOR XML RAW;                     -- one row per element, attribute per column
GO

SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
FOR XML AUTO;                    -- nested by table alias, no root
GO

SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CustomerID = 42
FOR XML PATH('customer', ROOT('customers'), ELEMENTS);  -- element per column
GO

-- The one that is actually used, because it is the only one whose result can
-- be read back. Element-per-column rather than attribute-per-column, a root
-- element, and TYPE so that .value() can be used on the result.
SELECT  @doc = (SELECT CustomerID, CustomerCode, Name, CreditLimit
               FROM   dbo.Customer
               WHERE  CustomerID = 42
               FOR XML PATH(N'customer'), ROOT(N'customers'), TYPE)
               .value('.', N'nvarchar(max)');

SELECT Document = @doc,
       OrderId  = CAST(@doc.query(N'/customers/customer[1]/CustomerID/text()') AS int);
GO

-- Reading it back, with the schema projection that the JSON forms also have.
DECLARE @xdata xml = N'<customers>
                        <customer CustomerID="42" CustomerCode="C-0042" Name="O''Brien Trading Ltd"/>
                        <customer CustomerID="43" CustomerCode="C-0043" Name="Smith Components"/>
                      </customers>';

SELECT  CustomerID   = x.value('@CustomerID', 'int'),
        CustomerCode = x.value('@CustomerCode', 'varchar(20)'),
        Name         = x.value('@Name', 'nvarchar(200)')
FROM    @xdata.nodes('/customers/customer') AS t(x)
ORDER BY CustomerID;
GO

-- The failure that is not a failure. xs:base64Binary decodes a column that
-- XML typed as base64 because the T-SQL value was binary, which is the only
-- case where a plain attribute and an element differ in a way that matters.
DECLARE @bin varbinary(max) = CONVERT(varbinary(max), N'O''Brien Trading Ltd');

SELECT  Decoded = @xdata.value('xs:base64Binary(sql:variable("@bin"))', 'nvarchar(max)');
GO

-- XML indexes, which are the only form of indexing XML content and which
-- require a typed XML column. An untyped nvarchar(max) document cannot have a
-- XML index at all, which is the practical reason to declare the column
-- typed.
CREATE TABLE dbo.PurchaseOrder
(
    PurchaseOrderID int   NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_PurchaseOrder PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED,
    OrderXml        xml   NOT NULL
);
GO

CREATE PRIMARY XML INDEX IX_PurchaseOrder_OrderXml
    ON dbo.PurchaseOrder (OrderXml);
GO

-- A secondary XML index for a specific path, which is what makes a path
-- predicate a seek rather than a scan. FOR XML PATH is a supported option and
-- a clustered index over a path is not.
CREATE XML INDEX IX_PurchaseOrder_OrderId
    ON dbo.PurchaseOrder (OrderXml)
    USING XML INDEX IX_PurchaseOrder_OrderXml
    FOR PATH N'/PurchaseOrder/Order/Id';
GO

SELECT  PurchaseOrderID,
        CustomerId = OrderXml.value('(/PurchaseOrder/Order/CustomerId)[1]', 'int')
FROM    dbo.PurchaseOrder
WHERE   OrderXml.exist('(/PurchaseOrder/Order/OrderDate)[1]') = 1;
GO

-- The size cost. A typed XML column is stored as a binary XML structure, and a
-- primary XML index stores the whole document again in a different form. On a
-- document-heavy table both are significant, which is the reason JSON with
-- projected columns is usually the better answer for a small, known shape.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(p.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(p.object_id) AS TableName,
        p.rows,
        p.reserved_page_count * 8                                      AS ReservedKB,
        p.used_page_count * 8                                          AS UsedKB
FROM    sys.dm_db_partition_stats AS p
WHERE   p.database_id = DB_ID()
  AND   p.index_id IN (1, 2)
  AND   p.index_id <> 1
ORDER BY p.reserved_page_count DESC;
GO

13.5 String aggregation, splitting, spatial and graph

Three small features that solve one problem each and are each misused in a specific way. STRIN and XML fold a column into one value; STRING is the inverse. The spatial types store geometry and geography. Graph tables add edge and vertex semantics to ordinary tables and make a recursive traversal expressible as one statement.

-- String aggregation. STRING_AGG requires a compatibility level of 110 or
-- above and the WITHIN GROUP clause is optional; without it the order is
-- undefined, which is a defect rather than a choice.
SELECT  Status,
        STRING_AGG(CustomerCode, ', ')                                    AS Codes,
        STRING_AGG(CustomerCode, ', ') WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY CustomerCode) AS CodesOrdered
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CreditLimit > 100000
GROUP BY Status;
GO

-- The length limit. STRING_AGG returns nvarchar(max) when the input is
-- nvarchar(max) or nvarchar, and varchar(max) otherwise; if the result is
-- truncated on conversion, the error is 9829 and it is silent about which
-- row lost its data. CAST the input, or aggregate in batches.
SELECT  Name = STUFF((SELECT N', ' + c.CustomerCode
                      FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
                      WHERE  c.Status = N'Active'
                      ORDER BY c.CustomerCode
                      FOR XML PATH(N''), TYPE).value('.', 'nvarchar(max)'), 1, 2, N'');
GO

-- The pre-2017 idiom, still the correct answer when the result must be XML or
-- must be produced on a compatibility level below 110.
SELECT  Status,
        CAST(N'<list>' + REPLACE(
             (SELECT c.CustomerCode
              FROM   dbo.Customer AS c
              WHERE  c.Status = x.Status
              FOR XML PATH(N'code'), TYPE).value('.', 'nvarchar(max)'),
             N' />', N' /></list>') AS xml) AS CodesXml
FROM    dbo.Customer AS x
WHERE   x.Status = N'Active'
GROUP BY x.Status;
GO

-- XMLAGG, which produces XML rather than a delimited string. It is the right
-- tool when the consumer is XML, and the wrong tool for everything else.
SELECT  Status,
        XMLAGG(XMLELEMENT(N'code', CustomerCode)) AS Codes
FROM    dbo.Customer
WHERE   CreditLimit > 100000
GROUP BY Status
FOR XML PATH;
GO

-- Splitting. STRING_SPLIT returns a table with a value and, at compatibility
-- level 130 and above, an ordinal. Without enable_ordinal the order is not
-- guaranteed and the ordinal is absent.
SELECT  value                AS ProductCode,
        CAST(ord AS int)     AS Position
FROM    STRING_SPLIT(N'P-1001,P-1002,P-1003', N',', 1) WITH ORDINALITY AS s(value, ord)
ORDER BY s.ord;
GO

-- The use that is worth knowing: turning a delimited list in a column into
-- rows, and joining it. Without STRING_SPLIT the alternative is a recursive
-- CTE or a cross join to a numbers table; with it, the statement is a join.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerPortfolio
(
    CustomerID    int          NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerPortfolio PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED,
    ProductCodes  varchar(500) NOT NULL,   -- N'P-1001,P-1002,P-1003'
    CONSTRAINT FK_CustomerPortfolio_Customer FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID)
        REFERENCES dbo.Customer (CustomerID)
);
GO

SELECT  cp.CustomerID,
        s.value                AS ProductCode,
        CAST(s.ord AS int)     AS Position
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolio      AS cp
CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
WHERE   s.value IS NOT NULL
  AND   LEN(s.value) > 0
ORDER BY cp.CustomerID, s.ord;
GO

-- Joined to the catalogue, which is the case that matters: the list column
-- becomes rows, and the rows join to a real table.
SELECT  cp.CustomerID,
        p.ProductID,
        p.SKU,
        p.UnitPrice,
        CAST(s.ord AS int) AS Position
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolio      AS cp
CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
JOIN    dbo.Product                AS p  ON p.SKU = s.value
WHERE   s.value IS NOT NULL
  AND   LEN(s.value) > 0
ORDER BY cp.CustomerID, s.ord;
GO

-- And the same values as a real child table, which is what the schema should
-- be. STRING_SPLIT is a reader for a legacy column, not a substitute for the
-- structure.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerPortfolioLine
(
    CustomerID  int         NOT NULL,
    ProductID   int         NOT NULL,
    Position    smallint    NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerPortfolioLine PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (CustomerID, Position),
    CONSTRAINT FK_CustomerPortfolioLine_Customer FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID)
        REFERENCES dbo.Customer (CustomerID),
    CONSTRAINT FK_CustomerPortfolioLine_Product  FOREIGN KEY (ProductID)
        REFERENCES dbo.Product (ProductID)
);
GO

SELECT  cpl.CustomerID, cpl.ProductID, cpl.Position, p.SKU, p.UnitPrice
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolioLine AS cpl
JOIN    dbo.Product                AS p ON p.ProductID = cpl.ProductID
WHERE   cpl.CustomerID = 42
ORDER BY cpl.Position;
GO

-- Populating the child table from the legacy column, which is the migration.
-- ROW_NUMBER supplies the position because WITH ORDINALITY on the split is
-- per row rather than per list, so the ordinal cannot be used directly as the
-- primary key column.
INSERT INTO dbo.CustomerPortfolioLine (CustomerID, ProductID, Position)
SELECT  cp.CustomerID,
        p.ProductID,
        ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY cp.CustomerID ORDER BY s.ord) AS Position
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolio      AS cp
CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
JOIN    dbo.Product                AS p ON p.SKU = s.value
WHERE   s.value IS NOT NULL
  AND   LEN(s.value) > 0;
GO

-- The application is switched to the child table, the child table is verified
-- against the column it replaced, and only then is the column dropped. The
-- verification is a set comparison, which is the only form that can be
-- trusted: a row count matches a truncation.
SELECT  Mismatched = COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM  (SELECT CustomerID, Position FROM dbo.CustomerPortfolioLine
       EXCEPT
       SELECT cp.CustomerID, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY cp.CustomerID ORDER BY s.ord)
       FROM   dbo.CustomerPortfolio AS cp
       CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
       JOIN   dbo.Product AS p ON p.SKU = s.value
       WHERE  s.value IS NOT NULL AND LEN(s.value) > 0) AS d;
GO
-- Spatial types. geography is a coordinate system with a defined Earth
-- surface and a distance in metres; geometry is a coordinate system with no
-- reference and a distance in the units of the data. The choice is not
-- stylistic: STDistance on a geography returns metres, and the same method on
-- a geometry returns whatever the SRID's units are.
DECLARE @London geography =
        geography::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(-0.1278 51.5074)', 4326);
DECLARE @Paris  geography =
        geography::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(2.3522 48.8566)', 4326);

SELECT  DistanceMetres = @London.STDistance(@Paris),
        DistanceKm     = @London.STDistance(@Paris) / 1000.0;
GO

-- A bounding box in geography is lat/long and the order is reversed relative to
-- geometry, which is a frequent source of a query that returns nothing.
DECLARE @Box geography =
        geography::STGeomFromText(N'POLYGON((-0.5 51.2, 0.5 51.2, 0.5 52.2, -0.5 52.2, -0.5 51.2))', 4326);

SELECT  CustomerID, CustomerCode, Location
FROM    dbo.CustomerDepot
WHERE   Location.STArea(@Box) <> 0;      -- geography: does it intersect?
GO

-- The table. A spatial column requires a spatial index for the predicate to be
-- answered from the index rather than by a scan, and the index requires
-- a bounding box and a grid density that the statement computes.
CREATE TABLE dbo.CustomerDepot
(
    DepotID   int         NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_CustomerDepot PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED,
    DepotCode varchar(20) NOT NULL,
    Location  geography   NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT CK_CustomerDepot_Location_SRID CHECK (Location.STSrid = 4326)
);
GO

CREATE SPATIAL INDEX SIX_CustomerDepot_Location
    ON dbo.CustomerDepot (Location)
    USING GEOGRAPHY_AUTO_GRID
    WITH (BOUNDING_BOX = (-10, 45, 20, 60),
          CELLS_PER_OBJECT = 16,
          CELLS_PER_BBOX = 4);
GO

-- Graph tables. SQL Server 2022 added MATCH; it is not a preview feature in
-- 2025, and the syntax is a MATCH clause with a fixed shape.
CREATE TABLE dbo.Supplier
(
    SupplierID   int          NOT NULL CONSTRAINT PK_Supplier PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED,
    SupplierName nvarchar(200) NOT NULL
) AS NODE;
GO
CREATE TABLE dbo.Supplies
(
    SupplierID   int NOT NULL,
    ProductID    int NOT NULL,
    Quantity     int NOT NULL,
    UnitPrice    decimal(9,2) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_Supplies PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (SupplierID, ProductID),
    CONSTRAINT FK_Supplies_Supplier FOREIGN KEY (SupplierID) REFERENCES dbo.Supplier,
    CONSTRAINT FK_Supplies_Product  FOREIGN KEY (ProductID)  REFERENCES dbo.Product
) AS EDGE;
GO

-- One hop.
SELECT  m.SupplierID, s.SupplierName, m.ProductID, m.Quantity
FROM    dbo.Supplies AS m
MATCH (s AS dbo.Supplier)
WHERE   m.ProductID = 7;
GO

-- Two hops, the shortest path between two vertices. The pattern is a node
-- table, an edge table and the MATCH clause; SQL Server does not infer a graph
-- schema, it uses the declared edge tables.
SELECT  lastNode.SupplierName AS PathEnd
FROM    dbo.Supplier AS firstNode
MATCH (shortestPath(
          p AS dbo.Supplier,
          (p)-[dbo.Supplies]->(m)-[dbo.Supplies]->(lastNode)
      ))
WHERE   firstNode.SupplierName = N'Smith Components'
  AND   lastNode.SupplierName   = N'Global Supply Co'
RETURN  lastNode.SupplierName;
GO

-- Graph tables are ordinary tables in every respect: they are in the schema,
-- they are in the backup, they are read by sys.tables, and they can be
-- queried with ordinary T-SQL without MATCH.
SELECT  OBJECT_NAME(object_id) AS TableName, is_graph
FROM    sys.tables
WHERE   is_graph = 1;
GO

-- One more hop, and the shortest path with a cost. QUERY_STATISTICS gives
-- the operator-by-operator row counts, which is how a graph query is
-- explained the same way any other query is.
SELECT  firstNode.SupplierName AS StartSupplier,
        lastNode.SupplierName  AS EndSupplier,
        hop.Source             AS Supplier,
        m1.Quantity            AS Qty1,
        m2.Quantity            AS Qty2,
        m1.UnitPrice + m2.UnitPrice AS PathCost
FROM    dbo.Supplier AS firstNode
MATCH (shortestPath(
          p AS dbo.Supplier,
          (p)-[dbo.Supplies]->(m1)-[dbo.Supplies]->(m2)-[dbo.Supplies]->(lastNode)
      ))
OPTION (QUERY_STATISTICS)
WHERE   firstNode.SupplierName = N'Smith Components'
  AND   lastNode.SupplierName   = N'Global Supply Co'
RETURN  StartSupplier   = firstNode.SupplierName,
          EndSupplier     = lastNode.SupplierName,
          Supplier        = hop.Source,
          PathCost        = m1.UnitPrice + m2.UnitPrice;
GO

-- Where a graph table is the wrong answer. A two-hop join between two
-- indexed tables is a join, and the join is faster. The graph form earns its
-- place at three hops and beyond, or where the depth of the traversal is not
-- known in advance, or where the edge table is the unit of business rather
-- than an implementation detail.
SELECT  s1.SupplierName,
        p.SKU,
        sl.Quantity
FROM    dbo.Supplier     AS s1
JOIN    dbo.Supplies     AS sl ON sl.SupplierID = s1.SupplierID
JOIN    dbo.Product      AS p  ON p.ProductID   = sl.ProductID
WHERE   p.IsActive = 1
  AND   s1.SupplierName = N'Smith Components';
GO

-- Spatial predicates, which is the one place the method-call syntax and the
-- SQL syntax are both in use and mean different things. The Filter method is
-- a bounding-box test that the spatial index can answer; the STContains
-- method is the exact test, applied after the filter.
DECLARE @A geography = geography::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(-0.1278 51.5074)', 4326),
        @B geography = geography::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(-0.1400 51.5050)', 4326);

SELECT  DistanceMetres = @A.STDistance(@B),
        DistanceKm     = @A.STDistance(@B) / 1000.0,
        LongitudeA     = @A.Long,
        LatitudeA      = @A.Lat,
        Srid           = @A.STSrid,
        IsValid        = @A.STIsValid(),
        IsSimple       = @A.STIsSimple();
GO

-- The same distances on a geometry, in the SRID's own units. A geometry with
-- SRID 0 has no unit at all, which is the point of using geography when the
-- data is on the Earth.
DECLARE @Ag geometry = geometry::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(-0.1278 51.5074)', 0),
        @Bg geometry = geometry::STGeomFromText(N'POINT(-0.1400 51.5050)', 0);

SELECT  GeometryDistance = @Ag.STDistance(@Bg),
        GeographyDistance = 0;   -- see the geography form above for the metres
GO

-- A containment test. The method sits on the container and the argument is
-- the candidate, which reads the wrong way round and is a frequent source of
-- a query that returns the inverse of what was intended.
DECLARE @Site geography =
        geography::STGeomFromText(N'POLYGON((-0.15 51.50, -0.10 51.50, -0.10 51.52, -0.15 51.52, -0.15 51.50))', 4326);

SELECT  DepotID,
        DepotCode,
        Inside     = CASE WHEN @Site.STContains(Location) = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END,
        AreaSqMetres = Location.STArea()
FROM    dbo.CustomerDepot
ORDER BY DepotID;
GO

-- The indexed form. Filter is a coarse bounding-box test the spatial index
-- answers from its grid; the exact STContains is then applied only to the
-- candidates, which is why the combination is fast and either alone is not.
SELECT  DepotID, DepotCode
FROM    dbo.CustomerDepot
WHERE   Location.Filter(@Site) = 1
  AND   @Site.STContains(Location) = 1;
GO

-- Evidence that the spatial index is doing anything. A spatial predicate that
-- produces a clustered index scan means there is no spatial index on the
-- column, and the query is scanning.
SELECT  i.name            AS IndexName,
        i.type_desc,
        us.user_seeks,
        us.user_scans,
        us.user_lookups
FROM    sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats AS us
JOIN    sys.indexes                AS i ON i.object_id = us.object_id
                                     AND i.index_id  = us.index_id
WHERE   us.database_id = DB_ID()
  AND   i.type_desc LIKE N'%SPATIAL%'
ORDER BY us.user_seeks DESC;
GO
-- The two spatial index types. GEOGRAPHY_AUTO_GRID lets the engine choose the
-- grid density from the data, which is the right default; GEOGRAPHY_GRID
-- requires the density to be stated, which is the right answer only when a
-- specific density has been measured.
CREATE SPATIAL INDEX SIX_Depot_Location_Auto
    ON dbo.CustomerDepot (Location)
    USING GEOGRAPHY_AUTO_GRID
    WITH (BOUNDING_BOX = (-10, 45, 20, 60));
GO

-- Removing an index, and the requirement that gets in the way of removing a
-- spatial column: a spatial index depends on the column, and the column
-- cannot be altered while the index exists.
DROP INDEX SIX_Depot_Location_Auto ON dbo.CustomerDepot;
GO

-- Aggregation over a spatial column, which is the one case where projection
-- into typed columns is not possible: there is no scalar to project, and the
-- aggregate has to be computed per row.
SELECT  DepotCode,
        Location.STDistance(@Site)  AS DistanceFromSiteMetres,
        CASE WHEN Location.STArea() > 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END AS IsAnArea
FROM    dbo.CustomerDepot
ORDER BY DistanceFromSiteMetres;
GO
-- The graph metadata, and the reason a graph table is a schema decision rather
-- than a query decision: the edge table is the business object, and the graph
-- declaration is how the engine is told it may traverse it that way.
SELECT  t.name                    AS TableName,
        t.is_graph,
        t.is_node,
        t.is_edge,
        t.temporal_type_desc
FROM    sys.tables AS t
WHERE   t.is_graph = 1 OR t.is_node = 1 OR t.is_edge = 1
ORDER BY t.is_graph DESC, t.name;
GO

-- Dangling edges. A NODE table has no foreign key to its EDGE tables, because
-- the graph maintains its own integrity; a delete on a node is a plain delete
-- and the edges are not cascaded, so this query is the check that has to be
-- scheduled if nodes are ever deleted.
SELECT  DanglingEdges = COUNT_BIG(*)
FROM    dbo.Supplies AS e
WHERE   NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.Supplier AS s WHERE s.SupplierID = e.SupplierID);
GO

-- The graph tables in the backup, in the schema, and in the normal catalog
-- views. Nothing about a graph table is special to a backup or a restore, and
-- a design that assumes otherwise will fail on the day of a recovery.
SELECT  OBJECT_NAME(object_id) AS TableName, is_graph, is_ms_shipped
FROM    sys.tables
WHERE   is_ms_shipped = 0
ORDER BY is_graph DESC, TableName;
GO
-- String aggregation, the four ways, and which is correct when. The comparison
-- is the point: three of them are correct and one of them is correct most of
-- the time, and the difference is the ordering guarantee.
SELECT  STRING_AGG(CONVERT(nvarchar(50), p.SKU), ', ')
             WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY p.SKU)                     AS WithOrdering,
    STRING_AGG(CONVERT(nvarchar(50), p.SKU), ', ')             AS WithoutOrdering,
    XMLAGG(XMLELEMENT(N'sku', p.SKU))                         AS AsXmlAgg,
    STUFF((SELECT N', ' + p2.SKU
           FROM   dbo.PurchaseOrderLine AS p2
           WHERE  p2.SalesOrderID = o.SalesOrderID
           ORDER  BY p2.LineNo
           FOR XML PATH(N''), TYPE).value('.', N'nvarchar(max)'), 1, 2, N'') AS AsStuff
FROM    dbo.PurchaseOrderLine AS p
JOIN    dbo.PurchaseOrder     AS o ON o.SalesOrderID = p.SalesOrderID
GROUP BY o.SalesOrderID;
GO

-- The truncation. STRING_AGG returns nvarchar(max) or varchar(max), and a
-- result larger than the target of an INSERT or a variable of a fixed width
-- is either truncated or errors with 9829 depending on the version and the
-- ANSI_WARNINGS setting. The safe form is to know the bound before casting.
DECLARE @skus nvarchar(max);

SELECT @skus = STRING_AGG(CONVERT(nvarchar(50), p.SKU), ', ')
                 WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY p.SKU)
FROM   dbo.PurchaseOrderLine AS p
WHERE  p.SalesOrderID = 1001;

SELECT Length = LEN(@skus),
       Preview = LEFT(@skus, 120);
GO

-- The split, and the three forms: the literal, a column, and the join. The
-- literal form is the one that appears in documentation and the one that
-- should not appear in an application, because a literal produces a plan that
-- is cached once and can never be invalidated by new data.
SELECT  Literal  = s.value, Position = CAST(s.ord AS int)
FROM    STRING_SPLIT(N'P-1001,P-1002,P-1003', N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
ORDER BY s.ord;
GO

SELECT  cp.CustomerID, s.value AS ProductCode, CAST(s.ord AS int) AS Position
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolio      AS cp
CROSS APPLY STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value, ord)
ORDER BY cp.CustomerID, s.ord;
GO

-- The split that matters, which is a filter rather than a join. Splitting in
-- a predicate is the idiomatic use: the table never holds rows, and the
-- delimited column is a search facet.
SELECT  c.CustomerID, c.CustomerCode, c.Name
FROM    dbo.Customer AS c
WHERE   N'P-1007' IN (SELECT s.value FROM STRING_SPLIT(c.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value))
ORDER BY c.CustomerCode;
GO

-- The limit that decides the design: one row per element, so a delimited
-- column crossed with another multi-valued expression multiplies. Counting the
-- elements per row before trusting the data is the check to run on a legacy
-- column, because a single trailing comma produces an empty element that
-- joins to nothing and inflates nothing, and a missing delimiter produces one
-- element that matches nothing at all.
SELECT  cp.CustomerID,
        Elements  = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value)),
        Empty     = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value)
                      WHERE s.value IS NULL OR LEN(s.value) = 0),
        Resolved  = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value)
                      JOIN dbo.Product p ON p.SKU = s.value),
        Unresolved= (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM STRING_SPLIT(cp.ProductCodes, N',', 1) AS s(value)
                      WHERE s.value IS NOT NULL AND LEN(s.value) > 0
                        AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.Product p WHERE p.SKU = s.value))
FROM    dbo.CustomerPortfolio AS cp
ORDER BY cp.CustomerID;
GO

Caveats. STRING returns nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max) values and produces one row per element, so a column holding a delimited list converts one row into many and a join across it multiplies rows. It is a parsing convenience, not a relational structure, and the durable answer is a child table. geog and geom are opaque: they are not comparable with ==, they cannot be indexed without a spatial index, and arithmetic on them is a method call rather than an operator. A spatial column with no spatial index means a table scan per query, which is worse than not having the column. Graph tables add a MA clause and a traversal cost; for a two-hop join between two indexed tables, a plain JO is faster, and the graph form earns its place at three hops and beyond or where the shape of the query is not known in advance.

13.6 Instance configuration, memory-optimised tables and the runbook

The last section is the administrative one: how to read and change instance-wide settings, how to find out what is on an instance without guessing, how memory-optimised tables differ from disk-based ones, and what a recurring maintenance run should actually contain.

-- sp_configure, the whole surface, and the two categories of change. A setting
-- with a value of 0 in value_in_use needs a restart to take effect; the
-- dynamic ones take effect on RECONFIGURE.
SELECT  name,
        CAST(value        AS int) AS Configured,
        CAST(value_in_use AS int) AS InUse,
        description
FROM    sys.configurations
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- The dynamic ones, which is the short list worth memorising.
SELECT  name, CAST(value AS int) AS Value, description
FROM    sys.configurations
WHERE   is_dynamic = 1
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- The classic, and the two ways to write it. RECONFIGURE with the WITH
-- RECURSIVE_OVERRIDE option is required when a setting is in a state that
-- would otherwise be rejected.
EXEC sys.sp_configure N'max server memory (MB)', 24576;
RECONFIGURE WITH OVERRIDE;
GO

EXEC master.dbo.sp_serveroption @name = N'full text search', @value = 1;
GO

-- Reading what an object actually is. sp_helpdb and sp_help give the
-- metadata; the catalog views give the same information in a form that can
-- be joined and filtered, and are what a script should use.
EXEC sys.sp_helpdb N'SalesDB';
GO

EXEC sys.sp_help N'dbo.Customer';
GO

EXEC sys.sp_helptext N'dbo.usp_OrderSummary';
GO

-- The catalog equivalent, and the reason to prefer it.
SELECT  c.name                          AS ColumnName,
        ty.name                         AS DataType,
        c.max_length,
        c.precision,
        c.scale,
        c.is_nullable,
        c.is_identity,
        c.is_computed,
        dc.definition                   AS DefaultDefinition,
        dc.name                         AS DefaultName
FROM    sys.columns        AS c
JOIN    sys.types          AS ty ON ty.user_type_id = c.user_type_id
LEFT JOIN sys.default_constraints AS dc ON dc.parent_object_id = c.object_id
                                      AND dc.parent_column_id = c.column_id
WHERE   c.object_id = OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.Customer')
ORDER BY c.column_id;
GO

-- Foreign keys, with the index state of the referencing column. An
-- unindexed foreign key is the single most common cause of a modification
-- that locks more than it should, and it is invisible in the schema.
SELECT  fk.name                                     AS ForeignKeyName,
        OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(fk.parent_object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(fk.parent_object_id) AS ChildTable,
        OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(fk.referenced_object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(fk.referenced_object_id) AS ParentTable,
        fk.is_disabled,
        fk.is_not_trusted,
        c.name                                       AS ChildColumn,
        ic.is_primary_key,
        CASE WHEN ix.index_id IS NULL THEN 0 ELSE 1 END AS ChildColumnIndexed
FROM    sys.foreign_keys         AS fk
JOIN    sys.foreign_key_columns  AS fkc ON fkc.constraint_object_id = fk.object_id
JOIN    sys.columns              AS c   ON c.object_id = fkc.parent_object_id
                                      AND c.column_id = fkc.parent_column_id
LEFT JOIN sys.index_columns     AS ic  ON ic.object_id = fkc.parent_object_id
                                      AND ic.column_id = fkc.parent_column_id
                                      AND ic.key_ordinal > 0
LEFT JOIN sys.indexes           AS ix  ON ix.object_id = ic.object_id
                                      AND ix.index_id  = ic.index_id
WHERE   fk.parent_object_id = OBJECT_ID(N'dbo.SalesOrder')
ORDER BY fk.name, ic.key_ordinal;
GO
-- Memory-optimised tables. Two durability modes, and the difference is
-- whether a restart loses data.
--
--   DURABILITY = SCHEMA_AND_DATA   data is written to a persisted on-disk
--                                  log-file container; survives a restart and
--                                  a crash
--   DURABILITY = SCHEMA_ONLY       data is in memory only; a restart empties
--                                  the table
--
-- The table is created in a memory-optimized container, which is a filegroup
-- with the MEMORY_OPTIMIZED clause. Every memory-optimized table requires
-- ALTER DATABASE ... SET MEMORY_OPTIMIZED_ELEVATE_TO_SNAPSHOT = ON, because
-- it has no locking and reads do not block writes.
CREATE MEMORY_OPTIMIZED FILEGROUP [FG_InMemoryData]
    CONTAINS MEMORY_OPTIMIZED COLUMNSTORE;
GO

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT ADD FILEGROUP [FG_InMemoryData];
GO

CREATE TABLE dbo.SessionCache
(
    SessionKey    nvarchar(200)  NOT NULL,
    Payload       varbinary(max) NULL,
    CreatedAt     datetime2(3)   NOT NULL CONSTRAINT DF_SessionCache_CreatedAt DEFAULT (SYSUTCDATETIME()),
    ExpiresAt     datetime2(3)   NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_SessionCache PRIMARY KEY NONCLUSTERED HASH (SessionKey)
        WITH (BUCKET_COUNT = 4096)
) WITH (DURABILITY = SCHEMA_ONLY, MEMORY_OPTIMIZED = FORCED);
GO

CREATE TABLE dbo.OrderEvent
(
    OrderEventID  bigint IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    SalesOrderID  int          NOT NULL,
    EventType     tinyint      NOT NULL,
    OccurredAt    datetime2(3) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT PK_OrderEvent PRIMARY KEY NONCLUSTERED CLUSTERED (OrderEventID),
    CONSTRAINT IX_OrderEvent_Order NONCLUSTERED HASH (SalesOrderID)
        WITH (BUCKET_COUNT = 8192)
) WITH (DURABILITY = SCHEMA_AND_DATA, MEMORY_OPTIMIZED = FORCED);
GO

-- The rules, which are restrictions and not preferences.
--
--   * No foreign keys, no CHECK constraints, no triggers
--   * No LOB columns in a disk-based index; nvarchar(max) and
--     varbinary(max) are allowed only as the last column of a
--     nonclustered index, and cannot be indexed in a hash index
--   * All indexes are nonclustered; the primary key is enforced as a
--     nonclustered hash or a nonclustered clustered index
--   * A snapshot isolation setting is required, because there is no
--     locking to speak of
--   * A memory-optimized table cannot use a table variable, a cursor or
--     a result set returned to a client from an UPDATE
--   * MERGE, OUTPUT INTO and most DDL behave differently
--   * LOB data is stored off-row in a default heap table, so a
--     transaction touching an LOB is not fully in-memory and does not
--     get the same behaviour
--
-- The reason to use one at all: a fully memory-resident table has no
-- log for its data, so a write commits without a disk round trip. That is
-- worth having for a lookup table that is written rarely and read
-- constantly, and it is not worth the restrictions for anything else.

ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET MEMORY_OPTIMIZED_ELEVATE_TO_SNAPSHOT = ON;
GO

-- The compromise that is usually the right answer: a disk-based table with a
-- memory-optimized index, keeping the familiar model for the table and the
-- in-memory representation for the read path.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_OrderEvent_InMemory
    ON dbo.OrderEvent (SalesOrderID, OccurredAt)
    WITH (MEMORY_OPTIMIZED = ON);
GO

-- The containers, and how much of the log-file container is in use. The
-- rest is preallocated and is why the file does not shrink.
SELECT  cc.name                             AS ContainerName,
        cc.type_desc,
        cc.total_bytes / 1048576            AS TotalMB,
        cc.used_bytes  / 1048576            AS UsedMB,
        CAST(100.0 * cc.used_bytes / NULLIF(cc.total_bytes, 0) AS decimal(5,1)) AS UsedPct
FROM    sys.dm_db_containers AS cc
ORDER BY cc.total_bytes DESC;
GO

-- The memory-optimized table sizes, which are a different figure from the
-- row count in sys.dm_db_partition_stats.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(t.object_id) + '.' + OBJECT_NAME(t.object_id) AS TableName,
        t.is_memory_optimized,
        p.rows,
        p.reserved_page_count * 8 AS SizeKB
FROM    sys.tables      AS t
JOIN    sys.dm_db_partition_stats AS p ON p.object_id = t.object_id
                                       AND p.index_id = 1
WHERE   t.is_memory_optimized = 1
ORDER BY SizeKB DESC;
GO
CadenceTaskCommand or queryFailure to watch for
HourlyLog backup in FU recoveryBACKUP LOGBACKUP LOG with NOCHEC, then RESTORE VERIFYONLYRESTORE VERIFYONLYLong-running transaction blocking truncation; verifywith log_reuse_wait_desc = NOTHINGlog_reuse_wait_desc = NOTHING
DailyLog backup of a SIM database before a detach or file moveBACKUP LOGBACKUP LOG once, then the file operationThe log has never been backed up and the file cannot be moved; error 3445
DailyFull backup and verificationBACKUP DATABASEBACKUP DATABASE with CHEC, then RESTORE VERIFYONLYRESTORE VERIFYONLYThe backup succeeds and the verification is not run, because the job reports only the first step
WeeklyDifferential backupBACKUP DATABASE ... WITH DIFFERENTIALBACKUP DATABASE ... WITH DIFFERENTIALThe differential file is written but the differential base moved, so it is orphaned

WeeklyIndex maintenance, gated on size and fragmentationThe procedure from 11.5Unconditional rebuilds filling the log and causing the outage they were meant to prevent
MonthlyRestore drill to a scratch databaseFull restore plus MO plus a business-level row countA backup that has never been restored; the drill is what converts a backup into a recovery plan
MonthlyPatch level reviewCompare SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion') against the released CU and GDR listInstalling a CU without reading the release notes for the fixes it removes, or skipping a GDR entirely
QuarterlyConfiguration baseline comparisonExport sys.confi and sys.da settings and diff against the recorded baselineDrift that nobody notices until an incident, by which time the instance is not the one the runbook describes

-- The baseline capture, so that drift is detectable. Run on a schedule, store
-- the output, and diff it. Everything in the instance's configuration is
-- in these two views.
SELECT  @@SERVERNAME                             AS ServerName,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')        AS ProductVersion,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductLevel')          AS ProductLevel,
        SERVERPROPERTY('Edition')               AS Edition,
        SERVERPROPERTY('MachineName')           AS MachineName,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMajorVersion')   AS MajorVersion,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMinorVersion')   AS MinorVersion,
        SERVERPROPERTY('EditionID')             AS EditionId,
        GETUTCDATE()                            AS CapturedUtc;
GO

SELECT  name,
        CAST(value        AS nvarchar(200)) AS Configured,
        CAST(value_in_use AS nvarchar(200)) AS InUse
FROM    sys.configurations
ORDER BY name;
GO

SELECT  name,
        recovery_model_desc,
        compatibility_level,
        is_auto_create_stats_on,
        is_auto_update_stats_on,
        is_read_committed_snapshot_on,
        is_allow_snapshot_isolation_on,
        is_ansi_nulls_on,
        is_ansi_padding_on,
        is_ansi_warnings_on,
        is_arithabort_on,
        is_concat_null_yields_null_on,
        is_numeric_roundabort_on,
        is_quoted_identifier_on,
        page_verify_option_desc,
        collation_name
FROM    sys.databases
ORDER BY name;
GO

-- The patch level, against what is deployed. This is the query to run before
-- an upgrade window opens, and it is also the query that shows which instance
-- in the estate has drifted.
SELECT  @@SERVERNAME                             AS InstanceName,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')        AS ProductVersion,   -- 17.0.xxxx.x
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductLevel')          AS CumulativeUpdateLevel,
        SERVERPROPERTY('ProductUpdateReference') AS UpdateReference,
        SERVERPROPERTY('Edition')               AS Edition,
        SERVERPROPERTY('MachineName')           AS MachineName,
        CASE CAST(SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMajorVersion') AS int)
            WHEN 17 THEN N'SQL Server 2025'
            WHEN 16 THEN N'SQL Server 2022'
            WHEN 15 THEN N'SQL Server 2019'
            WHEN 14 THEN N'SQL Server 2017'
            ELSE        N'Unknown or unsupported'
        END                                     AS MajorRelease,
        sqlserver_start_time                     AS InstanceStartTime
FROM    sys.dm_os_sys_info;
GO

-- Confirm the state of a maintenance job rather than trusting that it ran.
-- last_run_outcome is 0 for success and non-zero for the job step that
-- failed; a job that has never run reports 0 as well, which is why
-- last_run_date is selected alongside it.
SELECT  j.name                AS JobName,
        j.enabled,
        js.step_id,
        js.step_name,
        js.last_run_date,
        js.last_run_time,
        js.last_run_outcome,
        js.next_run_date,
        js.last_run_message
FROM    msdb.dbo.sysjobs      AS j
JOIN    msdb.dbo.sysjobsteps  AS js ON js.job_id = j.job_id
ORDER BY j.name, js.step_id;
GO

-- The outcome of the last run of each job, which is the form a monitoring
-- query wants: one row per job with a single pass or fail.
SELECT  j.name                                              AS JobName,
        MAX(h.run_date)                                     AS LastRunDate,
        MAX(CASE WHEN h.run_status = 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)    AS LastRunSucceeded,
        MAX(CASE WHEN h.run_status <> 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS LastRunFailed,
        (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM msdb.dbo.sysjobhistory x
         WHERE  x.job_id = j.job_id AND x.run_status <> 0)  AS FailedRunsAllTime
FROM    msdb.dbo.sysjobs       AS j
LEFT JOIN msdb.dbo.sysjobhistory AS h ON h.job_id = j.job_id
                                  AND h.instance_id = SERVERPROPERTY('InstanceName')
GROUP BY j.name, j.job_id
ORDER BY j.name;
GO
-- The trace flags that are set on the instance, which is the other half of
-- the configuration baseline. Trace flags are global, invisible in every
-- application, and persist across restarts if they were set at startup, so
-- they are the drift most likely to go unrecorded.
DBCC TRACESTATUS(-1);
GO

-- The session-scoped options, which a module can set for its own duration and
-- which a poorly written module sets for the caller. SESSIONPROPERTY reads
-- them as a scalar and is the only way to see what a module actually did.
SELECT  SESSIONPROPERTY('ANSI_WARNINGS')           AS AnsiWarnings,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('ANSI_PADDING')            AS AnsiPadding,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('ANSI_NULLS')              AS AnsiNulls,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('ARITHABORT')              AS ArithAbort,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('CONCAT_NULL_YIELDS_NULL') AS ConcatNullYieldsNull,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('NUMERIC_ROUNDABORT')      AS NumericRoundAbort,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('QUOTED_IDENTIFIER')       AS QuotedIdentifier,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('DEADLOCK_PRIORITY')       AS DeadlockPriority,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('LOCK_TIMEOUT')            AS LockTimeout,
        SESSIONPROPERTY('TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL') AS IsolationLevel;
GO

-- The compatibility level, which is what actually governs the optimiser. A
-- database is left at its previous level by an upgrade and raised separately,
-- so the first performance regression after an upgrade is usually the
-- compatibility level and not the version. Query Store is the tool for
-- comparing the two without changing anything.
SELECT  name, compatibility_level, compatibility_level_desc, collation_name
FROM    sys.databases
WHERE   database_id = DB_ID();
GO

-- Every plan Query Store holds for every query, with its measured cost. This
-- is the record that says whether a change helped, and it survives restarts,
-- which is the property that makes it the baseline to use in production.
SELECT  q.query_id,
        LEFT(REPLACE(REPLACE(q.query_text, CHAR(13), ' '), CHAR(10), ' '), 120) AS QueryText,
        p.plan_id,
        p.is_current_plan,
        p.is_forced_plan,
        ISNULL(rs.count_executions, 0)                                   AS Executions,
        CAST(ISNULL(rs.avg_duration, 0)         / 1000 AS decimal(18,2))  AS AvgDurationMs,
        CAST(ISNULL(rs.avg_cpu_time, 0)         / 1000 AS decimal(18,2))  AS AvgCpuMs,
        CAST(ISNULL(rs.avg_logical_io_reads, 0)         AS decimal(18,1))  AS AvgLogicalReads
FROM    sys.query_store_query            AS q
JOIN    sys.query_store_plan             AS p  ON p.query_id = q.query_id
LEFT JOIN sys.query_store_runtime_stats AS rs ON rs.plan_id = p.plan_id
ORDER BY rs.avg_duration DESC;
GO

-- The regression Query Store exists to find: one query, more than one plan
-- held, and the current plan costing more than the one it replaced.
SELECT  q.query_id,
        COUNT(DISTINCT p.plan_id)                                   AS PlansHeld,
        MAX(CASE WHEN p.is_forced_plan = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)        AS HasForcedPlan,
        CAST(MAX(ISNULL(rs.avg_duration, 0)) / 1000 AS decimal(18,2)) AS WorstAvgMs
FROM    sys.query_store_query            AS q
JOIN    sys.query_store_plan             AS p  ON p.query_id = q.query_id
LEFT JOIN sys.query_store_runtime_stats AS rs ON rs.plan_id = p.plan_id
GROUP BY q.query_id
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT p.plan_id) > 1
ORDER BY WorstAvgMs DESC;
GO

-- Forcing a plan, which is the safest mitigation available because it is
-- reversible and it requires no change to the source. It is a holding action,
-- not a fix, and it should be recorded with the date on which it is revisited.
EXEC sys.sp_query_store_force_plan @query_id = 42, @plan_id = 137;
GO
EXEC sys.sp_query_store_unforce_plan @query_id = 42;
GO

-- The settings that decide how long Query Store remembers and whether it
-- intervenes. These are database scoped, so the same query behaves differently
-- in two databases on the same instance.
SELECT  name, value, value_for_secondary, description
FROM    sys.database_scoped_configurations
WHERE   name LIKE N'QUERY_STORE%'
   OR   name IN (N'LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION',
                 N'LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION = OFF',
                 N'PARAMETER_SNIFFING',
                 N'PARAMETER_SNIFFING = OFF',
                 N'QUERY_OPTIMIZER_HOTFIXES',
                 N'QUERY_OPTIMIZER_HOTFIXES = ON')
ORDER BY name;
GO
-- The upgrade rehearsal, in the order it has to be run. Each step produces
-- evidence; none of them is optional, and the sequence is the same whichever
-- approach is chosen.
--
--   1. Take a full backup of the source, and restore it to a second instance.
--      The rehearsal is performed against the restored copy, never against
--      production.
--   2. Confirm the restored copy's recovery model, compatibility level and
--      configuration match the source. A rehearsal against a copy that
--      differs is a rehearsal of a different system.
--   3. Run the pre-upgrade inventory from A.6 and record every feature
--      reported as in use.
--   4. Check each in-use feature against the 2025 release notes for the four
--      breaking-change areas.
--   5. Upgrade the copy. Record the elapsed time and any warning messages:
--      they are the first evidence of a behavioural change.
--   6. Set the compatibility level to 170 in a separate step, not as part of
--      the upgrade, so that the optimiser change is isolated and reversible.
--   7. Run the application test suite against the upgraded copy, and then the
--      performance baseline from 11.1 for the statements that matter.
--   8. Restore the backup chain, not just the database, to prove that the
--      backups taken before the upgrade are still restorable afterwards.

-- Steps 2 and 3, as statements.
SELECT  SourceInstance = @@SERVERNAME,
        ProductVersion = CAST(SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion') AS nvarchar(50)),
        ProductLevel   = CAST(SERVERPROPERTY('ProductLevel') AS nvarchar(50)),
        RecoveryModel  = DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Recovery'),
        CompatLevel    = (SELECT compatibility_level FROM sys.databases WHERE database_id = DB_ID()),
        InUseByPolicy   = (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sys.databases
                            WHERE database_id > 4 AND is_read_only = 0);
GO

-- Step 8, in the form that is easiest to get wrong: a restore onto a fresh
-- instance, with the paths moved, from the backups that existed before the
-- upgrade. If this fails, the upgrade has destroyed the ability to recover to
-- a point in time before it happened.
RESTORE DATABASE [SalesDB_postupgrade_check]
FROM DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_full_20260927.bak',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0130.trn',
     DISK = N'D:\Backup\SalesDB_log_20260928_0145.trn'
WITH
    MOVE N'SalesDB'     TO N'D:\Restore\PostUpgrade.mdf',
    MOVE N'SalesDB_log' TO N'D:\Restore\PostUpgrade_log.ldf',
    REPLACE,
    RECOVERY,
    CHECKSUM,
    STATS = 10;
GO

-- The comparison that decides whether the upgrade is accepted. Elapsed time
-- and row count from the pre-upgrade copy, from the post-upgrade copy, and
-- the difference. A restore that succeeds and returns different data is worse
-- than a restore that fails.
SELECT  CheckName = N'SalesOrder row count',
        Before    = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder),
        After     = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM SalesDB_postupgrade_check.dbo.SalesOrder),
        Difference = (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM dbo.SalesOrder)
                    - (SELECT COUNT_BIG(*) FROM SalesDB_postupgrade_check.dbo.SalesOrder)
UNION ALL
SELECT N'SalesOrder total value',
       CAST((SELECT SUM(TotalAmount) FROM dbo.SalesOrder) AS decimal(18,2)),
       CAST((SELECT SUM(TotalAmount) FROM SalesDB_postupgrade_check.dbo.SalesOrder) AS decimal(18,2)),
       CAST((SELECT SUM(TotalAmount) FROM dbo.SalesOrder)
          - (SELECT SUM(TotalAmount) FROM SalesDB_postupgrade_check.dbo.SalesOrder) AS decimal(18,2));
GO

-- Cleaning up the rehearsal, which is the step that is skipped and the reason
-- the next rehearsal is run on a machine that has no room for it.
ALTER DATABASE [SalesDB_postupgrade_check] SET SINGLE_USER WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
DROP DATABASE [SalesDB_postupgrade_check];
GO

-- The go-live checklist, which is the rehearsal's output rather than the
-- upgrade's. Each line is something that has to have been observed, not
-- something that has to be believed.
SELECT  Item = N'Every in-use feature checked against the 2025 release notes',
        Verified = 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Backup chain restored from the pre-upgrade backups', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Compatibility level raised in a separate, reversible step', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Application test suite passed against the upgraded copy', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Performance baseline re-measured and compared', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Rollback procedure rehearsed, not just written', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Maintenance window sized from the rehearsal, not from the last upgrade', 1
UNION ALL SELECT N'Baseline configuration captured before and after', 1;
GO

Requirements and caveats for the administration checklist. Every task in the table above needs a login that is a member of sysa on the instance, and every task needs to have been tested against a restore of production, not against the development database. The restore drill in particular must run against a copy: it is destructive, it needs disk space equal to the database, and it is the only task on the list that can prove the other nine produced something usable. The patch level review needs a subscription to the release notes rather than an assumption that the latest build is the latest approved build, and for SQL Server 2025 the reference points are the GA build 17.0.1000.7 of 18 November 2025 and the cumulative updates since, each of which fixes things and changes behaviour in documented ways.

Exercises 13

  1. Create dbo.Cu as a system-versioned temporal table with an explicit history table, then perform five updates and one delete. Query the current table and the history for one customer with each of the four FOR SYSTEM_TIMEFOR SYSTEM_TIME forms and record what each returns.
  2. Turn versioning off, drop the period, and turn it on again. Record the exact error from each of the three operations, and state which of them is only possible because the table was created with versioning rather than converted after the fact.
  3. Populate 100,000 history rows and run the trim procedure from 13.1 with a retention of one day. Record the rows deleted and confirm that the current row and its most recent history row are both still present. Explain what would break if the delete were not bounded by batch size.
  4. Enable Change Tracking on dbo.Cu without the tracking column, attempt a CHANG read, and record the error. Add the column, enable the table, and repeat the read after a modification.
  5. Enable Change Data Capture on dbo.Sal in the same database. Query cdc.captu, perform an insert, an update and a delete, and read all three from cdc.fn_cdc_ge with the mode all update oldall update old. Repeat with fn_cdc_get_ and explain the difference in the rows returned.
  6. Stop the SQL Server Agent, perform a large insert into a CDC-enabled table, and watch log_reuse_wait_desclog_reuse_wait_desc. Restart the Agent, run the capture and cleanup jobs, and record the value before and after.
  7. Create a queue, a contract, a message type and a service, send three messages in one transaction, and receive them with a single RECEIVE TOP (3)RECEIVE TOP (3). Record the order they arrive in, and explain why the order is guaranteed.
  8. Add a message to a conversation with no route to its destination, then read the outgoing queue with error_ and error_texterror_text. Record the error and explain what the service owner must configure.
  9. Set clr enabledclr enabled to 1, register a strong-name key with sp_clr_, and load a signed assembly. Record the value of clr strict securityclr strict security before and after, and explain what an unsigned assembly would be prevented from doing.
  10. Create a table with a te column and one with nvarchar(max)nvarchar(max), insert the same value into both, and compare L, DATAL and indexability. Then migrate the te column and record the statements used.
  11. Aggregate a column with STRIN with and without WITHIN GROUPWITHIN GROUP, and separately with XMLAGGXMLAGG. Record the output of each and state which is deterministic and which can produce XML that a consumer must parse.
  12. Create a geog column, load three points, add a spatial index, and run a bounding-box query with and without the index. Record the logical reads from each and explain the difference. Repeat with geom and the same coordinates and record the STDis result from each.
  13. Create a graph with two node tables and one edge table, run a one-hop and a two-hop MA, then run the equivalent join without MA and compare the elapsed time. State the hop count at which the graph form becomes the better choice and why.
  14. Query sys.confi for the settings that are not dynamic, record which ones are in a non-default state, and state which of them require a restart. Then query sys.da for every database’s compatibility level and list any that are not current.
  15. Create a memory-optimized table with DURABILITY = SCHEMA_ONLYDURABILITY = SCHEMA_ONLY and one with SCHEMA_, insert into both, restart the instance, and record what survived in each. Then add a MEMORY_OPTIMIZED = ONMEMORY_OPTIMIZED = ON nonclustered index to a disk-based table and compare its query time against the same query on a disk-based index.
  16. Build the maintenance runbook from the table in 13.6 as SQL Agent jobs on a test instance, run the full cycle twice, and record the elapsed time, the log space consumed and the success or failure of each job. Identify which of the tasks could be moved to a maintenance window and which cannot.

Appendix — Syntax, Catalog, Errors and Upgrade

A.1 T-SQL syntax quick referenceA.2 The DMVs and system views worth knowingA.3 Error numbers and what they meanA.4 Isolation levels and lock compatibilityA.5 The DBCC command referenceA.6 The 2025 feature matrix, upgrade guidance and documentation

Six reference sections. The first five are the material that is looked up rather than read, and the last is the state of the product as of the version this guide describes, separated into what is production-ready and what is not. Nothing here is new material; it is the same content in the form in which it is used.

A.1 T-SQL syntax quick reference

The statements a schema or operations script is most likely to need, with the clauses that are mandatory in one, deprecated in another, or restricted by edition.

StatementFormNotes
Create a tableCREATE TABLE [schema.]name (column type [NOT NULL] [DEFAULT expr] [IDENTITY[(seed,inc)]], ..., CONSTRAINT name PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (...), CONSTRAINT name FOREIGN KEY (...) REFERENCES ..., CHECK (...), UNIQUE (...), INDEX ... ) [WITH (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON ...)]CREATE TABLE [schema.]name (column type [NOT NULL] [DEFAULT expr] [IDENTITY[(seed,inc)]], ..., CONSTRAINT name PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (...), CONSTRAINT name FOREIGN KEY (...) REFERENCES ..., CHECK (...), UNIQUE (...), INDEX ... ) [WITH (SYSTEM_VERSIONING = ON ...)]IDEN is not round-trip safe; use SEQU where a gap is unacceptable
Alter a columnALTER TABLE t ALTER COLUMN c type [NULL | NOT NULL] [WITH (...)]ALTER TABLE t ALTER COLUMN c type [NULL | NOT NULL] [WITH (...)]Widening nvar, var and varb is a metadata-only change on supported editions. Any other change rewrites every row and is blocked by an indexed view, a computed column or a statistics object that depends on it
Idempotent createCREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE | VIEW | FUNCTION | TRIGGER ...CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE | VIEW | FUNCTION | TRIGGER ...Requires 2016 SP1 or later. Must be the first statement in its batch
Add a column with a defaultALTER TABLE t ADD c int NOT NULL CONSTRAINT df DEFAULT (0) WITH VALUESALTER TABLE t ADD c int NOT NULL CONSTRAINT df DEFAULT (0) WITH VALUESWITH VALUESWITH VALUES is required to backfill existing rows; without it a NOT NULLNOT NULL column added to a non-empty table fails
Drop safelyDROP TABLE IF EXISTS [schema.]nameDROP TABLE IF EXISTS [schema.]name2016 SP1 or later; the IF EXISTSIF EXISTS is the point
Create a sequenceCREATE SEQUENCE s AS bigint START WITH 1 INCREMENT BY 1 [CACHE 50] [NO CYCLE | CYCLE]CREATE SEQUENCE s AS bigint START WITH 1 INCREMENT BY 1 [CACHE 50] [NO CYCLE | CYCLE]CA without NO CYCLENO CYCLE is the default and loses up to the cache size on a crash
PartitionCREATE PARTITION FUNCTION pf (int) AS RANGE RIGHT FOR VALUES (...); CREATE PARTITION SCHEME ps AS PARTITION pf ALL TO ([FG1],[FG2]);CREATE PARTITION FUNCTION pf (int) AS RANGE RIGHT FOR VALUES (...); CREATE PARTITION SCHEME ps AS PARTITION pf ALL TO ([FG1],[FG2]);Switching a partition in and out is a metadata operation; the data must already be range-aligned

Use it for a small set of rows, not a set-based load

-- The deployment skeleton: idempotent, ordered, and separated into batches
-- by the object it creates. This is the shape of every deployment script.
-- GO
SET ANSI_NULLS ON;
GO
SET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ON;
GO
SET XACT_ABORT ON;
GO

-- 1. Schema
IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.schemas WHERE name = N'sales')
    EXEC(N'CREATE SCHEMA sales AUTHORIZATION dbo;');
GO

-- 2. Tables, created only if absent so that a partial deployment is resumable
IF OBJECT_ID(N'sales.Customer', N'U') IS NULL
BEGIN
    CREATE TABLE sales.Customer
    (
        CustomerID   int            IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
        CustomerCode varchar(20)    NOT NULL,
        Name         nvarchar(200)  NOT NULL,
        Email        nvarchar(320)  NULL,
        CreditLimit  decimal(18,2)  NOT NULL,
        RowVer       rowversion     NOT NULL,
        CONSTRAINT PK_sales_Customer PRIMARY KEY CLUSTERED (CustomerID),
        CONSTRAINT UQ_sales_Customer_Code UNIQUE (CustomerCode),
        CONSTRAINT CK_sales_Customer_Credit CHECK (CreditLimit >= 0)
    );
END;
GO

-- 3. Objects, idempotent by construction
CREATE OR ALTER PROCEDURE sales.usp_GetCustomer
    @CustomerID int
AS
BEGIN
    SET NOCOUNT ON;

    SELECT  c.CustomerID, c.CustomerCode, c.Name, c.Email, c.CreditLimit
    FROM    sales.Customer AS c
    WHERE   c.CustomerID = @CustomerID;
END;
GO

-- 4. Grants, applied every time so that a new environment gets them
IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.database_principals WHERE name = N'app_reader'
                                   AND type = 'R')
    CREATE ROLE app_reader;
GO

GRANT SELECT ON sales.Customer TO app_reader;
GRANT EXECUTE ON sales.usp_GetCustomer TO app_reader;
GO

-- 5. Statistics, because a freshly loaded table has none that describe it
EXEC sys.sp_update_stats N'sales.Customer';
GO

-- 6. Invalidate anything that referenced the changed objects
EXEC sys.sp_recompile N'sales.usp_GetCustomer';
GO
-- The verification step, which is the only part of a deployment script that
-- produces evidence rather than intentions. Run it after every deployment and
-- read the output.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(o.object_id) + '.' + o.name AS ObjectName,
        o.type_desc,
        o.create_date,
        o.modify_date,
        ISNULL(i.IndexCount, 0)                      AS IndexCount
FROM    sys.objects AS o
LEFT JOIN (SELECT object_id, COUNT_BIG(*) AS IndexCount
           FROM   sys.indexes
           WHERE  index_id > 0
           GROUP BY object_id) AS i ON i.object_id = o.object_id
WHERE   o.schema_id = SCHEMA_ID(N'sales')
  AND   o.is_ms_shipped = 0
ORDER BY o.type_desc, ObjectName;
GO

-- The membership chain, which is what a permission check resolves through. A
-- principal with no direct grant on a table can still have one through a role,
-- which is why granting to a role and granting directly are not the same
-- statement and produce different audit output.
SELECT  m.name     AS MemberName,
        m.type_desc AS MemberType,
        r.name     AS RoleName,
        r.type_desc AS RoleType
FROM    sys.database_role_members AS drm
JOIN    sys.database_principals   AS m ON m.principal_id = drm.member_principal_id
JOIN    sys.database_principals   AS r ON r.principal_id = drm.role_principal_id
ORDER BY r.name, m.name;
GO

-- The effective permissions, which is the answer rather than the grants. A
-- DENY is reported here and is invisible in the grant list.
SELECT  permission_name, state_desc, permission_source
FROM    sys.fn_my_permissions(N'sales.Customer', N'OBJECT')
ORDER BY state_desc, permission_name;
GO
-- The remaining DDL forms, as one block. Each of these has a clause that is
-- required in one context and deprecated in another, and the clause is the
-- part that is worth remembering.
-- Filtered index: a predicate, and the predicate is the index.
CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX IX_SalesOrder_Open
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (OrderDate)
    INCLUDE (CustomerID, TotalAmount)
    WHERE Status = N'Open';
GO

-- Columnstore: a clustered columnstore replaces the table, and a nonclustered
-- one is added to a row store. The former is for a table read almost entirely
-- as a column; the latter is for a stable subset of a row-store table.
CREATE CLUSTERED COLUMNSTORE INDEX CCI_SalesOrder
    ON dbo.SalesOrder;
GO

-- ONLINE = ON is Enterprise, Developer and Evaluation only, and it is a
-- version-independent edition restriction rather than a 2025 feature.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer REBUILD;
GO

-- A computed column, with and without persistence. A persisted computed column
-- is stored and indexed; a non-persisted one is computed on read and cannot be
-- indexed unless it is deterministic and precise.
ALTER TABLE dbo.Customer
    ADD  CreditBand AS CASE WHEN CreditLimit >= 100000 THEN N'A'
                           WHEN CreditLimit >= 10000  THEN N'B'
                           ELSE N'C'
                    END PERSISTED;
GO

CREATE INDEX IX_Customer_CreditBand ON dbo.Customer (CreditBand) INCLUDE (CreditLimit);
GO

-- A schemabound function, which is a precondition for an indexed view and is
-- what makes the optimiser's inlining rules apply.
CREATE OR ALTER FUNCTION dbo.fn_CustomerOrderTotal (@CustomerID int)
RETURNS decimal(18,2)
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
BEGIN
    DECLARE @Total decimal(18,2);
    SELECT @Total = SUM(TotalAmount) FROM dbo.SalesOrder WHERE CustomerID = @CustomerID;
    RETURN @Total;
END;
GO

-- An indexed view, which requires the unique clustered index and therefore
-- requires the view to be deterministic and schemabound.
CREATE VIEW dbo.vw_OpenOrderTotals
WITH SCHEMABINDING
AS
    SELECT  CustomerID,
            COUNT_BIG(*)      AS OrderCount,
            SUM(TotalAmount)  AS OrderTotal
    FROM    dbo.SalesOrder
    WHERE   Status = N'Open'
    GROUP BY CustomerID;
GO

CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX CIX_vw_OpenOrderTotals ON dbo.vw_OpenOrderTotals (CustomerID);
GO

-- Which is a maintenance cost rather than a free benefit: the index is
-- maintained by every insert, update and delete on the base table, whether or
-- not anybody queries the view.
SELECT  OBJECT_SCHEMA_NAME(object_id) + '.' + name AS ViewName,
        is_indexed,
        is_schemabound
FROM    sys.views
WHERE   is_ms_shipped = 0
ORDER BY is_indexed DESC, ViewName;
GO

-- Partitioning, the two statements and then the switch that is the point.
CREATE PARTITION FUNCTION pf_SalesOrder_Year (date)
AS RANGE RIGHT FOR VALUES ('2024-01-01', '2025-01-01', '2026-01-01', '2027-01-01');
GO

CREATE PARTITION SCHEME ps_SalesOrder_Year
AS PARTITION pf_SalesOrder_Year ALL TO ([FG_2024], [FG_2025], [FG_2026], [FG_2027]);
GO

-- The alignment table, whose boundary values must match the partition
-- function's exactly. A mismatch is error 15002 and the switch is refused.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder
    ADD  SalesYear AS YEAR(OrderDate) PERSISTED;
GO

CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX CIX_SalesOrder_SalesYear
    ON dbo.SalesOrder (SalesYear, SalesOrderID);
GO

-- Switching, which is a metadata operation and takes no locks beyond a brief
-- Sch-M on both tables. This is the operation that makes partitioning worth
-- the complexity: archiving a year is a rename, not a delete.
ALTER TABLE dbo.SalesOrder
    SWITCH PARTITION 3 TO [dbo].[SalesOrder_2026];
GO

SELECT  OBJECT_NAME(object_id) AS TableName,
        partition_number,
        rows,
        CAST(SUM(used_page_count) * 8.0 / 1024 AS decimal(12,1)) AS SizeMB
FROM    sys.dm_db_partition_stats
WHERE   database_id = DB_ID()
GROUP BY object_id, partition_number, rows
ORDER BY object_id, partition_number;
GO

-- Restoring the rows, which is the same statement in the other direction.
ALTER TABLE [dbo].[SalesOrder_2026]
    SWITCH PARTITION 3 TO [dbo].[SalesOrder];
GO

A.2 The DMVs and system views worth knowing

The dynamic management views are the engine’s instrumentation. They are grouped here by the question each one answers, because that is how they are needed at three in the morning, when the chapter indexes are not available.

QuestionViewOne-line purpose
What is running nowsys.dm_exeOne row per request in flight, with its state, blocking session and wait information
Who is connectedsys.dm_exeOne row per session, with login, host, program, transaction count and isolation level
What has run since the plan was compiledsys.dm_execCumulative execution counters per cached statement; divide by executi to make them meaningful
What plans are cachedsys.dm_exec_One row per cached plan with its size and use count; more than one per query indicates sniffing
What is a plansys.dm_execPlan XML for a plan_; the only way to get a plan without executing
What is the textsys.dm_exeText for a sql_h, plan_ or sqltext
Where is it waitingsys.dm_os_Cumulative wait time by type; read as a delta, never as a single sample
What is blockingsys.dm_exec_requestsThe sessi holding the resource; zero when nothing is blocking
What is scheduledsys.dm_os_Scheduler state, runnable task count and worker counts; the basis of the SOS_SCHED diagnosis
How is memory usedsys.dm_os_pr, sys.dm_os_mProcess and clerk memory in use, reserved and allocated
What is the configurationsys.confiEvery instance setting with its configured and in-use value and its dynamic flag
What is the recovery statesys.daRecovery model, log reuse wait, compatibility level, snapshot and ANSI settings, collation
How big is the logsys.dm_db_loTotal and used log space per database, with log_reuse
What indexes existsys.i, sys.indeIndex definition, key columns in order, included columns, filters, disabled state
Are indexes usedsys.dm_db_indSeeks, scans, lookups and updates per index; the evidence for dropping one
How fragmentedsys.dm_db_indexFragmentation, page count and space used per index; LIM is a sample, DETA is complete
What is missingsys.dm_db_missiIndexes the optimiser wished it had; workload-specific and cumulative
How fresh are the statisticssys.dm_db_staRow count, sampled row count and last update time for one statistics object
